The Weight You Carry

The Weight You Carry

Being the third account of Riarhin, sometime scout of the Fenwood army, from the smoking wharves of Silkstrand to a city in a storm that nobody had put on a map; with an expedition she did not intend to lead, and the usual difficulty about coming back.

Sequel to The Thread.

Pack for the way back. — said in Adur-Gah to anybody leaving by the north road

Act I: The Sea

Chapter One: A Berth That Is Not for Sale

The port was working, which in Silkstrand was not the same as the port being open.

Riarhin stood at the head of the Chandlers’ Stair on the second morning after the fire and watched it work. Four streets of the lower city were still smoking behind her, thinly now, the way a fire smokes when it has run out of things it wants and is making do. Below her the quays went on as they had gone on for three hundred years: bales up, casks down, lighters in and out between the moored hulls like dogs working sheep. It looked like trade. It was mostly argument.

The berths had changed hands in the night. That was the first thing she had learned, standing here yesterday, and she had spent the rest of the day confirming it. A quay that had belonged to the Bassaret factors on the morning of the fire belonged, by the evening after, to a man who had bought the Bassaret factor’s debts at a third of their face from a clerk who wanted to be out of the city before anybody asked which books had burned. The man had papers. The harbourmaster’s deputy had stamped them. The harbourmaster had not been seen since the fire, and his deputy was stamping anything that was put in front of him with the air of somebody bailing a boat with a cup.

So every master with a hull afloat had a paper, and every paper had been issued by somebody, and half of the somebodies had ceased to exist between one day and the next. The honest berths were full of people who had booked before the fire and had nowhere else to go. The dishonest ones were being sold twice.

She could steal money. She had done it in this city before she could read. She could steal a document, or make one that would pass a deputy with a cup. She could not steal a wind, and she could not make an overloaded vessel ride any higher in the water by having the right papers for it, and those were the two problems in front of her, and she had been looking at them since dawn.

Na’ar stood beside her with his arms folded and looked at the sea.

He had never been on it. He had told her so at the top of the stair yesterday, in the same voice he used for the weather and for the quality of bread, and had then spent a quarter of an hour watching a lighter come across the harbour under two oars with the concentration of a man studying a horse he might buy.

“It moves,” he said now.

“Yes.”

“All of it. Not only the boats. The whole floor.”

“That’s the tide.”

He considered that. “I know the word,” he said. “I did not know it was the whole floor.”

She had looked at three ships yesterday and she had not liked any of them.

The first was a Kahfagian coaster bound south with a cargo of salvaged furniture and a master who was taking passengers at double rates because the passengers could not go back up the hill to where they had lived. He had sold deck space to forty people. The coaster drew a hand’s breadth more water than her marks allowed, and a hand’s breadth on a coaster in the southern run in this season was the difference between being wet for eleven days and being wet for rather longer. Riarhin had watched a family of six climb aboard with a clock and had gone away.

The second was sound. The second was bound north, to Ecktoria and then the Belworth, which was every direction she did not want, and whose master would not turn his bow for any sum because he had a cargo contracted at the other end and a factor there who would ruin him.

The third was not a ship. It was a man on the Long Quay selling berths on a vessel that was, he said, due in on the evening tide. Riarhin had stood behind a bollard for an hour and watched him sell the same four berths to nine different people, and none of the nine had asked to see the ship, because the ship was due in on the evening tide and everybody knew a vessel on the evening tide was a thing you could not inspect at noon.

She had not been angry with him. It was a good trade. There had been a man doing the same thing in the same place when she was eleven.

The Gerlind lay at the end of the old fish quay, where the stone was worst and nobody wanted to moor, and she was not selling anything.

That was why Riarhin had noticed her. On a morning when every hull in the port had a man at the foot of its plank calling a price, the Gerlind had a man at the foot of her plank who was mending a block and did not look up when people went past. Her crew were aboard and working. No cargo was going on or coming off. She rode light and square and her lines were fast in a way that suggested somebody had looked at the state of the fish quay and decided not to trust it.

She was a Vilikari build, long and narrow for the southern coast, with a high bow and a stern cut square. Somebody had painted her once, a long time ago, and had not done it again because they preferred to spend the money on other things. The other things were visible. Her rigging was new. Her pumps were greased. On her rail, near the bow, a small dark bird sat with its feathers pulled in and its head turned into the wind, and did not move when a gull came down beside it.

“Storm petrel,” Riarhin said.

Na’ar looked at the bird, then at her.

“They are supposed to be out at sea,” she said. “When they come in, weather is coming. That one is not waiting for weather.”

“Then what is it waiting for?”

“Its master.”

He looked at the bird for a little longer and then said, “Ah,” in the tone of a man who had been told a thing about a country and was prepared to believe it until shown otherwise.

The captain was a Vilikari of perhaps forty, broad in the chest and short in the leg, with fair hair gone ash at the temples and worn long, in three braids down the back. The left braid had knots tied in it at intervals. Riarhin counted them without meaning to and got to eleven before he turned round.

He did not ask what she wanted. He looked at her, and then at Na’ar, and then at the bundle over her shoulder with the shape of a sword in it, and said in Vilikari-accented Silkstrand:

“I am not selling berths.”

“I noticed. It’s why I came.”

“Then you have come to buy one I am not selling.” He put the block down on a coil of line. “My name is Varek. This is the Gerlind. I am going south as soon as I have done a thing I have to do, and not before, and I am not taking passengers, and there are two berths aft that I am not using. What are you offering me?”

“Not silver.”

“Good. I have silver. The city is full of people offering me silver.” He wiped his hands. “What?”

“I want to look at your ship first.”

That got a reaction. Nothing much: the eyebrows went up a little and one corner of the mouth, and behind him a sailor who had been pretending not to listen laughed and turned it into a cough.

“Look, then,” said Varek, and stood aside.

She looked.

She went down into the hold, which was clean and nearly empty and smelled of pitch and old rope and not of bilge, which was the first thing. She put her hand on the keelson and it was dry. She found the pump well and put a stick down it and brought the stick up, and it was wet to about two fingers, which on a ship her age was the right answer and not an answer somebody had arranged for her. She looked at the water casks, which were full, and at the bread, which was new, and at the cable, which had been turned end for end within the year.

She came up and looked at the standing rigging from the deck, and then went up the ratlines far enough to look at the shrouds where they came to the mast, and put her thumb on a seizing and found it tight.

She came down. The crew had stopped pretending not to watch.

Varek had sat down on a hatch cover with his forearms on his knees and was watching her with frank enjoyment.

“Well?”

“She’s sound.”

“I know she is sound.”

“You’d know it. I didn’t.” She sat down on the rail, which put her a few feet from the petrel. It turned its head and looked at her and turned it back. “There are thirty hulls in this harbour and twenty-six of them are carrying more than they should because the fire made everybody’s cargo into somebody else’s. You’re carrying nothing. You’re not selling. You’ve got fresh bread and full casks and you’ve been ready to sail for two days at least, and you haven’t.”

“Three,” Varek said.

“Three. So there’s something you have to do before you go, and you can’t do it, and every day it isn’t done is a day you’re paying a crew to mend blocks on a quay that’s falling into the sea.”

He looked at her for a moment without the enjoyment.

“That is a good deal of reasoning,” he said, “from a stick in a pump well.”

“It’s reasoning from the pump well, and the bread, and the bird,” said Riarhin. “The stick just told me you’re honest about your bilge.”

He took them aft, out of the wind, to a cabin the width of the ship and not much deeper than a man lying down, and poured something clear from a stone bottle into two cups, and did not pour one for Na’ar until Na’ar had sat, which Riarhin noted as good manners of an old sort.

“First,” he said. “What are you carrying out of this city?”

She had expected it and had decided on the stair what to give him.

She took the packet out of her coat. It was oilcloth folded twice and tied, and inside it were the nine leaves from Serafine’s fifth press-file, with the knife-stub and the Marcott chandler and the Oshiiran saddler, and three accounts of one object in three hands. She put it on the table between them and put her hand flat on it and left it there.

Varek looked at the packet. He looked at her hand on it. He did not reach.

“Paper,” he said.

“Paper.”

“Out of a burning house.”

She said nothing.

“It is not a deed. You would not carry a deed so near the skin, you would put it with a banker. It is not money. Money you would have spent already on one of the twenty-six.” He turned his cup on the table. “It is work. Somebody’s work, that you are taking away from where they did it. You do not want it read on my ship. You want it to arrive.”

“Yes.”

“Where?”

“South. Mbaro, to begin.”

“To begin.” He drank. “All right. I do not need to know the end. I have enough ends of my own.”

That was all he asked about it. She watched him not ask and filed it.

“Now I,” said Varek.

He put the cup down and put both hands on the table, the braided side of his head turned a little toward her, as though it were a thing she ought to be looking at while he talked.

“I am a captain of Raéyn,” he said. “You know what that is?”

“I know what it means in Kahfagia. I’ve heard it said differently in Linn.”

“It means the same everywhere and they say it differently because the sea is different.” He touched the knotted braid. “She keeps me. She keeps this ship. I have come through weather that should have had us, twice, and I did not do it on my own, and I do not pretend I did. And when she wants something done, she says so, and I do it. That is the whole of the arrangement. It is not a hard arrangement to understand. It is hard to keep.”

“And she’s said so.”

“She has said so.” He did not say how. Riarhin did not ask. “Two days’ sail south of here, on the shore, there is a sanctuary. It is a landing and a cistern and a store, and it has been a landing and a cistern and a store for longer than Silkstrand has had a Censor, and under it there are rooms cut into the rock, and in the deepest of them there is an idol of her son.”

He did not say the name. Riarhin knew it anyway, and understood that he knew she did.

“I am to break it,” said Varek. “Myself.”

“Why now?”

“I do not know why now. I did not ask why now. It is a service. When it is asked, you go.” He shrugged. “If you want a guess: the fire. Half the shipping on this coast is going south this month with too much in it, and there are people on that shore who keep their god fed with what the sea brings them. That is a guess. It is not what I was told.”

“What were you told?”

He looked at her, and she saw him decide something.

“That it must be my hand,” he said. “Not my crew’s. Not a hired man’s. Not a stone thrown from a boat. It is not enough that it be broken. I must be the one to break it, and I must be in the room when I do it.”

She asked the next questions carefully, one at a time, and he answered them.

The sanctuary was called Kessary by people who wanted to sell it things and something else by the people who lived there. It stood on a headland with a landing on the south side, in a cove, and above the landing a cistern cut into the rock that took rainwater off the whole headland and held it through the dry months. Ships called for water. Labourers came and went, hired from the villages along the coast for the cistern work and the stores, and were paid in coin and in water. The custodians of the sanctuary kept a count of every soul who went in and every soul who came out, which Varek said with a flatness that told her he found it worse than anything else he had to say.

His people had tried twice, in the three days. Not to break anything; to look. A boat had gone round the headland by night to see whether there was a way in from the sea side, and there was, a cut in the rock at the waterline that opened on the lower rooms at low water, and it had a lantern on it and two men with nothing to do but watch it. A sailor of his had gone ashore at the landing to buy water, and had been asked his ship and his master and had lied well, and had watched the custodian at the water-house write the lie down in a book and look at him afterwards in a way he had not liked.

“They know what I am,” said Varek. “Not me. My kind. They have known for a very long time that the Mistress of the Sea has captains, and that the captains are sent, and they have built for it. The sea entrance is for us. It is a door they have left open because they want a captain of Raéyn to use it.”

“And the landing?”

“The landing is watched and written. And the idol is not near the sea at all. It is at the back of the rock, away from the weather, where the air does not move.” He turned his cup. “They have thought about this for longer than I have been alive. I am good at a ship. I am no use in a cellar. And they will know a captain when they see one. I do not know how. I have heard it said they can smell the salt on us that is not on other men.”

“They can see your braid,” said Na’ar.

It was the first thing he had said since they came aft. Varek looked at him. Na’ar looked back with polite interest.

“They can see my braid,” Varek agreed after a moment. “Yes.”

Riarhin sat with it.

A headland. A landing, watched. A sea door, baited. A cistern, which meant labour, which meant men going in and out who were not custodians and who were counted but not known. A count kept in a book, which meant a number that had to come out right at the end of the day. An idol at the back of the rock where the air did not move.

And a man who must be in the room, and whose hand it must be, and who was recognisable as what he was from across a quay.

She could get into a place like that. She had got into worse. She could get to the idol, very likely, and she could break it; she could carry it out under her coat, if it was small, and put it on his table and let him put a hammer to it on his own deck. She could see at once that none of that was the job.

“If I bring it out to you,” she said, “and you break it here?”

“No.”

“If I break it and you’re standing at the door?”

“In the room,” said Varek. “My hand.”

“If I carry you in on my back like a sack of grain—”

“You may carry me in however you like,” he said, and smiled for the first time since they had sat down. “So long as I am in the room at the end of it, and awake, and it is my hand.”

She thought of every door she had ever gone through, and how few of them she had gone through with a companion who could not look like anything but what he was.

“Why me?”

“Because you went down my hold with a stick,” Varek said, “and nobody else on this quay has looked at anything since the fire. And because you have got a sword in a bundle and you are carrying it the way a woman carries a thing she does not want to have to explain. And because I have tried twice with my own people, and they are good people, and they are sailors, and they came back and told me what they saw and none of them could tell me what it meant.” He spread his hands. “I do not know what you are. You are somebody who goes into places. I need somebody who goes into places.”

“And if I say no?”

“Then you find another ship. There are twenty-six. One of them will go south.” He poured again. “And I sit on this quay until I think of something, or until she stops waiting. I do not know which is worse.”

She took her time answering, because he would expect her to and because she wanted it.

“Here’s what you get,” she said. “I’ll get you into the room. Getting in is the part I’m for. I’ll get you out of it again if I can, and your crew back to the ship, and I’ll tell you before we go what I think it will cost, and if I’m wrong about the cost I’ll tell you that too, as soon as I know. I won’t break it for you. I won’t touch it if I can help it. When it’s done you carry the two of us to Mbaro, and you feed us on the way, and you don’t read my paper and you don’t let anybody else.”

“And if you get me into the room and I fail?”

“Then that’s your end of it.”

He thought about that and nodded as though it were fair, which it was.

“Mbaro,” he said. “And food. My cook is bad. I tell you now so you cannot say you were not told.”

“I’ll cook,” said Na’ar.

They both looked at him.

He had been sitting with his cup untouched and his hands loose on his knees, listening to the whole of it with the attention he gave to things that interested him, which was a great deal of attention and very little expression.

“I am coming,” he said to Varek. “I will tell you now so it is said. I am not coming because she has said yes. She said yes for herself. I am coming because I have not been on a ship and I would like to, and because a room under the ground with a god in it that the sea does not like sounds like a thing I would be sorry to have missed.” He considered. “And because your cook is bad. I heard him this morning. A man who shouts at onions does not respect them.”

Varek looked at him for a long moment.

Then he took the knotted braid in his fingers and ran them down it, one knot to the next, the way another man might count coins, and let it go.

“Good,” he said. “Then it is three of us who have said it.”

They sailed on the evening tide.

The false berth-seller was still on the Long Quay when they cast off, calling the ship that was due in, and there were more people round him than there had been at noon. Riarhin watched him from the Gerlind’s rail as the fish quay slid away, and then watched the city go up behind him, grey on grey, the smoke of four streets standing up into a windless sky.

Somewhere up there, at the top of the hill where the Belworth road went out, a column of wagons was being told what it could and could not carry. She did not look for it. She knew the direction, and that was enough, and it would still be there in the morning whether she looked or not.

Her cloak was grey. It had been grey in Silkstrand all her life. It would be something else by the time she took it off again, and nobody would remember.

Na’ar stood at the bow with his feet wide apart and his hand on the stay, and the petrel sat a yard from his fist and did not move, and the Gerlind put her shoulder into the first of the swell beyond the mole and lifted.

He did not say anything. He laughed, once, from the belly, the way he had laughed at Aryka in a tavern in Kolmar, the way a man laughs when a thing turns out to be exactly as large as he had hoped.

Varek at the tiller looked forward at him and then at Riarhin.

“He will be sick,” he said.

“Probably.”

“Tomorrow. Not tonight. Tonight he is happy.” He put the tiller over a hand’s breadth and the ship answered. “I like him. I do not yet know if I like you.”

“That’s all right,” Riarhin said. “Neither do most people.”

“That is not what I meant,” said Varek. “I meant I do not know yet.” He looked south, where there was nothing to see. “Two days. Then I will know.”

Chapter Two: The Dry Entrance

Kessary sat on its headland like a man who had sat in the same chair for a very long time and had worn the chair to his shape.

Riarhin lay on her stomach in the thorn scrub on the next point along and watched it for a day and a half.

There was not much of it above ground. A landing on the south side, in a cove with deep water under the rock: a stone quay, a crane, a water-house with a slate roof where a ship’s boat could come in and fill its casks from a pipe in the wall. Above the landing, a road cut back and forth up the face to a gate. Above the gate, nothing but headland, brown and bare and grooved all over with little channels no deeper than a finger, running together into bigger ones, running together again, every one of them tending downhill toward the same place. The whole headland had been cut into a funnel. When it rained on Kessary, every drop that fell went into the rock.

And under the rock, somewhere, was the cistern, and under the cistern the rooms, and in the last room the thing Varek had been sent for.

She did not think about the thing. She thought about the gate.

On the first morning, a ship’s boat came in to the landing at the third hour and filled four casks. A man in a grey coat came out of the water-house with a book and wrote something, and the boat’s master wrote something in the same book, and they both looked at a brass dial on the wall of the water-house while the casks filled, and the master paid in coin and the grey coat wrote that down too.

Water, then, was sold by measure. Measure meant a dial, and a book, and a man who could read it. She liked that. A place that measures everything is a place that trusts its measures, and a place that trusts its measures does not look at anything the measures have already approved.

At the fourth hour, eleven labourers came down the switchback road from the gate with a custodian walking behind them and went along the shore to the village beyond the point, which was a straggle of stone houses and drying racks and one tavern with a vine over the door. At the fifth hour, eleven came back up with a different custodian, and at the gate the custodian stopped them and counted them and wrote it in a book, and they went in.

The same eleven. She checked the faces. They changed shifts at the fourth hour and the tenth, and the count happened at the gate going in and at the gate going out, and the books were carried down to the water-house every evening and something was done with them there.

On the second morning she saw the other kind of traffic.

A cart came along the shore from the village with two mules and a man walking at their heads and three more walking behind. It was loaded with sacking and planks and a long box. At the landing, it stopped at the water-house, and two men in grey came out and walked round it. One of them lifted the sacking and looked under it. The other had the carter put the long box on the ground and opened it. Then the whole cart, box and all, was led onto a platform beside the water-house that she had taken for a loading stage and had been wrong about, and a man in grey read something off a beam and wrote it down.

They weighed it.

She lay in the thorn and felt the shape of the thing come up under her hand, and took a long breath, and let it out, and did not smile, because there was nobody to smile at.

The cart went up the switchback. At the gate it was counted: four men, two mules. Then the cart went in.

It came out at the tenth hour with three men and two mules. It went down to the water-house and was weighed again. The fourth man came out at the evening shift change with the labourers, counted, and went home to the village.

“They weigh what goes in,” she said to Varek that night, in the cabin, “and they weigh what comes out, and the difference had better be what the job used. And they count every soul at the gate and they don’t care what the souls are called, and they don’t care what’s in the box once the box has been looked in and weighed.”

“That is a cistern,” said Varek. “They sell water by weight. Of course they weigh.”

“They weigh water. They weigh carts because somebody, a long time ago, took something out in one.” She turned her cup. “The weighing’s not about you. It’s about theft. They built the sea door for captains. They built the scale for thieves. The two kinds of men they’re expecting don’t come the same way, so they’ve never had to think about one coming dressed as the other.”

He was silent for a while.

“You want to bring me in on a cart.”

“I want to bring you in as something they’ve already weighed and let in a hundred times.”

The work was the hard part, because there was no work.

A cart went up that switchback when the cistern needed something. The cistern did not need anything. It had held water on this headland since before there was a Silkstrand, and the custodians looked after it the way the old look after a thing they are proud of, and there was no gang of masons waiting on the shore for a job that was not coming.

So she would have to make one.

She did not want to damage the cistern. Water that has been stored for the dry season on a coast like this one is not the custodians’ water only; she had watched four boats fill from it in two days, and a village behind the point that had a cistern of its own and a pipe running from the headland into it besides. She did not know who drank from that pipe in a bad year. She could guess.

She went looking for the channels instead.

On the second night she went up over the back of the headland in the dark, where nobody walked, and followed the grooves in the rock downhill the way the rain would follow them, and found where they came together. It was a basin cut in the stone the width of a small room, with a grating at the bottom of it and, below the grating, a shaft going down into the dark with a draught coming up out of it that smelled of wet stone and nothing else.

Around the edge of the basin, set into the rock, were lead flashings where the channels joined it, old and grey and patched, each one dressed over the lip so that the water ran into the basin and not down the side of it. One of the flashings had been patched perhaps ten years ago. The patch had been well done. It had a seam.

Riarhin sat on the edge of the basin for a while with Toothpick in her hand and looked at the seam.

Then she lifted it. Not far. A finger’s width along a hand’s length, enough that the next rain would run under the flashing instead of over it and go down a crack in the rock that did not lead to the cistern at all, but, if she had followed the grain of the stone correctly, to the roof of whatever was built alongside it. She had seen the vents of a storehouse on the headland’s flank, cut into the rock face on the far side from the landing. It would not flood anything. It would weep. There would be a damp patch on a storehouse ceiling, and then a drip, and a custodian who did not like drips.

There was no rain.

She knew there was no rain, and she lifted the seam anyway, and then she went down to the Gerlind and asked Varek how long it would be before there was.

He looked at the sky. He looked at the petrel, which had come down off the rail in the evening and was sitting on the cabin roof with its head under its wing.

“Tomorrow night,” he said. “Not much. A squall off the sea and gone again.”

“Is that you or her?”

He gave her a long look.

“That is me,” he said. “I have been doing this for twenty-six years. I can read a sky without asking her to write it for me.”

It rained for an hour the next night, hard and warm, and blew through.

At the fourth hour of the morning a custodian went along the shore to the village with her grey coat buttoned to the neck and knocked at the door of a man named Aske.

Riarhin was at the tavern by then with a cup of watered wine and a coat that was not her own and a bundle that did not have a sword in it. Her sword was aboard the Gerlind. So was Silence. She had Toothpick in her boot, because Toothpick was always in her boot, and a lead-burner’s roll of irons in her lap that she had bought from a shipwright’s widow in the harbour at Silkstrand with the fire still smoking behind them, because she had thought on the fish quay that she would want something like it before this was done and had not yet known what for.

Aske was a mason. He was about sixty, with forearms like a stevedore’s and a voice he had lost somewhere in a lifetime of lime dust, and he held the headland’s contract for masonry and leadwork because his father had held it and nobody had ever found a reason to take it from him. Riarhin knew all that because she had spent the day before sitting in his tavern listening to other people complain about him. He was not dishonest. He was slow, and he was dear, and he always had been, and the custodians paid him because in forty years nothing he had repaired had needed repairing twice.

He came in to the tavern after the custodian had gone and sat down heavily at the next table and ordered bread.

“Bad news?” said Riarhin.

He looked at her. She watched him try to work out whether he knew her.

“Leak,” he said. “Flashing on the high basin. Wept into the east store. The Mother wants it looked at this morning.” He tore bread. “Lead on the high basin. That’s a two-man job. You burn it from above and you dress it from the channel and you can’t be in both places.”

“Where’s your second?”

“In Silkstrand.” He chewed. “Went up to see his sister a month back. Sister lived in the Dye Yards.” He did not say anything else about that, and Riarhin did not make him.

She waited a moment.

Then she put the roll of irons on the table, and untied it, and let it fall open.

It was not the irons that did it. Aske looked at them and knew them for a dead man’s set, well kept and a little old-fashioned, and knew that a woman carrying them in a tavern the week after the fire could be anybody.

It was that she knew what the job was.

She had spent an hour on the high basin in the dark with her hands on that flashing, and she could describe the patch and the seam and how the lead had been dressed over the lip better than a man who had not been up there yet, and she said all of it in the flat, dull voice of somebody talking shop, as though it were the most boring thing in the world. She said the joint had been burned from above and the seam had failed at the upper end where the channel ran hardest. She said it wanted a new piece let in and not another patch on the patch. She said that if it were her she’d bring up a whole sheet and not trust the old lead, because lead that has sat on a rock for a hundred summers goes crystalline and cracks when you work it.

Aske looked at her the whole time and did not eat.

“Where did you learn that?” he said at the end.

“On roofs,” said Riarhin, which was true. She had been on a great many roofs. She had seen a great deal of lead, and she had watched it being burned in four countries from positions where nobody knew she was watching, and she remembered every one of them because she had been attending closely to the man with the iron at the time, for reasons that had nothing to do with lead.

“And you want what?”

“A day’s wage. And a lift south, if anybody’s going.”

He grunted. “Nobody here’s going anywhere. You’ll want a ship.”

“There’s a ship,” she said. “There’s one in the next cove. Vilikari. They’d have gone already but they’re short of water, and they won’t buy it here.”

He looked at her.

“Why not?”

“They don’t like the price.”

He thought about that and then laughed, a rasp with lime in it.

“Nobody likes the price,” he said. “Bring your irons.”

She did not have to invent the sheet of lead. Aske did not keep sheet; he had a village mason’s stock of scrap, and it was all old, and she had already told him what old lead does.

So she told him where there was new.

The Gerlind had come out of Silkstrand with a crate of it in her hold because Varek had been meaning to reflash his deckhouse for two years and a chandler on the Long Quay had been selling everything he had in the week after the fire before somebody took it off him. Riarhin had seen the crate on her first morning with the stick in the pump well. She had not known then what it was for. She had noted it the way she noted most things, and had set it aside.

Aske sent a boy along the shore with a note.

The boy came back with the Gerlind’s mate and a price. It was a fair price, a little high, the price of a Vilikari who does not like being asked. Aske paid it with his own money and swore about it, and Riarhin wrote nothing down anywhere, which was the whole of her contribution to that part of the morning.

The crate was carried ashore in the ship’s boat by four sailors and put on Aske’s cart. It was a long box with rope handles and a lid pinned with wooden pegs, about the length of a man lying down, because that is the size lead sheet comes in when it has been rolled for carriage and not cut.

On top, under the lid, there was lead: a roll of it, grey and new-bright where the chandler’s knife had trimmed it, heavy enough that it took both sailors at that end.

Under the lead there was a board.

Under the board there was Varek.

She had worked it out on the Gerlind the night before, with the ship’s scales, and a bag of sand, and the captain lying in the bottom of the crate with his knees drawn up and an expression of patient disgust.

The difficulty was not hiding him. Any fool can hide a man in a box. The difficulty was that the water-house would weigh the cart going in, and the weight had to be the weight of a sheet of lead, and a man is a good deal lighter than the lead that would fill the same box. If they opened the lid and saw lead and then read a weight from the beam that was a sack of meal short of a crate of lead, they would not need to think about captains. They would think about thieves, because they had built their scale for thieves, and they would take the lead out and look.

So she took most of the lead out and put Varek in, and then she put the lead back on top of him in a roll that looked like a whole sheet from above and was a third of one, and she made up the difference in the only way that would not show, which was by making the crate itself heavier. Four sailors spent the night pegging iron ballast to the underside of its floor, where no custodian lifting a lid would ever look, and planing the floorboards back smooth over it.

The Gerlind was a ship. Ships carry iron under their floors. Nobody on board thought this was clever.

What she had to be right about was the weight of a sheet of lead that size, as it would be read off a custodian’s beam by a custodian who had read a great many; and she had had to guess it from the one crate of lead she had seen come in on the second morning, and the weight she had seen the grey coat write down after it; and she had seen that from four hundred yards through thorn scrub.

She had seen it. She had attended to it. That was the part she could do.

The rest was a mason’s cart and a chandler’s box and a quantity of iron in the right place, and a captain of Raéyn lying very still in the bottom of it under a third of a sheet of lead, with a cup of water, and his braid wrapped twice round his fist so that it would not be in the way of anything.

Na’ar drove the mules.

Aske owned the cart and did not own the mules; they were hired by the day from a carter in the village who was not going up the headland on account of a bad knee and would be very glad, he said, of a big man to take them. Na’ar agreed to take them for a copper less than the carter asked, which made the carter trust him at once. He walked at the mules’ heads down the shore road in the dust with the sun on his back and a straw hat he had bought that morning, and the mules walked beside him the way animals walk beside a man who knows what he is doing and does not need to tell them.

Riarhin walked behind the cart with Aske and the irons.

At the water-house a custodian in grey came out and walked round the cart.

She lifted the sacking and looked under it: planks, a bucket, a brazier, two sacks of charcoal. She looked at Riarhin, and Aske told her who Riarhin was, and she wrote in her book.

The second custodian had the crate taken off the cart and set on the stones and had the lid pulled. He looked in. He put his hand on the lead and pressed it, and lifted one edge of the roll with his fingers, and let it fall back.

Riarhin stood beside Aske and thought about roofs.

The custodian nodded. The lid went back on. The crate went back on the cart and the cart went onto the platform. A third man in grey stood at the beam and moved a weight along it, a notch, another notch, back half a notch, and stopped.

He read. He wrote.

He said, without looking up, “Lead’s gone up.”

“Everything’s gone up,” said Aske. “The city burned.”

The man grunted and waved them on.

Four men went up the switchback. Aske, and the carter’s big man with the mules, and a woman with a roll of lead-burner’s irons, and a boy of Aske’s to carry charcoal. At the gate a custodian counted them and wrote four in the book, and two mules, and a cart with a weighed crate on it, and they went in.

The gate opened on a court cut back into the rock, open to the sky, with the upper stores round two sides of it and a gallery going back into the headland on the third. The mules would go no further than the court; there was a ramp with a rope and a windlass for the loads, and the high basin was reached by stairs cut up the side of the court to the top of the headland.

The cistern itself was in the gallery. You could feel it before you saw anything, the air going cool and heavy as you came under the rock, and a sound that was not quite a sound, that was the size of water held in a room larger than a church and not moving.

Na’ar stopped the mules in the court and stood with them and looked round, pleasantly, at nothing.

There was an overseer at the mouth of the gallery with a book of his own. He had a long face and a wart on the side of his nose and he was talking to one of the labourers about a slate that needed moving, and he did not look at them. Riarhin looked at him, and at the three passages that went off the gallery behind him, and at the bell on a bracket above the gallery mouth.

“He moves the crew,” Na’ar said, very quietly, in Ykrul, not turning his head. “When the bell goes. The carter told me. All the labourers are counted out through the lower way to the landing, so nobody is left in the rock who should not be, and the custodians go in behind. His name is Tobin. He is not a custodian. He is paid by the day like the rest.”

Riarhin said nothing, and did not look at Tobin again.

They took the crate up to the high basin with the windlass and a good deal of swearing from Aske, because a crate of lead going up a ramp on a rope is not a thing anybody enjoys, and at the top Riarhin and the boy and Na’ar manhandled it along the channel to the basin’s edge while Aske lit the brazier.

For an hour they did the job.

That was the part she had been most certain about and was most glad of, afterwards: that they did the job. She cut the old flashing out and Aske measured for the new. The roll came out of the crate and was laid on the rock and cut, and the boy fed charcoal, and Aske burned a joint along the lip while she dressed the lead into the channel with a boxwood mallet, and he watched her do it, and grunted, and did not say anything, which from Aske was high praise. Under the board, under their feet in the crate at the basin’s edge, the captain of Raéyn lay without moving and listened to them mend a thing he had come to break.

She had meant to take him down from there. There was a way; she had seen it on the second night. The grating at the bottom of the basin lifted for cleaning, and under it the shaft went down into the cistern’s upper gallery, and from the upper gallery there were stairs, and at the foot of the stairs was the inner gate, and beyond the inner gate was the rest of Kessary.

What she had not known, what she could not have known from four hundred yards of thorn, was that a custodian came up at the end of every repair to look.

The custodian came up the stair from the court at the second hour after noon.

She was perhaps fifty, with iron-grey hair cropped close and a grey coat with a key on a cord at the throat, and she walked like a woman who had walked this headland every day of her adult life and did not intend to be hurried by it. The labourers in the court had stood up when she crossed it. Aske stood up when she came over the top of the stair, and took his hat off, and called her Mother Hesper.

She looked at the new flashing for a long time.

She looked at the old flashing where Riarhin had laid it on the rock, the cut piece with the lifted seam, and turned it over with her foot.

She looked at the crate.

Riarhin went on dressing lead.

Mother Hesper stooped and picked a trimming off the rock, a curl of new lead a finger long where Riarhin’s knife had cut the sheet, and turned it to the light. On the inside of the curl, where it had been rolled against itself and not seen daylight until an hour ago, a stamp had been struck into the metal at the foundry: a ring with a bar through it.

“This isn’t Hullen lead,” she said.

Aske looked at the trimming. He looked at Riarhin. Riarhin went on dressing, and felt the whole of the afternoon go very quietly out from under her.

“It’s from Silkstrand,” said Aske. “Chandler’s stock. The ship—”

“Every sheet on this headland for forty years has been Hullen lead, Master Aske. Your father bought Hullen. You buy Hullen. It comes by Hullen’s boat and we take it at Hullen’s price and we write it at Hullen’s weight.” She held up the curl. “This is Silkstrand sheet. It’s thinner. A roll of this weighs less than a roll of ours the same size.”

She turned and looked at the crate, and then at the stair down to the court, where the beam and the book were.

“Yet it weighed in at ours,” she said.

There are a few seconds in every bad moment where the whole of it can go either way, and Riarhin had spent most of her life learning what to do with them.

She had not known about Hullen. She could not have known about Hullen; nobody had said the name in the tavern, and the stamp had been on the inside of a roll. The woman had caught a thing that was there to be caught and that Riarhin had never been given a chance to see. There was no cleverness in it and no error. She was right, and it was her headland, and she had caught it.

So the only question was what she thought she had caught.

Riarhin put the mallet down and stood up slowly and wiped her hands on her thighs, and looked at the crate, and then at Aske, and then at the ground between her own feet, and let the colour come up into her face.

She did not say anything. That was the whole of the art: not to say anything, and let a clever woman who had been running a count for thirty years do the rest.

Mother Hesper looked at her.

“Where’s the rest of it?” she said.

Riarhin did not answer.

“You brought a roll that weighs at ours. It’s lighter than ours. So you’ve made up the weight with something, and you meant to go out tonight with lead scrap at the weight of lead scrap, and whatever you made up the weight with, you meant to leave here.” Mother Hesper’s voice did not rise. “Or you meant to take it out full and weigh it out empty and nobody reads a scrap cart twice. What was it? Our lead? The old flashings off the low basin? Have you been at the low basin, Master Aske?”

“I haven’t been at anything,” said Aske. He had gone the colour of the lime dust. He looked at Riarhin with a horror that was entirely real, the horror of a man who had held a contract for forty years without a word against him and had just discovered what hiring a stranger in a tavern felt like.

Riarhin looked away from him. It cost her something to do it. She did it.

“Search the cart,” said Mother Hesper to the boy, who fled. And to Aske: “You’ll stay up here. So will she. I’m going down to the book.”

She turned for the stair, and turned back.

“And the big one,” she said. “The carter’s man. He can stay with the mules. Nobody leaves the rock.”

She went down.

Riarhin counted her steps on the stair, and got to eleven, and heard her voice in the court below calling for the gallery book, and for Tobin.

She had a few minutes. Not many. A woman like that did not go down to her book to read it. She went down to read it against something, and when she had done that she would have the numbers, and the numbers would be wrong in a way that was much more interesting than theft.

Riarhin knelt down beside the crate and put her hand flat on the lid.

“Captain,” she said.

Under the lead, very quietly, somebody drew a breath.

Chapter Three: The Room Beneath the Water

She had the pegs out of the lid in the time it took Aske to understand what she was doing, and the lead off in the time it took him to start forward, and by then it did not matter what he understood.

Varek came up out of the crate like a man coming up out of cold water: all at once, and badly. He had lain in the dark for five hours with his knees against his chest and a sheet of lead a hand’s breadth over his face, listening to two strangers burn a joint above him, and his legs did not work. He got a hand on the edge of the basin and hung there with his mouth open.

Aske stood with the mallet in his fist and stared at him.

“You’ll want to sit down,” Riarhin said to the mason.

“Who—”

“Nobody you hired. Nobody you’ve seen.” She had the grating by its bars and was lifting it, and it came up out of its seat with a grind of iron on stone and a breath of wet air out of the shaft beneath. “Sit down on the rock, Master Aske, and put your hands where the Mother can see them when she comes back, and tell her everything that’s true. That the woman in the tavern asked for a day’s wage. That you bought the lead off a ship and paid for it with your own money. That you’ve never seen him in your life.”

“She’ll think I—”

“For an hour,” said Riarhin. “After that she’ll know better, and she’ll know it from things you can’t have done.”

He looked at her. He looked at the captain of Raéyn hanging off the lip of the high basin with his braid wrapped round his fist. And then he did the bravest thing anybody did on that headland all day, which was to sit down on the rock with his hands on his knees and wait, when every part of him must have wanted to run down the stair and shout.

She would think about that later. She did not have time now.

The shaft went down twenty feet on iron rungs set in the rock, slick with the night’s rain, and let them out onto a ledge above the cistern.

Riarhin had known it would be large. She had not known it would be like this.

It was a room. That was the first thing and the worst thing. It was not a cave that somebody had filled with water; it had been cut, all of it, square, with a roof held up on rows of pillars that went down into the black and did not come up again anywhere she could see. The ledge they stood on ran along one wall with a rail of iron at its edge. Below the rail was water. It went away under the pillars in every direction, flat and dark and entirely still, and the light from the shaft above lay on it in a grey square and did not move at all.

There was no sound. There should have been drips. The roof was wet and there were drops hanging from it, she could see them, and none of them fell.

Varek stood on the ledge beside her and breathed through his mouth.

“Is it always like this?” she said, low.

“I have never been in it.” His voice was as low as hers. “I have been in the sea at night and I have been in a hold with the hatches battened in a gale. It is not like either.” He put his hand on the rail. “There is no weather in here. None.”

She had never heard a sailor say it and she understood what he meant at once. The air in the cistern did not move. It did not move in the way that the air in a closed room does not move, which is still a little; it did not move the way water at the bottom of the sea does not move, because there is nothing at all anywhere around it that is going anywhere. She had been in tombs that felt livelier.

The ledge went left. They went left.

There were people in the lower galleries and they were working.

Riarhin had expected custodians and found labourers: three men and a woman at the foot of a stair where the ledge came down to a lower level, sitting on buckets round a lamp and eating bread. They wore the same clothes as the eleven she had watched go up the switchback. They had a sledge of slates beside them and a coil of rope, and one of them was mending the rope while he ate.

They looked up when Riarhin came down the stair, and then at Varek, and then went back to the bread.

Nobody asked who they were. Nobody asked why they had come down from the high basin, where there was no stair, and not from the gate. The woman offered Riarhin the heel of the loaf, and Riarhin took it because a woman who refuses bread in a place like that is a woman who has something else on her mind, and ate it.

“Bell hasn’t gone,” the man with the rope said. He did not look up from the rope.

“No,” said Riarhin.

“Goes, and you’ll want the lower way.” He jerked his head down the gallery. “Tobin counts at the landing stair. Don’t be last. Mother doesn’t like last.”

“Thank you.”

He nodded. He tied off a splice and pulled it and looked at it and began another. Behind him, at the foot of the gallery wall, there was a puddle on the stone, and in the puddle there was a footprint, bare, a big man’s foot, the heel deep and the toes splayed.

As Riarhin watched, the heel went deeper.

It did it slowly. The water in the puddle did not stir. Nobody stood there. The print sank a little further into the wet stone as though somebody very heavy had shifted his weight onto it, and then stopped, and was a footprint again.

The woman with the bread saw her looking and followed the look to the puddle and then back to Riarhin’s face.

“That’s all right,” she said, kindly, as one would say it to a child who was frightened of a dog. “That’s just him. You don’t mind him.” She tore more bread. “He doesn’t come up past the third stair.”

They went down past the third stair.

Riarhin went in front. She had meant to all along; it was the part she was for. She went down a corridor cut out of the rock no wider than her shoulders, and then a second one, and then a flight of steps with every tread worn to a dish, and the air got colder and heavier and it did not get any darker, which should have been impossible and was not.

The sounds began at the third stair.

They came from behind the stone. Not in it; behind it, as though the rock were a wall a hand’s breadth thick and on the other side of it there was a great deal more of something. A knocking, far off. A sound like a door closing in a house the size of a country. A long slow creak that went on after it should have stopped, the sound of a ship’s timbers in a heavy sea, the sound of a hull working. There was no sea here. The sea was a quarter of a mile away through the rock and the tide was out.

Varek stopped on the stair and put his hand flat on the wall.

He did not say anything. He stood there with his palm on the stone and his eyes half shut the way she had seen men in the hills stand with their hands on the ground listening for horses, and his face did not change, and after a while he took his hand away and wiped it on his coat, slowly, as though there were something on it.

“He is here,” he said. That was all.

They went on.

She found the room at the bottom.

It was not large. After the cistern it seemed almost nothing: a chamber perhaps twelve paces across, cut square like everything else in Kessary, with a low roof and a floor of wet black stone that sloped very slightly toward the centre. There was a lamp in a niche. There was a bench along one wall, and on the bench a folded blanket and a cup, as though somebody sat here often and for a long time.

At the far end, on a plinth no higher than her knee, was the idol.

She had expected something to look at, and there was very little. It was a figure of dark stone, about the height of a seated child, and it was crouched, or folded, or it had been carved in a shape that did not quite settle into either. It was wet. The whole of it was wet, shining in the lamplight, although the air in the room was cold and nothing dripped on it. It had no face she could find. It had a mouth. She did not look at the mouth for long.

The air in the room pressed on her.

It was the only word for it. It pressed on the eyes and on the ears and on the joints of the fingers, a steady, even, unhurried weight, the way water presses on a swimmer who has gone down a little too far and is deciding whether to go further. It did not hurt. It was only there, all over her, and it had been there, she understood, since before she came into the room and would be there when she left it.

She could break it.

She stood in the doorway and knew it as certainly as she knew anything. She had Toothpick. She had her hands. She had been in the room for the time it takes to draw a breath and she was alone with it, and it was a thing of stone the size of a child on a plinth the height of her knee, and whatever it was, it had not been made to stand up to a woman who knew how to put a blade into a seam. She could see the seam. There was a flaw in the stone at the thing’s back, where the carving went in under a fold, and if she put the point of Toothpick in there and leaned—

And then it would be broken, and it would not have been his hand, and none of it would count.

She stood in the doorway for a moment longer than she needed to, knowing it, and then she turned round and went back up the stairs for the captain.

The bell went when she was halfway up.

It came down through the rock from very far away, a flat iron note, and then another, and then a third, and the sounds behind the stone stopped all at once, as though whatever was making them had heard it too and was listening.

Then another sound. Nearer. A grinding.

She knew it before she saw it: iron on stone, weight coming down. She took the last flight at a run and came out at the head of the stairs into the corridor, and there at the end of it, thirty feet off, between her and Varek, a gate was coming down.

It was a grille of iron bars as thick as her wrist, set in a slot in the rock, and it had been hanging in the roof of the corridor where she had walked under it on the way down without seeing it, because she had been looking at the floor and the walls and the footing and the way ahead, and had not looked up. She had not looked up. She had walked under it and filed the corridor as narrow, dry, one way, and the corridor had not been one way at all. It had been a door, left open.

Mother Hesper had done her reading.

She had gone down to her book and set the count against the weight and the weight against Hullen’s price, and then against a Vilikari ship in the next cove that would not buy water, and a crate carried ashore from that ship by four sailors, and a Silkstrand stamp; and she had come up with something that was not theft at all. And she had not sent everybody she had to the bottom of the rock to catch it. She had done the thing this place had been built to do. She had rung the bell and dropped the gates and shut the rock, and let whatever was in it be in it.

Varek was on the far side of the grille. He had seen it coming and was running for it, and he was not going to make it; the bars were at the height of his chest and coming down steadily on a chain that ran up over a sheave in the roof and down again on Riarhin’s side of the corridor, into a slot in the wall, where there would be a counterweight, and the counterweight had been let go.

She went for the chain.

She got it with both hands at the height of her head and it tore through them and she held on. It took the skin off her palms in one long stripe and she held on. The weight on the other end of it was more than hers and she felt her feet go on the wet stone and she let them go and hung, her whole body on the chain, and the gate came down another hand’s breadth and slowed and came down another and stopped, a yard off the floor.

She was on the wrong side of it. She had known that the moment she took the chain. The gate had been hung to be held open from the far side, the side from which custodians would come down; the slot and the sheave and the counterweight were all arranged so that a man standing where Varek stood could take the chain and walk it back up. From where she was there was nothing to brace against and no way to take the weight but to hang it off her own body.

Her shoulder made a sound she did not like.

“Under,” she said. It did not come out loudly. It did not need to.

Varek was already down. He went under the grille on his back with his heels driving him along the wet stone, and the bars passed a hand’s breadth over his face the way the lead had passed over it all morning, and he came out on her side and rolled and was up.

She let go.

The gate came down the last yard with a noise she felt in her teeth, and the chain went up through her torn hands and over the sheave and was gone into the roof, and somewhere in the wall a weight she would never see hit the bottom of its slot.

She stood there with her hands open in front of her and looked at them. Blood was running off her fingers onto the stone. Her left arm did not want to come up.

“Your hands,” said Varek.

“Later.” She turned. “Down. It’s at the bottom. Nobody’s with it.”

Nobody was with it. The lamp still burned in its niche. The cup still stood on the bench beside the folded blanket. The thing on its plinth sat wet in the lamplight with its mouth, and the pressure of the room came down on them both, evenly, and did not care.

Varek stopped in the doorway, as she had.

She watched his face. She had seen men look at a thing they had been told about for years and finally found, and she had seen men look at a thing they were afraid of. This was neither. He looked at the idol the way a man looks at a relation he does not speak to.

Then he went in.

He did not draw a weapon. He had a belaying pin through his belt, the kind sailors carry because it is to hand and nobody ever asks why, and he did not touch it yet. He went to the plinth and stood in front of the thing and lifted his left hand to the braid that hung down over his shoulder, and took hold of the last knot in it, the lowest one, the one nearest the end.

He began to untie it.

It took time. It had been tied a long while ago and pulled tight and wet and dried and wet again, and his fingers were stiff from five hours in a crate, and he did not hurry. He picked at it with his thumbnail. He worked the loop loose and worked a strand back through it. He said something under his breath in Vilikari that she did not catch, not a prayer, or not one she recognised; it sounded like somebody talking to a horse.

The knot came undone.

The air moved.

It was only a breath. It came from nowhere, from inside the room, from the space between Varek’s hands where the knot had been, and it was a wind: cold, and wet, and it smelled of salt so strongly that Riarhin tasted it on her lips. It went round the room once, low, along the floor, lifting the edge of the blanket on the bench. The lamp flame lay down flat in its niche and stood up again.

In a room where the air had not moved in longer than there had been anybody to notice, the air moved.

The idol cracked.

She heard it before she saw it: a sharp dry sound like ice on a pond in the first real cold. A line ran up the dark stone from the plinth, where the thing sat, into the fold where she had seen the flaw, and stopped.

Varek took the belaying pin out of his belt and hit it once, hard, on the crack.

It broke in three pieces and the pieces fell off the plinth onto the black stone floor and lay there. They were stone. They were only stone, dark and wet, and as she watched, the wet began to go out of them, the way the wet goes out of a pebble you have picked up off a beach and put in your pocket.

The pressure did not lift.

She had half expected it to, and it did not. It stayed on her eyes and her ears and her fingers exactly as before. What changed was that it was no longer still. It moved. It came round the room the way the wind had gone round it, low and slow, and she felt it go past her, and heard, very far away behind the stone, something begin to knock.

It knocked the way water knocks. The way a hull knocks when there is weather outside and something is loose in the hold.

“That’s done,” said Varek. He was looking at the pieces on the floor. He did not sound triumphant, or relieved, or much of anything. “That’s what I was asked.”

“Then let’s not stay for what you weren’t.”

Somewhere above them, water began to run.

It came down the stairs first. Only a little: a sheet of it on the worn treads, clear, and then a second sheet behind it, and the sound behind the stone was not a knocking any longer but a roar, very far off and not getting further.

She did not know what had held it. She would never know, and did not waste the next minute wondering. There had been water in the rock that had not moved, and a thing that had kept it from moving, and the thing was on the floor in three pieces, and the water had remembered what water did.

The stairs were no good. The gate was down across the corridor above. Whatever the custodians had dropped to close the rock, they had dropped between here and the lower way, and she had heard Tobin’s man say that the lower way went out to the landing, and the landing was where everybody would be.

That left the one door this place had kept open for them the whole time.

“The sea door,” she said.

Varek looked at her.

“They built it for you,” said Riarhin. “Let’s use it.”

It was not far. She had known it could not be far. A sea door into the lower rooms had to come into the lower rooms, and the idol’s chamber was the lowest room she had found, and the floor sloped toward its centre, and a floor that slopes has somewhere it is sloping to.

The drain was under the bench.

It was a cut in the rock the width of a man’s shoulders, dark, with water in the bottom of it already moving and a smell coming up out of it that was the true sea and not the thing’s cold salt at all. She went into it on her back with her feet first and her useless arm across her chest. Varek came after her. The water came after both of them.

There was light at the end of it, and then there was a lantern on a hook, and two men sitting on a ledge beside a great round opening in the rock through which she could see the sky going red with evening and the sea coming in and out over a sill of stone.

The two men were on their feet. They had heard the water. One of them had a boathook and the other had a lamp and they were both looking back into the rock, up the cut, with the fixed faces of men who have been told their whole lives that they are guarding a door against something that comes in from outside and have just understood that it has come the other way.

Riarhin came out of the cut into the lamp-light with blood all down her front and her hands open.

The one with the boathook looked at her and at the water behind her.

“The rock’s going,” she said. “There are people on the third stair. Four of them, eating bread. The lower gate’s down. They’ll need somebody to lift it from your side.”

He stared at her.

Then he dropped the boathook and went up the cut past her into the dark, toward the roar, calling a name.

The other one stood there with the lamp.

“You’ll want to go with him,” said Riarhin. “It’s a big gate.”

He went.

She did not know, afterwards, which of her reasons she had acted on.

She had sent the two men up into the rock because two men with a boathook and a lamp standing between her and the sea door were two men she would otherwise have had to go through, with one arm and no sword, and because a crowd of labourers coming down the lower galleries toward a gate that would not lift would have come to the only other way out they knew, which was this one, and she did not want to be standing in it when they did.

And she had sent them because there were four people on the third stair eating bread, one of whom had offered her the heel of the loaf.

She could have sorted the two reasons out and decided which was the real one, standing on the wet ledge above the sill with the sea coming in and out below her. She had done that sort of thing before. She did not do it now. She put it with the others.

Varek was at the lip of the opening, looking out at the red water and the headland black against it, and the long swell coming in from the open sea to break on the sill in slow white collapses.

“Can you swim?” he said. “With that arm?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then hold on to me.” He was already pulling off his boots. “She will not let me drown today. Not today. Today I have done what she asked.” He looked back at her, and the braid over his shoulder was one knot shorter than it had been that morning, and he had seen her look at it. “Tomorrow, perhaps. She is a mother. She does not make promises.”

The next swell came in over the sill, and went out, and they went with it.

Chapter Four: A Captain at Sea

The sea off Kessary was warm on the surface and cold a yard down, and it did not want them.

It was not rough. It was worse than rough: it was a long, slow, heavy swell coming in off the open water with the whole width of the Amaranthine behind it, lifting them up the face of each wave and letting them down the back of it, and every time it let them down it drew them a little further in toward the rock. Riarhin held on to Varek’s belt with her right hand and kicked and did what she was told, which was very little. He swam like a man who had been doing it since before he could walk, on his side, one arm, not fast, and every time the swell took them up he looked round once, quickly, and every time it let them down he changed his line.

She could not feel her left arm. She could feel her hands. The salt had got into them at once, and she had been in pain before and knew how to put it somewhere, and there was nowhere to put this.

She put it in the swell. Up. Down. Look. Change.

They came round the end of the headland into the landing cove in the last of the light.

It was full of people.

The labourers had come out of the lower way and down to the landing stair, and Tobin was standing on the bottom step with his book and counting them, loudly, a number for every head, the way he had been paid to do every day of his working life, and the labourers were standing in a long patient line on the switchback waiting to be counted, while behind them the rock of Kessary made a noise like a mill-race and water came out of the gate at the top of the road and ran down it in a sheet.

And at the foot of the landing stair, up to his thighs in the water with the bow line of the Gerlind’s launch wrapped twice round his fist, stood Na’ar.

The launch was a ship’s boat with four oars and four of Varek’s sailors in it, and it had come in on the evening tide to the landing because that had been the arrangement: the tenth hour, off the point, and if nobody came, in to the stair. Somebody had come to the stair. It had not been Riarhin.

On the landing above Na’ar, there were six men from the village with boathooks and cudgels, and in front of them Mother Hesper, soaked to the knee, her grey coat black with water and her key on its cord at her throat.

Riarhin did not hear what was said. She saw it. She saw Mother Hesper point at the launch, and two of the village men come down the stair toward it, and Na’ar look up at them.

He did not move. That was all. He stood in the water with the bow line in his fist and his other hand loose at his side and looked at them, pleasantly, the way he had looked at the three Rothari in Kolmar, a very large man at the foot of a stair who had not yet decided whether he was needed.

The two men stopped on the third step.

Mother Hesper said something sharp, and one of them came on. Na’ar let him get to the bottom step, and then leaned over and took hold of his cudgel, and took it from him the way one takes a stick from a child, and dropped it in the sea. He did not hit him. He did not have to. The man went back up the stair much faster than he had come down.

After that nobody came down the stair.

The launch picked them up off the end of the landing with the sailors shouting and Varek shouting back, and Riarhin went over the gunwale on her face into the bilge and lay there with her cheek on the wet boards.

Somebody had her by the collar and was pulling her up to sit. It was Na’ar. He had come over the bow with the painter still in his fist, and the boat was going out stern-first on four oars, and he was sitting in the bottom of it with his knees round her.

He looked at her hands. He looked at her shoulder.

“Out,” he said.

“I know.”

“I will put it in.”

“Not here.”

“Here,” said Na’ar, “or on the ship, with men watching, and you will have longer to think about it.” He put his knee against her ribs and took her wrist, and she looked at him, and he looked back at her with a complete absence of anything in his face except attention, and she nodded.

It went back in with a noise like a door.

She did not shout. She would remember that afterwards and not quite be able to account for it. She sat in the bottom of the boat and breathed, and Na’ar let go of her wrist and patted it twice, gently, and turned round to watch the landing.

Mother Hesper stood at the top of the stair. She had not moved. She was watching them go, and her face in the last of the light had no anger in it at all. It was the face of a woman doing arithmetic.

Then she turned and walked away up the landing, fast, toward the other side of the cove, where a cutter lay at a mooring under the crane with her sail furled on the boom.

“The oars,” Na’ar said, when Riarhin asked. He was watching the cutter. “The big boat at the water-house. It has twelve oars. They were in a rack in the water-house with a chain through them. When the bell went they sent us out through the lower way, all the day men, and I was counted, and we came out on the landing, and the custodians were all running to the big boat. They were going to row round to the ship.” He considered. “I thought: they will do that, and there are twenty men in that boat, and the ship has eleven.”

“So you took the oars.”

“I carried them. It is a light chain.” He moved his shoulders slightly. “I put them in the cistern pipe where it comes out on the rocks. It was running very fast by then. They will be in the village by now, or the sea.”

“And the chain?”

“I left the chain,” said Na’ar. “It was theirs.”

The Gerlind was burning when they came round the point.

Not the ship. The sail. Riarhin saw it from the launch before she understood what she was seeing: a square of yellow light in the dark of the next cove, flapping, and men on the deck beating at it with wet canvas, and a line of small bright sparks going up off the top of the cliff and coming down in slow arcs toward the water.

Mother Hesper had sent men along the shore. Of course she had. She had not put every soul she had in the rock to catch one captain; she had rung the bell and closed the rock and let it hold, and then she had sent people to the ship, because a captain of Raéyn without his ship was a man in a cellar, and a ship without her captain was a thing that could be burned from a cliff by six fishermen with a pot of pitch and a hunting bow.

Somebody on the Gerlind had had the sense to slip her cable. She was moving, slowly, out from under the cliff on her jib and the ebb, with the mainsail burning on its yard above her.

Varek stood up in the stern of the launch and looked at her. Riarhin saw his face. It was the face of a man seeing his own house afire.

He did not say anything. He sat down and took the steering oar from the sailor who had it and said, “Pull,” in a voice that did not need to be raised, and they pulled.

They came alongside as the mainsail went.

It burned through at the head and fell, in a great sheet of flame, half onto the deck and half into the sea, and hissed there. Men were cutting it free. One of them, the man she had seen mending a block on the fish quay the first morning, was hauling on a burning sheet with his bare hand because there was nothing else to haul it with, and she watched him let go and look at his palm and take hold again.

Then Varek was up the side and on his deck, and she heard him before she saw him.

He did not say very much. He said perhaps a dozen words in the next quarter of an hour, and every one of them was a name or an order, and every order was one his crew had heard a thousand times and needed no explaining. The burning canvas went over the side. The yard came down. The man with the hand was sent below, and went, cursing. A spare sail came up out of the forepeak in a long grey bundle and was bent on to the yard by six men who did not look at each other once and did not need to.

Riarhin sat on the deck by the mainmast where Na’ar had put her, with her ruined hands in her lap, and watched a crew that had practised this.

It was a pleasure to watch. She had spent her life watching people do things well and she had never got tired of it, and she would have liked to tell Varek so, and knew better.

The cutter came out of the cove behind them an hour later.

The cliff had stopped shooting by then; the Gerlind was well out of bowshot, standing south and west on a quartering wind under her new mainsail and a jib. Night had come down. There was a moon, three days off the full, rising out of the land, and in the moonlight Riarhin saw the cutter’s sail come round the point: small, pale, very fast, and heeled over hard.

Varek looked at it for a long time from the tiller.

“They know this coast,” he said, to nobody in particular. “That is a fishing boat. Those are fishermen. They have been out on this water every night of their lives. And she is quicker than us on this point of sailing, and they know it.”

“Can they catch us?”

“They can catch us. What they do when they have caught us is another question.” He looked up at the sky, and Riarhin followed his look and saw what he had seen and she had not: in the south-west, low down, the stars were going out one by one along a line, as though somebody were drawing a curtain across them. “And she is coming.”

He began to take in sail an hour before the squall reached them.

Riarhin did not understand it at first. Nobody on deck understood it except the mate, who looked at the captain once and then went forward without a word and began to put men on the halyards. The Gerlind was running with everything she had. The cutter was two miles astern and gaining. And Varek, with the stars going out ahead of him, put the ship’s head a point closer to the wind and began to reef.

The cutter gained. Riarhin watched her come up on the moonlit water, a mile, three-quarters, her sail full and hard and every man in her hauling to make it harder.

“You’re letting them catch us,” she said.

“I am letting them see us,” said Varek. “It is not the same.”

The curtain came across the moon.

She had been in weather before. She had been on the Violet Steppe in a thunderstorm with nothing between her and the sky but a horse, and she had been in the hills above Cair in rain so hard it knocked the leaves off the trees. She had never been in weather like this.

It came across the water like a wall. The moon went out and the sea went white in a line a mile long, and then the line was on them, and there was no sound in it at all for one instant, and then there was nothing but sound.

She could not hear. She could not hear Na’ar, who was a yard from her shouting something, or the sailor who was lashing her to the mast with a line round her waist, or anything but the roar. Rain came sideways and hit her face like thrown gravel. The deck went up under her and then went away sideways, and the whole ship lay down on her side until the lee rail was under, and hung there, and hung there, and came slowly, slowly back.

Men were moving through it. She could not hear them and they could not hear each other. And still they moved, each to his place, and she watched a man go up the weather shrouds in the dark with no sound in the world but the wind, and understood that nobody had told him to. Varek at the tiller had raised one hand, two fingers, and pointed, and three men had seen it and gone.

The Gerlind had come through the first blow with her canvas already down to storm size, because her captain had taken it in an hour before anybody else knew why. Riarhin had no way to guess what the same blow had done to a cutter that had been carrying everything she had.

She did not see the turn. She felt it.

The ship came round under her in the dark and the wind came round with it, from the side to the bow, and the ship put her shoulder into the sea and climbed. The noise changed. Under the wind, very near, there was another noise: a long heavy thunder that came and went and came again, and she knew it, she had heard it the first night on the headland at Kessary, lying in the thorn. It was surf. It was surf breaking on something.

Varek had turned toward the reef.

She would have the geography of it afterwards, from the mate, in pieces: there was a shoal south of the point, a long bank of sand and rock running out a mile under the water, which the Kessary men knew as well as their own doorsteps and would never go near on a night like this; and the Gerlind had turned toward it, in the squall, in the dark, with the cutter somewhere behind her; and she had gone up the edge of it close-hauled, a cable’s length from the breakers, in water that a captain with a sounding line and twenty-six years on this sea knew had just enough depth in it for a ship that drew what she drew.

And a cutter coming after her in the squall would have had two choices. Follow her in, where there was no room to turn a boat that size and where every man aboard knew what was under the water. Or bear away, run off before the wind, and lose her.

The fishermen of Kessary were good seamen. They had been out on that water every night of their lives.

They bore away.

The squall went through in an hour and a half.

When the moon came out again the sea was running high and white-flecked, and the wind had backed round into the north and was blowing steady and cold, and the Gerlind was standing south with the shoal a mile astern and nothing else in the whole of the moonlit water from one horizon to the other.

The sailor untied Riarhin from the mast. Her legs would not hold her and she sat down on the wet deck and stayed there.
