Varek gave the tiller to the mate and came forward. He stood over her, soaked to the skin, his hair plastered to his skull, the three braids black with water.

“That was her,” Riarhin said. “The squall.”

“That was her.” He looked south. “She does not do what she is asked. You understand? I did not ask her for that. I have never asked her for anything. She is enormous, and she is free, and she does not care what I want.” He wiped water from his face with his palm. “She sent weather. What I did with it was mine.”

“I know.”

“Good.” He looked at her for a while longer. “Now I know,” he said, and went aft.

Na’ar was sick in the morning.

He was sick over the lee rail, as he had been told, which was a thing he had evidently gone to some trouble to learn, and then he was sick again, and then he sat down on a hatch cover with his hands on his knees and looked at the horizon with the expression of a man who had been insulted by a horse he had trusted.

“This is the sea,” he said.

“Yes.”

“I did not know it would do that.”

“Neither did it,” said the mate, going past with a bucket, and laughed, and Na’ar looked after him with an interest that suggested he was remembering him for later.

It lasted a day and a half. On the second evening Na’ar went down to the galley, where the bad cook was shouting at an onion, and took the onion off him, and the knife, and the rest of the galley, and nobody on the Gerlind ever raised the subject of the bad cook again.

The man with the burned hand was called Teodric.

He had held the sheet of a burning sail because it had been the only thing to hold, and the palm had come up in one great blister that had burst in the salt, and by the second day the hand was hot and swollen and he could not close it.

Riarhin knew wounds. She knew her own better than anybody’s, and her own were no use to anybody else, because hers closed. His would not, not without help. She sat with him in the forepeak and cleaned it with spirit from Varek’s stone bottle while he swore at her in Vilikari, and bound it in clean linen, and made him keep it up; and Na’ar brought him broth, and sat on the next hammock, and told him a long and apparently pointless account of a horse he had once owned that had eaten a saddle, until Teodric was laughing too hard to swear.

It was three days before the heat went out of the hand. It would scar. He would keep it.

Her own hands had closed by then. The skin was new and pink and tight across the palms and it would be another week before she could hold a sword with any pleasure, and the shoulder was a dull deep ache that got into her sleep; but they had closed. She kept them in her lap, or in her pockets, when Teodric could see them.

The nine leaves had come through.

She had carried them into the rock in oilcloth, tied twice, inside the lining of her coat, because she carried them everywhere, and they had been in the sea for the better part of an hour with her. She laid them out on the table in Varek’s cabin on the first dry afternoon with the door shut and went through them one at a time.

Water had got in at one corner. The ink had run on the edge of the Marcott chandler’s page, where the payment was, and on the foot of the sheet with the knife-stub. None of it mattered. She had read every line of every leaf on the floor of Serafine’s counting room with Lena holding the lamp, and she had attended, and it was all where she had put it.

She dried them anyway, flat, with a weight on each corner.

The other paper had not come through.

It had been in the inside pocket on the left, with nothing round it, because it had been in that pocket for two years and she had never once thought of it as a thing that needed protecting. Three lines in a clerk’s hand, forwarded four times. The Duke of the Belworth’s condition was unchanged. The young gentleman had arrived and been received.

It came out of the pocket as a grey wad the size of her thumb. The ink had gone into the paper and the paper had gone into itself. She opened it on the table very carefully, with the tip of Toothpick, and it came apart into three pieces, and none of them had anything on them.

She sat and looked at it.

She had every word. She could have written it out again on clean paper in the clerk’s own hand, with the same blot on the descender of the y in unchanged; she had looked at it enough. That was not the point and she knew it was not the point, and she sat there a long while in the dry cabin with the ship working round her and did not look at what the point was.

Then she laid the three grey pieces out beside the nine leaves and put a weight on them, the same as the others, and let them dry.

When they were dry they were stiff and grey and blank. She folded them together and put them back in the pocket on the left.

On the ninth day out, at dusk, with the coast a low grey line on the port hand and the wind steady, she stood at the rail and found her fingers at her throat.

There was nothing there. There had not been anything there to see since the shieling above the burnt wood. There was only a place where something had been drawn tight, once, and then had gone slack; and her fingers had gone to it without her deciding they should, the way her tongue went to the gap in her teeth.

The tooth was more than half grown. She had stopped counting the days and knew exactly how many there had been.

The slack place at her throat did not do anything. It did not pull, or point, or warm. It was only slack, which is not the same thing as gone, and she stood there with two fingers on it and the whole of the southern sea going grey in front of her and did not know what she had expected.

She took her hand away.

Na’ar, sitting on the hatch cover behind her with a pot of beans between his knees, did not ask, and she was grateful.

They were at sea the better part of a month.

It was a long passage and a good one. The wind held in the north. The Gerlind ran south down a coast that changed colour by the week, from grey to yellow to a dull red-brown, and the cloak on Riarhin’s shoulders changed with it, and nobody noticed but her. Na’ar cooked. Teodric’s hand healed. Varek and Riarhin sat in the cabin in the evenings with the stone bottle between them, and did not talk about Kessary, and talked about everything else: ships, and horses, and the Vilikari, and what the Vilikari thought of the Ecktorians, which was a great deal and all of it amusing.

On the twenty-sixth evening he asked her, very briefly, whether she had got what she came to Kessary for.

“I didn’t come for anything,” she said. “I came for passage. You were paying.”

He nodded as though that were an answer.

“And I have paid,” he said. “Mbaro in three days. And food.”

“And food.”

“The food,” said Varek, “was not me.”

Mbaro came up out of the sea on the twenty-ninth morning as a smear of brown haze low on the water, and then as a line of towers, and then as the tallest of them, a great round drum of red mud brick standing up out of the flat land with nothing anywhere near it higher than its shoulder.

The Granary Tower. She had heard of it. Nothing in Mbaro may overtop it.

The Gerlind came in on the tide past a mole of cut stone into a harbour that was not a harbour at all but the mouth of a canal: wide and brown and busy, with barges three abreast going up it under sweeps and a long pier on the south bank where millet was going up ramps into grey Ecktorian hulls in a haze of chaff. The chaff got into everything. It was in the air, in her eyes, in the rigging; it lay on the brown water in drifts. The noise was enormous. Every voice on the pier seemed to be naming a price.

Na’ar stood at the rail with his face turned up into the chaff and the sun and breathed in through his nose.

“Grain,” he said. “Horses. Dung. Somebody is cooking goat.” He sounded happy. “This is a place.”

Varek brought the Gerlind alongside a quay on the north bank with about four words and a hand signal, and the lines went ashore, and the plank went down, and it was done.

He shook her hand at the head of the plank. He shook it carefully, because her palms were still pink, and held it for a moment.

“I am going to Kahfagia,” he said. “And then I do not know. She will tell me or she will not. If you are ever on a quay and you need a berth that is not for sale, ask for the Gerlind. Somebody will know where she is.” He let go. “Do not ask for me. Ask for her. I may be dead.”

“I’ll ask for her.”

“Good.” He looked at Na’ar. “And you. If you ever want to go to sea again, come and cook.”

“No,” said Na’ar, with great courtesy.

Varek laughed, and went back up his own deck without looking round, and began to shout at somebody about water.

Riarhin stood on the quay with her bundle over her shoulder and the nine leaves inside her coat and the red-brown chaff settling on her cloak, which was red-brown, and had always been, and looked along the waterfront.

It was full of people who knew how it worked.

A clerk at a table under an awning, with a brass stamp and an inkpot and a line of carters waiting to be written down. A woman on the next barge shouting a rate across the water to a man on the pier and getting one shouted back. A boy running a message. Two men in red coats with staves, walking slowly along the quay and looking at everything and writing nothing, which meant that somebody else would be writing it down for them later. A money-changer’s booth with three different scales on the counter. A shipping agent’s board with chalked sailings.

She looked at them, one after another, and knew what each of them was for.

She did not know what any of them knew. That was the job now.

Na’ar came down the plank behind her with his own bundle and the straw hat from Kessary, and stood beside her, and looked at the same waterfront.

“Where?” he said.

“I don’t know yet.”

He nodded as though that were a perfectly good place to start, and waited, and so did she.

Act II: Oshiira

Chapter Five: The Purchases

The first thing she bought in Mbaro was a person, by the hour.

Her name was Kadiatou and she sat on an upturned basket at the foot of the Water Stair on the north quay with a slate on her knee, and she was the fourth interpreter Riarhin had looked at and the first one who was not looking back.

The other three had been standing. They had seen a foreign woman come off a Vilikari ship with a sword-shaped bundle and a very large man, and they had come forward with their hands open and their rates ready, and one of them had already, before Riarhin said a word, offered her a good house in the Salt District, and a cousin with mules, and the name of a man who knew the Widow’s own scribe. All of that told Riarhin a great deal about what they thought she was. None of it told her anything she wanted.

Kadiatou had watched them do it, and had gone on writing on her slate, and when Riarhin came and stood in front of her she had looked up and said in good Ecktorian, “Words or doors?”

“What’s the difference?”

“Words, I say what they say and what you say, and nothing else, and I forget it at the end of the day. A copper-weight an hour, a half-silver for a full day, paid at sunset.” She had rubbed out something on the slate with the heel of her hand. “Doors, I tell you who to talk to. That’s dearer, and I remember it, because I have to go on living here.”

“Words,” said Riarhin.

Kadiatou had looked at her properly for the first time.

“Good,” she had said, and stood up, and picked up the basket, and that was the arrangement.

She was perhaps thirty. She had a face that had decided some time ago not to be surprised by anything that came off a ship, and she had four languages and a fifth she said was not good enough to charge for, and she was the daughter of a canal pilot and the widow of a weigh-house clerk, and she had worked the Water Stair for eleven years for anybody who would pay her by the hour and not by the favour. Riarhin learned all of this in the first morning, not because Kadiatou told her, but because Kadiatou told everybody else, in the course of doing her work, whenever somebody tried to make her into something other than an interpreter.

“I’m a mouth,” she said to a carter who had asked her to tell the foreigner that his mules were the best on the north bank. “Tell her yourself and I’ll say it.”

The carter told her himself. Kadiatou said it, word for word, in a voice with no more interest in it than a bell’s.

The mules were not the best on the north bank. Riarhin had not come to buy mules.

She had come to find a saddler.

She had him on the fourth of the nine leaves, in a clerk’s round hand eighty years dry: Diarra, saddler, at the Two Girths, Mbaro, rec’d of Hollis of Marcott for account of the consignment, as agreed. And then a sum, and a date, and nothing else. There were three more payments to the same name on the sixth leaf, and a fourth on the eighth that had been struck through and not paid.

It was not a great deal to go on. It was more than she had had for two years.

She did not go to the Two Girths. She did not, on the first day, go anywhere near it. She had learned a long time ago, and in a harder school than this, that a woman who walks up to a counter and asks about a transaction eighty years old is a woman whom everybody behind that counter will be describing to somebody else by nightfall. And Mbaro, she could see from the Water Stair, was a city where things were described to somebody else as a matter of course.

It was a city built on counting.

Every barge that came up the canal stopped at a weigh-house. Every cart that came off a barge stopped at a stamp. Every sack of millet on the south pier had a mark on it in black paint, and every mark had been written down somewhere, and the writing had been checked by somebody in a red coat or somebody in a blue one, and the red and blue coats were not, Kadiatou said, the same people, and had opinions about each other that it would take a whole day to translate.

“Blue is the weigh-houses,” she said. “Red is the Corps.”

“And the Corps is?”

“The Corps guards the grain.” She said it the way people say a proverb that they have heard too many times. “That’s what they’d tell you. What they do is decide whose grain it is. There’s a difference and people here argue about it all the time. Ask anybody in the Salt District and they’ll tell you the Corps has been choosing for the Widow for twenty years. Ask anybody on the Tower Bank and they’ll tell you the Corps is all that’s kept the Grain Covenant honest since the old Widow’s hands started shaking.” She shrugged. “They’re both right, some years.”

Riarhin said nothing. She was watching two men in red coats walk down the pier, slowly, looking at every sack.

A great deal of what she needed was ordinary, and she did the ordinary things first.

She needed money she could draw on in a city where nobody knew her. She went to a money house on the Tower Bank called Tiemoko and Daughters, which was the one Kadiatou named when asked where a foreign merchant with no friends in the city would go and not be robbed, and she found three women in a long cool room with a tiled floor and a row of scales, and a man asleep in a chair by the door with a stave across his knees.

They would hold her silver. They would not hold it on her word.

“A reference,” said the eldest of the three daughters, who was sixty if she was a day. She said it pleasantly. She said it the way the man at the water-house in Kessary had said lead’s gone up. “Somebody in Mbaro who has had dealings with you, or with somebody who has had dealings with you, and will put a mark on it.”

Riarhin had nobody in Mbaro who had had dealings with her. She had a Vilikari captain who was halfway to Kahfagia by now and would not be coming back, and whose ship had been berthed at the north quay for a single tide.

But a ship that berths at a quay, even for a single tide, has an agent.

She went back to the north quay and found him in a shed with a chalked board of sailings: a stout, cheerful, grey-bearded Oshiiran named Keita who had handled the Gerlind’s water and harbour dues and pilotage on four previous visits, and who knew Varek, and liked him, and had been paid well by him every time. He would put his mark on a reference that said the bearer had come in on the Gerlind as the captain’s passenger and had paid her way. He would not say anything more than that, because he did not know anything more than that. It cost her a small silver. It was worth it.

The eldest daughter read Keita’s mark and nodded.

“The Gerlind,” she said. “Vilikari. The captain pays in coin and argues about the weight.” She put the reference in a box. “Welcome to Mbaro.”

She checked the saddler through the trades.

It took two days, and she did it through the right door, with Kadiatou’s voice in her ear saying exactly what was said and nothing more. Every trade in Mbaro that dealt in anything the grain caravans used was on a roll at the Hall of Carriers, which was a long building on the Tower Bank where the carrier guilds kept their offices and where you could, for a fee and the right form of words, read who held what licence, and when they had got it, and from whom.

She did not ask for the Two Girths by name. She asked, through Kadiatou, for the roll of harness-makers on the north bank for the last hundred years, because she was a foreign buyer considering a large order and wanted to know which houses had been in the trade longest. That was dull, and plausible, and the clerk who brought her the roll was so bored by it that he left her alone with it for an hour.

The Two Girths was there. It had been there for a hundred and twenty years. It was there still.

But the name of the house was not the name of the family. Three generations after the Diarra who had taken Hollis of Marcott’s money, the Diarra line had run out in a daughter; and the business, with its name, its sign and its licence, had passed by sale to a family called Coulibaly, who had kept the name because the name was worth more than anything else in the shop. The daughter’s name was on the roll as having consented to the sale. Beside her name, in a clerk’s note, was another name: the man she had married, and his trade, which was dyeing, and his district, which was in the old south quarter across the canal.

Riarhin sat with the roll in the long quiet room and read the note three times and did not need to.

The book was not in the shop. The shop had been sold. A saddler’s day-book is not part of a sale; it is a family’s, it has the family’s quarrels in it, and the names of the people who never paid. It would have gone with the daughter when she married.

And the daughter would be old now, or dead, and the book would be in a dyer’s house in the south quarter with somebody else’s daughter, if it was anywhere at all.

That was the first setback, and it was an ordinary one, and she was almost glad of it. Nothing in two years had been ordinary. She knew what to do with a married daughter.

The purchases began on the third day.

She knew before she made the first one what they would look like. She had sat on the floor of Serafine’s counting room and built an eighty-year-old journey out of what it had cost, and she knew exactly how legible a list of costs was to anybody who had ever read one. Every thing she bought in Mbaro would be written down by somebody. That was how the city worked. And every thing she bought would have a good reason, taken by itself, and she was going to have to buy all of them.

So she began with lamp oil.

A great deal of lamp oil. More than a traveller would need, less than a trader would buy to sell on. The oil-seller in the Salt District market asked her, through Kadiatou, whether she was opening a house, and she said no, she was going somewhere without inns, and he nodded and weighed it out and wrote it down.

Then canvas: not sailcloth, which would have been a ship, but the heavy close-woven stuff the caravan houses used for water-skin covers and pack-sheets, the kind that would stand a season of sand being driven against it. She bought it at the canvas hall on the Tower Bank from a factor who asked no questions and wrote everything down.

Pack fittings: brass rings and buckles and breast-straps, from a harness-maker who was not the Two Girths and was two streets away from it.

Old route surveys: she paid a copying-house on the Tower Bank to make her fair copies of three published surveys of the northern caravan roads, the ones that anybody could buy, which went as far as the last watered stages and stopped.

And water rights. That was the one she thought about longest. You could not buy water in Oshiira; you bought the right to draw it, at a named well, for a named season, in a named quantity, and every right traced back to a grant the Widow herself had authorised, and the grants were kept by the canal-mothers at a bench under a tamarind tree by the Great Sluice. Anybody could ask to read the grants. Almost nobody did, unless they meant to go somewhere where the water was scarce and argued over.

She asked to read the grants for the northern stages.

Na’ar spent those days being mistaken for things.

He was mistaken for a caravan guard at the horse market, where he had gone to look at camels because he had never seen one close to and wished to form an opinion. He was mistaken for a debt-collector at a cookshop in the Salt District, where he had gone because he had smelled the goat on the first morning and had been following the smell for two days. He was mistaken, once, by a very old woman at the canal-mothers’ bench, for a son of hers who had gone north with the grain boats forty years ago; and he sat down on the bench next to her and let her hold his hand for a quarter of an hour, and when her granddaughter came to fetch her he thanked the granddaughter for the loan of her grandmother and walked away with the gravity of a man leaving a temple.

He formed an opinion of camels. It was that they were horses that had been told something very disappointing a long time ago and had never got over it.

He came back to the lodging house every evening with food.

“I have been listening,” he said on the fourth evening, putting a covered pot on the table between them. “In the cookshop. They talk.”

“About me?”

“About the woman with the lamp oil.” He took the lid off. It was the goat. He had got the recipe, or near enough, and was proud of it, and was trying to be offhand. “The oil-seller’s wife’s brother drives for the canvas hall. He says: first she buys oil for six weeks, then she buys cover-cloth for twenty skins. What does she want with twenty skins and six weeks of oil? That is a question they are asking.”

Riarhin looked at the goat.

“And what’s the answer?”

“They have three answers,” Na’ar said. “They are all wrong. I did not help them.” He sat down. “Eat. You have been eating bread standing up for four days. It is not respectful.”

She ate. It was good. She said so and he pretended not to hear.

Afterwards she sat by the window of the lodging house with the shutters open and the noise of the canal coming up from below, and laid it all out in her head the way she had laid out the nine leaves on the floor in Silkstrand.

Lamp oil for six weeks. Cover-cloth for twenty skins. Brass fittings for a string of pack animals. Fair copies of three road surveys that stopped at the last water. And a morning at the canal-mothers’ bench reading the grants for the northern wells.

Any one of them was nothing. A woman buying lamp oil is a woman buying lamp oil. A woman buying survey copies is a merchant, or a scholar, or a surveyor, or somebody’s clerk. A woman reading water grants is a lawyer, or a caravan house checking its rights, or a thief.

All of them together, bought by the same woman in the same week, made a sentence. It was not a long sentence and it did not say where she was going. But it said somewhere past the provisioned roads, with animals, for weeks, and she is going to carry her own water because she does not expect to find any.

Somebody would read that sentence. More than one somebody. Each of them would read it from where they sat, and would put an end to it that suited their business.

She could not keep them from reading it. The purchases had to be made; there was no expedition without them, and there was nowhere in Mbaro to make them where they would not be written down. So the only thing left was to choose, as far as she could, what ending each reader put on the sentence.

That was a game she knew. She had played it in six countries. She had never played it in one where everybody could count.

On the fifth morning she went across the canal to the south quarter with Kadiatou to find a dyer’s house.

It was not hard to find. The roll at the Hall of Carriers had given her a name and a district, and the district had a street of dyers, where the water in the gutters ran blue and ochre and the smell was so strong that Kadiatou tied a cloth over her nose, and at the end of the street a house with indigo cloths hanging from its upper windows and an old woman sitting in the doorway shelling beans into a bowl.

Riarhin did not go up to her.

She stood at the other end of the street for a quarter of an hour and watched the house. She watched who came and went. She watched a boy come out with a bundle of wet cloth and go down to the canal steps. She watched the old woman shell beans and not look up once, and then look up, when a man in a red coat walked past the end of the street, and watch him all the way along until he was gone, and then go back to the beans.

“That’s the daughter,” Kadiatou said. “Hawa. Her father’s father was Diarra of the Two Girths. Everyone on this street knows it; she tells them.”

“Tells them what?”

Kadiatou looked at her, over the cloth.

“That her family was owed,” she said. “She’s been telling them for fifty years.”

Riarhin stood very still.

It was not the book. It was the thing next to the book, and she had been learning her whole life that the thing next to the book was usually the better thing to have. A saddler’s day-book with an unpaid instalment in it is a record. A family with an unpaid instalment in it is a grievance, and a grievance keeps things. It keeps papers, and dates, and the names of people who should have paid, and it keeps them for fifty years and tells the whole street about them.

The eighth leaf. The fourth payment to Diarra, struck through, not paid.

She had it exactly, the line through the sum and the ink blotted where the pen had lifted. Somebody at the Marcott end had written that payment down and then decided not to make it.

And somebody at this end had never forgotten.

She did not go up to the house that day.

She went back across the canal with Kadiatou and paid her at sunset, a half-silver on the flat of the slate, and Kadiatou took it and put it away and said, “Tomorrow?”

“Tomorrow.”

“Words or doors?”

“Words.”

Kadiatou nodded, and went down the Water Stair with the basket on her hip and the evening going red behind her on the canal, and did not look back.

Riarhin stood at the top of the stair a while longer and watched the barges go up to the weigh-house, one after another, to be stopped and read and written down.

Twenty skins. Six weeks of oil. A morning at the grants.

Somewhere in this city, tonight, somebody was adding it up.

Chapter Six: Four Sensible Suspicions

She found out who was adding it up the way she found out most things: by watching who changed.

The oil-seller in the Salt District market changed first. On the fourth day he had weighed out her oil and written it down and asked whether she was opening a house. On the seventh, when she went back for a small flask of lamp-black for marking, he was a great deal friendlier, and asked after her health, and mentioned that his wife’s cousin was a camel-master of long experience who knew the northern stages like his own hand and was looking for work this season, at a very fair rate.

That was not the Corps and it was not a thief. That was a small man who had decided she was about to spend money and wished to be near it when she did.

She thanked him and took the lamp-black and did not take the cousin.

The Corps she saw on the eighth day, at the canal-mothers’ bench.

She had gone back to read the grants again, not because she needed to, but because a woman who reads the northern water grants once is a woman with a question and a woman who reads them twice is a woman with a plan, and she wanted to know who would notice the difference.

Somebody had. When she came round the Great Sluice with Kadiatou at the third hour, there was a man in a red coat sitting on the end of the bench under the tamarind tree with a cup of something and his stave leaning against the trunk, talking to the youngest of the three canal-mothers who kept the grants.

He was not writing anything. He had the stillness of a man who does not need to, because the person he is talking to will remember.

He was perhaps forty, lean, grey at the temples, with a pleasant tired face and a scar through one eyebrow, and when Riarhin came up he stood, courteously, and moved further down the bench to make room, and said something in Oshiiran that Kadiatou did not translate.

“What did he say?”

“Good morning,” said Kadiatou. “He said good morning, and that the tamarind gives good shade.”

“That’s all?”

“That’s all he said.” Kadiatou’s face did not move. “He said it in the old form, with the honorific for a guest of the Covenant. It’s what they say to foreign buyers at the Grain Exchange.”

So he knew what she was pretending to be, and had told her so, in a greeting.

Riarhin sat down on the bench and asked for the northern grants, and read them for an hour, and the man in the red coat sat at the other end of the bench and drank his cup and talked to the canal-mother about a sluice on the Tower Bank that was leaking, and never looked at her once.

When she got up to go, he got up too, and nodded to her, pleasantly, and went off along the canal in the other direction with his stave over his shoulder.

“His name is Sounkalo,” Kadiatou said, when he had gone. She said it without being asked, and then looked annoyed with herself. “That’s a door. I’ll charge you for it.”

“What does he do?”

Kadiatou considered whether to charge her for that too.

“He’s a warden,” she said. “Northern stores. Every well past the last canal, every depot the Covenant keeps on the caravan roads, the reserve granaries at the stages. When somebody draws water they have no grant for, or takes grain from a depot out of turn, Sounkalo is who comes.” She shifted the basket on her hip. “He’s not a bad man. People on the northern roads say he’s fair. They also say he’s never once let anything go.”

So that was the Corps, and she could see its sentence from where it sat.

A foreign woman reading the grants for the northern wells, twice, with cover-cloth for twenty skins in her lodging. The Corps did not care where she was going. The Corps cared that on the far side of the last canal there was a chain of wells and depots that the whole of northern Oshiira depended on in a bad year, and that the water in them was written down to the cup, and that somebody who meant to take a string of animals out past them, carrying her own water, was somebody who would, at some point, find her skins running low within a day of a Covenant well.

And there was a worse reading, and Sounkalo would have it too, because it was his work to have it. A buyer surveying the northern wells for somebody else. A foreign house that meant to run its own caravans north and would want to know which wells were full and which were argued over, and how much they could draw before the canal-mothers noticed.

An undeclared survey of the stores. That was what the Corps would think she was.

It was not a bad thing for them to think. It was nearly true. It had a shape they understood, and it put her into a file they had been keeping for years, labelled foreigners who want our water, and a woman in that file was a woman they would watch at the wells and not at the saddler’s.

She did not try to change it. She went and bought a surveyor’s chain.

The merchant house she found by the price.

She had needed pack animals, sooner or later, and had not meant to buy them yet; but she went to the horse market on the ninth day and spent a morning among the camel-lines with Kadiatou at her elbow, looking at mouths and feet and asking prices, and at the end of the morning she found the one camel-master in the market who was known to be the worst-tempered and most expensive in Mbaro and asked him, in a voice that carried, what he would take for thirty head delivered to the north gate before the end of the month.

He named a price. It was a very large price and he named it very loudly, because he was that kind of man, and because the whole market was listening. She thanked him and said she would think about it and walked away.

She did not want thirty camels. She wanted the market to have heard somebody ask for thirty.

By evening the price was all over the Salt District, and so was the number, and the number was wrong in a useful direction. Thirty head is not a traveller’s string. It is not even a small caravan. Thirty head to the north gate at the end of the month is a caravan house setting up a run of its own, and in Mbaro there were perhaps a dozen houses that ran the northern stages, and every one of them would hear about it by morning and ask itself which of the others she was working for, or whether she was working for somebody new.

On the tenth morning a boy came to the lodging house with a note in good Ecktorian from the house of Bah and Sons, who ran the stages north as far as the salt pans and had done for four generations, asking whether the foreign buyer would do them the honour of taking coffee at their counting house that afternoon.

She did them the honour.

It was a long, polite, careful afternoon, and she enjoyed it more than she had expected. The elder Bah was about seventy and had the manners of a man who had been lied to by the best merchants in six countries and had come to find it restful. He did not ask her what she was doing. He told her, at length and with a great deal of charm, what his house did, and how long it had done it, and how many seasons in a row his camels had come back from the salt pans with every animal they took out; and he mentioned, in passing, the names of three other northern houses and what had become of the foreign partners each of them had taken on.

None of it had been good.

She drank his coffee and admired his camels and did not tell him anything, and at the end of it he walked her to the door and said, “If you are buying for somebody, madam, you might tell them that the northern stages are not empty. They only look it from the Tower Bank.”

That was the merchants. They thought she was buying a route, and they were going to spend the next month trying to find out whose. It would cost them time and a few bribes and it would cost her nothing, and it would keep every caravan house in Mbaro looking at the camel-lines and not at a dyer’s house in the south quarter.

The thief she had not gone looking for. The thief came to her, the way thieves do.

She had taken a room in a lodging house on the north quay that took foreigners off the ships and did not ask questions, which meant that everybody who wanted to ask questions knew exactly where to find the foreigners. On the eleventh evening a man sat down across from her in the eating room downstairs, uninvited, while Na’ar was out, and put his hands on the table.

He was small and neat and pleasant, and he had a gold ring on each thumb, and he said his name was Mahamane.

“You came in on the Vilikari,” he said, in Silkstrand. It was a good Silkstrand. It was the Silkstrand of the lower city, of Bell Street and the Dye Yards, and he had learned it from somebody who had lived there.

“I did.”

“Out of Silkstrand. The week after the fire.” He smiled. “A lot of things came out of Silkstrand that week. Some of them are still looking for a buyer. Some of them came south because the north is full of people who might recognise them.” He turned one of the rings on his thumb. “And now here is a woman who has come south out of the fire, and is buying strong canvas and pack fittings and oil, and asking about the roads north. North, where there’s nobody. And I think to myself: what does a person carry north, where there’s nobody, with that much canvas round it?”

She looked at him.

“You think I’m moving something,” she said.

“I think you’re moving something that’s easier to sell a long way from where it was lost.” He spread his hands. “I don’t want to know what. I’m only saying I know people. In the north. On the far side. People who buy things that came out of fires, and don’t ask what the fire was. If you’re carrying something, madam, I could save you a very long walk.”

She thought about Silkstrand. She thought about a pillow house burning and a chair going onto a wagon and the whole of four streets of lower Silkstrand going north on Aryka Everblood’s carts, and about how many people in how many ports would be thinking what this man was thinking, this month, about anybody who came south from that city with a bundle.

It was a good reading. It was wrong, and it was the right kind of wrong, and she had not had to do anything to put it into his head.

“I’ll think about it,” she said.

“Please,” said Mahamane, and stood, and bowed, and left a small silver on the table for her supper, which was a message, and which she left there, which was an answer.

The next morning, to be sure he kept thinking it, she went to a locksmith in the Salt District and spent an hour and a half haggling over a pair of iron-bound strongboxes with double hasps.

She did not need strongboxes. She paid too much for them, eventually, and had them sent to her lodging house, where they sat under the bed and were never used for anything. She made sure the locksmith remembered her. She made sure he remembered that she had wanted the kind with the false floor and had been told he did not make that kind, and had gone away cross.

It cost her two silvers and most of a morning. She had known it would cost something. You cannot give four people four different answers from one desk and expect the desk not to notice; you have to go to four desks, and pay each one, and spend a morning at each, and walk between them. Everything she did to keep a sentence from being read correctly took time she did not have and money she had not meant to spend.

She did it anyway. The alternative was one reader, somewhere, getting it right.

The fourth reader she never saw at all.

She knew about him only because the clerk at the Hall of Carriers mentioned him to Kadiatou, in passing, as a piece of gossip, on the afternoon Riarhin went back to return the roll of harness-makers she had been lent.

A man had come in the morning after her first visit, the clerk said, and asked to see the same roll. Not the harness-makers: the roll she had actually spent the hour on, which was the one before it, where the Two Girths was. The man had been a factor for a salt house on the Tower Bank, a house that did a great deal of business with the river-island, and had said he was tracing an old inland consignment for a client: a debt eighty years old, he said, that a family in the north was trying to collect.

“Eighty years,” said the clerk, laughing. “As if anybody’s paying eighty-year-old debts.”

Riarhin stood at the counter with the roll in her hands and did not move.

She had not asked for the Two Girths. She had asked for the harness-makers. She had sat with the roll for an hour and turned every page at the same pace, and she had not lingered on the one that mattered. And a man had come in the next morning and asked for that page.

It did not mean he knew anything. It meant somebody had told him that a foreign woman had come in off a ship and looked at the roll of harness-makers, and he had come to look at it too, and had seen the Two Girths and the Diarra line and the married daughter, because anybody would, and had put a question in a letter to somebody else.

A salt house that did business with the river-island. With Ashaan.

She knew enough about that country to know it did not have to send an army to look at anything. It had people. It had people in every port on this coast, in salt houses and money houses and shipping sheds, who wrote letters home, and the letters went up the river and were read by somebody, and sometimes somebody read them carefully.

What the man had was small. An old consignment. Somebody asking. A foreign woman who had come from the fire. That was all that could go into a letter, and it would be weeks going up the river, and weeks coming back, and by then she meant to be somewhere else.

It was still the one she liked least. The Corps and the merchants and the thief were all looking at her. This one was looking at the thing she was looking for.

Na’ar noticed Kadiatou before she did.

It was the thirteenth morning, and they were crossing the Water Stair to meet her at her basket, and he stopped at the top of the stair and put a hand on Riarhin’s arm, lightly, the way he did perhaps once a year.

“Wait.”

She waited. She looked where he was looking.

Kadiatou was at her basket at the foot of the stair with her slate on her knee, as she was every morning. There was a man standing beside her. He was a big man, bigger than most of the carters, in a plain brown robe, and he was not doing anything; he was standing with his hands folded and his weight back, looking out over the canal, as though he were waiting for a barge.

“He was there yesterday,” said Na’ar. “Also the day before. He stands by her in the morning. He goes when we come. He has not bought anything, he has not asked for anything, he has not talked to anybody else.” He watched the man. “He is not waiting for a barge. He stands like a man who is sent. And he stands on the side of her where she has to look past him to see the stair.”

Riarhin had been down that stair three mornings running and had seen a big man in a brown robe waiting for a barge and had filed him exactly as that.

She did not like how easily she had done it.

“How long have you known?”

“Since yesterday. I wanted to see if he came again.” Na’ar took his hand off her arm. “He is not dangerous. He is there so that she knows somebody can stand there.”

As they came down the stair, the man in brown turned and walked away along the quay without hurrying, and did not look at them.

Kadiatou did not wait to be asked.

She stood up when they reached her, and put the slate in the basket, and said, in the same flat voice she used for carters and their mules: “A man called Mahamane sent to me three days ago. He offered me two silvers a week to tell him where you go and who you talk to. I said I was a mouth. He said then I could be a mouth for him too. I said no.” She looked at Na’ar, briefly. “Then his man started standing next to me in the mornings.”

“Has he done anything?”

“He’s stood next to me.” Her face did not change. “He’s very good at it.”

“I’m sorry,” said Riarhin.

“I don’t want sorry. Sorry doesn’t pay anything.” Kadiatou picked up the basket. “My rate’s gone up. A silver a day, from today, and a half on top every day his man stands there. You’re not paying me for words now. You’re paying me for standing at the foot of this stair every morning while a thief’s man stands next to me, and still being a mouth when I get to the end of it.”

Riarhin looked at her.

It was a great deal of money for an interpreter. It was not a great deal of money for what Kadiatou had just described. She had priced it exactly. She had looked at the risk that had walked up and stood next to her and put a number on it, and she had come and told them the number to their faces instead of taking Mahamane’s two silvers and going on smiling.

Riarhin counted a silver and a half into her hand.

“For today,” she said. “And tomorrow, and every day after, until I go. And if his man does anything but stand, you tell me, and I’ll see to it.”

Kadiatou weighed the coins in her palm and put them away in two places.

“That’s a door,” she said. “What you just said. That you’ll see to it. You’ll be charged.”

“I know.”

“Good,” said Kadiatou, and turned for the canal. “Where are we going?”

“The south quarter,” said Riarhin. “To see a woman about her grandfather.”

Chapter Seven: Six Weeks of Lamp Oil

Hawa of the dyers’ street would not talk to her.

It was not rudeness. Riarhin had been refused by rude people all her life and knew the difference. The old woman in the doorway heard Kadiatou out, all of it, the careful dull form of words about a foreign buyer with an interest in the old harness trade, and went on shelling beans the whole time, and at the end of it looked up at Riarhin for the first time with a pair of eyes gone milky at the edges and entirely clear in the middle.

She said something short.

“She says,” said Kadiatou, “that her grandfather’s house was owed a sum by a man in Marcott for four journeys and was paid for three. She says she has told that to eleven buyers with an interest in the old harness trade, and a man from the Corps, and a priest, and a lawyer from Ecktoria who wanted to write a book. She says none of them paid it.” A pause. “She says come back when you’ve got something in your hand that isn’t a question.”

The old woman went back to her beans.

Riarhin stood in the dye-smelling street for a moment and looked at her. Then she said, through Kadiatou, “Thank you, grandmother,” in the form Kadiatou gave her, and went away.

It was a good answer. It told her what the price was going to be. It did not tell her what the sum was, or whose name it had been owed under, or who in Mbaro would say that the debt was real and not an old woman’s grievance. She would need all three before she came back, and none of them would be in the dyers’ street.

She needed, also, to think about the road.

Every road north out of Mbaro began at the north gate and went up onto the plateau along the canals, and then along the chain of wells and depots that Sounkalo wardened, and then out past the last of them to the salt pans; and there, if you wanted to go further toward the Wastes, the roads divided.

The obvious way went east.

It went east along the old caravan water toward the river-island, through country that the Ashaani had held once and had been argued out of, and still argued over, and there were stages on it that were Oshiiran and stages that were Ashaani and stages whose ownership depended on which season it was and who was asking. From the last of those stages, by the older surveys, you could turn north and be in the Wastes in four days.

It was the way the consignment had gone, eighty years ago. It was the way every foreigner who asked about the Wastes was told to go. And by the fourteenth day there was a man in Mbaro who very much wanted to sell it to her.

His name was Oumar. He had come to the lodging house on the twelfth evening with a letter of introduction from the oil-seller’s cousin, and he was lean and weathered and wore the indigo head-wrap of the salt caravans, and he had four hours of detail about the eastern route that he laid out on the table in front of her in beans and bits of bread. Which wells were sweet. Which stage-master took a present and which one took offence. Where the Ashaani inspectors sat and what they looked at and how a sensible traveller got past them without being written down in a book that went up the river. He had been out that way, he said, eleven times. He would take her out a twelfth, with her string, for a fee that was high but not insulting.

It was good detail. It was, she thought, a little too good. It had the shape of something that had been learned very well a long time ago and had not been looked at since.

She said she would think about it.

On the fourteenth morning, at the Hall of Carriers, she met the woman who had noticed her.

She had gone back to the Hall for the pack-animal allotments. Nobody took a string out of Mbaro’s north gate without one: it was a paper, stamped by the Hall and countersigned by the Corps, that said how many animals of what kind you were entitled to walk through the gate and water at the first three Covenant wells, and it was the paper on which every caravan house’s season depended. She did not want one yet. She wanted to know how they were issued, and by whom, and how long it took; and she was standing at the long counter in the allotment room with Kadiatou, reading the posted scale of fees, when the woman at the next counter said, in excellent Ecktorian, without turning her head:

“Six weeks of lamp oil.”

Riarhin did not turn her head either.

“I’m sorry?”

“Six weeks of lamp oil,” the woman said again, conversationally, still reading the paper in front of her. “From Sissoko in the Salt District. I’ve been trying to buy oil for my own people for a fortnight and he told me he’d just sold six weeks of it to a foreign woman in one go and his price had gone up.” She turned a page. “So either you’re going somewhere very dark, or you read in bed.”

Riarhin looked at her then.

She was perhaps forty-five. She was tall, and very straight in the back, and had close-cropped hair going grey in two streaks at the temples, and she wore a long white cotton wrap with a border woven in green and red over a plain dark dress, and there were ink stains on the second and third fingers of her right hand that had been there so long they had gone into the skin. She had a lined, clever, deeply tired face, and she was not smiling, and the corners of her mouth were doing a thing that suggested she had to work at not smiling.

“Both,” said Riarhin.

The woman looked at her for a moment. Then she did smile, briefly, and looked back at her paper.

“Sada Alemu,” she said. “I’m the Taharkan envoy. I’ve been in Mbaro nine years, and I’ve been in this room for nine days, and I am going to be in this room until somebody in a red coat countersigns forty pack allotments for the Crown of Taharka that were agreed in principle in the spring and have not been issued because the Corps says the northern wells are under pressure this season.” She wrote something on her paper. “And this morning they’re under more pressure than they were yesterday, and I’d like to know why, and so far all I’ve been able to find out is that there’s a foreign woman who’s bought six weeks of lamp oil and asked Old Tall Diallo for thirty camels to the north gate.”

Riarhin did not say anything for a moment.

She had been reading four readers for a fortnight. She had watched the Corps and the merchants and the thief and the salt house each pick up her sentence and finish it their own way, and she had pushed each of them a little further along the way they had chosen, and she had been rather pleased with herself.

She had not thought about the room she was standing in.

It was the room where pack animals were allotted. It was the room where the northern wells were divided up, on paper, between everybody who needed them. And somebody had been standing in it for nine days trying to get forty animals out of a gate, and every rumour Riarhin had put into the Salt District had come in through the door of this room and landed on this woman’s desk as a line of red ink.

The thirty camels had been for the caravan houses. The caravan houses were not the only people who could count camels.

“I’m sorry,” said Riarhin, and found that she meant it, which was inconvenient.

“Don’t be sorry. Sorry doesn’t countersign anything.” Sada Alemu turned another page. “I’m not asking you to be sorry. I’m asking you a question.”

“Which is?”

Sada put her pen down, and turned, and looked at her properly.

“Are you taking a string past the last Covenant well,” she said, “or are you making people think you are?”

It was a good question.

It was the best question anybody had asked her in Mbaro, and it had been asked by somebody who was not looking at the lamp oil, or the camels, or the water grants, or the strongboxes. She was looking at the fact that all four of those things had been visible. She had noticed that they had been visible to exactly the right people, in exactly the right order, and she had asked the question that fact raised.

Riarhin could have lied. She had three lies ready and any of them would have done.

She found that she did not want to.

“Both,” she said again. “I’m going past the last well. Not with thirty camels. Not this month. I’m making some people think I’m doing it sooner and bigger than I am, and I’m making some other people think I’m doing something else altogether.”

“Why?”

“Because I’m looking for something,” said Riarhin, “that other people have spent eighty years making sure nobody would find, and I’d like to find it before anybody else notices I’m looking.”

Sada looked at her for a long time.

Beside Riarhin, Kadiatou had gone perfectly still, in the way of an interpreter who has just heard something she will be asked about for the rest of her life and has decided, very firmly, that she did not hear it.

“That’s an honest answer,” said Sada eventually. “It’s not a whole one.”

“No.”

“Good. I didn’t want a whole one. I’d have had to do something about it.” She picked up her pen. “What I want is to get forty mules through that gate before the passes close. Can you stop buying camels?”

“I never bought any.”

“Can you stop asking for them?”

“Yes.”

“Then do that, and I’ll be grateful, and I won’t mention you to anybody in a red coat.” She wrote something. “Which I wasn’t going to anyway. I don’t mention things to the Corps. I have a treaty with the Corps. It’s much more efficient.”

Riarhin should have left then. She had been noticed; she had given an answer; she had been told what it would cost her. That was the transaction. She did not leave.

“You said the wells are under pressure,” she said. “Are they?”

“Some of them.” Sada did not look up. “The ones on the eastern water are. Not because of you. Because of the east.”

“What about the east?”

Sada put her pen down again. She looked at Riarhin for another moment, the way a woman looks at a coin somebody has handed her, deciding whether it is worth the weighing.

“I’ll tell you something,” she said, “because you told me something, and because I’ve got nine days in this room and I’d like at least one of them to have been useful to somebody. The eastern route out of the salt pans. The one everybody tells foreigners to take to the Wastes. There’s been a new inspection on it since the rains. The Ashaani put it at the fourth stage, at the old well at Tin Kerru, and they’re not looking at loads, they’re looking at people, and they’re writing them down.” She said it plainly, without emphasis. “I know because it’s in my report. I’m supposed to be negotiating how Taharkan traffic gets handled on that road and I’ve had three merchants in this month complaining that they were held at Tin Kerru for four days while somebody copied their names.”

Riarhin said nothing. She was thinking about Oumar, and his beans, and his four hours of detail, and where the Ashaani inspectors sat.

“And if somebody,” Sada went on, “has been selling you the eastern route, you might ask him when he last went out on it. Because nobody who’s been out on it since the rains would be selling it to anybody this season. Not to a foreigner. Not with the Ashaani at Tin Kerru writing names.”

She went and checked.

She did not doubt Sada. She had no reason to doubt her and several reasons not to, and she checked anyway, because she had spent her whole life checking things that she did not doubt, and that was where half the useful things had come from.

It took her two days. She went to the salt caravans’ own house on the north quay, where the indigo-wrapped caravan men drank and argued and settled their shares, and sat in a corner with Kadiatou and a pot of millet beer, and listened. She did not ask about Oumar. She asked, through Kadiatou, and in the dullest voice she owned, whether the eastern road had been hard going since the rains.

It had. Everybody in the house had an opinion about Tin Kerru and wanted to give it to her. It had cost one man a fortnight’s wages and another a whole string of animals, which had had to be watered at a Covenant well out of turn while he waited, and he had been fined for it by the Corps on his way home. They had all been held. They had all been written down. They were all furious about it, in the way men are furious about a thing that has happened to them rather than a thing they have heard about.

Nobody mentioned Oumar. She asked, at last, idly, whether a man called Oumar had been held too.

The house went quiet for a moment. Then an old caravan man by the wall laughed, a short laugh without much in it.

“Oumar hasn’t been past the pans for three years,” he said, and Kadiatou gave it to her word for word. “Not since his knees went. He sells the road now. He’s very good at it. Sells it to people off the ships.” He drank. “He can tell you where every well was, three years back.”

She did not see Sada Alemu at the Hall of Carriers again for four days.

When she did, it was in the courtyard, in the evening, when the Hall was closing, and Sada was sitting on the edge of the dry fountain in the middle of it with her white wrap pulled up round her shoulders and a clay cup in her hand and a look of a woman who had been in one room for thirteen days.

Riarhin crossed the courtyard.

“You checked,” said Sada, before she got there.

“How do you know?”

“Because the caravan house on the north quay is full of men who’ve been talking about Tin Kerru for two days, to anybody who’ll listen, and every one of them has told me about the foreign woman who sat in the corner and asked whether the eastern road had been hard going.” Sada drank. “You didn’t ask about me. You asked about the road. That was well done.”

“You’re not offended.”

“That somebody checked what I told her?” Sada looked up at her. It was a long look, and at the end of it her mouth did the thing at the corners again, and this time she let it. “I’m a diplomat. People take what I tell them and go and act on it without checking, every day of my life, and then come and tell me it was my fault when it turns out I left something out. You’re the first person in Mbaro who’s gone and checked, in a way I’d have done it, and then not come back to tell me about it.”

“I’ve come back.”

“You have.” Sada moved over on the edge of the fountain. “Sit down. You’re making the courtyard look untidy.”

Riarhin sat down.

Sada had a clay pot beside her on the stones, wrapped in a cloth to keep it warm, and a second cup, and she poured without asking. It was coffee: black, strong, very hot, with a smell of something smoky under it that Riarhin did not know.

She drank it. It was very good.

“They’re closing the Hall,” Sada said. “I’ve got my forty allotments. I got them this afternoon. The Corps countersigned them at the third hour with a note that said the pressure on the northern wells had eased, and I’m going to have that note framed.” She drank. “I’m going home.”

“To Taharka.”

“To Adur-Gah. I’ve been nine years in Mbaro. I’ve got a report that’s four hundred pages long and an agreement on grain and pack transport and the border traffic that’s going to make at least two people very unhappy when it’s put into effect, and I’ve got to go and stand in front of the Crown Council and explain why I agreed to it.” She said it without complaint. “I was due home at the end of the season. I’ll be going a little sooner. There’s a delegation to organise.”

Riarhin held her cup.

She could see what was coming. She did not know how she could see it, and she saw it anyway, the way she sometimes saw the shape of a room before she went into it. She did not want it and she did not want to not want it.

Sada poured again. A second cup.

Riarhin had seen the Taharkan clerks at the Hall do this with each other, over thirteen days, and had seen what happened when an Ecktorian factor had held up his hand and refused the second cup, and how the room had gone quiet and pleasant and very slightly colder, and stayed that way for the rest of the afternoon.

She held out her cup, and it was filled.

Sada watched her do it.

“You noticed that,” she said.

“I’ve been standing in your Hall for three days.”

“Most people stand in it for a year and don’t.” Sada set the pot down. “Where are you actually going? Not the whole answer. Just the direction.”

“North.”

“From here?”

“From wherever I can.”

Sada looked at the fountain for a while, and then at the sky, which was going dark and violet over the Tower Bank, with the Granary Tower black against it.

“I’ll tell you what I’d do,” she said. “If I were somebody else. If I were going north, into the Wastes, and I didn’t want the Ashaani writing my name in a book at Tin Kerru.” She drank. “I wouldn’t go east at all.”

She did not say any more than that. She did not have to.

Riarhin sat on the edge of a dry fountain in a courtyard in Mbaro with a cup of Taharkan coffee going cold between her palms and looked at the woman beside her, and understood that she was being offered something, and that it had been offered very carefully so that it could be refused without anybody losing anything.

She did not refuse it. She did not accept it either. She drank the coffee.

“Thank you for the road,” she said instead.

“Thank you for the camels,” said Sada, “or rather, for not buying them.” She stood up and gathered the pot and the cloth. “I’m at the Taharkan house on the Tower Bank, by the tamarind with the broken branch. If you want to talk about lamp oil.”

She went off across the courtyard, straight-backed, with the white wrap round her shoulders, and did not look back.

Riarhin sat on the fountain in the dark until a porter came and told her the Hall was closed. She had forgotten she was sitting there. She had been thinking about a woman who had looked at four purchases and one camel price and seen, not what Riarhin was doing, but that she was doing something on purpose.

It was a long time since anybody had done that. She found that she wanted to go on being seen doing it.

She went back to the lodging house and told Na’ar that they were not going east.

He looked up from the pot he was stirring.

“Good,” he said. “I did not like the man with the beans.”

Chapter Eight: The Daughter Who Kept the Book

The sum was on the eighth leaf, and it had a line through it.

Riarhin had it by heart and took the leaf out anyway, in the lodging house with the shutters closed, and laid it on the table under the lamp. Diarra of the Two Girths. For account of the consignment, fourth journey: harness and fittings for eleven animals, water-skin frames for six, and the return allowance as agreed. A sum in Marcott silver. A date. And a single stroke through the sum, in a different ink from the entry, made by somebody who had not bothered to blot it.

She had looked at that stroke on the floor of Serafine’s counting room and filed it as not paid. It was more than that. Somebody at the Marcott end had written the entry, meaning to pay, and later somebody had come back to the book and decided not to. The stroke was a decision. And the old woman in the dyers’ street had been telling her neighbours about it for fifty years.

She could not walk into the dyers’ street with a Marcott sum on a stolen leaf and say this is what you’re owed. Hawa had had eleven buyers and a priest. Every one of them had come with a question and a sum of their own. The old woman did not want to be told what her family was owed by a foreigner. She wanted it paid.

And for that, somebody in Mbaro had to say the claim was real.

It took her three days, and it was dull, and she liked it.

A saddler in Mbaro who had not been paid by a chandler in Marcott for work done on a caravan out of Mbaro would not have sat and complained about it. Not in this city. He would have gone to whoever settled such things, and they would have written it down, because in Mbaro somebody always did.

The Hall of Carriers kept the carriers’ disputes. Kadiatou told her so, and then told her that she was going to charge for that, and then took her to a long low room at the back of the Hall where two elderly clerks sat at facing desks under a skylight and between them, on shelves that went up into the roof, were the disputes of every carrier’s guild in Mbaro for a hundred and fifty years, tied in bundles by year and labelled in a hand that had changed four times.

The clerks were very pleased to see her. Nobody had asked to see the old disputes, they said, in years.

She did not ask for Diarra. She asked, through Kadiatou, for the disputes of the harness-makers against foreign parties for the decade of the date on the eighth leaf, and sat down at a third desk under the skylight, and read.

It was in the fifth bundle.

The Two Girths, Diarra, saddler, against Hollis of Marcott, chandler, for work done and fittings supplied on a fourth journey north from the gate of Mbaro, the consignment being as before. The Hall had heard it. The Hall had found for Diarra, and ordered the sum paid, and sent the finding to Marcott by the next ship. And beneath that, in another hand, two years later: No answer received from Marcott. Hollis reported dead. Claim stands. And beneath that, in a third, twenty years later still: Claim stands.

It had never been closed. Nobody had ever paid it and nobody had ever cancelled it. It sat in the fifth bundle in a room at the back of the Hall of Carriers, a finding in the saddler’s favour that the Hall had made and could not enforce, and it had sat there so long that the only person in Mbaro who still remembered it was an old woman shelling beans.

Riarhin sat under the skylight with the finding in her hands for a long time.

She had the eighth leaf in her head. She had the finding in front of her. The sums were the same, to the coin. The date of the journey was the same. The consignment was as before in both, in two books, in two countries, eighty years ago, and nobody writing either had known what the other was.

She did not need anything else to know it was real. She would need a great deal more to make anybody else believe it, and that was not what the old woman wanted either.

She went back to the dyers’ street on the nineteenth morning with Kadiatou and nothing in her hand.

Hawa was in the doorway with the beans. She looked up, saw who it was, and went back to them.

“Grandmother,” said Riarhin, and waited for Kadiatou to say it, and went on. “The Hall of Carriers found for your grandfather against Hollis of Marcott for the fourth journey. It’s in the disputes room, in the fifth bundle for that year. The finding stands. It was never paid and it was never cancelled.”

The beans stopped.

“I’ve read it,” said Riarhin. “I know the sum. I haven’t brought it.”

Hawa looked up at her.

“I haven’t brought it,” Riarhin said again, “because I don’t know how you want it paid. It’s your grandfather’s claim. I’d like to settle it. I’d like to settle it however you think it ought to be settled, and then I’d like to ask you whether he left any papers about the journey.”

Kadiatou said all of that, in exactly the words, and stopped.

The old woman sat on her step with the bowl of beans on her knees and looked at Riarhin for a very long time. She had the eyes that were milky at the edges and clear in the middle, and they went over Riarhin’s face slowly, and then her hands, and then her face again.

Then she said something, and Kadiatou’s face moved very slightly before she translated it.

“She says: you’re the first one who’s read it.”

Hawa wanted it paid at the Hall.

Not to her. To the Hall, in the room with the skylight, in front of both clerks, so that it would be written into the finding under the three hands already there that the claim of Diarra of the Two Girths against Hollis of Marcott had been settled in full, in the eightieth year after, and by whom. She wanted her grandfather’s name in the book with the word settled after it. She did not want the money. She said so, several times, with some force. The money could go to the Hall’s fund for carriers’ widows, which her grandfather had paid into all his life, and which had paid her grandmother’s rent for a year after the Marcott debt had put the Two Girths under.

Riarhin did not argue. She would not have argued if Hawa had wanted it paid at the top of the Granary Tower.

It took most of a day. The clerks had never had a claim settled from that far back and had to find out whether they were allowed to write under a finding made before either of them was born, and then which of them should do it, and then which ink. Hawa came across the canal in a borrowed litter with her granddaughter and a neighbour and sat in the room with the skylight on a chair the clerks fetched for her, and watched the whole of it without saying anything. The neighbour said a great deal. Half the dyers’ street had come, by the end, and stood in the corridor outside.

Riarhin paid in Oshiiran silver, counted onto the clerk’s desk, and signed the book in the name she had given at the money house, which was not her own and which she would never use again.

The elder clerk wrote settled under the finding and turned the book round so that Hawa could see it.

Hawa looked at it. Then she reached out one hand and put two fingers on the word, very lightly, the way one puts two fingers on the forehead of somebody asleep.

She took her hand back and said to her granddaughter, in Oshiiran, something that made the granddaughter laugh and wipe her eyes.

“She says,” said Kadiatou, “that now she’ll have to find something else to talk about.”

The papers were in a cedar box under Hawa’s bed.

There was not a day-book. Riarhin had half known there would not be; a saddler’s day-book goes where the business goes, and the business had gone to the Coulibaly family and been used for a hundred years and thrown out when it was full. What there was, was what a family keeps when it is going to be angry for eighty years: the grievance.

A copy of the finding, in a clerk’s hand. Two letters to Hollis of Marcott, unanswered, in draft. A note in a woman’s hand, Hawa’s grandmother’s, of what the debt had cost: the shop, the house, a son’s apprenticeship. And, tied together with a strip of leather, the carriers’ papers for the four journeys, because the saddler had kept them to prove his claim, and had never been asked to prove it, and had kept them anyway.

Riarhin read them in Hawa’s front room with the indigo cloths hanging in the window and the smell of the dye vats coming in off the street, while Hawa sat on her step outside and shelled beans and talked to the neighbour about the Hall.

The first three journeys were ordinary. They were bills of carriage for a caravan going north out of Mbaro with a load that was listed by weight and by the number of animals and not by what it was: so many head, so much weight on each, fodder, water, the stages it would water at. Those three had gone north and come back. They had used the eastern water, out past the salt pans toward the river-island, and turned north from there.

The fourth was different.

The fourth had gone north out of Mbaro on the same road and then turned west.

She did not see it at once. She read the bill three times before she understood what she was looking at.

The saddler had been paid to fit out a caravan in two parts. That was plain from the first line: harness for eleven animals as far as the last watered stage, and frames for six water-skins beyond it, and one carrying-chest with poles. The bill set out the weight each animal would carry to the last stage, and it set out, separately, in a second line, the weight that would be carried past it.

And the second weight was divided again. So much, packed: tools, food, the load itself. So much, water.

Riarhin had built the journey out of fodder and water on the floor of Serafine’s counting room. She had got eleven days beyond the last watered stage from the fodder, and she had thought, at the time, that eleven days was the length of the unwatered leg.

The bill did not say eleven days. The bill said that the water carried past the last stage was very nearly half again what eleven days would need for the party that was carrying it. She worked it twice. She did not need to.

Six skins. A small party, then. Light. Every animal carrying water and almost nothing else. And more water than they needed to get there.

And the return allowance as agreed.

It had been on the eighth leaf. She had read it as a phrase. It was not a phrase. It was a line on a bill, and the saddler had been paid for it separately, and it was the reason the water was half again what the outward journey needed.

Somebody, eighty years ago, had fitted out a light party to go eleven days past the last water carrying something. And they had given it enough water to come back slowly. Much more slowly than it had gone.

As though they had expected somebody to come back hurt. Or carrying somebody. Or walking a long way round.

The last watered stage had a name on the bill, and the name had been damaged.

It was at the fold of the paper, where the bill had been folded into four and kept that way for eighty years, and the fold had worn through, and the ink along it had gone. What remained was the end of a word in the old Oshiiran hand, which she could not read, and the beginning of a second word after it, which she could also not read. She had Kadiatou copy the letters that were left for her, sound by sound.

…madhaq. Wells of.

Kadiatou looked at it and shrugged. “It’s not a place I know.”

Riarhin sat with it.

She had seen it before. She knew she had seen it, and she did not know where, and that was a thing that almost never happened to her; if she had seen it she had attended to it, and if she had attended to it she had it. She sat in Hawa’s front room and went back through the last three weeks, day by day, the way she would go back through a road she had walked.

Not the leaves. Not the survey copies. Not the Hall rolls.

The grants.

She had read the northern water grants twice at the canal-mothers’ bench under the tamarind, for the benefit of a man in a red coat. She had read every one. And the oldest of them, the grants that were so old that nobody drew on them any longer and the canal-mothers kept them only because nothing in Oshiira is ever thrown away, had been made out in the old names for the old districts, some of which had not existed for three hundred years.

One of them had been a grant to draw at the wells of a district called Imadhaq: the great well of Imadhaq, of seven courses, under the seal of the keepers, by the high road.

It had not been on the eastern water. It had been far to the west of it, north and west, on a road that the modern surveys did not show at all.

She asked, carefully, at the canal-mothers’ bench the next morning.

She did not ask where Imadhaq was. She asked the youngest of the three canal-mothers, through Kadiatou, which of the old district names in the grants were still used by anybody.

The canal-mother thought about it and gave her a list. Imadhaq was not on it.

“That one’s gone,” she said, when Riarhin asked. “Long gone. It was a district of the old high road, when the high road went north from the plateau to the Taharkan side. The road silted up two hundred years ago. There’s nothing there now but a well or two the herders use.” She looked at Kadiatou and said something else, amused. “She says the relic dealers in the Salt District have been putting your green glass at a shrine on the salt pans this month. East. They put everything there. She says the salt pans must be very full of green glass by now.”

Riarhin did not ask how the canal-mother knew about the green glass. She had not told anybody in Mbaro about the green glass. She had only asked a clerk for a roll of harness-makers, and a salt house had come in the next day and asked for the same roll, and somewhere between the two somebody had begun to put a price on a word.

It did not matter. The relic dealers were selling a shrine to the east. The bill said west.

It was not a map. It was the last watered stage on a road that had silted up two hundred years ago, on the far side of the plateau from the one everybody sold, and a party that had been given enough water to come back slowly.

It was the best thing she had had in her hand since Silkstrand.

The offer came on the twenty-first day, and it came through the money house.

The eldest of Tiemoko’s daughters gave it to her in the long cool room with the tiled floor, with the air of a woman handing over a letter she has read and does not approve of. It was from the factor of the salt house on the Tower Bank: the one who had asked for the roll at the Hall of Carriers. It was very polite. It said that the factor had a client with an interest in the history of the old northern trade, and that the client understood the foreign lady had lately acquired some family papers relating to that trade, and that the client would be glad to purchase copies of any such papers, at a generous rate, for purposes of scholarship.

Riarhin read it standing at the counter.

A week ago, the salt house had been tracing an old inland consignment. That was what the clerk had said. It had been interested in a debt and a direction: eighty years old, a family in the north. The kind of thing a man puts in a letter because he has been told to report anything that touches on old consignments and has found something that does.

It was not interested in a debt now. It did not mention a debt. It did not mention a direction, or a route, or the northern stages, or camels, or the wells. It had been told, by somebody who had been in the corridor outside the room with the skylight, or in the dyers’ street, or in Hawa’s front room, that a foreign woman had paid an eighty-year-old debt to get at a box of papers.

Nobody surveying a route for a caravan house pays an eighty-year-old debt. Nobody doing that pays anybody anything they can avoid.

So the salt house had stopped thinking she was surveying anything. It had begun thinking she was looking for a particular thing, in particular papers, and it wanted copies of the papers.

That was the whole of what the letter told her, and it was a great deal. It did not tell her what the factor had written to the river-island. It did not tell her whether anybody there had read it, or cared, or what they would do. She could not see into that room and never would. She could only see that the man who was writing to it had changed his mind about her, and had changed it in the direction she least wanted, and that she had given him the reason herself, in the only way she could have got the papers.

“Is there an answer?” said the eldest daughter.

“No,” said Riarhin.

“Good,” said the eldest daughter, and put the letter in the box with Keita’s reference, and did not say anything else.

That evening she laid it out for Na’ar in the lodging house, all of it, because she had been carrying it round Mbaro for three weeks in her own head and had begun to notice that she was talking to Kadiatou more than was good for either of them.

He listened. He ate. He did not ask anything until she had finished.

Then he said, “The western road. The one that is silted.”

“Yes.”

“It goes to the Taharkan side.”

“It went there.”
