He looked at her across the table for a while. There was a pot of millet porridge between them with a crust on it, and he broke the crust with his spoon, thoughtfully, and did not eat any.

“The woman with the coffee,” he said. “She is going to Taharka.”

“She’s going home.”

“With a delegation.”

“Yes.”

He put the spoon down.

“You were going to tell me that,” he said, “at some point.”

It was not a question. He said it mildly, the way he said most things. She looked at him and saw that he was not being mild.

“I was going to tell you when I’d decided.”

“Yes,” said Na’ar. “That is what I mean.” He picked the spoon up again and began to eat, and did not say anything else about it, and after a while she understood that he was not going to, and that he had said the whole of what he meant to say, and that he had meant her to hear it.

She sat and watched him eat, and did not decide anything, and went to bed.

Chapter Nine: Something Larger and Legitimate

She told Na’ar in the morning, before she had decided, which was the hardest thing she did that week.

He was on the roof of the lodging house, where he had taken to sitting at dawn with a cup of the bitter green tea the Oshiirans drank and watching the barges come up the canal to the weigh-house, and she came up the ladder and sat down on the parapet beside him and said it all in one piece, without arranging it first.

“I want to go to Taharka with the envoy’s party,” she said. “I don’t know if she’ll have me. If she will, it’s a long way round and it’ll take months, and when we get there I’ll have to find some other way north, and I don’t know what that is either. It means leaving the salt pans and the eastern road and the Ashaani to whoever’s watching them. It means going somewhere on somebody else’s timetable.” She looked at the canal. “I haven’t asked her. I’m going to ask her today. I’m telling you first.”

Na’ar drank his tea.

“Why?”

“Because you asked me last night why I hadn’t.”

“I did not ask why you had not.” He set the cup down on the parapet. “I said you were going to tell me. You were going to tell me after. I said that was what I meant.”

“And now I’m telling you before.”

“Yes,” said Na’ar. He looked at her with a sort of patient interest, as though she were a horse that had done something slightly unexpected with a gate. “So now I can say. I will come.”

That was all. He picked up the cup again.

She sat on the parapet for a while longer and found, to her annoyance, that her hands were not quite steady, and put them in her lap.

“That’s it?” she said.

“That is it. I have never been to Taharka. They have horses there that are said to climb like goats. And coffee.” He considered. “And the woman is interesting.”

“She’s a diplomat.”

“Yes,” said Na’ar. “I said she was interesting.”

The Taharkan house on the Tower Bank stood behind a wall of red mud brick with a gate in it painted green, and the tamarind with the broken branch leaned over the wall and dropped its pods into the street, where children fought over them.

The gatekeeper was an old man with a white beard and a white shawl and the air of somebody who had kept this gate for the whole of Sada Alemu’s nine years and several envoys before that, and did not propose to be impressed by any of them. He listened to Riarhin’s name and went away across the courtyard without hurrying and came back with a boy, and the boy took her through two courtyards and a passage into a room on the upper floor that had been an office and was in the process of becoming luggage.

There were chests on the floor with their lids open. There were papers in stacks on every surface, tied with red and green tape. There was a secretary at a desk by the window, a thin young man with spectacles, writing something with enormous speed. And in the middle of all of it, sitting on one of the chests with a board across her knees and a pen in her hand, was Sada Alemu, in the same white wrap, looking as though she had slept four hours and resented all four.

“You’ve come about lamp oil,” she said.

“I’ve come about a job.”

Sada put the pen down.

“What job?” she said.

Riarhin had worked it out on the walk across the Tower Bank. She had worked it out several times, in several shapes, and had discarded all of them but one, and was now about to find out whether the one was any good.

“You told me four days ago,” she said, “that you’re taking home an agreement on grain access and pack transport and the handling of border traffic, and that you’re going to have to stand up in front of your Council and defend it. And you told me that three merchants had come to you this month to complain about being held at Tin Kerru. And your forty allotments were held up for nine days because the Corps said the wells were under pressure.” She stood in the middle of the room with her hands at her sides. “So your agreement is about how Taharkan traffic moves on Oshiiran roads, and who contracts for it, and how it’s guarded, and who’s responsible when it’s held. And your Council’s going to ask you whether any of it works. And the only people you can call on to tell them are Oshiiran carriers, who have an interest, and Taharkan merchants, who have a different one, and your own staff, who work for you.”

Sada did not say anything. The secretary had stopped writing.

“I’ve spent most of my life on roads that other people were paying to keep open,” said Riarhin. “I’ve scouted for an army. I’ve been hired to guard caravans and hired to rob them and hired to find out who was robbing them. I know what a contract for carriage looks like when it’s going to be honoured and when it isn’t. I know what a guard’s worth by the way he stands at a gate. I can tell you what’s wrong with a stage before the stage-master’s finished pouring the tea.” She paused. “You need somebody who doesn’t belong to either side to walk your road with you and write down what happens on it. For your Council. I’m offering.”

The room was very quiet.

Sada looked at her for a long time. Then she looked at the secretary.

“Tesfaye,” she said. “What did I say to you on the second day at the Hall?”

The secretary pushed his spectacles up his nose. “You said,” he said carefully, “that what this report needed was an independent assessor of northern contracting practice and road security, and that the Council was going to ask for one, and that there wasn’t one to be had in Mbaro for any money because anybody who knew enough about the roads to be worth hiring was already being paid by somebody who used them.”

“Thank you, Tesfaye.”

“You also said—”

“Thank you, Tesfaye,” said Sada, and he went back to writing.

She did not accept at once.

She made Riarhin sit down on another chest, and she asked her questions for an hour, and they were the kind of questions that a person asks when they mean to write the answers down and be held to them later. What roads. Whose armies. What years, roughly. What she had been paid, roughly, and by whom, and whether they had got their money’s worth. Riarhin answered all of it, and told the truth about all of it, and left out the parts that were nobody’s business, and Sada watched her leave them out and did not press.

At the end of the hour Sada put her board down.

“You understand what you’d be,” she said. “You’d be on the delegation’s roll as a contracted assessor. You’d be paid from the delegation’s funds at the rate for a clerk of the second grade, which is not very much. You’d have a paper with the Crown’s seal on it that says who you are and what you’re for, and anybody on the road who has the right to see it will see it. The Corps will see it. The Taharkan border wardens will see it. And when we get to Adur-Gah you’ll be expected to write a report, a real one, and put your name to it, and stand behind it if the Council has questions.” She looked at Riarhin steadily. “It’s not a cover. I’m not giving you a false title so you can walk through gates. If you come with me as an assessor, you’ll assess.”

“I know.”

“You’ll have to use your own name.”

Riarhin had not used her own name in Mbaro. She had not used it anywhere, for any purpose that would be written down, in a very long time.

“Yes,” she said.

Sada went on looking at her.

“All right,” she said. “Then the next question is the one you came here to answer, and you haven’t yet. What are you looking for, and who’s going to come after my delegation because you’re in it?”

Riarhin had known it was coming. She had known it would be asked in this room, by this woman, before anything was signed, and she had spent a long part of the walk across the Tower Bank deciding how much to say.

She had decided on a sentence. She had decided on two. She had decided, at the corner by the tamarind, on half of the truth, well arranged, and she had been quite confident about it.

She sat on the chest in the middle of a room full of luggage with the whole of the half-truth ready, and looked at Sada, and said the whole thing instead.

She said Kalrantha. She said it was a word in three northern books, and that in all three it was the same paragraph, copied, and she said the paragraph: green glass, brass frame, the Himadri, a wish with a clause. She said that a man she had known had spent years and a great deal of money on it and had died without it, and that she did not think he had known what it was either. She said that she had nine leaves out of a file in Silkstrand that she had not come by fairly, and that the leaves showed a consignment moved north out of Mbaro eighty years ago in four journeys, and that the fourth had gone west up a road that had silted up, to the wells of a district that no longer existed, and then eleven days past the last water with enough water to come back slowly.

And she said the thing she had not meant to say at all.

“I don’t know what it is. I don’t know if it’s dangerous. I don’t know if it’s worth anything to anybody. The only thing I’m sure of is that for eighty years, four different sets of people in four different places, who weren’t working together and didn’t agree about anything else, went back to the records by hand and cut things out so that the next person would know less. That’s the one fact I’ve got that I trust. Everything else is dealers.”

Sada did not move.

“Some people think it’s a demon,” Riarhin went on. “Or a piece of one. The Esoiti think so, I’m told. The relic trade thinks so. I can’t tell you they’re wrong. I can tell you that nobody who says so has ever held it, and neither have I.” She stopped. “If anybody comes after your delegation because I’m in it, it’ll be because they’ve heard that word, or because they’ve heard that somebody’s following an old consignment north and they want to know what was in it. Right now I think three people in Mbaro are close to that. One of them’s a salt factor on this bank who writes letters up the river. I don’t know what he’s written. I don’t think he knows the word yet.”

It was very quiet. The secretary, Tesfaye, had not written anything for some time.

Sada sat on the chest with her hands folded on the board on her knees and looked at the floor between them, and Riarhin watched her think, and did not try to read what she was thinking, because she found that she did not want to know before she was told.

“Why did you tell me that?” Sada said at last.

“Because you asked.”

“People don’t answer because I ask. They answer as much as they think will get them what they want.” Sada looked up. “You came in here with a sentence ready. I watched you have it ready. And then you didn’t say it.”

Riarhin opened her mouth, and closed it again.

“I don’t know,” she said.

It was true. It was also the most frightening thing she had said in the whole conversation, and she felt it go out of her into the room and not come back, and sat with her hands in her lap and let it be there.

Sada looked at her a moment longer. Then she nodded, once, as though a figure had come out right at the bottom of a page.

“All right,” she said. “Here’s what I can do and what I can’t.”

What she could not do was pledge the Crown of Taharka to an undisclosed venture into the Red Wastes. She said it plainly and more than once. She was an envoy. She had authority to negotiate agreements on grain and transport and traffic, and to bring them home for the Council to accept or refuse. She did not have authority to commit a single Taharkan soldier, mule or coin to anything that was not in her instructions, and a private expedition to find a relic that might be a demon was not in her instructions, and she was not going to pretend it was to anybody, on the road or off it.

What she could do was take an assessor home with her delegation.

“You’ll come to Adur-Gah as what you are,” she said. “On my roll, on my road, under my protection, which on the southern road is worth a good deal and in the highlands is worth rather more. You’ll do the work. When we get there, you’ll write your report. And then what you do next is your business and the Council’s, and not mine, and I won’t pretend otherwise to either of you.”

“And the road north?”

“There’s a road north from Adur-Gah. It goes up through the highland stages to the border wardens’ country, and past that to places that nobody’s gone to on purpose for a very long time.” Sada picked up her pen. “If your old high road ever went to the Taharkan side, it went through country the Crown Council has an opinion about. I’m not going to tell you what the opinion is. I don’t know yet. You’ll have to ask them.”

“You’ll tell them what I’ve told you.”

“I’ll tell them what they need to know, when they need to know it.” Sada met her eyes. “I’m not going to lie to my Council to keep your secret. I’m not going to hand it to them over the first cup of coffee either. If something you’re doing comes to affect the passes, or the border, or the people who live on the road north, I’ll say so. In the report, if it belongs there. To your face, first.”

It was said very steadily. It was not a threat and it was not a courtesy. It was a description of where the edge of the thing was, laid down in advance so that neither of them could later pretend not to have seen it.

Riarhin found that she liked it better than anything she could have been offered.

“That’s fair,” she said.

“It’s not fair. It’s what I can do.” Sada wrote something on her board and then looked at it and crossed it out. “There’s one more thing.”

“Yes?”

“The longer route. Through Taharka.” Sada put the pen down. “It isn’t invisible. Nothing that goes out of this city with forty mules and a Crown seal on it is invisible. The Corps will write us down at the south gate, and the salt house will hear about it by nightfall, and anybody who cares to can find out that a foreign woman left Mbaro on the Taharkan envoy’s roll.” She shrugged. “What it does is change whose authority you have to satisfy. The eastern road runs past the Ashaani. This one runs past us. I think you’ll find us easier to argue with. I don’t think you’ll find us easier to fool.”

“I wasn’t planning to fool you.”

“No,” said Sada. “You weren’t planning to tell me any of that either.”

The paper was drawn up that afternoon by Tesfaye in a clear round hand, and it named her as Riarhin, of no fixed nation, contracted to the delegation of the Crown of Taharka as independent assessor of road security and carriage, at the rate of a clerk of the second grade, for the duration of the journey from Mbaro to Adur-Gah and the writing of a report thereafter. Sada signed it and sealed it with a seal that she took from a chain round her neck, a small bronze thing with a lion on it, and then passed it across to Riarhin to sign.

Riarhin looked at it for a moment before she did.

Her name was on it. Her own name, the one her mother had given her, written in a Taharkan clerk’s hand on a Taharkan Crown paper, with a lion on it. Anybody who wanted to find her, from now until the paper rotted, could come to the house by the tamarind and ask to see the delegation’s roll.

She signed it.

Sada watched her do that too.

“Na’ar,” Sada said, while Tesfaye was sanding the ink. “Your companion. Is he on the roll?”

“He hasn’t asked to be.”

“Will he?”

“You’d have to ask him.”

Sada’s eyebrows went up a fraction.

“I thought he was your man.”

“He’s his own man,” said Riarhin. “He’s coming because he wants to see the horses.”

Sada considered that. Then, for the first time in the whole of that long afternoon, she laughed, a short, low, surprised sound that seemed to take her by surprise as much as anybody.

“Then I’ll ask him,” she said. “We could use somebody who knows what a horse is for.”

She walked back across the Tower Bank in the evening with the paper inside her coat, next to the nine leaves and the three grey pieces of a letter that said nothing, and found that she was walking slowly.

She did not usually walk slowly. She walked the way she had walked since she was twelve, at the pace of somebody who has somewhere to be and does not want anybody to know where. She walked slowly now because she was thinking about what she had done in that room, and she did not want to arrive at the lodging house before she had finished.

She had told a stranger the truth. Not all of it; the part about the man above the dry well and why she had wanted a thing to find had stayed where it lived. But the object, and the file, and the fact that she could not substantiate the account everybody else believed about it. She had told all of that to a woman who had said, before she told it, that she would not keep it from her own Council.

She had never in her life told anybody anything of that kind without first working out what it would buy her.

She did not know what it had bought. She thought it had bought her a road. She thought, turning it over under the red evening sky with the Granary Tower black against it, that she would have told Sada anyway, road or no road, and that was the part she did not want to arrive at the lodging house with, because she did not know what to do with it and Na’ar would see her not knowing.

He did see. He was on the roof again, with a pot of something, and he looked at her when she came up the ladder and did not say anything for a while.

“She asked me,” he said eventually. “She sent a boy. With a paper. She wants somebody on the roll who knows animals.” He stirred the pot. “I said yes. I will be on a Taharkan paper. My brothers would laugh until they died.” He tasted, and added salt. “You told her.”

“How do you know?”

“Because the boy said she said you should both come to supper at the house tomorrow and she would introduce you to the people you would be travelling with.” Na’ar put the lid on the pot. “She does not ask people to supper when they have lied to her. I think she is not a woman who has to.”

Riarhin sat down on the parapet.

“No,” she said. “I don’t think she is.”

Chapter Ten: A Correct Inspection

The supper at the Taharkan house was the first meal in a month that Riarhin had not been working at, and she spent most of it working.

It was in the lower courtyard, under the tamarind, at a long low table with cushions round it, and there were eleven people at it and a great deal of food on flat grey bread that was also the plate, and a woman in the corner roasting coffee beans on an iron pan over charcoal so that the whole courtyard smelled of smoke and something like burnt sugar. Sada sat at the head of the table and did not preside. She ate with her fingers and argued with Tesfaye about a comma and laughed at something the muleteers’ headman said, and Riarhin watched her do it, and then made herself stop, and watched everybody else instead.

The delegation was not large. Sada, and Tesfaye with his spectacles, and two older clerks who had been in Mbaro nearly as long as she had and wanted very badly to go home. A junior envoy called Dawit, twenty-three or so, very correct, very anxious, who said yes, Envoy to everything and looked at the gate every time it opened. A sergeant of the Ridge-Watch called Gebre with a grey beard and a face like a walnut, who had eight men in the barracks at the back of the house and had been in Mbaro for six years guarding a building, and was so pleased at the prospect of guarding a road instead that he could not stop smiling. And Abdi, the muleteers’ headman, who was about seventy and had taken strings between Mbaro and Adur-Gah for fifty years and had opinions about everybody’s mules, including the Crown’s.

Na’ar sat next to Abdi. By the end of the meal they were not speaking the same language and were arguing about hooves with the whole of their hands.

Nobody asked Riarhin what she was for. She had been introduced as the assessor, and they had nodded, and passed her the bread, and the whole table had accepted that the envoy had hired an assessor in the same way it accepted that the envoy had hired more mules. It was not a thing that required an explanation. The envoy had decided it was needed.

She sat at the end of the long table with the grey bread in her fingers and found it very strange to be a line in somebody else’s arrangements.

The hold came two days later.

She had put the desert stores in a bonded warehouse on the north quay: the lamp oil, the cover-cloth, the brass fittings, the surveyor’s chain, and a quantity of dried meat and hard bread she had bought on the tenth day to go with the rest. She had put them there because that was where caravan stores went in Mbaro while their owners waited for an allotment, and because bonded goods were written into the warehouse’s book with a declared purpose, and she had declared the purpose that fitted what she had bought, which was provisioning a party for the northern stages beyond the Covenant wells.

It had been exactly true on the day she wrote it. It had been meant to be read.

On the morning of the twenty-fifth day she went down to the warehouse to begin moving what she could into the delegation’s loads, and found a red seal on the door of her bay and a man in a red coat sitting on a bale beside it with a cup.

It was Sounkalo.

He stood when he saw her, courteously, as he had at the canal-mothers’ bench, and said something in Oshiiran, and then, when she did not answer, said it again in slow careful Ecktorian.

“Good morning. The Covenant has placed a hold on these goods.”

He explained it to her very clearly, and he was right about all of it.

The goods had been bonded for provisioning a party going north beyond the Covenant wells. The party so provisioned would be drawing on the wells for its first stages and carrying its own water after. Stores for that purpose were admitted to bond on the understanding that they would go out by the north gate, with an allotment, and be written through the stages in the ordinary way.

The owner of the goods was now on the roll of the Taharkan envoy’s delegation, which had been issued its allotments three days ago, and those allotments were for the south gate and the southern road to Adur-Gah.

“So the goods are for the north,” said Sounkalo, “and the owner is going south. And the quantities are not a clerk’s. Six weeks of oil for a party of perhaps ten. Cover for twenty skins. A surveyor’s chain.” He did not smile. “Either the owner is not going south, or the goods are not for the purpose declared. Either one is a matter for the Corps. Until it is clear which, the goods stay.”

He was not unkind about it. She had spent three weeks feeding the Corps exactly this sentence, in exactly this order, and he had read it exactly as she meant him to, and had kept reading it past where she had meant him to stop. He had seen what she did at the bench. He had waited until she moved. And when she moved, he had found the one place where the thing she had declared on paper no longer fitted the thing she was doing, and had put a seal on it.

It was the rule she had been leaning on the whole time. It was a good rule. She would have written it herself.

“What happens to them?” she said.

“That depends on you.” Sounkalo turned his cup in his hand. “You can declare a new purpose. If it’s true and fits, the hold comes off. If it’s not true, I will know eventually, and the goods go to the Covenant stores, and you will be written down.” He looked at her with his pleasant, tired face. “Or you can leave them. People do. They leave goods in bond and go away and do not come back. After a year and a day the Covenant takes them for the northern depots. The oil will be useful. It is good oil.”

The offer came that afternoon, and it came from the man with the beans.

Oumar found her in the eating room of the lodging house with a pot of tea and sat down across from her without being asked, as Mahamane had, and put his hands on the table, and was very sorry to hear about her trouble at the warehouse.

She had not told anybody about the warehouse. She had been back from it two hours.

“I know a man,” said Oumar. “In the Corps. Not a warden. A clerk who keeps the holds. There are many holds, madam, many seals, and not every seal is looked at twice. A hold can be misfiled. A seal can be broken by a careless porter and never renewed.” He smiled. His knees were bad and he had sat down carefully, and she noticed that too. “It would not cost very much. Almost nothing. Only a small thing.”

“What thing?”

“You bought fair copies of three surveys at the copying-house on the Tower Bank,” said Oumar. “And I think you have made notes on them. And you have some papers from an old woman in the south quarter.” He spread his hands. “Copies. That’s all. Of the notes, and the papers. For a friend of mine who is interested in the old roads. And your hold goes into the wrong drawer.”

Riarhin looked at him across the table.

She had wondered, for three weeks, how Mahamane had known about the canvas and the fittings and the oil so soon, and how the salt house had known to ask for the roll the morning after she read it. She had assumed several people. She had assumed the city.

And here was one man with bad knees who had sat at her table for four hours on the twelfth evening laying out beans, and who had been sent to her by the oil-seller’s cousin, and who knew about the warehouse and the copying-house and the old woman in the south quarter. He was a man who sold roads he could no longer walk. He had found a way to go on selling them.

She did not know, yet, how many people he was selling her to. She could guess at two.

“I’ll think about it,” she said.

“Please,” said Oumar, as Mahamane had said it, and got up carefully, and went.

She went to Sada that evening and told her about the hold. She did not tell her about Oumar. Oumar was hers.

Sada listened in the half-packed office with a candle and a board on her knee and did not interrupt.

“He’s right,” she said, when Riarhin had finished.

“I know he’s right.”

“Good. Because I’m not going to ask him to lift it.” Sada put the board down. “I can’t. It’s a proper hold on a proper declaration, and the Taharkan envoy’s got no standing with the Corps over a private party’s bonded goods, and if I tried to get some I’d spend it all, and I need all of it to get forty mules through the south gate in four days’ time.” She looked at Riarhin steadily. “What I can do is take what belongs on my road.”

“Which is?”

“Whatever an assessor of road security needs for a journey from Mbaro to Adur-Gah.” Sada ticked it off on her fingers. “A good tent. Water for the southern road, which has wells and doesn’t need twenty skins. Oil for a lamp to write your report by. Bread. Your own weapons, which are yours. And whatever animals the Crown’s allotment already covers, which is one riding mule and a share of a pack mule, the same as Tesfaye.” She dropped her hand. “None of that’s in bond. Everything else is. And everything else was bought for the north.”

Riarhin sat with it.

Six weeks of lamp oil. Cover for twenty skins. A surveyor’s chain. Brass fittings for a string of pack animals. Three weeks of work and a good deal of money, and she had bought every piece of it knowing somebody would read it, and it had done exactly what she bought it to do.

And it was the wrong shape for the road she was actually going to take. It had been bought to say one thing and she had decided to do another. The Corps had noticed.

“Leave it,” she said.

Sada looked at her.

“The Covenant gets it after a year and a day,” said Riarhin. “The oil’s good. He said so. It’ll go to the northern depots.” She stood. “I can buy more in Adur-Gah. I’ll know what I actually need by then.”

Sada watched her for a moment longer. Then she picked the board up again.

“That’s the first thing you’ve done since I met you,” she said, “that nobody else will be able to make any sense of at all.”

She dealt with Oumar in two days, and it cost her very little, and she enjoyed it more than she should have.

She did not threaten him. She did not go to the Corps. He was a man with bad knees who sold what he saw, and he had seen a great deal because she had let him, and she did not have any wish to put him in front of a warden for it; the Corps would have been interested in a clerk who misfiled holds, and she did not believe there was any such clerk, and the Corps would have found that out too, and then Oumar would have been a man with bad knees and a Corps record.

She wanted him busy. That was all. She wanted him busy for four days, until the delegation was through the south gate and down the road, and she wanted him busy in a way that would stop the people he sold to from trusting what he sold.

So she let him steal a survey.

It was the fair copy of the second of the three published surveys, the one that showed the eastern water out past the salt pans toward the river-island. She took it out of her bundle in the lodging house and spent an evening with a pen writing notes on it in the margin in her own hand: wells, stage-masters, the old well at Tin Kerru with a question mark beside it and a line drawn round the inspection, a turn north from the fourth stage marked with a small neat cross and the words eleven days. All of it was out of Oumar’s own four hours with the beans. None of it was hers, and none of it was true in any sense that mattered, and every word of it looked exactly like what a woman following an old consignment east into the Wastes would write in the margin of a survey.

She left it on the table in her room with the shutters open and went out for the evening with Na’ar.

When they came back it was gone, and so was the lodging-house keeper’s boy.

Then she made sure the two customers found out about each other.

That was the part that took a morning. She went to the money house on the Tower Bank and asked the eldest of Tiemoko’s daughters, very politely, whether she might send a reply after all to the letter from the salt house. And she wrote, in her best Ecktorian, that she regretted she had no family papers of the kind the factor’s client wished to purchase; that she had lately had a survey stolen from her lodging, with some notes of her own upon it; and that she understood from certain quarters that it might already have been offered for sale in the Salt District.

The eldest daughter read it, and her face did not change at all, and she sent it.

Then Riarhin went down to the Salt District, to the cookshop where Na’ar ate goat, and sat there in the afternoon where Mahamane’s man could see her, and when he came and stood at the end of the bench with his hands folded, she looked up at him and said, in her lower-city Silkstrand:

“Tell your master the survey’s genuine. Tell him the salt house on the Tower Bank thinks so too. They were offered it this morning.”

The man in the brown robe looked at her for a moment without any expression. Then he went away.

She did not see what happened afterwards. She never did see it, and she did not need to.

What she knew, from Kadiatou, who heard it at the Water Stair, was that on the evening of the twenty-eighth day a man with bad knees and an indigo head-wrap had been seen having a long and very loud conversation outside the salt house on the Tower Bank with the factor’s clerk, about a price that had been paid for something that had, it seemed, been sold elsewhere the same afternoon. And that on the morning of the twenty-ninth, the same man had been seen at the cookshop in the Salt District having a much quieter conversation with a small neat man with gold rings on his thumbs. That one had gone on a long time, and at the end of it the man with bad knees had walked away very carefully, looking over his shoulder.

And that on the thirtieth, the day the Taharkan envoy’s delegation went out by the south gate, Oumar had been in the Salt District all day explaining himself to people, and Mahamane had been in the Salt District all day not believing him, and the salt house had been in its counting room all day writing a letter up the river to say that its source had proved unreliable and that the survey it had obtained suggested an eastern route, and that the foreign woman had left Mbaro that morning with the Taharkans.

The letter would be weeks on the river. It would say Taharkans, and eastern, and an old consignment, and a foreign woman. It would not say Kalrantha. Nobody in Mbaro who had it to sell had ever heard the word.

Kadiatou came to the south gate to be paid.

She stood at the side of the road in the grey light before dawn, with her basket on her hip and her slate under her arm, while forty mules and a Crown seal and eleven Taharkans and a very large Ykrul went past her into the gate-passage to be written down. Riarhin stopped beside her and counted coins into her palm: the silver and a half for the last day, and the half on top, because Mahamane’s man had stood beside her on the Water Stair every morning until the twenty-eighth and had stopped then, and she had charged for every one.

“And the door,” said Kadiatou. “You said you’d see to it. You did. I’m charging.”

Riarhin counted another silver.

Kadiatou weighed it in her palm and put it away. Then she stood looking at the mules going past for a while.

“You paid Hawa’s debt,” she said.

“Yes.”

“The whole street’s still talking about it. They say a foreigner came and read the finding and paid it at the Hall and wrote settled. They say the old woman went home and slept all afternoon.” She shifted the basket. “Nobody on that street knows why. They think you were a relative. Some cousin from Marcott, come to make it right.”

“Let them.”

“I will.” Kadiatou looked at her. It was the second time she had done it properly, the first being on the morning they met. “That’s a door too. What they think. I’m not charging for that one.”

She turned and went back into the city without looking round, the way she always went, and Riarhin watched her go until the grey light took her.

At the gate a clerk in blue wrote down forty mules, and a clerk in red wrote down the Crown’s seal, and neither of them looked twice at an assessor of road security on a riding mule with a sword-shaped bundle tied behind her saddle.

Sounkalo was at the gate.

She had not expected him and was not surprised. He was standing by the wall with his cup and his stave, and he was not writing anything, because the clerks were doing that for him, and he watched the delegation go through as he had watched her at the bench, pleasantly and completely. When she drew level with him he looked up and nodded to her.

“Your goods are still in bond,” he said in his careful Ecktorian.

“I know.”

“After a year and a day, they go north.”

“I know. It’s good oil.”

He looked at her for a moment. Then, very slightly, he smiled for the first time since she had met him.

“I will write down,” he said, “that the owner went south.”

The south road ran out of Mbaro between irrigated fields, green and flat and cut into squares by the canals, with the Granary Tower behind them going pink in the dawn. The mules went in a long string with Abdi walking at the head of it and Gebre’s Ridge-Watchers strung out along its length, and the clerks rode in a knot in the middle arguing about the order of the chests, and Na’ar walked beside Abdi and asked questions about mules in a mixture of Ykrul, bad Ecktorian, and gestures.

Riarhin rode where she had been told to ride, which was behind the envoy.

She had not organised any of it. She had not chosen the day or the hour. She had not chosen the road, or the stages, or who walked where. It would go at the pace of forty mules and a seventy-year-old headman and a delegation with a hearing at the end of it, and she could no more hurry it than she could hurry a canal. For the first time since she was sixteen she was leaving a city at a time somebody else had fixed.

Ahead of her, Sada Alemu rode very straight on a grey mule with her white wrap round her shoulders, and did not look back, and then did.

“You’re going to hate this,” she said. “Aren’t you.”

“Probably.”

“Good. So am I. I’ve been nine years in one room and I’ve forgotten how to sit on a mule.” She faced forward again. “It’s four hundred miles. You can tell me about it as we go.”

Riarhin rode on behind her into the green morning, and found that she had no wish at all to be anywhere else, and did not look at that either.

Act III: The Road

Chapter Eleven: The Bridge with Two Owners

The bridge at Gedo had been standing for a hundred and forty years and had been closed for nine days, and there were armed men at both ends of it who had not been introduced.

The delegation came down to it on the nineteenth day out of Mbaro, in the late afternoon, out of a country that had been changing under them the whole way. The flat green squares of the canal land had given out in the first week to a long dry rise of red earth and thorn, with baobabs standing up out of it at wide intervals like old men who had stopped to rest and forgotten to go on. Then the rise had become hills, and the hills had begun to have terraces cut into them, and there was a smell on the wind in the evenings of something green and damp that had not been there before. Riarhin’s cloak had been red-brown when they left Mbaro. It was the colour of dry grass now. Nobody had said anything about it.

And the road came down through a cut between two hills to a river, brown and fast and wider than it should have been, and there was the bridge.

It was a good bridge. Three stone arches on two piers, with a roadway wide enough for two carts. The middle arch had cracked. Not fallen; cracked, from the crown down the upstream face, and the flood that had cracked it had taken a length of the parapet with it and left the roadway on that span sagging a hand’s breadth from true. Somebody had put a barrier of thorn across each end. Somebody else had put men behind the barriers.

On the near bank there were perhaps a dozen of them, with spears and one old musket, standing about the toll-house with the air of men who had been standing about it for nine days. On the far bank there were about as many, with spears and no musket, sitting on the ground in the shade of a fig tree.

Between them, backed up along the road on both sides, were the carts.

Sada rode down to the barrier and looked at the bridge for a long time without saying anything.

Riarhin looked at the carts. There were forty or fifty of them on the near bank, drawn up nose to tail along the road and pulled off onto the verges, with their mules unhitched and grazing and their drivers sitting in the shade underneath them. Grain carts. Oshiiran millet in marked sacks, going south, and on the far bank another line of carts going the other way, with coffee and hides and the Taharkan salt that came up from the Iron Coast. Nine days. The mules had eaten the verges bare. The drivers had the look of men who had been told every morning that the bridge would open tomorrow.

“This is in my report,” Sada said at last, to nobody in particular.

“The bridge?”

“The grain.” She did not turn round. “Four hundred cartloads a month of Oshiiran millet come south over this bridge into Taharka at the terms we fixed in Mbaro. That’s the first article of the agreement. It’s the one the Council cares about. It’s the one I’m going to have to stand up and defend.” She looked at the carts. “And it’s sitting on the verge at Gedo eating the grass.”

It took Riarhin half the evening to find out what had happened, and the other half to understand it.

The bridge belonged to the toll-holders. That much was simple. It had been built a hundred and forty years ago by a family of Gedo merchants under a grant from a Taharkan king, who had given them the right to take a toll on every cart that crossed it, forever, in return for building it and keeping it up. The family was still there. They lived in the town on the near bank and they had a toll-house and a book, and a steward called Ato Mesfin who was sixty and very tired and whom Riarhin liked at once, and they had been keeping the bridge up for a hundred and forty years without complaint, because the toll paid for it.

What the family did not own was the ground.

The bridge landed on the far bank on a strip of pasture that belonged to the herding villages on that side of the river. It always had. The toll-holders had the bridge and the road across it; the herders had the ground the road ran over for a quarter of a mile beyond the bridge-foot, and had let the road cross it for a hundred and forty years in return for a share of the toll that was paid to them at the end of every dry season.

The flood had cracked the bridge. The toll-holders wanted to mend it. They could not mend it without shoring the far pier. They could not shore the far pier without bringing stone and timber and men across the herders’ ground, and digging a coffer in the herders’ riverbank, for a whole season. And the herders said that the old agreement gave the toll-holders a road across their ground, not a quarry, and that if the toll-holders wanted a quarry on it they could pay for one.

And meanwhile, said the toll-holders, there was a ford a mile downstream where carts could cross in the dry season if they were careful. They would open a temporary crossing there and collect the toll on it.

The ford ran between two hamlets that were not in the old agreement at all. They had not been there a hundred and forty years ago. They were there now: thirty households and their fields, and the ford went through the middle of both, and nobody had asked them.

So there were men on the far bank with spears, because the herders would not have their pasture dug. And men on the near bank with a musket, because the toll-holders would not have their bridge taken off them by a pack of goatherds. And the two hamlets at the ford had put a barrier of their own across it that morning. And the grain was eating the grass.

Riarhin found a way across in the first hour, because it was what she did.

Two miles upstream, where the river came down out of the hills through a narrow place between rocks, there was a spot where it spread out over a gravel bar and ran shallow and very fast. You could not take a cart across it. You could take a string of mules across it, in the dark, if you knew where the gravel was and did not mind getting wet to the waist, and if you had a big man on the upstream side of each mule and a rope across. It would take most of a night. Nobody on either bank would see it. By morning the Taharkan envoy’s delegation would be on the far side of the Gedo river and a day down the road, with all its papers dry, and the bridge could sort itself out.

She came back and told Sada.

Sada was sitting on a folding stool under the delegation’s awning with Tesfaye and a lamp and a sheaf of papers, and she listened to all of it without interrupting, as she listened to everything. When Riarhin had finished she was quiet for a moment.

“No,” she said.

Riarhin waited.

“Not because it wouldn’t work. I’m sure it would work.” Sada put the papers down. “Because it would get forty mules and eleven people across a river, and it wouldn’t get a single sack of grain across it. And the grain is what I’m for.” She looked at the bridge in the dark, at the two lines of watch-fires on either bank. “If I go round this, I arrive in Adur-Gah with an agreement that says four hundred cartloads a month come over the bridge at Gedo, and the bridge at Gedo is shut, and nobody’s working on it, and I went round it in the night so I wouldn’t be late for my own hearing.”

“You’d still be on time.”

“I’d be on time with nothing.” Sada picked up her pen again. “Go to sleep. I’ll need you tomorrow.”

She needed her for five days.

Riarhin had seen negotiations before. She had seen them in Vhasia, where they were conducted in a court language so ornate that nothing was ever said, and in the Black Banners, where they were conducted over a table with the swords on it, and in Silkstrand, where they were conducted by people who had already decided the outcome and were arguing about the price. She had never seen one like this, and on the second day she understood that she had never before watched anybody do it well.

Sada did not go to the bridge. She went to the toll-house, and sat with Ato Mesfin for a morning, and let him tell her the whole history of the bridge and his family and the grant, all of it, and wrote it down. Then she went across the river in a boat she hired from a fisherman, which caused a certain amount of shouting from both banks, and sat under the fig tree with the headmen of the herding villages for an afternoon, and let them tell her the whole history of the pasture and the road and the share of the toll, and wrote that down. And on the third day she walked down to the ford, alone except for Tesfaye and a clerk, and sat in the dust outside the house of the headwoman of the larger hamlet and was given coffee, and drank the second cup, and let the headwoman tell her how long the hamlets had been there and why nobody had ever asked them anything.

She did not propose anything. For three days she did not propose anything at all. She listened, and she asked questions, and she wrote everything down, and in the evenings she sat under the awning with the lamp and read it all over and said very little.

Riarhin, who had been told she was needed, spent three days wondering what for.

She found out on the fourth morning.

“There was an award,” said Sada. “A boundary award.”

She had the papers spread out on the folding table under the awning, three days of them, and she put her finger on a line in Ato Mesfin’s history, and then on a line in the herders’, and then on a line in the headwoman’s.

“The toll-holders say it. The herders say it. The headwoman at the ford says her grandmother said it. When the bridge was built, there was a quarrel about where the road would go on the far side, and a royal judge came down from Adur-Gah and walked it and made an award. Where the road would run. How wide. What the herders would be paid. What happened if the bridge ever had to be mended.” She tapped the table. “Everybody remembers it. Everybody says it’s on their side. And nobody’s got a copy.”

“None of them?”

“The toll-holders’ copy burned in a fire in the town forty years ago. The herders never had one; they had the judge’s word, which was the custom then. The copy in Adur-Gah is in the royal archive and it’ll take a month to find and another to send.” Sada looked up. “And there was a fourth copy. Everybody agrees on that too. It was given to whoever held the ferry.”

“What ferry?”

“There was a ferry, before the bridge. Downstream. At the ford.” Sada’s face did not change. “The judge gave the ferryman a copy, because the bridge was going to take his living and the award set out what he’d be paid for it. The ferry’s long gone. The ferryman’s family’s still here. They run carts now. They run carts across the ford when the river’s low.” She paused. “And they’re the only family in Gedo that’s making money this week.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“You want me to find out if they’ve got it,” she said.

“I know they’ve got it. The headwoman told me.” Sada folded her hands on the table. “She didn’t know she was telling me. She said the carter’s father used to read out a piece of it at weddings, for luck, because it had the king’s name in it. She said he stopped reading it about nine days ago.” She looked at the bridge. “I want you to find out what it says. Without anybody taking it from anybody. I’ve got to be able to stand at that bridge tomorrow and ask for it in front of everybody. I can’t do that if it’s been stolen.”

The carter’s family was called Worku and they lived in a long stone house on the rise above the ford with a yard full of carts and a great many cousins.

Riarhin did not break in. She had meant to, when Sada began talking; she had had the house half worked out before Sada finished the sentence. She put that away.

What she did instead was go down to the ford at midday, when the carters were working, and stand at the barrier with the hamlets’ people and watch the Worku carts go across.

They were taking grain. Not the grain from the verges at the bridge; Oshiiran merchants who had got tired of waiting had begun to sell their loads to the Workus at the near bank for less than they were worth, and the Workus were carting them over the ford and selling them on at the far bank for rather more. Every cart that went across had to pay the hamlets a copper at their barrier. The Workus paid it cheerfully. They were making a great deal more than a copper a cart.

The eldest Worku son was running the crossing. He was about thirty, big and cheerful and sunburned, and he talked the whole time: to the hamlets’ people at the barrier, to his drivers, to the mules, to Riarhin when she came and stood next to him. He talked about the bridge. He talked about the flood, and the toll-holders, and the herders, and how there was no knowing how long it would all take, and how a man had to make a living meanwhile.

And somewhere in the middle of it, while a cart was being hauled up out of the water on the far side, he said, cheerfully, that of course when the old bridge was mended the toll-holders would have to pay the ford-keepers for every day the ford had carried their traffic, that was in the award, everybody knew it.

Riarhin said, “Is it?”

“Oh, yes.” He grinned at her. “For every day the ford shall bear the bridge’s burden, a copper a cart to the keeper of the ford. My father used to say it at weddings. It’s the only part anybody remembers.”

She went back up to the awning and told Sada, word for word.

Sada sat very still for a while.

“A copper a cart,” she said. “To the keeper of the ford.”

“Yes.”

“The Workus haven’t been the keepers of the ford for a hundred and forty years. The ferry’s gone. There’s no keeper. But they’re the ones with the paper.” She put her pen down. “And every day the bridge stays shut, they’ve got a claim on the toll-holders for every cart they take over. And the hamlets at the ford, which weren’t in the award because they didn’t exist, are standing at their barrier charging a copper a cart too. And don’t know that the award says the copper’s owed to whoever keeps the ford.” She looked at the ford, a mile away in the heat. “Which, this week, is them.”

The fifth day was the one Riarhin would remember.

Sada called everybody to the bridge. The toll-holders, from the near bank. The herders, from the far bank, brought over in the fisherman’s boat. The headwoman of the larger hamlet and two of her people. And the eldest Worku son, who came because the Taharkan envoy had sent a very polite note inviting him, and who came cheerfully.

They stood on the near approach of the bridge with the barrier of thorn behind them and the cracked arch in front of them, and Gebre’s eight Ridge-Watchers standing along the road in a line looking very solemn, and the grain drivers from both banks sitting along the verges to watch, because it was the most interesting thing that had happened in Gedo in nine days. Riarhin stood behind Sada where the assessor stood, and watched.

Sada did not make a speech. She said that she was the envoy of the Crown of Taharka and that she had a hearing in Adur-Gah in a month’s time at which she would have to explain why four hundred cartloads of grain a month were not coming over the bridge at Gedo, and that she would very much rather not have to. She said that she had listened to everybody’s history of the award for four days and that they all agreed there had been one, and that they all agreed it was on their side, and that nobody in this place had a copy except the family that had kept the ferry.

Then she turned to the eldest Worku son and asked, in front of everybody, whether his family would do Gedo the kindness of bringing it out and reading it.

The whole bank went quiet.

He was a cheerful man. He had been cheerful for nine days, and his cheerfulness had been very profitable, and she watched it go out of his face and be replaced with something slower, while he looked at the toll-holders, and at the herders, and at the headwoman of the hamlet, and at forty grain drivers sitting on the verge who had just heard the envoy of Taharka say, in so many words, that the ford-keepers had a paper that would settle the question.

He could say no. Everybody on the bank would know what that meant.

He sent a cousin for it.

It supported nobody entirely.

Sada read it aloud herself, slowly, standing on the bridge approach, with the eldest Worku son holding one side of the old paper and Ato Mesfin holding the other. It was a long award and a careful one, and the royal judge who had made it had evidently foreseen a great many of the ways in which a bridge and a road and a strip of pasture could go wrong, and had made provision for about half of them.

The toll-holders had the right to mend the bridge. They had the right to cross the herders’ ground to do it. They did not have the right to dig there without paying for every day the ground was out of use, at a rate the award set out, and they had to put it back as they found it.

The herders had a share of the toll. They did not have the right to stop the road across their ground for any reason, and a barrier of spears under a fig tree was a reason the award had not foreseen and would not have liked.

And for every day the ford carried the bridge’s traffic, a copper a cart was owed to the keeper of the ford. The award did not say who the keeper was. It said the keeper was whoever kept it.

The headwoman of the larger hamlet listened to that, standing at the back with her two people, and her face did not move at all, and Riarhin watched her understand it.

It took the rest of the day, and it was not finished.

What Sada got, by evening, was this. The herders would let the toll-holders bring stone and timber across the pasture and dig their coffer, and would be paid for it at the award’s rate, which was lower than the herders wanted and higher than the toll-holders wanted, and both sides said so, loudly. The toll-holders would shore the cracked arch with timber as a first measure, and open the bridge to one cart at a time, loaded light, walking, with a man at each end to count, until the pier was mended and the arch could be trusted. The count would be witnessed by a herder and a toll-holder together, at both ends, every day. And for every day the ford went on carrying traffic, the copper a cart would go to the hamlets, whose barrier stood on it, and not to a family with a paper.

The Workus did not like that last part. Sada did not ask them to. She thanked them, very courteously, for the loan of their award, and asked Tesfaye to make a fair copy of it on the spot for the toll-holders and another for the herders, so that there would never again be only one copy in Gedo.

The bridge opened at sunset. One cart. It was a grain cart from the near verge, Oshiiran millet in marked sacks, and it went out onto the cracked span at a walk, with its driver leading the mules and a toll-holder’s man walking in front and a herder walking behind, and every soul on both banks watching it. The roadway groaned where it sagged. The cart went over it and on, and down onto the far bank onto the herders’ pasture, and the herder who had walked behind it put a stone on a pile at the bridge-foot, to count it.

Somebody on the verge cheered, and then everybody did.

One cart at a time, walking, loaded light. It would take a week to clear the verges. It would take a season to mend the pier. The agreement Sada was carrying to Adur-Gah said four hundred cartloads a month, and the bridge at Gedo could take perhaps half that until the next dry season.

It was open.

They sat under the awning afterwards with the lamp and the last of the coffee, and argued.

“Five days,” said Riarhin.

“Five days.”

“I could have had you across in one night. You’d be five days nearer Adur-Gah.”

“I’d be five days nearer Adur-Gah with a bridge that was shut behind me.” Sada was sitting on her stool with her feet up on a chest, which Riarhin had not seen her do before, and her white wrap round her shoulders, and her eyes half closed. “And now I’m five days late with one that’s open to one cart at a time. Which is worse, and better. I’ll have to explain both. At least I’ll be able to explain it.”

“They’ll say you should have gone round.”

“Some of them will.” Sada opened her eyes. “Some of them will say I should have stayed a month and got it properly mended. Some of them will say I should never have agreed four hundred cartloads in the first place, because anybody could have told me the bridge at Gedo was a hundred and forty years old.” She looked at the lamp. “They’d all be right. That’s what the Council is for.”

Riarhin did not say anything for a while. She was thinking about the headwoman of the hamlet at the back of the crowd, understanding the award.

“You didn’t need me,” she said.

“I needed you on the fourth day.”

“You’d have got it out of the carter yourself.”

“I’d have got it out of the carter in three more days, by being tactful, and he’d have known I was doing it and hidden it better.” Sada closed her eyes again. “You stood at the ford for an hour and he told you the only line that mattered because he liked talking to you. I couldn’t have done that. He’d never have liked talking to me. I was the envoy.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“You’re very tired,” she said.

“I’ve been awake since the second hour arguing about stones.” Sada did not open her eyes. “And you’re still annoyed about the five days.”

“Yes.”

“Good. Stay annoyed. It’ll make the report better.”

The argument was over. Both of them knew it. Riarhin sat on the chest beside the stool with her cup gone cold, and Sada sat with her eyes closed and her feet up, and neither of them moved to go. Below them in the dark, a second cart went out onto the bridge, walking, with a lantern, and a stone went onto the pile at the far end.

They stayed where they were until the lamp needed filling, and Sada said, without opening her eyes, “There’s oil in the chest you’re sitting on,” and Riarhin got up and filled it, and sat down again.

Chapter Twelve: The Missing Envoy

Dawit was gone in the morning, and so was the dispatch case.

Tesfaye found out first, because it was Tesfaye’s habit to count the chests before he counted anything else, and he came across the courtyard of the caravanserai at Dessa in the grey light before dawn with his spectacles in one hand and his face the colour of paper. The long black case with the brass corners and the Crown’s lion on the lid, which held the originals of every agreement Sada had signed in nine years at Mbaro, was not in the chest where it had been locked the night before. The chest was still locked. The key that opened it was the one Dawit carried.

Dawit’s blanket was folded on his pallet. His riding mule was not in the stable.

Sada came out of her room in her white wrap with her hair uncovered and listened to Tesfaye and did not say anything at all for perhaps ten seconds.

Then she said, “Gebre,” and the sergeant was already there; and “Riarhin,” and Riarhin was already there too.

Everybody thought the same thing, and nobody said it for almost an hour.

They were eight days past Gedo, in the lowland marches where Oshiira ran out and Taharka had not quite begun, and the road ran through a country of coffee terraces and market towns where half the people spoke one language and half the other and all of them spoke the trade tongue of the Iron Coast when there was money in it. Dessa was the largest of the towns. It had a caravanserai and a market and a Covenant church with a round roof, and it had, the clerks said in low voices, merchants from every country that had an interest in what the Taharkan envoy was carrying home.

A dispatch case full of signed originals. The grain terms. The border-traffic article, which two people in Mbaro would be very unhappy about. The confidential annexes that set out what Sada had conceded and what she had been told she could not. All of it in the hand of a junior envoy who said yes, Envoy to everything and looked at the gate every time it opened.

Gebre wanted to send riders down the road in both directions. Tesfaye wanted to write to Adur-Gah. One of the older clerks, who had been in Mbaro with Sada for seven years and had never liked Dawit, said quietly that he had always thought the boy was too anxious to be honest.

Sada said nothing. She went back into her room and dressed, and came out again, and sat down on the edge of the courtyard well with her hands folded in her lap.

“Find him,” she said to Riarhin. “Just find him. Don’t bring him back. Don’t take the case off him. Find out where he is and what he’s doing, and come and tell me.”

He had not gone down the road.

Riarhin knew that inside a quarter of an hour. The caravanserai at Dessa had one gate onto the road and a second, smaller, onto the lanes behind the market, where the stables were; and the stable boy, who was about ten and very frightened of Gebre, was not at all frightened of a foreign woman who sat down on an upturned bucket and asked him about mules. The young Taharkan gentleman had come for his mule at the second hour. He had not saddled it himself; he had not known how, quite, and the boy had helped him. He had asked the boy the way to the house of Weyzero Hirut, and the boy had told him, and he had given the boy a copper, and gone out through the back gate.

“Who is Weyzero Hirut?”

The boy looked at her as though she had asked who the sun was.

“The coffee lady,” he said. “She’s got the biggest house. On the hill.” He pointed. “She’s got the terraces all down that side and she sends coffee to the coast. Everybody works for her.”

Riarhin looked where he was pointing. Up the hill above the market, among the terraces, a long white house with a wall round it and a gate and, even at this hour, two men standing at the gate.

A defector with a dispatch case full of signed treaties does not ask a stable boy the way to the biggest house in town at the second hour of the morning, and give him a copper, and go out the back.

She did not know yet what a young man did who did those things. She knew it was not the thing everybody in the courtyard was afraid of.

The house of Weyzero Hirut did not admit official visitors.

Riarhin learned that from the men at the gate, who told it to a woman in a grass-coloured cloak who said she was looking for a lost mule. They were polite about it. The Weyzero was not receiving. The Weyzero had not been receiving since yesterday. There had been, they said, some trouble with a boy, and the Weyzero had had enough of officials and envoys and papers and people waving seals, and anybody with a seal could go and wave it at the Covenant judge when she came through on circuit, and in the meanwhile the gate was shut.

She thanked them and went away down the hill.

Then she went round the back.

The wall was nine feet high and old and built of field stone, and the terraces ran right up to it, and on the uphill side there was a place where a coffee bush had been allowed to grow against it for thirty years and had grown a trunk as thick as her wrist. She went up the bush and over the coping and down into a garden of lemon trees without a sound, which was the first time she had done anything of the kind since Kessary, and her palms, which had healed, did not like it.

The house was built round a courtyard, like the house by the tamarind in Mbaro. She came into it through a kitchen passage where two women were grinding coffee on a flat stone and did not look up. And there in the courtyard, sitting on a bench by a dry cistern, with the Crown’s dispatch case on the flagstones between his feet, was Dawit.

He had not been hurt. He was not bound. He was sitting with his elbows on his knees and his face in his hands, and a steward in a white shawl was standing over him saying something in a low, patient, implacable voice, and at the far end of the courtyard, in a doorway, a young woman of perhaps nineteen was standing with her arms wrapped round herself, looking at Dawit.

She had his face. The same long jaw, the same anxious eyes.

Riarhin stood in the kitchen passage in the shadow and listened to the steward for a while. She did not have the language, and she had enough of it now, after a month of Taharkan clerks, to follow the shape of it. Contract. Two more years. Her passage. Her keep. The Weyzero paid. Your seal does not. Go home.

Dawit said something into his hands. It came out broken.

The steward said something back, and pointed at the dispatch case, and said a word Riarhin did know, because Tesfaye used it forty times a day: Crown.

She understood it then, all of it at once, the way she sometimes understood the shape of a room.

And she did not go into the courtyard.

It would have been easy. The steward was an old man and there was nobody else in the courtyard but the girl. She could have walked out of the passage and picked up the case and taken Dawit by the collar and been over the wall and down the terraces before anybody in the house had understood what had happened. It was what she would have done a year ago. It was what she had been sent to not do.

She went back through the kitchen, where the two women were still grinding coffee, and over the wall by the coffee bush, and down the hill.

“His sister,” she said.

Sada was still sitting on the edge of the well. She had not moved, as far as Riarhin could tell, in the two hours Riarhin had been gone; Tesfaye had brought her coffee and it was on the stone beside her, cold.

“His sister is in service at the house of a coffee merchant called Hirut, on the hill,” said Riarhin. “She’s about nineteen. She’s under a contract of some kind. Her passage and keep, I think, paid in advance, and two more years to work off. She wants to leave. The house says she can’t. Dawit went up there at the second hour to get her. He took the case.”

“Why did he take the case?”

“Because it’s got the Crown’s seal on it, and he thought it would open the gate.” Riarhin paused. “And I think because he was afraid that if he left it in the chest and went up the hill, we’d be gone down the road by the time he got back, and he’d be a junior envoy who’d been left behind in Dessa with nothing.”

Sada closed her eyes.

“He waved it at them,” said Riarhin. “Yesterday, I think, too. They’ve shut the gate to officials. The steward’s been telling him for an hour that a seal isn’t a contract and to go home. He’s sitting in their courtyard with his head in his hands and the case on the ground between his feet. Nobody’s touched it.”

“And the girl?”

“The girl’s standing in a doorway watching him.”

Sada opened her eyes and looked at her.

“You didn’t bring the case.”

“You told me not to.”

“I know I told you not to.” Sada’s voice was very even. “I didn’t think you’d listen.”

She went up the hill herself, in the afternoon, and she did not go as the envoy.

That was the first thing that surprised Riarhin, and it went on surprising her for the rest of the day. Sada took off the white wrap with the Crown’s green-and-red border and put on a plain dark shawl, and left Gebre and his Ridge-Watchers at the caravanserai, and walked up through the market to the gate of Weyzero Hirut’s house with nobody but Riarhin a pace behind her. At the gate she told the two men that she was not an official, that she had no seal about her and would not produce one, and that she had come to fetch a member of her household who had been a nuisance, and to apologise for him.

The men looked at each other. One of them went in.

He came out after a quarter of an hour and they were let in.

Weyzero Hirut was about sixty, and small, and very sharp, and she received them on a raised seat at the end of the courtyard with the steward standing beside her and Dawit still sitting on his bench by the dry cistern with the case at his feet. He had stood up when Sada came in. His face, when he saw who it was, went through so many things so quickly that Riarhin, who had seen a great many faces do a great many things, felt something close to pity.

Sada did not look at him.

She apologised. She apologised properly, at length, in the form the highlands used for a guest who has disgraced a house, and the steward’s face moved slightly at the form, and the Weyzero’s did not. She said that the young man was of her household and that he had come to this house twice with a seal that was not his to use and had used it to try to take a member of the Weyzero’s household out of it, and that she was ashamed of it, and that it would be dealt with.

Then she said: “Weyzero. I’d like to take my property home. And I’d like to ask about the young woman.”

The Weyzero looked at her for a long moment.

“Her contract is good,” she said. “I paid her passage from the coast and her keep for a year and the fee to the agent. She’s got two years to work.”

“I don’t doubt it.”

“Her brother doesn’t like it.”

“Her brother doesn’t have to.” Sada’s voice was quite level. “But I’d like to hear from her whether she does. If she wants to stay and work her two years, I’ll take him home and he’ll never trouble your gate again. If she doesn’t, then there’s a question about the contract, and I’d like it heard. Not by me. I’m not an official here today. By the Covenant judge, when she comes through on circuit.”

“The judge comes in three weeks.”

“Then in three weeks. With somebody to speak for her. Somebody she chooses.”

The Weyzero’s eyes went to the doorway at the far end of the courtyard.

The girl was still there. She had not moved since morning, as far as Riarhin could tell. She was standing with her arms round herself looking at Sada, and at her brother, and at the case on the flagstones, and she had the face of somebody who has been talked about for a day and a half and has not yet been asked anything.

“Selam,” said the Weyzero. Not unkindly. “Come here.”

It took the rest of the afternoon and a great deal of coffee, and it did not end with anybody leaving the house with everything they wanted.

Selam wanted to go. She said so in a very small voice, and then, when Sada asked her to say it again, in a louder one. She did not want to go home to her mother on the coast; she wanted to go to Adur-Gah, where her brother was going, where there was work for a woman who could read, and she could read, and that had been the whole of the trouble. The agent on the coast had told her mother that she would be clerking in a coffee house, keeping the accounts. She had been grinding coffee on a flat stone in the kitchen for a year.

The Weyzero said that grinding coffee was work, and that she had never promised anything about accounts, and that if the agent had, the agent had lied, and that was a matter for the agent.

Sada said that it was, and that it was also a matter for the contract, if the contract had been sold on a lie; and that neither she nor the Weyzero could settle that in a courtyard, and that the judge could.

The Weyzero thought about that for a long time.

In the end she agreed to a thing that Riarhin had not known was possible. Selam would leave the house that evening and go with the delegation as far as Adur-Gah, where she would be found work, and would pay out of her wages what the Weyzero had actually spent on her passage and her keep, less the value of a year’s work at the stone, at a rate the judge would fix when the matter was heard. The Weyzero would bring her contract to the judge in three weeks and have the question of the agent’s promise heard, and if the judge said the contract was good, Selam would pay the rest; and if the judge said it was not, the Weyzero would go after the agent. Selam would be represented at the hearing by somebody of her own choosing.

Selam chose, after some thought and in a very quiet voice, the woman who kept the dyed-cloth stall at the end of the market, who was a cousin of her mother’s and had once been a clerk herself. Not her brother, who stood up to speak and was told to sit down. Not the envoy.

Sada wrote it all down. Tesfaye was not there, so she wrote it herself on a sheet of the Weyzero’s paper, in her own hand, and read it aloud, and the Weyzero signed it and the steward witnessed it and Selam put her mark on it, and then her name next to the mark, in a clear careful hand, to show that she could.

The Weyzero looked at the name for a while. Then she said, not to anybody in particular, “She never told me she could write,” and her face did something complicated that Riarhin did not try to read.

Dawit carried the case down the hill himself. Sada made him. He walked in front of them with it in both arms like a man carrying something very heavy, and his sister walked beside him and did not speak to him, and nobody else spoke either.

At the caravanserai Sada handed the case to Tesfaye and had him open it in the courtyard in front of everybody and count every document in it against the list, which took an hour. Everything was there. Nothing had been opened. Dawit had carried the originals of nine years of the Crown’s business up a hill and waved them at a coffee merchant’s gate, and he had not once thought to open the case.

Then Sada sent everybody away except Dawit, and Riarhin, who went to the far side of the courtyard and sat on the well with her back to them, and could hear every word.

She had expected it to be quiet. She had expected, if she was honest, a great many things. She had expected that the envoy would take the boy aside and tell him very kindly that he had been a fool and that it would go no further. She had expected a sum of money, perhaps, from Sada’s own purse, to the Weyzero and the steward and the men on the gate, so that nobody in Dessa would ever say that a junior envoy of Taharka had walked into a coffee merchant’s house with the Crown’s dispatch case. She had expected the thing she would have done herself, which was to make it not have happened.

Sada did none of those things.

She told Dawit what he had done, all of it, in order, without raising her voice. She told him that he had taken the Crown’s property without leave and used its seal without authority to try to enforce a claim that was nothing to do with the Crown. She told him that she would be writing it into the delegation’s journal that night, and that it would go into her report to the Council. She told him that the Council would read it, and that it would be on his record when he was considered for his next posting, and for every posting after that. And she told him that she would also be writing into the journal why he had done it, and what had come of it: that his sister was going to Adur-Gah with a hearing in three weeks and a chance of a clerk’s work, and that she would not have had either if he had not been a fool, and that the Council could make of that what it liked.

Dawit said, “Yes, Envoy,” in a voice that was hardly a voice at all.

“Don’t yes, Envoy me,” said Sada. “Go and sit with your sister. She hasn’t spoken to you all afternoon and I don’t blame her. Go and make her.”

He went.

Riarhin found her later, on the flat roof of the caravanserai, sitting on a mat with a lamp and the delegation’s journal open on her knees, writing.

She sat down on the parapet without being asked. Sada did not look up.

“You’re putting it in,” said Riarhin.

“I’m putting it in.”

“All of it.”

“All of it. The case, the seal, the gate. The Weyzero. The agreement.” Sada wrote. “My name at the bottom.”

“It’ll cost you.”

“It’ll cost me a good deal.” Sada did not stop writing. “There are three people on the Council who’ve been waiting nine years for a reason to say I’ve been too long in Mbaro and let my staff run wild. I’ve just given them one. In writing. With my seal on it.” She dipped her pen. “And one of them is going to stand up at my hearing and read it out, and then ask me about the grain.”

“You could have paid them.”

Sada stopped writing.

She looked up for the first time, and it was a long look, and there was nothing in it that Riarhin could have called angry.

“Paid who?”

“The Weyzero. The steward. The men on the gate. The stable boy.” Riarhin heard her own voice saying it and did not like how reasonable it sounded. “Nobody outside this courtyard knows what happened. A few coins, a word, and it’s a junior envoy who went to fetch his sister and came back. Nothing in the journal. Nothing for the three on the Council.”

“And Dawit?”

“Dawit would know.”

“Dawit would know,” said Sada, “that when he did something disgraceful with the Crown’s seal, his envoy bought it quiet. He’d know that for the rest of his life. He’d know it the next time he was frightened and had a seal in his hand.” She looked back down at the journal. “And Selam’s hearing would be for a girl whose brother never did anything, with an agreement that nobody had written down, that the Weyzero could forget the day after we left.”

She wrote another line.

“I don’t buy things quiet,” she said. “I’ve had nine years of watching people in Mbaro buy things quiet, and every one of them came back. The thing about writing it down is that then it’s been done and it stays done, and everybody can see it, including me.” She sanded the page. “It’s more expensive. I’ve never once regretted paying.”

Riarhin sat on the parapet and did not say anything for a long time.

She was thinking of a sack with a head in it on a tavern floor in Kolmar, and a chair on a wagon going north out of Silkstrand, and a name signed at the Hall of Carriers in Mbaro that she had never used before and would never use again. She was thinking of forty years of leaving towns before anybody could write down what she had done there. She was thinking of how very good she was at making things not have happened.

Sada closed the journal and put the pen in its case, and sat back on her mat with her hands in her lap, and looked out at the market going dark below them and the lamps coming on in the coffee terraces up the hill.

“You think I’m a fool,” she said.

“No.”

“You think I’m going to get hurt for it.”

“Yes.”

“So do I.” Sada turned her head and looked at her. “Does it bother you?”

Riarhin thought about it properly, which took a while.

“It bothers me,” she said at last, “that I’ve never done it.”

Sada did not answer that. She looked at her a moment longer in the lamplight, and then she looked back out at the terraces, and after a while she reached across and put her hand for a moment on Riarhin’s wrist where it lay on the parapet, lightly, the way the old woman in the dyers’ street had touched the word settled in the book, and took it away again.
