Below in the courtyard, Dawit was sitting on the edge of the well next to his sister, talking very low and very fast. After a long time she said something back.

Chapter Thirteen: Supper Before the Ford

The staging camp at the Dera ford was full, and Na’ar was enjoying it.

It lay on a broad flat shelf of grass above the river where the road came down out of the coffee country and waited for the water to drop. The river was high with rain from the hills, brown and loud, and the ford-master had put a pole in the shallows with notches on it and would not let anything with wheels across until the water was below the third notch. It had been above the fourth for two days. So the shelf was covered from one end to the other with everybody who had come down the road in those two days and could not go any further: carters, drovers, a salt caravan with forty camels that complained all night, a party of pilgrims walking to the old Covenant church at Adur-Gah with their donkeys and their children and their cooking pots, two Oshiiran grain merchants who had come through Gedo one cart at a time and had not stopped talking about it, a dozen men nobody could place, and the Taharkan envoy’s delegation, with its forty mules and its Crown seal and its eight Ridge-Watchers, picketed in the best spot on the shelf by the right of being the Crown.

Na’ar walked through all of it on the first evening like a man walking through a market in a country he likes.

He looked at the camels and repeated his opinion of them to anybody who would listen. He looked at the pilgrims’ donkeys and told one of the pilgrims that her grey had a stone bruise in the near fore, and showed her where, and she thanked him and gave him a piece of bread with honey on it. He stood for a long time at the edge of the salt caravan’s fire watching the cook do something with a pot of lentils that interested him. And at some point in the course of the evening he went and sat down at a fire at the far end of the shelf, near the scrub, where the dozen men nobody could place were drinking, and was offered a cup, and took it.

Riarhin saw him go and saw him sit, and did not think about it. He was very often mistaken for things. It was one of his pleasures.

He came back late and sat down on the ground outside her tent with his back against the pole and his arms on his knees.

“They think I am a guard,” he said.

She came out and sat down beside him. The camp was quiet except for the camels and the river.

“Between contracts,” Na’ar said. “A big man, alone, no colours, who has been looking at everybody’s animals all evening. They think I have been let go by some caravan and am looking for the next one. They asked me which one I had been with. I said, the last one. They laughed.” He sounded pleased. “They are not stupid men. They are careful. They asked me a great many things before they asked me the thing they wanted to ask me.”

“Which was?”

“Whether I would like to earn a silver a night for three nights by being somewhere else.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“They have counted the Crown’s escort,” said Na’ar. “Eight Ridge-Watchers. A sergeant. They know the Ridge-Watchers stand two at a time, in the old way, at the head and tail of the mule-lines, and change at the fourth hour and the eighth. They know which mules carry the Crown’s silver, because the silver mules are the ones with the double girths, and anybody who has ever loaded a mule knows why you double-girth a mule. They know the pilgrims are camped on the downhill side of the mule-lines, between the mules and the scrub, and that when there is trouble in a camp at night, the pilgrims will run, and when the pilgrims run, their donkeys will run, and nobody will be able to see anything for a quarter of an hour.” He rubbed his jaw. “And they thought that a big man who was not with anybody might be useful on the night, in the dark, near the mule-lines, if he was paid to go and look at something else.”

“What did you say?”

“I said I would think about it.” He looked at her sideways. “I let them finish telling me first. It is rude to interrupt a man who is telling you his plans. And it was a good plan, for the escort they had counted.”

Sada heard it an hour later, sitting on her stool under the awning with her shawl round her and a lamp, and Riarhin watched her listen.

She did not ask whether Na’ar was sure. She did not ask whether he had been drinking. She listened to the whole of it with her pen in her hand and did not write anything down, and when he had finished she was quiet for a while.

“When?” she said.

“Tomorrow night. If the river is still high. They do not want to do it on a night when everybody will be crossing in the morning. They want a night when everybody is tired of waiting and has stopped watching.” He shrugged. “The ford-master says it will still be high.”

“How many?”

“Eleven at the fire. I think more in the scrub. They talked about the second party.”

Sada turned the pen in her fingers. Then she looked at Riarhin.

“The pilgrims,” she said.

“Yes.”

“That’s the part I don’t like.”

“I know.”

“Not the silver. The silver’s the Crown’s and I’d rather not lose it and it’s insured in Adur-Gah by people who’ll be very annoyed. The pilgrims.” She put the pen down. “They’re going to stampede the pilgrims’ donkeys through my mule-lines in the dark so nobody can see. There are children in that camp. There’s a woman in it who’s eighty if she’s a day.”

“They’ll run downhill,” said Na’ar. “Toward the scrub. That is where the second party is.”

Sada looked at him for a long moment, and then back at the lamp.

“Then the pilgrims don’t sleep there tomorrow,” she said.

They spent the next day moving people without anybody noticing they were being moved, and it was the kind of work Riarhin liked best, and she found that Sada was very good at it.

It could not be done by telling the pilgrims to go. Pilgrims who are told by an envoy to move their camp will ask why, and then everybody on the shelf will know why by noon. So Sada went and sat with the pilgrims’ leader, an old priest with a white beard to his belt, and drank coffee with him, and talked about the church at Adur-Gah, which she knew well, and about the old liturgy they kept there that the Covenant’s homeland had revised away, which he very much wanted to hear. And in the course of an hour it came out that the envoy’s delegation had more room than it needed on the uphill side, by the Crown’s fire, where the ground was drier and there was shade from a fig tree, and that the envoy would take it as a kindness if the pilgrims would share it for the last night before the ford, since she would like to hear the evening office sung.

The priest was delighted. By the middle of the afternoon the pilgrims’ camp had moved, donkeys and children and cooking pots, up to the Crown’s fire, and the old woman who was eighty was sitting under the fig tree with Tesfaye explaining to him in great detail everything that was wrong with the road.

And the ground on the downhill side of the mule-lines, between the mules and the scrub, where the pilgrims had been, was empty.

The rest of it was Gebre’s, and he did it beautifully.

He did not change the watch. That was the first thing, and the most important. The Ridge-Watchers stood two at a time at the head and tail of the mule-lines and changed at the fourth hour and the eighth, in the old way, and anybody watching from the scrub would have seen exactly what they had counted the night before. He did not double them. He did not move the silver.

What he moved was everything else.

The double-girthed mules stood where they had stood, on the downhill end of the lines, nearest the scrub. What was on them was not the Crown’s silver any longer. It was, as far as Riarhin could tell, most of the ford’s supply of river stones, packed in the same chests and roped the same way, and the silver was up under the fig tree with the pilgrims, in the middle of forty sleeping people and an old priest who did not know he was guarding it. And the six Ridge-Watchers who were not on watch were not asleep. They were lying out in the grass on the downhill side, in the empty ground where the pilgrims had been, in their dark cloaks, flat, and very still.

“I have been guarding a building for six years,” Gebre said to Riarhin, in the dusk, with enormous satisfaction. “A building. With a gate. In Mbaro.” He looked at the scrub. “This is better.”

Na’ar went back to the fire at the far end of the shelf in the evening and drank another cup with the men nobody could place, and told them he would take the silver, and would be at the salt caravan’s fire at the fourth hour looking at the camels, and would not look round until he heard the donkeys.

They paid him the first night’s silver in advance. He brought it back and gave it to Sada, who looked at it for a long time and then put it in the Crown’s chest and had Tesfaye write it in the journal.

They came at the fourth hour, when the watch changed.

Riarhin was lying in the grass above the mule-lines, near the tail, where she could see the whole of the downhill ground and the edge of the scrub below it. She had Remorse on her hip for the first time since the fish quay at Silkstrand. She had strung Silence and had it beside her in the grass. The moon was down. The river was loud. Up at the Crown’s fire, under the fig tree, the pilgrims had sung their office and gone to sleep, and the old priest was snoring.

The watch changed. Two Ridge-Watchers walked up the lines and two walked down. It took perhaps a hundred heartbeats, and it was exactly as it had been the night before.

In the middle of it, something went through the downhill ground at a run.

They were fast. They were good. There were seven of them, dark shapes low in the grass, coming up out of the scrub to where the pilgrims’ donkeys should have been, and they had torches ready to light and something in a pot that they were going to throw, and they came up into the empty ground and understood, all at once and far too late, that it was empty.

And Na’ar came down the hill.

She would remember it afterwards the way she remembered very few fights: as a whole thing, from outside, with room around it.

He had been at the salt caravan’s fire looking at the camels, as he had promised. He came down from it at a walk, not hurrying. He came into the empty ground behind the seven men while they were still standing in it, looking at the place where the donkeys should have been, and the first one who turned round and saw him had time to say something, and not very much.

Na’ar hit him. It was not a large movement. It was very short and very fast and it came from somewhere round his hip, and the man went down in the grass and did not get up.

The other six turned.

They were good. She had thought so from the first, from Na’ar’s account of them at the fire, and she was right. Two of them had knives out before the first one hit the ground, and they came at him from two sides, the way men come who have done it together before. And the ground was flat and dry and open and there was nothing on it at all, because an envoy had spent the day moving forty pilgrims and their donkeys out of it. Na’ar had room. He had good footing and the whole of a flat dark field to move in, and he moved in it the way a big man moves who has been doing this for thirty years and has always, always enjoyed it.

He took the first knife hand at the wrist and turned it, and the man went over his hip into the second man, and both of them went down. He stepped over them. The third one had a club and swung it, and Na’ar was not where the club went, and then he was; and the third man found himself sitting on the ground with no club and no clear idea of how he had got there. The fourth and fifth came together. She saw one of them realise, halfway through, what he was fighting. She saw his face change in the light from the ford-master’s lantern on the far side of the shelf: the moment when a man who has been measuring an opponent understands that the opponent has been measuring him, for considerably longer and a great deal better. He tried to stop. He could not stop.

The sixth one ran.

And six Ridge-Watchers stood up out of the grass all round the empty ground in their dark cloaks, and Gebre said something short and loud in the highland tongue, and it was over.

It had taken about as long as the watch change.

The second party came for the mules.

She had been waiting for it. Na’ar had said the second party at the fire, and she had spent the day working out where she would put a second party if she were running this, and she had put it where the men in the scrub had put it: along the riverbank, below the tail of the mule-lines, where the ground fell away steeply to the water and a man could come up it in the dark without being seen from the camp at all. When the donkeys stampeded through the lines and the whole camp was looking at the downhill ground, the second party would come up the bank, cut the double-girthed mules loose and take them down the bank to the river and across.

There were five of them. They came up the bank when the shouting began.

She shot the first one in the thigh from forty yards, lying in the grass. Silence made no sound at all. The arrow did. The man went down on the lip of the bank with a noise like a sack of grain dropped off a cart and began to scream, and the four behind him stopped dead, halfway up the slope, with the mule-lines above them and a screaming man in front and no idea where the arrow had come from.

She put a second arrow into the ground a foot in front of the next one. It stood there quivering in the lantern light. He looked at it.

Then she stood up out of the grass with Remorse drawn and walked down to the edge of the bank.

Three of them went back down the slope. The fourth came up it at her, with a hatchet, because he was the kind of man who does, and she met him at the lip and took the hatchet off him and put him on the ground beside his friend with the arrow in his leg, and stood over both of them, and nobody else came up the bank.

One of them got away.

It was the sixth man, the one who had run from Na’ar. She saw him from the top of the bank as she stood there: going across the downhill ground at a dead run toward the scrub, with the torch he had never lit still in his hand. She had Silence. She had perhaps three heartbeats before he was into the scrub and gone.

And she had two men on the ground at her feet, one of them bleeding badly from the thigh, and three more at the bottom of the bank who had gone down the slope but not very far, and could come back up it the moment she turned her back.

She did not shoot. She stood where she was, over the two on the ground, with the drawn sword, facing down the slope. The sixth man went into the scrub and was gone.

Gebre came down the lines at a run with two of his men and took the three at the bottom of the bank without any trouble at all, because they had been standing at the bottom of the bank for a full minute looking at a woman with a sword who had not moved, and had forgotten, in that minute, that there was a camp full of Ridge-Watchers behind her.

“One went into the scrub,” she said to Gebre.

“I saw.” He looked at the man with the arrow in his thigh, and then at her. “You could have had him.”

“I could have had him.”

He looked at the three men his Ridge-Watchers were tying at the foot of the bank, and then back up at her standing over the other two.

“Yes,” he said. “I saw that too.”

There was no map.

Riarhin would think about that, later, with a certain dry amusement at herself. When the eleven men had been tied and laid out in a row by the ford-master’s hut to wait for the morning and the judge’s men from Dessa, she went through their things, all of them, the way she went through everything, because a woman who does not is a woman who finds out later. She found knives, and a pot of pitch with rags in it, and two purses with rather more silver in them than eleven men should have had between them, and a flask of something that Gebre confiscated with a solemn face. She found nothing with the Crown’s name on it. Nothing that anybody in Mbaro or on the river-island could have given them. No letter, no drawing, no word she knew.

They were thieves who had seen a Crown delegation with double-girthed mules in a crowded camp with the river high. That was all they were. They had never heard of her.

She was, very slightly, disappointed, and it annoyed her.

The woman whose cart had been nearly taken was called Tirunesh.

She was a carter from the coffee country, a widow of about fifty with a two-mule cart of green coffee going down to the coast, and she had been camped on the downhill side of the Crown’s lines, on the very edge of the empty ground, because that had been where there was room. She had not been moved with the pilgrims. Nobody had thought of her. When the seven men came up out of the scrub, they had come up past her cart, and one of them had cut her mules’ tethers on the way by, meaning to use them in the stampede; and the mules had gone, and she had come out from under the cart with a stick and gone after them in the dark, alone, into the middle of the fight.

Na’ar had brought the mules back. He had found them grazing by the river, where mules go when nobody is chasing them, and led them back up to her cart, and tied them, and then stood there looking at her.

She had been sitting on the tailboard with the stick across her knees, shaking.

“You have not eaten,” he had said.

She had looked at him.

“Neither have I,” said Na’ar. “I was busy.” And he had gone and got a pot.

That was where Riarhin found them, an hour before dawn: at the tail of the green-coffee cart, with a small fire and Tirunesh’s iron pan and a pot of lentils that Na’ar had got off the salt caravan’s cook at some point in the evening and was now doing something to with onions and a spice he had bought in Dessa and was very proud of. Tirunesh was roasting a handful of her own green coffee in the pan, the way the woman at the Taharkan house in Mbaro had roasted it, and shaking it, and the smell was coming up off it into the dark, smoky and sweet.

They were not talking much. They did not have a great deal of language in common. Tirunesh said something and pointed at the lentils, and Na’ar tasted them and added salt, and she nodded.

Riarhin sat down on the grass on the other side of the fire.

Na’ar gave her a bowl without asking. Tirunesh gave her a cup of the coffee, when it was ground and boiled, and then a second, and Riarhin took both.

Nobody said anything about the fight. Nobody said anything about the silver, or the men laid out by the ford-master’s hut, or the one who had got away. The river was loud below them. Somewhere up at the Crown’s fire, under the fig tree, a child woke and cried and was hushed.

It was the best meal she had eaten since Mbaro. She told him so. He pretended not to hear.

In the morning Sada and Riarhin compared notes, because the judge’s men from Dessa would want an account, and Sada was writing it.

They did it under the awning, with the journal. Sada had heard Na’ar’s account at midnight, in the lamplight, when he came up from the fight to tell her it was done. Riarhin had heard it at the fire, over the lentils, in pieces, between tastings.

It was not the same account.

Na’ar had told Sada about the men at the fire, and what they had offered him, and how many silvers, and how he had taken the first night’s silver and brought it to her, and that he had told them he would be looking at the camels. He had told her that the man who ran had been the youngest of them and had looked, at the fire, as if he had never done this before.

He had told Riarhin about the fight. The footing. The first man, and the knife hands, and the one who had understood too late. He had told her that the one who ran had been the best of them, and the fastest, and that he had been glad to see him go, because he would not have wanted to fight him tired.

They sat and looked at the two accounts side by side for a while.

“He told me the boy was young,” said Sada. “He told you he was good.”

“Both,” said Riarhin.

“Both.” Sada wrote something. “He hasn’t told either of us anything that wasn’t so.”

“No.”

“He’s told each of us the part we’d want.”

“He’s told each of us the part he thought we’d listen to,” said Riarhin. “He does that.”

Sada put her pen down and looked out across the shelf to where Na’ar was helping Tirunesh put her mules to the cart. The river had dropped in the night to the third notch on the ford-master’s pole, and the whole camp was packing to cross.

“I like him very much,” Sada said.

“So do I,” said Riarhin, and was surprised to hear herself say it out loud. She had not, as far as she could remember, said it to anybody before.

Chapter Fourteen: The House with Seven Doors

The rain caught them two days past the ford and did not let go.

It came down off the highlands in the middle of the afternoon, out of a sky that had been clear at noon, and it came as a grey wall that walked down the valley toward them at the pace of a man and then was on them. Riarhin had been in the squall off Kessary and thought she knew what weather was. This was different. It was not wind. It was water, falling straight and heavy and warm out of a sky gone the colour of slate, so hard that it bounced knee-high off the road and the mules put their heads down and stopped and would not move. Abdi walked back down the string shouting, and nobody could hear him, and it did not matter, because there was only one place to go.

The relay house at Harbu stood on a terrace above the river where the road turned up into the hills. It had been built to shelter the Crown’s couriers when there were still Crown’s couriers on this road, and it had been a relay house ever since, and when the Taharkan envoy’s delegation came up the last half-mile to it through the rain, with the water running down the road in a brown sheet over the mules’ hooves, it was already full.

The keeper was a woman called Tsehay, and she did not turn them away.

She was about sixty, broad and grey and entirely unflustered, and she stood in the gateway of the relay house’s courtyard in the rain with a sack over her head and looked at forty mules and a Crown seal and eleven Taharkans and a very large Ykrul, and at the courtyard behind her, which was already packed from wall to wall with other people’s animals and other people’s carts. She said something to Sada that made Sada laugh, which Riarhin had not expected; and then she stood aside, and they came in.

“She said,” said Tesfaye, pushing his wet spectacles up his nose, “that a relay house is never full. It only gets smaller.”

It got a great deal smaller.

There was a salt merchant from the coast with his family and six servants. There were four drovers with a herd of goats that had been put in the stable because there was nowhere else to put them. There were three Oshiiran grain carters who had come through Gedo one cart at a time. And there was a delegation of the Pereshi Fire, a dozen solemn men in saffron with an escort of twenty spearmen and four wagons, on their way to Adur-Gah to explain to the Taharkan Crown that its Pereshi congregation had been keeping the winter liturgy incorrectly for two hundred years.

“Every year,” said Sada, very low, as the saffron delegation looked at the Crown’s seal on the mules and looked away again. “Every single year. And we give them a valley, and they go and argue with the Everflame for us.” She pushed her wet hair back off her forehead. “This one’s early.”

The relay house had seven doors.

Riarhin counted them the first evening, because she counted everything in any building she slept in, and she had counted doors in inns and barracks and pillow houses in eight countries without ever finding a reason to stop. It was a long low house of stone and whitewash built round three sides of the courtyard, with the stable on the fourth. And off the courtyard, round the three sides, there were seven doors into the house: the kitchen, the hall, the keeper’s rooms, and four more into the long range of sleeping rooms where the couriers had slept and the travellers slept now.

There should have been eight.

The long range ran the whole length of the river side of the courtyard, and it had four doors into it from the courtyard and a passage running down its whole length behind them, and at the far end of the passage, where it should have come out into the courtyard by the stable, there was a door. Riarhin could see it from the courtyard. It was a good door, oak, with iron hinges, and it had a bar across it on the courtyard side and a length of rope tied round the bar and knotted, and Tsehay had put the salt merchant’s family in the room nearest it and told them, very firmly, not to go down the passage.

She had also, Riarhin noticed, put nobody at all in the room at the other end of the range, beside the kitchen, which was dry and warm and had a hearth. Its door stood open onto the courtyard with nothing across it, and nobody went in. Tsehay’s household went round it. When the salt merchant’s youngest boy ran in there to get out of the rain, one of Tsehay’s daughters went and fetched him out by the wrist, kindly, and closed the door behind him, and then opened it again and left it standing open as it had been.

Riarhin watched all of that and filed it.

A closed passage with a knotted rope on its door. An empty room that must be left open and not entered. A relay house on the old courier road, on the way to a highland capital, with a Crown delegation and a foreign delegation and a merchant with six servants all sleeping under one roof.

She had seen a house like that before. She had seen several. They were the houses where the passage went somewhere.

She went down the passage on the second night, when the rain was still coming down and everybody was asleep.

She did not go through the barred door. She went in at the other end, through the room nearest the kitchen where the salt merchant’s family was sleeping in a heap, past them without a sound, and into the passage behind the sleeping rooms, and along it in the dark with her hand on the wall.

The wall was wet.

That was the first thing. It was not wet with rain; the roof was sound, she could hear the rain on it and there was no drip. It was wet the way a cellar wall is wet, from inside, with water that was coming through the stone from the other side. The further she went down the passage, the wetter it got. And the floor under her feet, which was beaten earth, went from dry to damp to soft, and then, near the far end, near the barred door, to something that gave under her weight like a pudding.

She stopped.

She knelt down and put her hand flat on the floor. It was mud. Under the mud, a hand’s breadth down, there was nothing: her fingers went through into a hollow where there should have been earth, and cold air came up round them.

She took her hand out very slowly and stayed where she was for a while, kneeling in the dark, listening.

Under the sound of the rain there was another sound. It was the river. She had heard it all the time and had not attended to it, because a river beside a house is a thing everybody hears and nobody listens to. It was much closer than it should have been. It was not beside the house at all. It was underneath the far end of the passage, very close, working at something.

In the morning she found the rest of it, in daylight, from outside, and felt a fool.

The relay house stood on its terrace above the river. The long range, the sleeping rooms, ran along the river side, with the passage behind them against the outer wall; and from the courtyard you could not see what was below the outer wall. From the road below the terrace, in the rain, you could.

The river had been at the terrace. Not this year: last year, or the one before, in some flood that had come down out of the highlands higher than anybody had seen, and taken a bite out of the bank below the far end of the range, under the passage, under the barred door. Somebody had started to mend it. There was a wall of new stone half-built along the foot of the bank, with scaffolding poles rotting on it and a pile of dressed blocks lying in the mud beside it, and a stain on the outer wall of the house above, in a long brown tide-line, that ran along the range at the height of a man’s chest and stopped at the room nearest the barred door.

The work had been abandoned. The rains had come before it was done. And the river, which had taken one bite, had been quietly taking another all season, from underneath, where nobody could see it, into the soft ground under the far end of the house.

Tsehay had not been hiding a passage. She had been keeping people off a floor that was about to fall into the river.

Riarhin stood in the rain on the road below the terrace and looked at the brown tide-line on the wall, and the rotting scaffold, and the pile of dressed stone, and said something to herself in Silkstrand that she would not have said in front of Sada.

Then she went back up to the house and looked at the open room by the kitchen that nobody went into.

It was dry. It was sound. It was warm, with a hearth. There was nothing wrong with the floor or the walls or the roof. Nobody went into it. Its door stood open and must not be shut.

She asked Tsehay’s eldest daughter, through Tesfaye, very politely, why.

The daughter looked at her as though she had asked why the sun came up in the east.

“It’s the house’s,” she said.

Tesfaye translated it and then looked at Riarhin over his spectacles, and she could see that he was not going to ask any more, and that if she asked any more he would translate it and be unhappy.

She did not ask any more. She had learned what she needed to learn from the passage. The room was not a clue. It was the house’s.

The bank went on the third night.

She was awake. She had been lying on her blanket in the Crown’s room by the hall, which she shared with Tesfaye and the two older clerks and Dawit, listening to the river under the rain, and she had heard it change. The working sound she had heard in the passage had been going all night, low and steady; and at some time after the fourth hour it stopped, and in the silence where it had been there was a long slow sound like somebody tearing canvas very carefully.

She was on her feet before it finished.

Tsehay was already in the courtyard. She was standing in the rain in her nightclothes with a lamp in one hand, looking at the far end of the long range, and she was not shouting. She was giving orders in a low steady voice to her three daughters, one after another, and they were going.

The far end of the range was moving.

It was moving very slowly. Riarhin could see it in the lamplight: the whitewashed wall at the corner by the barred door had a crack in it that had not been there at dusk, running up from the ground to the eaves, and as she watched the crack widened by a finger’s breadth, and the roof above it sagged, and a tile came off and fell in the courtyard and broke.

Tsehay turned her head and saw Riarhin, and said something short.

Riarhin did not have the language. She did not need it. The keeper had pointed at the salt merchant’s room, nearest the crack, and then at Riarhin, and then at the door.

There were eleven people in the salt merchant’s room and none of them was awake.

She went in through the door from the courtyard and the floor tilted under her. Not much. Enough that she felt it in her knees, the way she had felt the deck of the Gerlind lie down in the squall. The room was dark and warm and smelled of wet wool and sleep, and there was a heap of blankets on the floor with a family in it, and six servants along the wall, and a baby in a basket by the hearth.

She did not shout. Shouting in a dark room full of people asleep makes them do exactly the wrong thing, all at once. She went to the salt merchant and put her hand over his mouth and shook him until his eyes opened, and put her face close to his and said, very clearly, in the trade tongue, “The floor is going. Get your people out through the courtyard door. Now. Quietly.”

He stared at her. She took her hand off his mouth.

“Now,” she said.

He was a good man. She would think that afterwards. He did not argue. He sat up and began to wake his wife.

Riarhin went to the basket by the hearth and picked it up, baby and all, and carried it to the door and put it into the arms of the first person she saw in the courtyard, who was Dawit. Dawit stood in the rain holding a basket with a baby in it and a completely blank face. She went back in.

The floor tilted a little further.

There were twenty spearmen in the courtyard by then, and four wagons, and a very loud argument.

The Pereshi delegation had been sleeping in the hall, which was sound, on the far side of the courtyard from the crack; and their captain had turned out his spearmen at the first noise and put them round the wagons, which were loaded with the delegation’s vestments and books and the gifts it was bringing to the Crown, and was standing in front of them with his arms folded. And Tsehay’s daughters were trying to get the wagons out of the gate.

Because the stores were in the long range. The relay house’s stores, the grain and the oil and the salt and the season’s coffee, in the two rooms next to the salt merchant’s; and so were the delegation’s own loads, the Crown’s chests and the clerks’ papers and the forty mules’ packs, in the room next to those. All of it on the river side of the courtyard, all of it on a floor that was beginning to move. And the only way to get it out quickly was on wheels, and the only wheels in the courtyard were four Pereshi wagons, loaded, with twenty spearmen round them.

The Pereshi captain was saying no. He was saying it in the trade tongue, loudly and with great dignity, and the gist of it was that the wagons carried the delegation’s sacred vestments and the gifts for the Crown, and would not be unloaded in the rain in the middle of the night on the word of an innkeeper’s daughter.

Sada came across the courtyard in her dark shawl with her hair uncovered.

Riarhin was carrying a sack of grain out of the second store room and did not see all of it. She saw enough. She saw Sada stop in front of the Pereshi captain in the rain, and not raise her voice, and say something to him. It was short. It had the word Crown in it, and the words guest’s fire, which Riarhin had heard Tesfaye say at supper when he was explaining how the Pereshi had come to have a valley. And she saw the captain’s face change: not to agreement, but to the face of a man who has been reminded, in front of his own spearmen, of the terms on which his people were living in the country he was standing in.

He said something to his men. They began to unload the wagons, very carefully, onto the sound side of the courtyard, under the hall’s eaves, in the rain.

Sada was already walking away. She did not wait to see it done.

Na’ar moved the rest.

He did not make anything of it. He went into the long range and came out with sacks, one on each shoulder, and put them on a wagon, and went back in. He did it thirty times. Then he went and got two of the Pereshi spearmen, who had finished unloading their own wagons and were standing about in the rain not knowing what to do, and showed them how to take the other end of a chest, and they did it together. Abdi was in the stable getting the mules out, and the drovers were getting the goats out, and the goats did not want to go, and there was a great deal of noise from the stable end of the courtyard that nobody paid any attention to.

And Tsehay stood in the middle of the courtyard with her lamp, in her nightclothes, and told everybody where the house was going to fail.

She knew. That was the thing Riarhin would remember best. She had built none of it and she had kept all of it, for thirty years, and she knew it the way Varek knew the Gerlind. She told them the far corner would go first, and it did. She told them the salt merchant’s room would follow, and the first store room, and that the second would hold for an hour, perhaps two, because there was an old wall under it that her husband’s father had built; and that the Crown’s room beyond it would hold till morning. She told them to empty the second store first and leave the first, because the first was already lost. And when a grain carter tried to go back into the first store room for his own sacks, she sent a daughter after him, and the daughter brought him out by the wrist, the way she had fetched the salt merchant’s boy out of the empty room.

Riarhin, carrying the last of the Crown’s chests out of the delegation’s room at the fifth hour, stopped in the doorway and looked back along the range.

The far corner had gone. It had gone quite quietly in the end: a long settling, a groan, and the corner of the house by the barred door had leaned out over the river and folded down into the dark, with the rotten scaffold and the pile of dressed stone, and the water had taken it. The salt merchant’s room had gone after it. The first store room was going now; she could see the roof beam sagging over it in the lamplight, and a brown tide of river coming in under the door.

They had lost the salt merchant’s room and the first store and whatever was in them. They had not lost anybody. They had not lost the second store, or the Crown’s room, or the stable, or the hall, and they had not lost a single mule or goat, or the Pereshi vestments, or the gifts for the Crown.

It was because Tsehay had given up the first store room an hour before anybody else would have. Everybody had listened to her.

By dawn the rain had stopped, and the relay house at Harbu had six doors, and every single one of its rooms was full of wet clothing.

It hung from the beams in the hall. It hung from lines strung across the courtyard. It hung from the Pereshi wagons and from the stable rails and from the scaffolding of the kitchen roof. Salt merchant’s silks, and pilgrims’ shawls borrowed from the Crown’s baggage, and the Pereshi delegation’s plain grey undergarments, which the saffron was worn over, and which it turned out they were rather sensitive about. It steamed in the first sun. Everybody who had been up all night was asleep in whatever corner was dry.

Riarhin was not asleep. She did not need much. She was sitting in the passage between the hall and the kitchen, which was the only stretch of the house that was dry and not full of either clothing or people, with her back against the wall and her wet boots beside her and her feet bare on the cool stone floor.

Sada came along the passage from the kitchen with two cups.

She sat down against the other wall, facing Riarhin, with perhaps three feet of stone floor between their bare feet, and handed her one of the cups. It was coffee. It was very hot. They drank it.

“The Pereshi captain,” said Riarhin, after a while. “What did you say to him?”

“I reminded him that the Pereshi Fire came over the passes three hundred years ago with nothing but a quarrel, and a Taharkan king gave them a valley and told his council that a guest’s fire is not an argument.” Sada drank. “And that I’d be very sorry to have to tell the Crown, when we got to Adur-Gah, that the Pereshi delegation had watched the Crown’s relay house fall into the river and kept its hands folded.”

“That’s not a reminder. That’s a threat.”

“It’s a reminder,” said Sada, “in the form we use for threats.” She looked at her cup. “He’ll write a letter of complaint to the Council about it. It’ll be very long. I’ll enjoy reading it.”

They sat there for a while without saying anything.

It was a narrow passage. It was, Riarhin realised, the only place in the whole house where they could have been alone, and they were alone in it only because everybody else was asleep, and there was a line of wet shawls hanging from a cord at the kitchen end of it, dripping slowly onto the floor, that anybody could have walked through at any moment. Sada’s feet were bare. Riarhin had not seen them bare before. They were long and narrow and there was mud dried on one ankle.

“Can I ask you something?” said Sada.

“You’ll ask it anyway.”

“I’ll ask it anyway.” Sada put her cup down on the floor beside her. “When you’ve done a job. The job you were hired for, or the one you set yourself. Whatever it is. When it’s done.” She looked at Riarhin across the three feet of stone. “Do you ever stay?”

Riarhin looked at her.

She had half a dozen answers. She had had them for years. She had given them in taverns and at campfires and in the beds of people who had asked it more or less that way, and every one of them was true, and every one of them was built to be the end of the conversation. She could hear herself about to give one now. It depends on the job. There’s usually somewhere else to be. I’ve stayed longer than I meant to more often than you’d think.

“There’s usually something else,” she said. It was the weakest of them.

Sada looked at her for a long moment and did not say anything. She did not argue with it, or smile at it, or let it end the conversation either. She let it lie there in the passage between them, a thing that had been said instead of an answer, and looked at it; and then looked at Riarhin; and let her see that she had noticed which it was.

And then she let it go.

That was what Riarhin would remember. Not that she had been caught in it. Sada had let her have the evasion without once pretending it was anything else, and had been neither hurt by it nor fooled.

Riarhin put her own cup down on the floor.

She did not decide to. She would think about that afterwards and find that she could not locate the moment at which it had been decided, and would conclude that it had been decided by both of them some time before, perhaps on the edge of a dry fountain in Mbaro, perhaps under an awning at Gedo, and had only been waiting for a stretch of dry floor with nobody on it. She leaned across the three feet of stone, and Sada leaned across it too, and met her halfway.

It was not long. Somebody was snoring in the hall. A shawl dripped onto the floor at the kitchen end. Sada’s mouth tasted of coffee and smoke, and her hand came up and rested on the side of Riarhin’s neck, very lightly, just below the place where a thread had once gone slack.

When they drew apart neither of them said anything.

Sada picked up her cup again and drank from it and looked at Riarhin over the rim. The corners of her mouth were doing the thing they did.

“That wasn’t an answer either,” she said.

“No.”

“Good.” Sada put the cup down. “I didn’t want it to be.”

She did not move back to her own wall. Neither did Riarhin. They sat there with their bare feet on the cool stone and their shoulders nearly touching, in the only dry passage in the relay house at Harbu, while the rest of the house slept round them and the wet clothing steamed in the sun and the river went on below, a little further off now than it had been.

Chapter Fifteen: The Inscription in the Wrong Place

The sanctuary at Ayna had kept the Crown’s transit agreements for four hundred years, and nobody had ever asked to read the oldest of them until Sada Alemu did.

It stood on a spur of rock above the road where it began, at last, to climb in earnest into the highlands: a round church of dressed grey stone with a conical roof of thatch, and round it a wall, and inside the wall a cluster of low stone buildings where the Covenant’s brothers and sisters lived and kept bees and grew barley on terraces so steep that Riarhin could not see how anybody stood up on them. The air was thinner here. She had noticed it two days before, at the top of a long climb out of the river country, when she had found herself breathing a little harder than the climb deserved; and Na’ar had noticed it before she did and said nothing, and Abdi had noticed it before either of them and had begun, without saying anything, to shorten the day’s stages.

The country had changed again. It was green now, a dark hard green of juniper and heather and a tall grass that the wind combed flat, with grey rock coming through it everywhere and the valleys dropping away on both sides into blue distance. Her cloak was the colour of the juniper. Sada had looked at it that morning, in the cold, and said it suited the highlands, and Riarhin had said it always had.

The transit agreements were Sada’s business, and Riarhin went along because the assessor was supposed to.

There was a question in the border-traffic article of the agreement, Sada explained on the climb up to the sanctuary, that the Council was certain to ask about: whether the Crown had ever before agreed to let Oshiiran carriers use the Taharkan road north of Gedo without a Taharkan escort. Sada thought it had. She thought it had done so under an old king, in a treaty that had lapsed and been forgotten. If she could show the Council a precedent, the article would be an old arrangement renewed rather than a new concession, and two of the three people who were waiting to stand up at her hearing would have nothing to stand up about.

The copies of the old treaties were at Ayna because the old kings had used the sanctuary as a strongroom. The originals were in Adur-Gah. The copies at Ayna were older than the originals, as it turned out, because the originals had burned in a fire at Adur-Gah two hundred years ago and been remade from Ayna’s copies, which were therefore the only copies of the originals, which were copies.

“Nobody in the Council knows that,” said Sada. “I’m going to enjoy telling them.”

The keeper of the sanctuary’s writings was a sister of the Covenant called Abeba.

She was small and brown and very old, with a white shawl over her head and a face like a walnut and hands that were entirely steady, and she received the Taharkan envoy in a low stone room with a single window, where the writings were kept in cedar chests on shelves round the walls, with the courtesy of somebody who has received envoys before and will receive them again and does not expect any of them to be interesting. Then Sada told her what she wanted, and the courtesy changed into something else, and the old woman got up from her stool with an energy that Riarhin would not have expected from her and went to the shelves herself.

It took all afternoon. The treaty was there. It was in a chest of the oldest writings, written on vellum in the old highland script, which Sada could read slowly and Abeba could read as fast as she could speak. And it said, in a clause toward the end, that the carriers of the grain country might pass north of the river crossings with their loads and animals in time of peace, under the protection of the king’s peace and the escort of their own people, upon the payment of a toll at the crossings, which was specified.

Sada sat back on her stool and looked at it for a long time.

“There,” she said, very quietly, to nobody. “There you are.”

Abeba said something, and Sada laughed.

“She says,” said Tesfaye, who had come too and was copying the clause with enormous care, “that she has been waiting forty years for somebody to ask for that chest.”

Riarhin had nothing to do in the writings room, so she went outside.

She walked round the church. It was the thing to do at a sanctuary, Tesfaye had said; you went round the church, once, sunwise, before you left. She did it slowly, looking at the stone, because she looked at everything.

The church was old, and it had been rebuilt, and whoever had rebuilt it had used stone from wherever stone could be had. Most of it was the grey local rock, roughly squared. Here and there in the lower courses there were blocks of something else: a paler stone, finer-grained, better dressed than the rest, with sharp arrises and faces rubbed smooth. Old stone, brought from somewhere else and used again. It was not unusual. Every church Riarhin had ever walked round had a few blocks in it that had come from somewhere else.

On the north side, at the height of her knee, one of the pale blocks had writing on it.

It was upside down. Whoever had built it into the church wall had not cared or had not been able to read it, and had laid it in the course with its face outward and its writing inverted, and the rain of two centuries had softened the edges of the letters. Riarhin crouched down and looked at it with her head on one side.

She could not read it. It was the old highland script, or something like it; she had seen Abeba reading the same letters all afternoon and had attended to the shapes without learning what they meant. It was only part of an inscription. The block had been cut out of something larger, and the writing ran off both edges.

In the middle of it, there was a mark.

She knew it before she knew where she knew it from.

It was a small thing, cut into the stone among the letters: a horizontal line, and above it three short vertical strokes, evenly spaced, like the teeth of a comb laid on its back. And above the middle stroke, a dot.

She had seen it on the ninth leaf.

Not in the text. In the margin of the forwarding record, the one that ended at the foot of a page and was taken up on the next by a different consignment in a different hand. Somebody, eighty years ago, had made that mark in the margin beside the last entry, small and quick, in the same ink as the entry. She had seen it on the floor of Serafine’s counting room with Lena holding the lamp, and she had attended to it, and filed it as a clerk’s mark, the kind of thing clerks put in margins to show that an entry had been checked or passed or paid. She had not thought about it since.

It was cut into a block of pale stone in the wall of a church in the highlands of Taharka, upside down, in the middle of an inscription somebody had broken up for building stone.

She crouched there for a long time.

Then she stood up and went back to the writings room.

Abeba did not know what the mark meant.

She said so at once, and without any embarrassment, when Riarhin, through Tesfaye, asked her to come and look at the block on the north side of the church. She came, slowly, leaning on a stick, and bent down and looked at the inscription for a long time, and read what she could of it aloud, upside down, in the old script, and Tesfaye wrote it down.

It was not much. …of the wells… by the high… under the seal of… and then the mark, and then the block ended.

“She says,” said Tesfaye, “that it’s old. Older than the treaty. She says the letters are the kind they used before the letters in the treaty.” He listened. “She says she doesn’t know the mark. She’s seen it once or twice on very old things. Nobody’s ever told her what it was.”

“Where did the block come from?”

Tesfaye asked. Abeba straightened up with her hand on her stick and looked at the wall for a while, and then she said something that made Tesfaye’s eyebrows go up.

“She says she knows exactly where it came from,” he said. “Because there was a quarrel about it.”

The quarrel was in a chest in the writings room, and Abeba found it in a quarter of an hour.

The church at Ayna had been rebuilt sixty years ago after a lightning strike had brought down half the north wall. The brothers and sisters had needed stone, and the local grey rock was bad for dressing, so they had hired carters to bring better stone from a ruin they knew of, up in the hills to the north, where there was a great deal of dressed stone lying about that nobody wanted. The carters had brought it. And then the carters had asked to be paid by the distance, at a carter’s rate, and the sanctuary had said the distance was less than the carters said, and the carters had said it was more, and there had been a quarrel that went on for two years and ended in front of a judge.

And because it had ended in front of a judge, somebody had written down exactly where the stone had come from.

Abeba read it out. Tesfaye wrote it down. Riarhin stood by the window and listened to every word.

The stone had come from a ruin on the old road north of Adur-Gah that the herders called the goat track, three days’ carting beyond the last village with a church, at a place where the track crossed a dry river bed on the remains of a causeway. The ruin was a building beside the causeway, long fallen. The carters said there was a great deal of it and that it went on under the ground. They said it had been a waystation, very old, and that there were more of them further on, every day or so along the goat track, all fallen, going north toward the country where nobody went.

The judge had found for the carters. The distance had been measured, and was what they said.

“The goat track,” said Riarhin that evening, in the guest house of the sanctuary, where the delegation was lodged.

“Yes.”

“North of Adur-Gah.”

“Yes.” Sada was sitting on a bench by the fire with the precedent clause in Tesfaye’s fair copy on her knee, reading it for the fourth time with an expression of enormous private satisfaction. She looked up. “I know it. Everybody in Adur-Gah knows it. It’s a herders’ track. It goes up into the high grazing north of the city and then peters out in the rocks. Nobody uses it for anything.”

“It had waystations on it. Every day or so. With dressed stone, and inscriptions, and a mark that’s on the ninth leaf of my file.”

Sada put the clause down.

“Tell me,” she said.

She told her, and then she did something she had not done before. She asked for help reading it.

Not from Sada. From somebody who could. There was a brother at Ayna, Abeba had said, who had spent his whole life on the oldest writings in the sanctuary, and knew the letters from before the treaty letters better than anybody north of the coast, and was very old and very deaf and did not like visitors. Riarhin went to Abeba in the morning with Tesfaye and asked, with a good deal of care over the form, whether the sanctuary would allow a copy to be made of the inscription on the north wall, and of the judge’s finding about the carters, and whether the old brother might be asked to read the copy, for a fee to the sanctuary, and for his time.

Abeba looked at her for a long while.

Then she named a sum. It was not small. Riarhin paid it.

The old brother read it that afternoon, sitting in the sun outside his cell with the copy on his knees and a boy beside him shouting Tesfaye’s questions into his ear. He read the letters from before the treaty letters as if they had been written yesterday. He read the inscription. He looked at the mark for a long time, turning the paper round so that it was the right way up, and then he said a word, and then another, and the boy shouted them at Tesfaye, and Tesfaye wrote them down.

“He says,” said Tesfaye, “that it’s a seal-mark. Of an office. He says it means the wells.” He listened. “The keeping of the wells. The high wells. He says it was the mark of the people who kept the wells on the high road, for the old kingdom, before the kings of Taharka. He says he’s seen it on three stones in his life and all three came from the north.”

“What old kingdom?”

Tesfaye asked. The old brother shook his head and said something short.

“He says,” said Tesfaye, “that it was the one before. He doesn’t know what it was called. He says nobody does. He says the name went out of the writings a very long time ago and he has never found where it went.”

She sat with it that night by the fire in the guest house, with the copy on her knee, and Sada beside her reading the precedent clause for the sixth time, and Na’ar across from them mending a strap.

It was not a map. She kept telling herself that, and it kept being true. It was one broken inscription, upside down in a church wall, with a mark on it that matched a mark in a margin; and a judge’s finding about a quarrel over carting; and an old man’s reading of a word. It did not tell her where the thing was. It did not tell her what it was.

What it told her was the shape of the country she was going into.

She had thought, on the floor of Serafine’s counting room, that she was looking for a place eleven days beyond the last water: a tomb, or a vault, or a shrine at the end of nowhere, where somebody had taken a thing to hide it. She had thought of it the way the relic dealers thought of it: a single place, alone in an empty desert.

A road with waystations every day. Dressed stone. Inscriptions. An office that kept the wells, with a seal-mark of its own, whose mark was still being made by a clerk in a margin eighty years ago, when the consignment went north. A clerk who had known, then, what the mark meant, and whose office it was, and had marked the entry with it as though he were still answerable to it.

It had not been empty when the road was made. Somebody had kept wells along it. Somebody had taken the trouble to cut the name of their office into stone at every stage, and there had been an office to cut it for. The thing beyond the last water had not been in an empty desert. It had been reached through a country that had been lived in, and governed, and argued over, and written down.

And nobody knew what that country had been called.

“You want to go now,” said Na’ar.

He did not look up from the strap. He said it mildly, as he said most things.

Riarhin looked at him across the fire.

It was true. She had wanted to go since the moment she crouched down on the north side of the church and saw the mark. She had wanted to go in the writings room, and in the sun outside the old brother’s cell, and she wanted to go now, sitting by the fire with the copy on her knee. The goat track was north of Adur-Gah. Adur-Gah was six days away. The goat track was three days’ carting beyond the last village with a church, and then a waystation every day or so, going north. She could see it. She could see herself leaving the delegation at the next fork and going round the city and up the goat track with Na’ar and two mules, and being at the first waystation before Sada was standing up in front of her Council.

She did not say any of that.

“No,” she said.

Na’ar looked up from the strap.

“Not now,” said Riarhin. “I haven’t got an expedition. I’ve got a copy of a stone and a judge’s finding about some carters. I don’t know how much water it takes to get from one waystation to the next. I don’t know if the waystations have wells in them that still work or whether they’ve all gone dry. I don’t know who grazes that country, or whose leave I’d need to go through it, or what they’d do if I didn’t ask.” She looked at the copy. “Eighty years ago somebody went up that road with a light party and enough water to come back slowly. And I don’t know why they needed to come back slowly. Until I do, I’m not going anywhere.”

Na’ar went on looking at her for a while.

Then he went back to the strap.

“Good,” he said.

Beside her on the bench, Sada had stopped reading the precedent clause. She did not say anything. She was looking into the fire, and Riarhin could see the side of her face in the firelight, and the corner of her mouth, which was not doing the thing it usually did.

“You’ll need to ask the Council,” Sada said eventually.

“I know.”

“They’re going to want to know what’s up there as much as you do. More.” Sada turned her head and looked at her. “It’s their country. Or it’s on the edge of it. And somebody used to keep wells on it, and now nobody does, and they’ll want to know why.” She paused. “I’ll tell them what I know. When they need to know it. To your face first.”

“I know,” said Riarhin. “You said.”

“I’m saying it again. So you can’t pretend later I didn’t.”

Riarhin looked at her. Then she folded the copy of the inscription and put it inside her coat, next to the nine leaves and the Crown’s paper and the three grey pieces of a letter, and sat back on the bench, and let her shoulder come to rest against Sada’s.

Sada did not move away.

Across the fire, Na’ar finished the strap and held it up to the light and pulled it, twice, hard, to see if it would hold. It held.

Chapter Sixteen: The Last Ascent

The pass at Kolo Ber closed on the morning of the fifty-second day, two days’ march short of Adur-Gah, and the Ridge-Watch captain who closed it would not say why.

He did not need to. Riarhin could see why from the foot of the last climb, where the road went up in a series of long switchbacks across a face of grey scree toward a notch in the ridge, and the notch had a stone watchtower in it with a signal fire on top that was burning in the daylight. Above the notch the sky was a hard clear blue. Below it, in the notch itself, something was moving across the road: a haze, grey-white, low to the ground, pouring through the gap from the far side like water over a weir and falling down the scree toward them in slow long streamers that thinned and vanished halfway down.

It was not cloud. It was too low and too fast. It was wind, carrying something: ice, or dust, or snow blown off the high peaks on the far side. And where it went over the scree the scree moved. She watched a stone the size of a sheep go down the face in the middle of the morning with nobody near it, and then another, and then a long whispering slide of smaller stones that crossed two of the switchbacks and buried a stretch of road.

The Ridge-Watch captain came down from the tower to meet the Taharkan envoy’s delegation at the foot of the climb. He was an old man with a face burned nearly black by the high sun and a horn of ibex slung at his hip, and he bowed to Sada, and said something, and pointed back up at the notch; and Sada listened with her face perfectly still, and bowed back.

“What did he say?” said Riarhin.

Tesfaye pushed his spectacles up his nose. He looked, for a moment, as though he would rather not say.

“He said the wind on the pass has no name,” said Tesfaye. “And that nobody crosses while it’s on it.”

“What wind?”

“The one that has no name,” said Tesfaye, and went to see to the chests.

Nobody explained, and after the first hour Riarhin stopped expecting anybody to.

The Ridge-Watchers at the foot of the climb did not explain. Gebre, who had been a Ridge-Watcher for thirty years, did not explain; he stood at the foot of the switchbacks and looked up at the notch with his arms folded and his face shut, and when Na’ar asked him about the wind he said, in his bad Ecktorian, “It comes. It goes. When it is on the pass, the pass is not ours.” And went to tell his men to unload.

Abdi did not explain. Abdi had crossed Kolo Ber two hundred times in fifty years and had seen the wind on it perhaps a dozen, and he unloaded the mules without a word and picketed them in the lee of a boulder field and sat down and lit a pipe.

It would blow for three days, or for ten. Nobody knew. When it stopped, the scree would settle, and the Ridge-Watchers would go up and clear the road, and the pass would open, and the delegation would go over it.

And Sada’s hearing before the Crown Council was in six days.

She sat in the tent that evening with the lamp and the journal and looked at the six days for a long time.

“We go round,” said the elder of the two old clerks. He had been with her seven years and wanted very badly to go home, and he said it as a man says a thing he knows will be refused. “By Debre Fel. It’s twelve days round by Debre Fel. We’d be six late.”

“And the hearing would go ahead without me,” said Sada. “Or it would be postponed, and the three who’ve been waiting nine years to ask me about Dessa would have another month to write their questions.” She did not look up. “No.”

“Then we wait.”

“And if it’s ten days, we’re four late, and it’s the same thing.”

Nobody said force the pass. Nobody needed to. The Ridge-Watch captain had closed it on the authority of the Crown, which was the authority Sada carried on the seal round her neck, and on the authority of a custom so old that nobody at the foot of the climb could say where it had come from. An envoy of the Crown who pushed a delegation over a pass that the Crown’s own Ridge-Watch had closed, in order to get to a hearing at which she was going to be asked whether she respected the Crown’s authority on the roads, would not need three people to stand up and ask her questions. She would have answered all of them already.

Riarhin sat on a chest by the tent-flap and watched Sada think.

She had watched her think at Gedo and at Dessa and at Harbu, and she had begun to be able to see it: the stillness, the pen not moving, the eyes on the page without reading it. It was a long, careful, patient kind of thinking, and it did not go round a thing. It went through it, slowly, to the other side.

“The captain,” Sada said at last. “What did he actually say?”

Tesfaye looked at his notes.

“That nobody crosses while the wind is on the pass.”

“Nobody. Not nothing. Not no traffic. Nobody.” Sada put the pen down. “Ask him if anybody’s ever crossed while it was blowing.”

The captain came down to the tent in the morning and drank two cups of coffee and thought about the question for a long time.

Yes, he said, in the end. Once in his memory, and three or four times in his father’s. Couriers, in the old days. A royal messenger, once, in the war. They had not gone over the notch. There was an older way, the courier’s way, that went up a gully on the east side of the face and over the ridge by a saddle half a mile from the tower. It was steep and bad and nobody used it, and it had been built for men on foot with nothing to carry, and when the wind with no name was on the pass the courier’s way was out of it, mostly, because the saddle was sheltered by a shoulder of the mountain.

It was not permitted. It was not forbidden either. It was a thing that had been done, when the Crown had needed a thing to go over the mountain and the mountain had not wanted it to.

“And who decides,” said Sada, “whether the Crown needs it?”

The captain looked at her over his cup. It was a long look.

“The Ridge-Watch,” he said, through Tesfaye. “At the tower. Me.”

What Sada asked for was this, and she asked for it the way she had asked at Gedo, which was all at once and plainly, with nothing kept back.

Not the delegation. Not the forty mules and the eight Ridge-Watchers and the clerks and the chests. Those would wait at the foot of the climb until the wind dropped and the pass opened, with Gebre in charge, and would come over in the ordinary way.

A small party. Herself, because she had to stand at the hearing. Tesfaye, with the fair copies of the agreement and the precedent clause from Ayna, because he had made them and could swear to them. Dawit, with the originals in the Crown’s dispatch case, because the originals had to be produced and because it was his case to carry, and he had carried it up a hill in Dessa and she wanted him to carry it over a mountain. The assessor, because the assessor’s report was going to be asked for. Two porters for what could not be left, and a Ridge-Watcher from the tower who knew the courier’s way. No mules. On foot, by the courier’s way, under the eye of the captain’s own men.

And before they went, one more thing. Sada sat down with Tesfaye in the tent and decided, document by document, what could be lost.

Riarhin watched them do it. It took an hour. The originals in the case could not be lost; there were no others. The fair copies Tesfaye had made at Mbaro could be lost, because the originals would get through or they would not, and if they did not, the fair copies would be no use by themselves. The precedent clause from Ayna could not be lost; Tesfaye made a second fair copy of it that night and gave it to Gebre to bring over with the train, and kept the first. Sada’s journal, with Dessa in it, could not be lost. The clerks’ working papers could all be lost. Everything was sorted into two piles. The porters would carry the second pile. If anything had to be dropped on the mountain, it would be the second pile first.

“You’ve done this before,” said Riarhin.

“I’ve never crossed a mountain in a wind with no name.” Sada tied the tape round the first pile. “I’ve done this before.”

The captain gave his answer at dawn. He would allow it.

He would allow it because the Crown needed its envoy at its Council, and because the envoy had come to him and asked instead of going round him, and because she had asked for a small party on foot by a way that had been used before, instead of a delegation over the notch; and because, he said, looking at the notch with the grey-white haze still pouring through it, he did not think the courier’s way would kill anybody who was careful. He did not say what he thought would happen to anybody who was not.

“He wants somebody to go ahead and look at it,” said Tesfaye. “Before the envoy goes up. His man knows the way. He wants somebody who isn’t his man to look at it too.”

Riarhin was already standing up.

She went up the courier’s way at first light with the captain’s man, a young Ridge-Watcher called Yared who was about twenty and moved on the scree like a goat, and she looked at it the way she had looked at the Gerlind’s hold.

It was bad. It was not as bad as the notch. It went up a gully on the east side of the face, a crack in the mountain full of broken rock and old ice, steep enough that they were on their hands half the time; and then out onto a ledge that ran along under a cliff for three hundred yards with nothing below it but air and the scree; and then up again, over a lip of rock, onto the saddle. The wind with no name was on the other side of the shoulder. She could hear it, a long thin whine that did not rise or fall, and once, crossing the ledge, she felt the edge of it come round the shoulder and touch her face, and it was so cold that her eyes watered and the water froze on her lashes.

Yared did not look at it. He put his head down and went on.

She did not look at it either. She looked at the rock. She looked at where the ledge was sound and where it had cracked. She looked at the old ice in the gully and which of it would take a boot and which would not, and at the lip of rock above the ledge and where the handholds were. She looked at the saddle. It was flat and sheltered, and on the far side of it the courier’s way went down a long easy slope of grass toward a valley. Far down the valley, at the end of it, in the blue distance, there was a city on a hill.

She had it all. She went down again and told Sada where every bad place was.

The fall happened on the ledge.

It was one of the porters, a man called Yonas, from Abdi’s people, about thirty, who had been carrying loads on this road since he was a boy and had never been on the courier’s way in his life. He was carrying the second pile, the working papers, in a bundle on his back. The ledge had a place about two-thirds of the way along where the rock had cracked and a slab had come away from the cliff face and was lying tilted across the path. Riarhin had seen it on the way up and had told everybody about it, and Yared had shown them where to put their feet, and five people had gone over it in the right place.

Yonas put his foot on the slab.

It moved. Not much. It tilted a hand’s breadth under his weight with a grinding sound, and he threw himself sideways against the cliff, which was the right thing to do, and the bundle on his back caught the rock and swung him out again, which nobody could have helped. His feet went off the edge of the ledge and he went down.

He did not go far. The scree below the ledge was steep but not sheer, and there was a knob of rock ten feet down, and he hit it and stopped. He lay there on his side with one leg under him at an angle a leg should not be at, and did not make any sound at all for a long moment, and then made a great deal.

It took them two hours to bring him up, and they did it together, and it cost them everything that was not in the first pile.

Riarhin went down. There was nobody else who could. She went over the edge of the ledge on a rope that Yared tied off round a horn of rock, and down the scree on her back with her heels driving, the way Varek had gone under the gate at Kessary, and got to Yonas and got the bundle off him, and it went down the mountain, all of it, the working papers of nine years in Mbaro, bouncing and spilling down the scree in the wind.

Nobody said anything about it. It was the second pile.

She got the rope round Yonas under the arms and tied it the way Varek’s sailors had tied her to the mast, and then Yared and the other porter and Tesfaye and Dawit hauled from the ledge while she went up beside him on the scree with her hands under his hip, keeping his leg from dragging. It took a long time. Sada stood on the ledge above them with the dispatch case in one hand and Tesfaye’s first pile in the other, because there was nobody else to hold them and they could not be put down, and watched.

When they had him on the ledge he was grey and sweating and conscious, and his leg was broken below the knee.

“He can’t walk,” said Yared.

“No,” said Riarhin.

They looked at the ledge in front of them, and the gully below, and the saddle above. It was three hundred yards to the lip of rock and the saddle. It was half a mile back down the gully to the tower.

“Up,” said Sada. “Over the saddle and down the grass. It’s easier going than the gully.”

“It’s further from anybody who can set the leg.”

“There’s a village at the foot of the grass. Yared says so. There’s a Covenant house with a sister in it who sets legs.” Sada looked at Yonas. “And there’s a Ridge-Watch post at the head of the valley, on the far side of the saddle, that can send down for her. And he’ll be carried on grass, not ice.”

She was right. Riarhin had seen the grass. She had seen the ice.

They carried him up over the lip and onto the saddle on a litter of two Ridge-Watch spears and Na’ar’s cloak.

Na’ar had not been meant to come. He had been meant to stay with the train and the mules at the foot of the climb and come over with them when the pass opened. He had come up the gully behind them anyway, an hour later, carrying nothing, having told Gebre that he wished to see the courier’s way; and he had reached the ledge while Riarhin was on the scree with Yonas. He had not said anything. He had taken off his cloak and laid it on the ledge and waited for the spears. And when Yonas was on it, he had taken the head end, and the other porter and Yared had taken the foot, and they had carried him over the worst of the ledge and up over the lip of rock onto the saddle, and down the first long easy slope of the grass on the far side toward the Ridge-Watch post, in the lee of the shoulder, with the wind with no name whining behind them on the other side of the mountain.

At the post there were two Ridge-Watchers and a hut and a fire, and one of them went down the valley at a run for the sister at the Covenant house.

And there, below them at the end of the valley, not two days away but one, in the late afternoon sun, was Adur-Gah.

Riarhin stood at the door of the hut and looked at it.

She could see it quite clearly. A city on a hill, the whole hill, with walls round the top of it and round the bottom of it and houses of pale stone climbing up between them in terraces, and at the very top the roofs of what must be the palace and the churches, three round roofs and a square one, catching the sun. There was smoke going up from it in a hundred threads. It was a long day’s walk down the valley. If they left now, they would be at the gates by the middle of the next afternoon.

The hearing was in four days. There was time. There was a great deal of time.

She wanted to go now.

She had got Sada over the mountain. She had got the dispatch case over the mountain and the precedent from Ayna and Tesfaye’s first pile. And there, at the end of the valley, was the city where her own undertaking was going to begin: the Council, and the question of the goat track, and whoever in Adur-Gah knew what a mark like a comb with a dot over it meant. She had been on somebody else’s road for fifty-two days. She could see the end of it.

Behind her, in the hut, Yonas was lying on Na’ar’s cloak by the fire with his leg splinted between two spear-hafts, grey and sweating, and Na’ar was sitting beside him giving him water a mouthful at a time.

“The sister will be here in the morning,” said Riarhin. “Yared says. She’ll set it, and they’ll take him down on a mule when the post can spare one.” She turned. “Na’ar. When she’s been, bring him down, and catch us up at—”

She stopped.

She heard it. She heard it the way she had heard her own voice at the canal-mothers’ bench in Mbaro, reading the grants aloud for a man in a red coat. She heard exactly how it sounded: a woman who had decided what everybody was going to do, and was telling the last of them, and had not asked.

Na’ar was looking up at her from beside the fire. He had not said anything. He was waiting, with a cup of water in his hand, with a very patient expression, to see if she would finish the sentence.

She did not finish it.

“I’m staying until the sister’s been,” said Na’ar. “And until he is on the mule. And then I will come.” He gave Yonas another mouthful of water. “You can go. It is your road.”

Riarhin stood in the doorway of the hut with the whole of Adur-Gah in the sunset behind her.

Then she came in and sat down on the other side of the fire.

Na’ar looked at her for a moment. He did not say anything, and his face did not change, and after a while he went back to the water.

The sister came up from the Covenant house in the morning on a mule, a tall stern woman of about fifty with a bag of splints and a flask of something that made Yonas sleep, and she set the leg in a quarter of an hour while Na’ar held him. It was a clean break. He would walk on it, the sister said, in three months, and carry loads again in six, if he was not a fool.

Yonas said, drowsily, that he was not a fool. The sister said she would be the judge of that.

The post spared a mule at noon, and Yonas went down the valley on it with the sister walking beside him, to the Covenant house, where he would stay until the train came over the pass and Abdi could take him home. He waved at them from the mule, once, drowsily. Na’ar lifted a hand.

Then the small party went down the valley after him, on foot, in the afternoon, and was a day late for nothing at all.

They came to the gate of Adur-Gah in the late afternoon of the fifty-fourth day.

It was the lower gate, in the lower wall, at the foot of the hill, and it was a great arch of pale stone with a square tower on either side and a road running up through it into the city in a long slow curve. There were people in the gateway, a great many of them, the way there are people in any gateway at the end of the day: carters and herders and a woman selling roasted barley from a tray. And among them, standing a little apart, a group of perhaps six men and women in white wraps with green and red borders, who had evidently been told by the Ridge-Watch post at the head of the valley that the envoy was coming down on foot, and had been standing there for some time.

They saw Sada and came forward.

Riarhin watched it from a pace behind her. She watched Sada’s back straighten in the way it had straightened at the Hall of Carriers in Mbaro, the first day, and at Gedo, and in the courtyard at Dessa.
