She watched her be greeted: an older man with a grey beard who took both her hands, and a woman of her own age who kissed her on both cheeks and then on the first cheek again, and a young clerk who took the dispatch case off Dawit with an expression of enormous relief. She watched them all begin to talk at once, and Sada answer them, and none of it was in any language Riarhin could follow.

They were her colleagues. They had arguments in progress that had been waiting for her for nine years. They had the Council in four days and a report of four hundred pages to go through and the three people who were going to stand up and ask about Dessa. They had a house for her somewhere up the hill, with her household in it, who had been waiting for her too. And none of them had the slightest interest in the foreign woman standing a pace behind her with a sword-shaped bundle on her back.

Sada turned round.

She did not come back. There were people all round her, talking, and a clerk with the dispatch case, and the greybeard still holding one of her hands. She looked at Riarhin over them, across perhaps six feet of gateway, for a long moment.

Then she said, in Ecktorian, clearly, over the talk: “The assessor’s lodged at the Covenant guest house by the lower market. Tesfaye will take you. I’ll send for your report.”

It was exactly right. It was what an envoy said to a contracted assessor at the end of a road, in front of her colleagues, at a gate. It was on the roll. It would be in the journal.

The corners of her mouth were not doing anything at all.

“Envoy,” said Riarhin, and bowed, in the form she had learned at Mbaro for a guest of the Crown.

Sada held her eyes a moment longer. Then the greybeard said something, and she turned away, and the whole group went off up the long curve of the road into the city, talking, in their white wraps, with the clerk carrying the dispatch case and Dawit trailing behind, and did not look back.

Riarhin stood in the gateway of Adur-Gah in the last of the afternoon with a woman selling roasted barley on one side of her and Na’ar on the other, and watched them go until the curve of the road took them.

It was the ordinary thing. She had known it would happen, and exactly how, since the courtyard in Mbaro. The envoy had a life here and the assessor did not. Neither of them had pretended otherwise at any point in fifty-four days. They had both known, at the relay house at Harbu, in the passage, that this was where the road ended; that was part of what they had both known.

She had not known it would feel like this. She had not known that she would stand in a gateway watching a white wrap go up a hill and find that it was difficult to breathe, and that the difficulty was not the altitude.

Tesfaye cleared his throat beside her.

“The Covenant guest house,” he said. “By the lower market. It’s this way.”

“Thank you, Tesfaye.”

He did not move for a moment. He was looking at her over his spectacles with an expression that she had seen on his face perhaps twice in fifty days, and that she would not have wanted to name.

“She’ll send for your report,” he said. “She said so.”

“I heard her.”

“She doesn’t say things,” said Tesfaye, “that she doesn’t do.”

He turned and went up into the lower market. After a moment Riarhin followed him, and Na’ar followed her, looking about at the city with his hands in his belt, with the frank and happy interest of a man who has come a long way to see some horses that climb like goats and is now, at last, in the right country for it.

Act IV: Taharka

Chapter Seventeen: Her Own Rooms

She wrote the report in three days, in a whitewashed room at the Covenant guest house with a window onto the lower market, and it was the most honest thing she had ever put her name to.

She had not expected that. She had expected it to be a performance: a piece of work done to fit the job she had been hired for, the way the lead-burning at Kessary had been done to fit the job she had been hired for. And she sat down on the first morning with Tesfaye’s paper and a borrowed pen, and began to write down what had happened on the road from Mbaro to Adur-Gah, and found that she could not make any of it fit anything. It had happened, and she had been there, and she had attended.

So she wrote it down.

The bridge at Gedo, and the award, and what one cart at a time would do to four hundred cartloads a month. The eastern road and the Ashaani at Tin Kerru, which she had not seen but had heard about from eleven men in a caravan house who had. The Workus at the ford. The contract at Dessa and what the agent on the coast had promised. The camp at the Dera ford, and eleven men who had counted a Crown escort correctly and a staging camp’s ground incorrectly. The relay house at Harbu and the scaffold that had been left to rot for two seasons because nobody had been paid to finish it. The courier’s way at Kolo Ber.

She wrote what she thought each of them meant for the agreement. She was not kind about it. She wrote that the grain article could not be met at Gedo for at least a season, and that the Council should expect to hear so from the Oshiiran side before it heard it from anybody else. She wrote that the border-traffic article would be tested first at Tin Kerru and that the Council should decide now what it meant to do when Taharkan carriers were held there, because they would be. She wrote that the relay houses on the courier road were the Crown’s and were falling into the river, and that somebody ought to pay to mend them before the next rains.

She did not write about Dessa. That was in Sada’s journal, and it was Sada’s.

On the third evening she read it through, all of it, once. Then she signed it, with her own name, and sat for a while looking at the signature.

Na’ar was not at the guest house much.

He had found the royal stables on the second day. They were on the far side of the hill, below the palace, in a long building of fitted stone with a yard in front of it, and in the yard there were horses in brass halters. They were small and deep-chested and very sure-footed, and they did, when the grooms took them out onto the steep ground behind the stables, climb like goats. He came back that first evening in a state of quiet exaltation that Riarhin had seen on him perhaps three times in the years she had known him, and sat on the end of her bed and described every one of them to her in detail, with his hands.

On the third day, the master of the royal stables, a vast grey man called Tamrat, asked him whether he would care to look at a mare that had gone lame in a way nobody could account for. He looked at her for an hour. On the fourth day he was helping with the evening feed. On the fifth he came back to the guest house to say that he would be sleeping at the stables for a few nights because the mare was due to foal, and Tamrat did not like the look of it, and neither did he.

“You don’t need me,” he said. “You are writing.”

“I’ve finished writing.”

“Then you are waiting.” He considered her. “You do not need me for waiting. Nobody does. It is the one thing a person does alone.”

He went off to the stables. She did not see him for three days, and when she did he smelled strongly of horse and was very pleased with himself, and the foal was a filly, and was standing.

Sada sent for the report on the fourth day.

It was not Sada who came. It was a boy in a white shawl with a folded paper, who waited in the doorway of the whitewashed room while Riarhin wrapped the report in oilcloth and gave it to him, and then took it away up the hill.

The paper said, in Sada’s hand, in Ecktorian: Thank you. I’ll read it tonight. Come and eat with us on the sixth day, in the evening. Tesfaye will bring you. — S.

That was all.

Riarhin read it several times. She did not need to. She sat on the bed in the whitewashed room with the paper on her knee and looked at the two words with us, and found that she was, for no good reason at all, afraid.

The house was halfway up the hill, on a street of pale stone houses behind walls, with a gate painted blue, and a eucalyptus tree in the courtyard that had been planted by Sada’s grandfather and had grown taller than the house.

It was full of people.

There was Sada’s aunt, who was the head of the household and about eighty and sat in the best chair and was addressed by everybody, including Sada, in a form that Riarhin did not know and Tesfaye did not translate. There was Sada’s younger sister, Tigist, who was a physician at the royal hospital and had her sister’s face and none of her patience, and three children who belonged to Tigist and ran in and out of the courtyard all evening in a state of high excitement that had nothing to do with anybody at the table. There was a cousin who was a deacon at the old Covenant church and had views about the Pereshi delegation. There was Tigist’s husband, who was a carpenter and did not say very much and laughed at everything the deacon said. And there was the woman who had kissed Sada three times at the gate, whose name was Hiwot, and who was a colleague from the Crown’s chancery and had evidently known Sada since they were both girls, and who looked at Riarhin once, very carefully, when she came in, and then passed her the bread.

And there was Sada, in a plain blue dress with her hair uncovered, sitting on a low stool by her aunt’s chair, arguing with Tigist about something to do with the children’s school.

She looked up when Riarhin came in, and smiled. It was not the thing her mouth did at the corners. It was a whole smile, brief and ordinary, the smile of a woman at home in her own house who is glad somebody has come.

Then she went back to arguing with her sister.

Nobody asked Riarhin what she was.

She had been ready for it. She had been ready for it all the way up the hill. She had been at tables before where she was the thing everybody wanted to look at, and she had been at tables where she was the thing nobody wanted to look at, and she knew how to be both. She had half a dozen answers for where are you from and a dozen for what do you do, and she had come up the hill with all of them ready.

Nobody asked.

The aunt asked whether the Covenant guest house was comfortable, and whether they were feeding her properly, because they had not fed Sada properly when she had lodged there as a girl. Tigist asked whether the altitude had given her headaches, and told her what to do about it if it did, in the tone of a physician who has told a great many people and expects to be ignored. The deacon asked whether she had seen the old church yet, and was disappointed that she had not, and told her when the evening office was sung. The carpenter asked nothing at all and passed her the lentils.

And the children, at one point, ran up to the table all three together and stood in a row and looked at her, and the eldest, who was about eight, asked her something very fast in the highland tongue.

Tesfaye cleared his throat.

“She asks,” he said, “whether it’s true that your friend is at the royal stables, and whether he’s the one who got the foal out.”

Riarhin looked at the child. The child looked back.

“Yes,” said Riarhin. “He is.”

The three children looked at each other with enormous satisfaction and ran off again.

That was the whole of it. That was all anybody at the table wanted to know about her. They were kind. They were kind in the ordinary way of a household that has people to supper often and makes room for them, and was glad she had come, and would be glad if she came again, and was not in the least fascinated by her. The house was busy. The work in it was not hers. There were arguments going on at the table that had been going on for years and would go on after she left, about the children’s school and the Pereshi and the aunt’s hip, and nobody explained any of them to her, and after a while she stopped waiting to have them explained.

She sat on a cushion at the end of the table with the grey bread in her fingers and listened to a family she did not know arguing in a language she mostly did not speak, and it was the most restful evening she had spent in a long time.

The coffee came at the end, and it was Tigist who made it, in the corner, over charcoal: green beans roasted in an iron pan until they cracked and smoked, and carried round the table for everybody to smell before they were ground; and then boiled, and poured, three times.

Riarhin had learned the three by now. The first cup was the strongest. The second was weaker, from the same grounds, and was the one you must not refuse. The third was the weakest, and was the blessing, and you drank it and then you could go.

Tigist poured the first cup, and the second. The aunt was talking about the Pereshi. The deacon was agreeing with her very loudly. Hiwot was telling a long tale about somebody at the chancery that was making the carpenter laugh until he wept.

Sada, on her stool by her aunt’s chair, was not talking. She was holding her second cup in both hands, and looking across the table at Riarhin over it, and she had been looking for some time.

Riarhin looked back.

Neither of them said anything. There was a whole table between them, and a family, and the Pereshi. It did not seem to matter very much.

Tigist poured the third cup.

Sada walked her to the gate afterwards. It was dark. The eucalyptus was full of wind.

They stood in the gateway for a while without saying anything. It was the gateway of Sada’s own house, with her aunt and her sister and her sister’s children twenty yards away behind a lit window, and Tesfaye standing politely out in the street with a lantern, looking at the sky.

“I read your report,” said Sada.

“And?”

“It’s very good. It’s much better than I wanted. Half the Council is going to hate it.” She leaned against the gatepost. “The half that doesn’t is going to use it against the other half. And I’m going to stand in the middle and be quoted by both.”

“I’m sorry.”

“You’re not. Neither am I.” Sada looked at her in the dark. “You didn’t put Dessa in.”

“It’s in your journal.”

“It is.” A pause. “Thank you.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“The hearing’s in two days,” said Sada. “You’ll be called. They’ll want the assessor to swear to the report and answer questions. Most of the questions will be stupid. Some of them won’t be.” She straightened up off the gatepost. “And there’s something else, and I said I’d tell you to your face first, so I’m telling you.”

She told her in the gateway, quietly, with Tesfaye’s lantern out in the street and the eucalyptus moving overhead, and she did not leave anything out.

She had spoken to the warden of the northern passes. His name was Dejen and he had been a Ridge-Watch captain for twenty years and was now the officer of the Crown who answered to the Council for everything that happened on the northern side of Adur-Gah: the grazing, the herders’ disputes, the watchtowers, the passes into the high country, and the goat track. She had not told him everything. She had told him that the assessor on her delegation had found evidence, in a sanctuary at Ayna and in papers out of Mbaro, that the goat track had once been part of an old road with waystations on it, going north into the Wastes, maintained by an office older than the Crown; and that the assessor believed a consignment had gone up that road eighty years ago, carrying an object that had been deliberately hidden, and that she meant to follow it.

“He asked me what the object was,” said Sada.

“What did you say?”

“I said I’d been told a word, and that the person who told me couldn’t say what it meant, and that nobody who’d ever claimed to know what it meant had ever held it.” Sada did not look away. “I didn’t say the word. I told him he’d have to ask you. He’s going to.”

“And what does he want?”

“What anybody would want.” Sada said it plainly. “He’s the warden of the north. There are four thousand herders on the high grazing past the goat track. There are six watchtowers and two Covenant houses and a salt road. And somebody wants to take a party up an old road nobody’s used in two hundred years, into country nobody’s been to on purpose for longer than that, to find something that four sets of people spent eighty years hiding. And then bring it back down the same road, through his country, past his herders, into his Crown’s capital.” She paused. “He wants to know what happens if it’s dangerous. He wants to know what happens if the road turns out to be good, and you write it down, and next year there are fifty parties on it with maps, all of them looking for green glass. And he wants to know what happens if you find it and sell it to somebody in Mbaro on your way home.” She was quiet a moment. “And after that. He said to me, and I’m giving you his words, that he’d rather it were looked at and declared and then carried a long way from his passes by somebody who’ll put their name to where it went. He doesn’t want it sold. He doesn’t want it kept here either.”

Riarhin stood in the dark of the gateway for a long moment.

“Those are good questions,” she said.

“They’re the only questions.” Sada put her hand on the gatepost. “I’ve recommended something. To him, and I’ll recommend it to the Council at the end of the hearing, if they let me. I want you to hear it before they do, so that if you want to say no, you can say it to me and not to them.”

It was a survey commission. A limited one.

The Crown would commission a survey of the old road north of Adur-Gah, from the goat track to wherever it ended, under the authority of the warden of the north. The survey would be led by whoever the Crown appointed, with whoever they chose to take, and would be supplied through the warden’s stages and watered at the warden’s wells as far as those went. Past that, it was the survey’s own business.

And there were conditions.

The route would be recorded, fully, and the record would belong to the Crown and to the survey jointly, and would not be published by either without the other’s leave. Anything recovered on the road or at the end of it would be declared, truthfully and in full, to the Crown’s representative on the survey, and written down. Nothing recovered could be sold or given away or taken out of Taharka until it had been examined in Adur-Gah and the Council had reviewed what should be done with it. And whatever the Council then decided, whether to keep it or destroy it or release it into somebody’s declared custody to be carried out of the kingdom, would be written down with the name of whoever held it and the place it was going.

“And the Crown’s representative?” said Riarhin.

Sada looked at her.

“I’ve recommended,” she said, “that it should be somebody who already knows the evidence, and has read the assessor’s report, and has been party to the negotiations on the northern traffic, and has standing with the warden and the Council both.” She did not smile. “There aren’t very many of those.”

Riarhin walked back down the hill to the guest house with Tesfaye and his lantern, and did not say anything the whole way, and Tesfaye did not either.

She could say no. Sada had made sure she could. She could go to the warden of the north and say that she did not want a commission and did not want conditions, and that she would find her own way up the goat track, and she thought, from what she had seen of the Ridge-Watch at Kolo Ber, that she would find it very hard going and that nobody would stop her. She could go back to Mbaro and try the eastern road after all, past Tin Kerru, with the Ashaani writing her name in a book. She could go anywhere. She had been going anywhere for forty years.

Or she could take the Crown’s terms.

The terms were not bad. That was the difficulty. She had turned them over the whole way down the hill looking for the catch, the way she would turn over any offer, and the catch was not hidden anywhere. It was in plain sight, in the conditions, and it was exactly what it looked like. The Crown would know where she went. The Crown would know what she found. The Crown would decide what happened to it. And in return the Crown would give her stages and wells and a warden’s leave to go through his country, and a road that had been walked by his herders for two hundred years without anybody knowing what it was. And it would give her Sada, beside her, as the Crown’s representative, who would write everything down and tell the Council all of it, and would tell her so to her face first.

She did not want to be written down. She had never wanted it. She had spent forty years arranging not to be.

She wanted to find it more than she wanted not to be written down.

She stopped in the lower market, which was dark and shuttered and smelled of roasted barley, and stood there for a while with Tesfaye waiting patiently beside her with the lantern.

That was the first time she had known it, all the way down. That there was a thing she wanted more than the freedom to leave it.

“Tesfaye,” she said.

“Yes?”

“Will you tell the envoy something for me? In the morning.”

“Of course.”

“Tell her,” said Riarhin, “that I’ll take the terms.”

The hearing was two days later, in a long hall at the top of the hill with a roof of dark beams and a floor of worn stone, and it went on for nine hours.

Riarhin was called in the fifth. She stood at a low table in front of a long curved bench where eleven members of the Crown Council sat, in white wraps with borders of different colours, and swore to her report, and answered questions.

Most of them were stupid. Sada had been right about that. An old man with a gold border asked her whether the Oshiiran carriers at Gedo had been properly deferential to the Crown’s envoy, and she said that they had been properly deferential to the award, which was a hundred and forty years old and had been made by a Taharkan king, and he seemed satisfied. A younger man asked whether the Ashaani at Tin Kerru were a threat to Taharkan sovereignty, and she said that she had not been to Tin Kerru and could only report what eleven carriers had told her, and that the carriers had seemed less concerned with sovereignty than with the four days’ wages they had lost, and he seemed less satisfied.

Some of the questions were not stupid.

A woman with a red border, sharp-faced and about sixty, asked her whether, in her assessment, the grain article could be met within the year. Riarhin said no. The woman asked her why not, and Riarhin told her about the pier at Gedo, and the coffer, and the one cart at a time, and the woman wrote it down, and then asked her what she would recommend. Riarhin said that she would recommend the Crown pay for the pier. The woman asked her how much. Riarhin told her what Ato Mesfin had told her it would cost. The woman wrote that down too, and looked at it for a long time, and said, “Thank you,” and asked nothing else.

It was not a speech. Riarhin did not make one. She answered what she was asked, and she did not say anything she had not seen or been told, and when she did not know a thing she said so. She did not rescue anybody. She did not rescue Sada.

Sada did not need rescuing.

Sada was called in the seventh hour, and stood at the same table for two.

A man with a green border stood up and read the entry about Dessa from her journal, in full, in a clear carrying voice, and then asked her whether she considered that her staff had been properly supervised during nine years in Mbaro. Sada said that she considered that her staff had been supervised by her, and that a junior envoy had behaved disgracefully under her supervision, and that she had written it down, and that the Council was welcome to judge her supervision by that, as she had. The man asked whether she would not agree that nine years was too long for an envoy to be away from Adur-Gah. Sada said that she had been saying so herself for four years, in writing, and that the Council’s replies were in the chancery if he would like to read them.

The hall laughed. The man sat down.

Then they went through the agreement, article by article, and it took the rest of the day. They approved the grain article, and ordered the pier at Gedo paid for out of the Crown’s road fund; the sharp-faced woman with the red border moved it. They sent the border-traffic article back, and told Sada to renegotiate the Tin Kerru clause before the dry season. They approved the precedent from Ayna, and the old man with the gold border was so pleased to learn that the originals in the royal archive were copies of the copies at Ayna that he asked Tesfaye to repeat it twice. And they required three changes to the annexes on pack transport, which Sada had expected, and one more, which she had not, and which she accepted with a face of perfect stillness.

It was not a victory. It was a hearing. Some of it went her way and some of it did not, and at the end of nine hours she was still the envoy, with a report accepted in part and a task to go back to Mbaro in the dry season and do half of it again.

At the very end, when the light in the high windows had gone gold and the Council was beginning to shift on its bench, the warden of the north stood up at the side of the hall where he had been sitting all day, and asked to be heard on a separate matter.

He was a lean grey man of about fifty with a Ridge-Watch horn at his hip, and he spoke for a quarter of an hour, plainly, without any ornament. He said that a survey of the old road north of the goat track had been proposed. He said what it was for, and what the conditions were, and whose they were. He said that he supported it, on the conditions. And he said that he asked the Council to appoint as the Crown’s representative on the survey the envoy Sada Alemu, on the grounds that she had the evidence and the standing and had already been party to the matter, and that he could think of nobody else who had all three.

The sharp-faced woman with the red border asked whether the envoy was not needed in Mbaro in the dry season.

The warden said that the dry season was four months away, and that he did not expect the survey to take longer than that, and that if it did, the Council would have other things to worry about than the Tin Kerru clause.

Nobody laughed at that.

They voted. It was eight to three. The man with the green border was one of the three.

Riarhin found her afterwards, outside the hall, on the steps, in the last of the gold light, sitting on the top step with her white wrap round her and her chin on her knees and her eyes closed.

She sat down beside her.

“Eight to three,” she said.

“Eight to three.” Sada did not open her eyes. “I’ve got a pier at Gedo and a clause to renegotiate and three changes to the annexes and a fourth I didn’t want. I’ve been told I’m too long away and I’ve been sent away again. And I’ve been appointed to go up a road nobody’s used in two hundred years with a woman I met in a pack-animal allotment room.” She opened her eyes. “My aunt is going to be furious.”

“Is she?”

“She’s going to say I’ve been nine years in Mbaro and a month at home and now I’m going up a goat track.” Sada looked out over the city, going gold and then grey below them. “She’ll be right.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“You took the terms,” said Sada, after a while.

“I took the terms.”

“You know what they are. I’ll write it all down. I’ll tell the Council all of it.”

“I know.”

“And you still want to go.”

“I still want to go.” Riarhin looked at her own hands. “I want to go more than I want not to be written down. I didn’t know that until two nights ago, in the lower market. I’ve never wanted anything more than I wanted to be able to leave it.”

Sada turned her head and looked at her. It was a long look, in the grey light, on the steps of the Council hall, with the clerks going past below them carrying chests.

She did not say anything about it. She reached across and took Riarhin’s hand, where it lay on the step, and held it; and they sat there together while the light went out of the sky over Adur-Gah and the lamps came on in the city below, one by one, all the way down the hill.

Chapter Eighteen: Two People Who Say No

Meret did not believe a word of it, and said so in the first quarter of an hour, and Riarhin liked her at once.

She was the warden’s choice. Dejen had named her the morning after the hearing, sitting in his office in the northern gatehouse with a Ridge-Watch horn on the table in front of him and a map of the high grazing on the wall behind him that stopped, very firmly, a finger’s breadth past the last watchtower. He had said that the survey would need somebody who could read old stone and old waterworks, and that there was one person in Adur-Gah who could do both, and that she would almost certainly refuse.

“Why?”

“Because she’s been asked before,” said Dejen. “By people with maps.” He looked at Riarhin across the table. “She’ll want to see yours.”

She lived in two rooms at the back of the royal archive, which was a long low building below the palace full of cedar chests and the smell of old vellum, and she was about forty, small and wiry, with close-cropped greying hair and ink on her fingers to the second joint, and a pair of spectacles pushed up onto her forehead that she did not seem to know were there.

And there was a lizard on her threshold.

It was a small thing, the length of a finger, stone-grey, with a blunt head and round bronze eyes and toes like tiny leaves, and it was lying flat on the stone sill of the door between her two rooms with its chin on the step. It did not move when Riarhin came in. It turned one bronze eye toward her and looked at her for a long moment and then turned it away again.

“That’s Sill,” said Meret, without looking up from the table where she was working. “Don’t step on him. He won’t move.”

“Does he ever?”

“In the evening. He likes the lamp.” Meret put her pen down. “He sleeps on thresholds. Any threshold. If there’s a door in the room, he’s in it. He was in the doorway of the Council hall for the whole of your hearing, if you want to know, and nobody noticed him at all.” She pushed back her chair and looked at Riarhin for the first time. “Dejen says you’ve got papers.”

She read them at her table under the window with her spectacles pulled down onto her nose, all of them, slowly: the nine leaves, and Hawa’s bill of carriage, and the copy of the inscription from Ayna and the old brother’s reading, and the judge’s finding about the carters. She did not say anything while she read. Every so often she turned a page back and read it again. Once she took a glass out of a drawer and looked at the comb-and-dot mark on the ninth leaf for a long time through it.

Then Riarhin told her the word, and the paragraph, and what Kargath had paid for it.

Meret listened to all of that with her spectacles pushed back up onto her forehead and her hands folded on the table. When Riarhin had finished, she was quiet for a while.

“Varash,” she said.

“That’s what most people think.”

“I know it’s what most people think. The Esoiti think it. The relic trade thinks it. There was a man from Sekogo through here four years ago who wanted to borrow the archive’s Himadri papers, and he thought it; he wanted to know if we had anything on a green-glass nkisi that ought to have a nail driven into it.” She tapped the table. “None of them have held it. None of them have read anything but that paragraph. And the paragraph doesn’t say demon. It says a wish with a clause. Somebody put those two together because the thing came out of the Himadri and Varash is under the Himadri, and the relic trade puts everything that comes out of the Himadri under Varash, because it sells.”

“So it isn’t Varash.”

“I didn’t say that.” Meret looked at her over the table. “I said nobody has any evidence that it is. Those are different. I don’t know what it is. You don’t know what it is. You’ve got a word and a paragraph and eighty years of people cutting things out of books. That isn’t a patron. It isn’t a demon. It isn’t anything. It’s a gap with a rumour in it.” She pulled her spectacles down again. “I’m not going to tell you it’s safe. I’m not going to tell you it’s dangerous. I’m going to tell you I don’t know, and that anybody who says they do is selling something.”

Riarhin looked at her for a long moment.

“Will you come?” she said.

Meret did not answer at once. She took the copy of the Ayna inscription and laid it beside the ninth leaf and looked at the two marks together, the one cut in stone and the one made with a pen, for a long time.

“There’s a survey,” she said at last. “In this archive. Of the northern passes. It was made forty years ago by a Crown surveyor called Wolde, and it was never finished, because he died on the way back. It stops at the last watchtower. It says, in his hand, on the last page, that beyond the watchtower the goat track continues north as a herders’ path without works of any kind.” She tapped the table. “That survey has been cited in every dispute about the northern grazing for forty years. The warden’s office cites it. The herders’ headmen cite it. There’s a salt house in Mbaro that cites it in a claim on the northern salt road, because if there are no works north of the watchtower, then there’s no Taharkan road there, and if there’s no Taharkan road there, the salt road isn’t Taharkan either.”

“And there are works.”

“There’s a waystation three days north of the last village with a church, with dressed stone and an inscription with an office’s seal on it, and a judge’s finding sixty years old that says so, and the stone’s in the wall of a church at Ayna.” Meret looked at her. “Wolde never went three days north of anything. He turned back at the watchtower because his mules were lame, and wrote without works of any kind because it was late and he wanted to go home. And forty years of lawyers have been reading it as though he’d walked to the end.”

She took off her spectacles and rubbed her eyes.

“I want it corrected,” she said. “Properly. With measurements. With every waystation drawn and every inscription copied and every mark recorded where it was found, before anybody moves anything. I want a survey that will still be sitting in this archive in forty years, and that nobody can misread because it was done badly.” She put the spectacles back on. “That’s why I’d come. Not for your green glass.”

“I understand.”

“I don’t think you do yet.” Meret folded her hands. “Here are my conditions.”

There were two.

She would document every inscription and every mark and every work of any kind that the survey found, fully, in place, before anything was moved, dismantled, lifted or disturbed. Not afterwards. Not if there was time. Before. If that meant the survey waited a day at a waystation while she drew a wall, then the survey waited a day.

“And the second,” said Meret, “is that I don’t open anything I think is shut for a reason.”

Riarhin waited.

“I’m a Runekeeper,” said Meret. She said it without any ceremony at all, the way another woman would have said she was left-handed. “Sworn to the Sealed Gate. You know what that is?”

“I know what it’s called in Aeler. And in the Mistlands.”

“It’s the same thing everywhere. It’s the one that decides what crosses and what doesn’t.” Meret reached into the neck of her dress and drew out a chain. It was a short chain of iron rings, each ring about the size of a thumbnail, dull and dark, and some of them had marks cut into them and some did not. “That’s what I carry instead of a book. Every ring’s a crossing. A way in, a way out, a door that was shut on something, a door that was opened. Once a week I test every one of them.” She let the chain fall back inside her dress. “I can tell you a great deal about a door. I can tell you whether it was shut to keep something in or out, and sometimes by whom, and sometimes whether it still means it. I can’t tell you what’s on the other side. I’ve never been able to do that and nobody I’ve ever met who serves the Gate can do it either.”

“And if you find a door you think was shut for a reason?”

“Then I say so, and I say why, and I don’t open it.” Meret looked at her very steadily. “And you don’t either. Not while I’m on the survey. Not with my help, and not round me. If you want somebody who’ll open anything you point at, go and find somebody from the relic trade. They’re very good at it. They’re mostly dead.”

Riarhin thought about a room under the rock at Kessary, and a stone thing on a plinth that had been wet with nothing, and an iron gate coming down on a chain.

“Agreed,” she said.

Meret looked at her a moment longer, as though she had expected to have to argue for it and was faintly disappointed.

“Good,” she said. “Then I’ll come.”

On the threshold between the two rooms, the lizard turned its head and looked at them both with its round bronze eye, and then put its chin back down on the step.

Kebede would not quote a price.

He was the warden’s second choice, and Sada’s first. She had known him for twenty years. He was a route surveyor and transport contractor who had spent his life taking people over the highland passes and down onto the salt road and back, and he had brought home three parties that should never have gone out, and had been blamed by all three of them for the parts that had gone wrong. He was about fifty-five, square and heavy and slow-moving, with a grey beard cut short and very small, very pale eyes in a brown face, and he kept his office in a stone shed at the north gate with a yard behind it full of pack saddles and water-skins and broken things waiting to be mended.

He looked at Riarhin’s list for a long time. It was the list she had drawn up in the guest house, carefully, over three evenings: animals, water, food, fodder, tents, tools, rope, oil, the whole of an expedition from the goat track to eleven days beyond the last water, costed. She had been rather proud of it.

He put it down on his table.

“No,” he said.

“No to what?”

“No to all of it.” He did not say it unkindly. He said it the way Abdi had said the Crown’s mules are too fat at the supper under the tamarind in Mbaro, as a plain fact about the world. “This is a list for getting there. You’ve got eleven days out from the last water, and water for eleven days, and fodder for eleven days, and a margin of two. You’ve got nothing for coming back.”

“I’ve got water for coming back. It’s in the—”

“You’ve got water for coming back the same way you went, at the same speed, with the same animals, all of them alive.” Kebede leaned back in his chair. “Nobody ever comes back the same way they went. You’ll be slower. You’ll be carrying something, or somebody. You’ll have lost animals. You’ll have been held up a day somewhere by something you couldn’t see from here, and then another day by something else. You’ll have one person who can’t walk and three who are carrying him.” He tapped the list. “I’ve brought three parties home from the salt road who’d made a list like this. Every one of them had water for eleven days out and eleven days back. Every one of them came back in nineteen, carrying two, and I was the one who went out with the water.”

He went through the list with a stick of charcoal, line by line, and it took all afternoon, and by the end of it there was very little of Riarhin’s list left.

He struck out every tool that did the same work as another tool. Two kinds of spade: one. Three kinds of rope: two, but more of each. Four lamps: two, and the oil for four. He struck out the tents she had chosen, which were good tents for the highlands and would be ovens in the Wastes, and wrote in lower ones of a different cloth. He struck out a quantity of dried meat and wrote in dried dates and a hard barley bread the highland herders carried, which kept longer and needed less water to eat.

He struck out the camels.

“Not for the climb,” he said. “Camels die on scree. They’re flatland animals. The goat track goes up two days before it comes down. You’ll want mules up, and horses for the riders, highland horses, the royal stable’s kind if Tamrat will let you have them. And the camels go round by the salt road and meet you at the bottom, at the last station.” He wrote something. “That’s a week’s hire for camels standing still. It’s cheaper than a camel with a broken leg halfway up the goat track and a load to carry on your backs.”

He wrote in things she had not thought of. Repair materials: leather, thread, awls, spare buckles, tar for the skins, spare girths, a small bag of iron nails and a hammer. Spare containers: not for water, but empty, folded, because a skin that fails with water in it needs somewhere for the water to go. Poles for a litter. A splint kit, and a physician’s bag, which Tigist would fill if Sada asked her.

And a station.

“Here,” he said, and put the charcoal on the map where the goat track came down out of the highlands onto the edge of the Wastes, beside a mark that Dejen had drawn in the warden’s office and labelled with the name of the last well the herders used. “Before you go into the unwatered country. A camp. Staffed. People who stay there the whole time you’re gone, with water and fodder and the spare animals and the splint kit. People who know how long you’re meant to be, and how long after that to wait, and how far out to go looking, and when to stop looking and go home.” He looked up at her. “And you tell them what you’ll look like when you’re coming back. What signs you’ll make. Where you’ll be if you’re not where you said.”

Riarhin looked at the mark on the map.

She had spent forty years going places where nobody knew she was going, and coming back from them before anybody noticed she was gone. She had never once in her life told anybody where to come and look for her.

“And one more thing,” said Kebede.

The one more thing was a paper, and it took Sada two days to draw up, and it was the thing Riarhin hated most in the whole of the preparations, and she signed it.

It gave Kebede the authority to halt the march.

Not the authority to lead it. That was Riarhin’s; the warden had written her name on the commission as its leader, and Kebede had not asked for it and did not want it. The authority to stop it. If the water carried fell below a line that Kebede would set before they left, measured in the actual skins, at the actual rate of use; if the animals fell below a condition that he would set before they left, measured by him, at the end of each day; then he could say stop, and the march would stop, and turn round, and go back to the station, whatever anybody else wanted.

Whatever Riarhin wanted.

“You’re not going to like that,” said Kebede, when she read it.

“No.”

“Good. It’s not for you to like. It’s for the day when you can see the thing you’ve come for on the horizon, and the water’s below the line, and you want to go on anyway.” He took the paper back from her. “That day always comes. On every march I’ve ever been on. Somebody always wants to go on. And the reason I’ve brought three parties home is that on that day, I was the one who said stop, and it wasn’t my decision to argue with, because it was written down before we left.”

She looked at him.

“Why do you do it?” she said. “If they always blame you.”

Kebede thought about it. He had small pale eyes and they did not blink very often.

“Because they come home,” he said. “And because every time somebody comes back from a road like this one, they write about it, and the next year there are three parties on it who’ve read what they wrote and not what it cost.” He put the paper in a drawer. “I want the conditions written down with the route. How much water. How many animals. Where the station was and why. So the next people who read it read that too.”

The last thing was hiring, and it was done in the open, and it took a week.

They needed people. The five of them, Riarhin and Na’ar and Sada and Meret and Kebede, could not carry the water for the station, or the fodder, or drive forty mules up the goat track and bring them back, or keep a camp at the last well for however long it took. They needed drivers and carriers and a cook for the station and a physician’s helper who could set a leg, and herders who knew the country past the watchtowers, and two of the warden’s Ridge-Watchers to carry the Crown’s authority as far as the Crown had any.

Kebede hired them. He did it at a table in the yard behind his shed, with a clerk from the warden’s office writing everything down, and every one of them was told the same thing: where the expedition was going, as far as anybody knew; what it was looking for, which was an old road and whatever was at the end of it; how long it would be, as far as anybody knew; what they would be paid, and when; and that they would be staying at the station and would not be going into the unwatered country at all, unless it was to come looking for the five who had.

Some of them said no. Three drivers said no. A herder’s son from the high grazing, who had been offered the post of guide as far as the last well, listened to the whole of it and then said that his grandmother would not want him on that road, and would not say why, and went home. Kebede let him go without a word and wrote in the next name.

The ones who said yes, said yes knowing it. Riarhin watched them sign their marks or their names, one after another, at the table in the yard, and found that she knew every one of their faces, and could not remember having decided to attend to them.

They measured the water on the ninth day.

Kebede would not take anybody’s word for how much a skin held. He had the skins brought out, the actual skins that would go, forty of them, new and tarred; and he had them filled at the north gate cistern, one at a time, from a measure, and counted, and weighed full on a beam he had borrowed from the salt house, and weighed again empty. Four of them leaked. He had them taken apart and restitched and tarred again and filled again. Two of them still leaked. He threw them out.

Then he had the ones that did not leak hung up in the sun for a day, full, and weighed again in the evening, to see what they lost.

It was a great deal. More than Riarhin had allowed on her list. More than she would have guessed, standing in the yard in the late afternoon with Kebede’s beam and a row of fat grey skins sweating in the sun.

“That’s what the sun takes,” said Kebede. “Here. In the highlands. It’ll take more out there.” He wrote the number down. “We’ll carry for that.”

On the tenth day they walked the first day of the route.

Not with the animals. The five of them, and two of the drivers, and one of Dejen’s Ridge-Watchers, on foot, up the goat track from the last village with a church, as far as a day’s walk would take them and back, with a surveyor’s chain and a pair of Meret’s drawing boards and nothing else. Kebede wanted to see the going. Meret wanted to see if there was anything on the first day that Wolde had missed. Riarhin wanted to see the road.

It was a bad road. It was a goat track. It went up out of the high grazing through a country of heather and grey rock and small cold streams, very steep in places, with the wind coming over the ridges from the north carrying a smell she did not know, something dry and hot that did not belong in the highlands at all. There were no waystations on the first day. There was nothing that anybody would call a work.

Except that at about the fourth hour, where the track crossed a stream on a place of flat stones, Meret stopped and knelt down and looked at the stones for a long time. Then she took out a knife and scraped at the moss between two of them, and sat back on her heels.

“Those were laid,” she said.

They were. Now that she had said it, Riarhin could see it: the stones were not a natural ford at all but a line of flat slabs set across the stream on edge, with smaller stones packed between them, like the paving of a road. They had been there so long that the moss and the stream had taken them back. Wolde had crossed them. Wolde had not looked down.

Meret drew them. It took an hour. Kebede sat on a rock and watched her do it with his small pale eyes, and did not say anything about the time, and when she had finished he got up and walked a hundred yards further up the track, and came back, and said that the going beyond the stream was worse than he had thought, and that the mules would need an extra half-day for the first stage, and that he was changing the plan.

He changed it that night, in his shed, with the charcoal. An extra half-day. An extra day’s fodder at the station. A different place for the first camp.

Riarhin sat and watched him do it and did not argue with a single line.

On the evening of the eleventh day, Na’ar came to the guest house from the stables with two horses on a lead.

They were small and deep-chested, one bay and one dun, in brass halters, and they stood in the lane outside the guest house and looked at her with the calm, interested, slightly superior expression of animals who have been told they are going somewhere and have views on it.

“Tamrat says,” said Na’ar, “that these two will climb anything. And that if they come back lame he will have my ears.” He stroked the dun’s nose. “He says also that the man who brought the foal out may take the mare’s sister’s colt when it is weaned, if he wants it. And that he would not want a man like me to go into the Wastes and not come back, because he has not yet finished arguing with me about feet.”

Riarhin looked at him. He had a line of dried blood across the back of one hand where something had bitten him, and straw in his hair, and the frank uncomplicated happiness of a man who has found, in a strange country, some other men who know about horses and do not wish him to die.

“Are you coming?” she said.

He looked at her in some surprise.

“You are asking me,” he said. “Not telling.”

“I’m asking.”

Na’ar considered this with great seriousness, holding the lead of two highland horses in the lane outside the Covenant guest house in the last of the evening light.

“Yes,” he said. “I am coming.” He paused. “Thank you for asking.”

Chapter Nineteen: A Visitor with Another Answer

The man in the long coat was in Meret’s reading room when Riarhin came in on the fourteenth morning, and Sill was asleep on the toe of his boot.

That was the first thing she noticed. The lizard slept on thresholds; it did not sleep on people. It was lying across the toe of a black boot that had been set, precisely, in the doorway between the reading room and the stacks, with its chin on the leather and its bronze eyes closed; and the man who owned the boot was sitting very still on a stool with one leg stretched out so as not to disturb it, reading a vellum sheet under the window with a glass.

He was perhaps forty. He was lean, and dark, and his coat was long and grey and old and had been very good once, and had been mended with care in several places by somebody who did not sew for a living. He had a narrow clever face and close-cut hair and the look, which Riarhin had seen on very few people and all of them dangerous, of a man who was paying attention to everything in the room at once and to nothing in particular.

He did not look up when she came in.

“You’ll be the assessor,” he said, in Ecktorian. “The one with the paragraph.”

Meret came in from the stacks behind him with an armful of cedar boxes and stopped, and looked at the two of them, and at the lizard on the boot, and put the boxes down.

“Saikou Ira,” she said. “From Kahfagia. He came up on the salt road yesterday. He wanted the archive’s Himadri papers.” She looked at Riarhin. “The same ones. The ones I told you about. The ones the man from Sekogo wanted.”

“Is he from Sekogo?”

“I’m from Kahfagia,” said Saikou Ira, still not looking up. “As she said. I have a shrine there, and a house I don’t live in, and a mother I don’t speak to about my work.” He moved the glass a little along the vellum. “I’ve come a long way to read one sheet in this archive and I’ve been reading it for two days, and your warden’s clerk told me yesterday that there was a foreign woman in Adur-Gah with a Crown commission to go north into the Wastes after something out of the Himadri. And I thought, well. There aren’t so many things out of the Himadri that anybody goes into the Wastes after.”

He put the glass down, then, and looked at her.

His eyes were very dark. There was nothing in them that she could read at all.

“Kalrantha,” he said.

She did not answer at once. She looked at Meret.

Meret looked back at her with her spectacles on her forehead and her arms folded, and her face said, quite plainly, I didn’t tell him the word. The warden’s clerk didn’t know it. He got here by himself.

“Who are you?” said Riarhin.

“An exorcist.” He said it the way Meret had said Runekeeper, without ornament. “I take things out of people. Occasionally out of places. Once or twice out of objects.” He turned the vellum sheet round on the table so that it faced her. “I’ve been following a lead in the relic market for two years. Not your thing. Something near it. A set of pieces that came out of an old repository in the Himadri a long time ago and have been turning up in dealers’ catalogues ever since with the wrong names on them. One of them came into my house, into my shrine, with a thing in it that had to be taken out. I took it out. It took some time.” He did not say anything about what it had cost. “And I’ve been trying since to find out where it came from, so that I could stop the next one coming.”

“And the next one’s Kalrantha?”

“No,” said Saikou Ira. “That’s what I’ve come to tell you. Sit down. This will take a while and I’d like to do it properly.”

He did it properly. It took all morning.

He had a satchel of papers, and he laid them out on Meret’s table in order, very precisely, each one squared to the edge of the table, and he went through them one at a time. He did not hurry and he did not skip anything, and when Meret asked a question he stopped and answered it fully and then went back to exactly where he had been. Riarhin had met methodical people. She had never met one who seemed to find method restful.

What he had was this.

There was a temple in the Himadri, nine miles from a mine that the relic trade called the Ninth Seal. Everybody who had ever heard of Kalrantha had heard of that temple, because every account of Kalrantha said it came from there. There was also a repository, near the mine, older than the temple, where things had been kept that had come up out of the mine’s workings. Saikou had a copy of a page from a catalogue of that repository, made by a scholar in Thepyrgos a hundred and thirty years ago, and on the page there were descriptions of eleven objects.

One of them was a green stone in a setting of brass, with a note that it was not to be worn.

“And that’s what the relic trade’s been calling Kalrantha,” said Saikou, “for a hundred and thirty years.”

He laid down another sheet beside it. It was the paragraph: the one Kargath had recited in three languages. Green glass in a brass frame, out of the Himadri, and a wish with a clause folded into it. It was in a northern hand, from a dealer’s catalogue in Vhasia, and it was dated sixty years after the Thepyrgos scholar’s page.

“Somebody,” said Saikou, “sixty years later, needed a description of the Himadri amulet everybody was talking about. And he went to the only published catalogue of anything from that range, which was the Thepyrgos page, and he found a green stone in brass, not to be worn, and he copied its description into his own account of the amulet.” He put his finger on the two sheets side by side. “Look at the wording. In a setting of brass. In a brass frame. He’s rewritten it, but he’s rewritten it from this. And every account after his copies him.”

Riarhin looked at the two sheets for a long time.

“So the stone in the repository,” she said, “isn’t Kalrantha.”

“I don’t know what the stone in the repository is. I know it was nine miles from the temple, in the same range, and that it was green and set in brass, and that one man put the two things together because they were near each other and he needed a description.” Saikou sat back. “And after that everything that came out of that range with green in it was Kalrantha. Including, I think, the thing that came into my shrine. Which was not. And which was dangerous for its own reasons, and nobody treated it with the right care, because they were all expecting a wish.”

Riarhin sat very still.

She had carried the paragraph for years. She had carried it longer than she had carried the nine leaves. She had said to Aryka, in a room above a tavern, that it was a rumour wearing a description. She had known that. She had not known whose description it was wearing.

“Then what do you know about the real thing?” she said.

“Very little.” He did not seem troubled by it. “I know that there was an object held at the temple, and that it was taken away from the temple a long time ago, and that it left the Himadri, and that it was carried about the world for some while after that by people who wrote down less about it each time. I know that four sets of people cut it out of their records.” He looked at her. “I followed the repository’s pieces. I didn’t follow the temple’s. I know the Himadri end of your trail. I don’t know anything about the other end.”

“I do,” said Riarhin.

She showed him. Not all of it.

She had thought, sitting there all morning, about how much. Sada’s commission said that the route would be recorded and that the record belonged to the Crown and the survey jointly, and would not be published by either without the other’s leave. It did not say anything about showing an exorcist from Kahfagia a page at a table in the archive. She had asked Sada the night before, when Meret sent word that a man had come about the Himadri papers, and Sada had thought about it and said: show him what he needs to check you, and nothing he’d need to follow you.

So she showed him the third description in Serafine’s file, the one in the careful hand that said the article twenty-two times and nothing else. She showed him the dates on it, which were eighty years old. She showed him that it recorded the object moving north from the southern coast, and that the three descriptions before it, over the preceding years, showed it moving south, through a chain of hands, out of the north.

She did not show him the bill from the dyers’ street, or the inscription at Ayna, or the name of the goat track.

Saikou read the third description three times. Then he took a sheet of his own out of the satchel and compared a date on it with a date on hers, and then another, and then took up his glass and looked at the corner of her page, where, below the last entry, there was a small impression in brown wax, crushed and cracked, of a seal.

He looked at it for a long time.

“Where did you get this?” he said.

“Out of a burning house.”

He looked up at her. For the first time that morning, something moved in his face. It was not amusement, quite. It was the expression of a methodical man who has been handed an answer by somebody who has not been methodical about it at all.

“That’s not an account of where you got it,” he said.

“No.”

“I’d like one.” He put the glass down. “I’d like to know whose house, and why it was burning, and whether the file’s been kept the same way the whole time or whether anybody’s been at it since the last entry. I’d like to know the whole chain of custody of these nine leaves from the day the ink went on to the day they came to this table. I’d like it written down.”

“So would I.”

He looked at her a moment longer, and she watched him decide that he was not going to get it, and not like it, and put it aside.

“Very well,” he said, dryly. “Then I’ll have to take it that you’re telling me the truth about the dates. Which I’d rather not do. But the dates match mine.” He turned his own sheet round to face her. “And the seal is known to me.”

It was a small seal. A square, and inside the square a knot, the kind of knot that has no ends, drawn in a single line that went over and under itself four times. Riarhin had seen it in the corner of the third description on the floor of Serafine’s counting room, and had filed it as a merchant’s seal, a house mark, and had not looked at it again.

Saikou had the same mark on a sheet of his own. It was a copy of a page from a dealer’s daybook in Kahfagia, and the mark was on it three times, beside three entries, each for an object from the Himadri that had been bought at a high price and then, a note said, returned.

“There was a house,” he said. “In Kahfagia, and later on the southern coast, for about thirty years. About ninety years ago to about sixty. It called itself nothing. It had no shop and no sign and it didn’t sell. It bought. It bought things that had come out of old places, and paid well for them, and then it took them away, and the dealers who sold to it wrote returned in their books, because that was what the house told them it was doing.” He touched the knotted square with the tip of his glass. “Returning things. To where they came from.”

Meret had gone very still at the end of the table.

“That’s a keeper’s mark,” she said.

Both of them looked at her.

“A square with a closed knot in it.” She pulled her chain of iron rings out of the neck of her dress and held one of them up to the light. Cut into it, very small, was a square with a line inside it that went over and under itself. It was not the same knot. It was the same idea. “It’s a form of it. Something shut, that doesn’t open. It’s older than any house in Kahfagia. Some of the people who serve the Gate still use a version.” She let the chain fall. “Whoever those people were, they thought they were putting things back behind doors.”

Riarhin sat with that for a long time after the other two had gone back to their papers.

Four sets of people had cut the record, eighty years apart, and had not agreed about which half was dangerous. She had known that since Silkstrand. It was the one fact she trusted. And now there was a fifth set of people, a house that called itself nothing and bought things and took them away, whose mark was in the corner of the last description of the thing she was following. And the last description said the thing had gone north from the southern coast, eighty years ago, in four journeys. The fourth journey had gone up a road that had silted, to a district that no longer existed, and eleven days beyond the last water, with enough water to come back slowly.

Returned. To where they came from.

But the thing had not come from there. It had come from the Himadri. Everybody agreed about that; it was the one thing in the paragraph nobody had ever disputed.

Unless it had been somewhere else in between. Unless it had gone from the temple to somewhere else, a long time ago, and been there for a long time, and come out of there, and gone round the world for a while in the hands of people who wrote down less about it every time. And then a house with a knot for a mark had bought it, and looked into where it had last been kept, and had not taken it back to the temple nine miles from the mine. It had taken it back to the other place.

She did not say any of that aloud. It was not evidence. It was a shape, and she had been wrong about shapes before, on the floor of Serafine’s counting room, about eleven days east.

She did say, to Meret, when Saikou had gone out to find his lunch: “Can you date that seal?”

Meret looked at the knotted square on the third description for a long time with her glass.

“With his page,” she said, “yes. His dealer’s book gives me thirty years it was in use. The wax on yours is the same make as the wax on the last entry in his, and the last entry in his is dated.” She put the glass down. “Your file’s last entry is in the last four years that house existed. They took it north near the end.” She looked at Riarhin. “And then nobody bought anything from anybody with that mark on it ever again.”

He left on the sixteenth morning.

He came to the guest house to say so, which Riarhin had not expected. He stood in the doorway of the whitewashed room in his long grey coat with his satchel over his shoulder, and did not come in.

“I’m going east,” he said. “To the coast, and then back to Kahfagia by sea. The repository’s pieces went that way. There are three more in dealers’ hands that I know of and one that I think is in a house in the Salt District at Mbaro, where it will kill somebody before the rains if it isn’t taken out.” He said it without any drama at all. “I’d rather it didn’t.”

“You’re not coming north.”

“I’m not coming north.” He looked at her. “It’s not my thread. I’ve been following the wrong green stone for two years; I’d like to follow the right one now. Yours isn’t mine.”

Riarhin looked at him in the doorway. She thought, for a moment, of asking him what he thought was at the end of the road. She thought of asking him whether he had any idea at all what the thing was; whether, in all his careful reading, he had found anything that would tell her what she was walking toward. She could see, from the way he stood, that he knew she wanted to ask. She could see that he would answer her fully and precisely, if she did, and that the answer would be I don’t know, set out at length with references.

She did not ask.

“You’ve got the page,” she said instead.

“I’ve got the page.” He touched the satchel. It had in it a fair copy, in Tesfaye’s hand, of the third description in Serafine’s file, with the dates and the knotted seal and nothing else: no route, no place, no name of any road. Sada had signed it at the foot, as the Crown’s representative on the survey, with a note that it was released to Saikou Ira of Kahfagia for purposes of comparison only. He had read the note twice and been, Riarhin thought, faintly amused by it. He had signed for the copy in a very small precise hand.

“And you’ve got the dates on the seal,” he said. “Meret’s a good scholar. She’ll do more with them than I would.” He paused. “I’d still like to know where your leaves came from.”

“I know you would.”

“You’re not going to tell me.”

“No.”

He looked at her with an expression of dry, patient, entirely professional annoyance, the look of a man who has been handed a gap in his records by somebody who does not care about records, and will be carrying it about with him now for some time.

“Then I’ll have to write down that the provenance is unknown,” he said. “I dislike doing that. I have a great many unknowns already.”

He did not say goodbye. He nodded to her once, and turned, and went away down the lane toward the lower market in his long coat, and she watched him go until he turned the corner, and then she went back to Meret and the seal.

That evening she told Sada what Saikou had brought, all of it, sitting on the step of the Council hall where they had sat after the hearing, because it had become a place they went.

Sada listened.

“So the paragraph’s somebody else’s stone,” she said at the end.

“The paragraph’s somebody else’s stone. The description, anyway. Green glass in a brass frame.” Riarhin looked out over the city. “The wish with a clause. I don’t know where that came from. He didn’t either. That part’s not in the Thepyrgos page at all.”

“Then you don’t know what it looks like.”

“I don’t know what it looks like.”

Sada was quiet for a while.

“That’s worse,” she said. “Isn’t it. Than not knowing what it does.”

Riarhin thought about it.

“It’s better,” she said at last. “It means everybody who ever went looking for it was looking for the wrong thing. For a hundred and thirty years. Everybody with a paragraph went to the Himadri and looked for green glass nine miles from a mine.” She was quiet. “And a house with a knot for a mark bought it and took it somewhere else, and nobody with the paragraph ever thought to look there. Because the paragraph said Himadri.”

“You think they meant that.”

“I think four sets of people cut the records so that the next person would know less. I think a fifth set of people bought it and took it away and called that returning it.” Riarhin looked down at her hands. “I don’t know what any of them knew. I know they all decided the same thing.”

Sada did not say anything to that.

After a while she said, “Meret dated the seal?”

“Meret dated the seal.”

“Then write it in the record,” said Sada. “With the date. And where the date came from. And that the man who gave it to us had the provenance of our file asked of him and we didn’t give it.” She pulled her wrap round her shoulders against the evening wind. “So that the next person to read it can see what we knew and what we didn’t.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“You sound like him,” she said.

“I sound like anybody who’s ever had to explain a record to a Council.” Sada did not smile. Then, a moment later, she did. “He was very handsome. Tigist saw him in the lower market and asked Hiwot who he was. Hiwot said he was an exorcist. Tigist said that was a waste.”

Chapter Twenty: Permission to Turn Back

The first offer came on a tray of dates.

It was brought to the Covenant guest house on the eighteenth morning by a young man in a very good coat who said he was a factor of the Spice House of the western coast, and who put the tray down on the table in the whitewashed room and stood back from it as though it were a document. The dates were the large dark kind from the Dhaharan oases, that cost a silver a handful in the lower market. Under the dates, on the tray, was a letter.

The Spice House had heard, the letter said, that the Crown had commissioned a survey of the old northern road. The Spice House had interests on the salt road and had long felt that the northern country was insufficiently known. The Spice House would be honoured to underwrite the survey’s costs, in whole or in part, as a service to the Crown and to learning. And in return, as a small consideration, the Spice House would ask only for the first right to purchase, at a fair valuation, any object of trade recovered by the survey that the Crown did not wish to retain.

Riarhin read it twice and gave it to Sada, who read it once and laughed.

“First right to purchase,” she said. “At a fair valuation. Anything the Crown doesn’t wish to retain.” She put the letter down. “They know the terms of the commission. They know nothing can be sold until the Council’s reviewed it. So they’re offering to pay for the road now, and be first in the queue when the Council finishes reviewing. And they’ve written object of trade, so that if anybody asks later, they only meant salt.”

“Does the Council need their money?”

“The Council always needs everybody’s money.” Sada handed the letter back to the young man in the good coat, who had stood through all of this with an expression of polite absence. “Thank the Spice House for the dates. Tell them the survey is funded by the Crown and the warden’s office, and that the terms of the commission don’t permit anybody to be first in any queue. Tell them the envoy will be pleased to discuss salt with them in the dry season.”

The young man bowed, and took the letter, and left the dates.

Na’ar ate them.

The second came three days later, and came from further off.

It was a man. He appeared at Kebede’s shed at the north gate one afternoon with a letter of recommendation from a salt house in Mbaro and another from a water-master in the Dhaharan oases, and he said that he was a well-finder, a man who could walk over dry ground and say where the water was under it; and that he had heard the Crown’s survey was going into country where the wells had been lost; and that he would be glad to be of service, for a modest fee.

Kebede read both letters very slowly with his small pale eyes, and then he asked the man a number of questions about the wells on the salt road between Adur-Gah and the coast, which the man answered well.

And then Kebede asked him about the wells at a place on the Dhaharan side that the water-master’s letter said the man had worked at for six years. The man answered that well too. And Kebede said, mildly, that the wells at that place had been dry for eleven years, since the earthquake, and that he knew because he had taken a caravan past them the year it happened and had had to go round.

The man looked at him for a moment. Then he picked up his letters and said that there must be some confusion, and left.

Kebede told Riarhin about it that evening, and she went and read the letter from the salt house in Mbaro, which the man had left a copy of on the table in his hurry. She knew the hand. She had read a letter in it at the money house of Tiemoko and Daughters, standing at the counter, asking whether the foreign lady had any family papers relating to the old northern trade.

The salt house on the Tower Bank. The factor who wrote letters up the river.

She sat with it in Kebede’s shed with the lamp for a long time.

It had taken the letter she had watched go out of Mbaro something like three months to go up the river, and be read, and be answered, and for an answer to come back down, and for a man to be found and sent to Adur-Gah with a letter of recommendation. It had arrived after the commission was granted, after the hiring was done. It had arrived at Kebede’s table, where it had lasted about as long as it took him to remember an earthquake.

It did not know the word. The man had not said it. He had said lost wells and old country, and he had wanted to be on the survey, and he had not got on it.

It still knew where she was.

The third came from the Crown itself, and it was the one Kebede minded most.

There was an office in the chancery, Hiwot’s office, that kept the Crown’s roads: the royal survey of every road in Taharka that the Crown maintained or claimed or collected a toll on. Its master was a brisk, clever, ambitious man called Ato Fikru who had been trying for years to have Wolde’s forty-year-old survey of the north replaced with something better, and who had heard about the commission, and was delighted.

He came to the warden’s office on the twenty-second day with a paper. The paper said that the route surveyed by the commission, on its return, would be entered in the royal survey and published in the ordinary way, in full, with its wells and stages and distances, for the use of all the Crown’s subjects and all lawful traffic.

Kebede read it standing up in the warden’s office, with Dejen at his desk and Sada by the window and Riarhin by the door. He read it twice. Then he put it down on the desk.

“No,” he said.

Ato Fikru looked at him with bright professional surprise.

“It’s a Crown road,” he said. “Or it will be. That’s the point of the commission, surely. To establish that there’s a Crown road north of the watchtowers.”

“The point of the commission,” said Kebede, “is to find out whether there’s a road at all, and what’s on it, and whether anybody should be on it.” He did not raise his voice. “If we find a road, and it has water on it, and we come back and write down where the water is and publish it, then next year there’ll be twenty parties on it. Salt traders. Relic hunters. Herders who’ve heard there’s grazing. Half of them won’t have read the part about how much water we carried and why. They’ll read the wells and the distances and go.” He looked at Ato Fikru. “And I’ll be the one they send for when they don’t come back. I’ve done it three times on the salt road. I’m not doing it on this one.”

Ato Fikru looked at Dejen. Dejen looked at the commission, which was on his desk.

“The route is recorded jointly by the Crown and the survey,” said Sada, from the window, “and not published by either without the other’s leave. That’s the term. It’s in the commission. The Council voted it.” She did not turn round. “Kebede’s the survey’s master of route. If he says he won’t give leave until the route’s conditions are written down beside it, then the Crown’s office can publish nothing until he does.”

“That could be years.”

“It could be,” said Sada. “It’ll be as long as it takes to write the conditions down properly. I’d have thought the royal survey would want them written down properly. It’s been using Wolde’s for forty years.”

Ato Fikru looked at her for a long moment. Then he smiled, quite charmingly, and picked up his paper, and said that of course the chancery would respect the terms of the commission, and that he looked forward to reading the conditions. He left.

Kebede stood by the desk with his hands at his sides.

“He’ll try again,” he said.

“He’ll try again,” said Dejen. “He’s very good at his work. So are you.”

Riarhin had been watching all three of them, and it was not until the twenty-third day that she understood they had nothing to do with each other.

She had been waiting for it. She had spent a month in Mbaro learning to read four readers, and she had come to Adur-Gah expecting to find that the Spice House and the well-finder and Ato Fikru were three faces of one thing, the way Oumar had turned out to be two. She had lain awake one night fitting them together: the Spice House wanting the object, the salt house wanting a man inside, the chancery wanting the route published so that anybody could follow. It fitted beautifully.

It was wrong. She found that out by checking, the way she had checked Sada’s word about Tin Kerru.

The Spice House had heard about the commission from its own people at the Council, and wanted what spice houses want. The salt house in Mbaro had heard about a foreign woman three months ago and sent a man, and did not know about the commission at all; the letter it had given the well-finder was dated before the hearing. And Ato Fikru had wanted a better northern survey for eleven years and would have written the same paper if the commission had been looking for goats.

Three separate pressures from three separate places, each knowing a different piece. None of them knew the word, except, now, the Council; and the Council had it because she had given it to them.

She did not know whether that made her feel better or worse.

The fourth thing was not an offer. It was the price of barley.

She noticed it because she had been watching the north gate for a month and knew what things cost there. Kebede had ordered the fodder for the station at the last well a fortnight before: a great deal of barley and dried lucerne, to be carted up the salt road round the highlands to the herders’ depot at the last well, and held there for the expedition’s animals and the station’s. He had ordered it from a fodder merchant at the north gate whom he had used for twenty years, at a price they had agreed.

On the twenty-third morning the fodder merchant came to Kebede’s shed and said, very apologetically, that he could not deliver the barley to the last well at the agreed price after all. The depot at the last well was not taking any more barley this season. It was full. He would have to deliver to the other depot, the one on the salt road two days west, at the junction, which was not full; and then the expedition would have to cart it the last two days itself.

Kebede looked at him.

“The depot at the last well is a herders’ depot,” he said. “It’s never been full in its life.”

“It is now,” said the merchant, unhappily. “Somebody bought the space. All of it. For the season.”

“Who?”

The merchant did not want to say. It was not that he knew anything; he was a fodder merchant, and did not.
