But he had been told by his carter, who had been told by the depot-keeper at the last well, that the space had been bought by the depot on the junction, and that the depot on the junction belonged to the Spice House.

Riarhin followed it for two days, and it was the most ordinary piece of work she had done since Mbaro, and she enjoyed every hour of it.

The depot at the last well was a herders’ depot, a stone barn and a walled yard where the grazing families kept their winter fodder, and it had been bought out for the season by a factor who had come up the salt road a week ago with coin and a paper. The paper did not say what the space was for. The factor had been very generous with the coin.

The depot at the junction was the Spice House’s. It stood where the salt road met the track up to the last well, two days west, and it had water and a yard and a keeper; and it had, since a week ago, been advertising on the salt road that it had fodder and water and space for any party going north, at rates that were high, and would be higher, and would have to be paid there.

So the expedition’s fodder could not be held at the last well. It would have to be held at the junction, two days away, in the Spice House’s yard, at the Spice House’s rates. The station at the last well, with the spare animals and the splint kit and the people who would wait, would have to send two days west every time it needed feeding. The expedition would depend, for every day it was out, on the Spice House’s depot, and the Spice House would know to the sack what it needed and when.

And the Spice House knew that. It knew exactly how much fodder the expedition had ordered, and when, and where it was meant to be held, and how many animals it was for. It had bought the space at the last well to the sack.

Somebody had given it a copy of Kebede’s load schedule.

She found the somebody on the second day, and it was not interesting.

It was a clerk in the warden’s office. He was a young man who copied the warden’s papers, and his brother-in-law was a carter for the Spice House, and he had made a copy of Kebede’s schedule for his brother-in-law, who had asked for it, and been paid a silver for it, and had not thought about it very much. He had not thought about the station, or the last well, or how far two days was with a broken leg. He had thought about the silver.

She did not do anything to him. She told Dejen.

And Dejen, who was the warden of the north and answered to the Council for everything that happened on the northern side of Adur-Gah, including its depots and its grazing, did the thing that Riarhin had spent her whole life going round and had never once seen done from the inside.

He wrote a paper.

It was a short paper. It said that the herders’ depot at the last well was a depot of the northern grazing, held under the warden’s authority for the use of the grazing families and of the Crown, and that space in it could not be let for a season to any party without the warden’s leave, and that no such leave had been given. It said that the space was requisitioned for the Crown’s survey, and that the Spice House’s factor would be repaid his coin, and that he would not be permitted to buy space in any depot of the northern grazing for three years. It said that the clerk in the warden’s office was dismissed. He signed it and sealed it and sent it up the salt road with two Ridge-Watchers.

It took him perhaps a quarter of an hour. He did not raise his voice at any point.

“That’s all?” said Riarhin.

Dejen looked up at her from his desk with his grey Ridge-Watch face.

“What else would there be?” he said. “It’s my depot.”

It did not end there, because nothing did. The Spice House wrote a letter of complaint to the Council, which Sada said would be read in the dry season along with the Pereshi’s. Kebede, who did not trust any depot he had not seen with his own eyes since the paper was sealed, changed the arrangements for the station again. The fodder would go to the last well as planned, and so would a second load, carried by the expedition’s own mules, which would mean that the expedition went up the goat track more heavily laden than he liked and more slowly; and at the last well he would count every sack himself.

He did it without complaint. He sat in his shed with the charcoal and wrote a new line, and then another, and then a third, and when he had finished he looked at the whole schedule for a long time and said that it would do.

And he had a new copy made, in his own hand, and kept it on his person, and gave nobody else a copy of anything.

On the twenty-sixth evening Riarhin sat the four of them down in Kebede’s shed and told them all the same thing.

She had thought about it for a long time. She had been thinking about it since the lower market, when she had stood in the dark with Tesfaye’s lantern and understood what she wanted. She had never in her life told everybody on a job the same thing. She had told each of them what they needed to do their part, and kept the whole of it in her own head, because that was where it was safest, and because a woman who holds the whole of a thing is a woman nobody else can leave behind.

She told them the whole of it.

What they knew. The nine leaves and what they showed: four journeys, eighty years ago, the fourth one up the old high road to the wells of Imadhaq and eleven days beyond, with a light party and water enough to come back slowly. The mark on the ninth leaf and the stone at Ayna and the old brother’s reading. Wolde’s survey and the laid stones in the stream. The knotted seal and Saikou’s dates. The paragraph, and whose stone it had been describing.

What they did not know. What the thing was. Whether it was dangerous, and if so, how. What had happened to the country the road had run through. Why the light party had needed to come back slowly. Whether the wells at Imadhaq still held water, or whether there was anything eleven days beyond them but sand.

Who had tried to interfere. The Spice House, and its depot. The salt house in Mbaro, and the well-finder who had not been. Ato Fikru, who would try again. The Corps in Mbaro, who had written down that she went south and had good oil. And a factor on the Tower Bank who had written a letter up the river three months ago, whose letter had come back down in the form of a man, and who might write again.

And what would turn them back. Kebede’s line on the water. Kebede’s line on the animals. Meret’s doors that were shut for a reason. Anybody hurt who could not be carried. Anything they found that Sada judged the Crown must be told of before they went further.

She did not tell them about a man above a dry well with his brothers’ knives. She did not tell them about the room above the tavern in Kolmar, and how the reason had sounded when she said it out loud to Aryka, or about the road that had begun to feel like standing still. That was hers. It was not a hazard. It was only why.

She did not keep anything back that was a hazard.

When she had finished, nobody said anything for a while.

“Does anybody want to stay?” said Riarhin. “You can. Now. Nobody will think less of you. I’d rather you stayed now than halfway.”

Meret pushed her spectacles up her forehead. “You’ve asked that before,” she said. “Separately. We’ve all said no.”

“I’m asking it together. With everything on the table.”

Kebede looked at her with his small pale eyes and did not say anything. Na’ar was eating a date. Sada was sitting on a sack of barley by the door with her hands folded in her lap, looking at Riarhin, and her face was entirely still.

Nobody stayed.

She and Sada had the harder conversation that night, on the steps of the Council hall, because that was where they went.

It was harder because neither of them wanted to have it, and because both of them had known for a month that they were going to.

“I’ll write it all down,” said Sada. “Everything that touches the passes. Everything that touches the border, or the grazing, or the road. If there’s something up there that’s a danger to the people on the northern side of Adur-Gah, it’ll be in my report, in full, and the Council will read it. If the thing you’re looking for is dangerous, that’ll be in my report. If it’s dangerous and you want to keep it quiet, it’ll still be in my report.” She did not look at Riarhin. “I’m telling you now so that when I do it you can’t say I didn’t.”

“I know,” said Riarhin. “You’ve told me four times.”

“I’m telling you a fifth.” Sada was quiet a moment. “And the other thing.”

“Which other thing?”

Sada turned her head and looked at her.

“You’re coming because you want to find it,” she said. “Not because of me. I know that. I’ve known it since Mbaro. And when you’ve found it, or not found it, you’ll come back down that road, and then you’ll go wherever you go next.” She said it very evenly. “I’m not asking you to stay in Adur-Gah. I’m not going to ask you. I want you to know that I know you haven’t promised.”

Riarhin sat on the step and did not say anything for a long time.

It was true. She had not promised. She had not thought, until this moment, about whether she would, and she found now, sitting on the step in the dark with the whole of Adur-Gah below her, that she did not know. She did not know what she would do when she came back down the goat track. She had never once in forty years come back down a road and not known where she was going next.

“I haven’t,” she said. “I don’t know what I’ll do.”

“Good,” said Sada. “Neither do I.”

“And you’re still coming.”

“I’m the Crown’s representative. I’m coming because the Council sent me.” Sada paused. Then, more quietly: “And because I want to. Both. I’m not going to pretend it’s only one.”

“Neither am I.”

They sat on the step. The wind came up off the valley, cold, and Sada pulled her wrap round her shoulders, and after a while she leaned sideways until her shoulder was against Riarhin’s, the way she had at Ayna, and stayed there.

Neither of them had given the other anything. Neither of them had asked for anything. They had set it all out on the step between them, in the dark, the report and the leaving and the not knowing, the way Kebede set out a load schedule, every line of it where it could be seen.

And then they had gone on sitting there.

The expedition went out of the north gate of Adur-Gah on the thirtieth morning, in the dark before dawn, with forty mules and twelve horses and twenty-three people, and Tamrat of the royal stables standing at the gate in a sheepskin coat to watch his horses go.

It went up the goat track through the high grazing for three days, past the last village with a church and the last watchtower, where the Ridge-Watchers came out and stood on the wall and watched them go by without saying anything. It crossed the stream on the laid stones that Wolde had not looked down at. It camped on the third night in a hollow of heather under a ridge where the herders’ track divided, one branch going east toward the salt road and one going north toward nothing at all.

They took the north branch in the morning, and there was nobody on it.

That night, at the first camp beyond the last of anybody’s traffic, Riarhin sat by the fire and watched the camp settle.

The drivers had picketed the mules and were eating. The two Ridge-Watchers had set a watch. Meret was sitting on a rock at the edge of the firelight with her drawing board, drawing something, and Sill was asleep on the flat stone at the mouth of her tent, which was the nearest thing to a threshold in forty miles. Na’ar was cooking. Sada was writing in her journal with a lamp.

And Kebede was going round the water.

He went along the line of skins where they lay in the lee of the baggage, one after another, forty of them, and put his hand flat on each one and pressed, and felt it give, and moved on. He went along the line of spare containers, folded, and counted them. He went to the tar pot and the repair bag and opened them and looked inside and closed them again. It took him a quarter of an hour.

Everybody else had checked the water at dusk. Riarhin had watched them do it: the drivers, and the Ridge-Watchers, and Na’ar, who had checked the skins on his own two horses, and Meret, who had checked her own. She had checked it herself. It was all there. Nothing had leaked.

Kebede checked it again, alone, in the dark, when everybody else had finished, and did not say anything to anybody about it, and came back to the fire and sat down.

She watched him do it the whole time. She did not say anything either.

She let him.

Act V: The Wastes

Chapter Twenty-One: The Last Watered Stage

The wells at Beles had been kept by the same family for six generations, and the woman who kept them now did not think much of expeditions.

Her name was Almaz. She was perhaps fifty, lean and brown and weathered to the colour of the ground, with her hair bound up in a black cloth and a herder’s staff that she used for pointing at things; and she stood at the head of the track where it came down out of the last of the highland scrub onto the edge of the flat country, with the wind blowing her cloth about, and watched the Crown’s survey come down to her wells with an expression of patient forbearance.

“She says,” said the elder of the two Ridge-Watchers, a quiet man called Berihun who spoke the herders’ tongue, “that the last people who came down this track with that many animals were the Crown’s surveyor forty years ago. She says he stayed two nights and drank her grandfather’s well down by a hand and went home. She wants to know if we’re going home too.”

“Tell her no,” said Kebede. “Tell her we’re going north. Tell her we’ll pay for every skin we draw. And tell her we’re leaving people here, and that they’ll need her.”

Berihun told her. Almaz listened with her head on one side, and then looked along the string of mules, and at the twelve horses from the royal stables, and at the drivers, and at the five people at the front of it who were plainly the ones who meant to go on.

She said something short.

“She says,” said Berihun, “then you’d better come and look at the water.”

It took four days to make the station, and Riarhin had never in her life spent four days making a place to come back to.

Beles was not a village. It was three wells in a hollow at the foot of the last highland slope, where the ground broke up into gullies and then flattened out into gravel and then into the pale country beyond; and round the wells there were stone pens for the herds, and a stone barn that was the warden’s depot, and two houses with flat roofs where Almaz’s family lived through the dry season, and a great deal of goat dung. The wind came off the flat country from the north all day, dry and warm and carrying a fine grit that got into everything. At night it dropped, and the stars came out so thick that Na’ar sat outside for an hour on the first night with his head back and did not say anything at all.

The station went up beside the depot. Kebede built it the way he had drawn it in his shed at the north gate, and changed it twice as he went, because the ground was not quite what he had drawn. A walled yard for the spare animals. A shelter of the low tents for the people who would stay. The fodder in the depot, counted, every sack, by Kebede himself, twice: the barley and the lucerne the fodder merchant had carted round by the salt road and the second load they had carried up the goat track on their own mules. A physician’s tent with Tigist’s bag in it and a young woman called Mulu, who had been Tigist’s assistant at the royal hospital for three years and could set a leg and had never been further from Adur-Gah than the lower market.

And the camels. They came up the salt road on the second day with their drivers, twelve of them, lean and grumbling and unimpressed, having stood at the junction for a week at the Crown’s expense. Na’ar went and looked at them and restated his opinion. The camel drivers, who had heard it before from other people, laughed at him and offered him a bowl of camel’s milk, and he drank it and conceded that it was better than he had expected, which was the nearest thing to an apology Riarhin had ever heard him make to an animal.

The mules would stay at Beles. The horses would stay at Beles, all of them; Na’ar said goodbye to the dun at some length. They had been built for the climb, and the climb was behind them now; ahead was flat ground and heat and sand, and the camels had been built for that.

The people who stayed were not waiting to die. Riarhin had seen expeditions where they were. She had seen men left at the last stage of a campaign with half rations and a vague order to wait, and she had seen what they became after the first week, when nobody came back and nobody came to tell them anything. She had never, until Beles, seen people left at the last stage who had been told exactly what to do.

Kebede told them. He did it in the yard of the station on the third evening, with Almaz and her eldest son and the Ridge-Watchers and Mulu and the four drivers who were staying, sitting on the ground in a half-circle; and Berihun translated for the herders, and Kebede drew it in the dust with a stick while he talked.

The survey would be gone twenty-one days. That was the plan. If it went well, less.

If the survey was not back on the twenty-second day, nobody was to do anything. It would be late. Surveys were late.

If it was not back on the twenty-fifth day, Almaz’s son Getu and the younger Ridge-Watcher and two drivers would take four camels loaded with water and go north along the survey’s track, which would be marked, as far as the third cairn. They would wait at the third cairn for two days. They would light a fire of green wood at dusk on each of those days, for the smoke. Then they would come back.

If it was not back on the thirtieth day, the station would send to Adur-Gah, to the warden, and the warden would decide.

Nobody from the station was to go further north than the third cairn. Not for any reason. Not if they saw smoke beyond it. Not if they thought they heard somebody calling. Not if one of the five came back alone and begged them. They were to go to the third cairn and wait and come home.

Kebede said all of that in a flat heavy voice and drew it in the dust, and at the end of it he looked round the half-circle and asked whether anybody had a question.

Almaz had one. She asked it through Berihun.

“She asks,” said Berihun, “what the five of you will look like. When you’re coming back. So that her son will know it’s you and not something else.”

Kebede looked at Riarhin.

She had never done it.

She sat on the ground in the yard of the station with twenty people looking at her, and understood that she had never once in her life told anybody what she would look like coming back. She had spent forty years making sure that nobody knew what she looked like going. She had worn a cloak that nobody could describe the same way twice and she had slept in an alley with two ways out of it in eight countries. She had left towns before the morning and had come back to them, when she came back at all, by a different road.

Almaz was waiting, with her staff across her knees and her head on one side.

“Five of us,” said Riarhin. Her voice sounded strange. She went on. “Two camels, one of them carrying a litter pole across the saddle, so that you’ll see the pole sticking out on both sides even from far off. One of the five will be a very big man. One will be a woman in a white wrap with a green and red border, and she’ll be the one in front if we’re in any trouble, because she’s the one who’ll talk to you.” She stopped, and made herself go on. “And I’ll be at the back.”

“What does she look like?” said Almaz, through Berihun. She was looking at Riarhin.

Riarhin opened her mouth, and closed it.

She could have said the woman with the red hair. She could have said the one with the sword. She could have said the cloak, and did not, because she knew that by the time she came back down out of the north Almaz would remember the cloak as whatever colour it was then, and would have always remembered it that way.

“I’ll be carrying a bow,” she said finally. “Slung. Short, pale, curved back on itself at the ends. Nobody else has one like it.” She paused. “And we’ll make signs. On the track, going north. Three stones on a flat rock, one on top of two, every half day. Coming back, if we’re coming back, we’ll knock the top stone off. If Getu finds a cairn with the top stone off, it means we’ve passed it going home.”

Almaz listened to Berihun give her all of it. Then she nodded, and said something, and got up and went away to see to her goats.

“She says,” said Berihun, “that’s clear.”

Riarhin sat where she was in the dust.

She had given a stranger a description of herself and a sign that meant I was here, going home. She had done it in front of twenty people, and a clerk from the warden’s office had written it down on the station’s copy of the orders. When she came back down that track, if she came back down it, a herder’s son would be watching for her, and would know her.

She did not say anything to anybody about it. She found, sitting in the dust in the last of the light, that her hands had gone into her lap and were holding each other.

Sada, who had been sitting next to Kebede, did not look at her. She reached across, without looking, and put her own hand over both of Riarhin’s for a moment, and took it away, and went on listening to Kebede talk to the drivers about camels.

The farthest cache went out on the fourth day.

Kebede would not leave the station until he had seen it. He took two camels loaded with water and fodder and went a day and a half north onto the flat country with Berihun and a driver, and buried the load in a pit at the foot of a rock that he could find again, and built a cairn on the rock, three stones, one on top of two; and came back. It took him three days. He came back grey with dust and said that the going was hard for the first day and then easier, and that the rock was a good rock, and that he had seen nothing at all in a day and a half but gravel and wind.

That was the last day’s walking anybody had done, on the northern side of Beles, in forty years.

While he was gone, Meret found the well.

It was the oldest of the three. The herders called it the grandmother, and Almaz’s family did not draw from it except in the worst years, because it was very deep and the water came up slowly. It had a lip of rough fieldstone round the top that Almaz’s great-grandfather had built, and a stone trough beside it, and a rope with a leather bucket that took four people and the whole of the morning to haul.

Meret went down it.

Nobody had asked her to. She had spent the first three days at Beles drawing everything: the pens, the barn, the houses, the other two wells; and on the fourth morning she tied a rope round her waist and gave the other end to Na’ar and went down the grandmother well on the old footholds cut in its side, with a lamp on a cord round her neck and her drawing board slung on her back. Sill watched her go from the edge of the lip with his chin on the stone. She was down for two hours. When she came up she was soaked to the waist and filthy and she sat down on the lip and did not say anything for a long time.

“The top is the herders’,” she said at last. “The first ten feet. Fieldstone, mud mortar, built on what was there.” She took her spectacles off and wiped them on her sleeve and made them worse. “Under it there’s a lining of dressed stone. Pale stone, like the block at Ayna. Laid in courses. Beautiful work. Every course is the same depth as every other.” She put the spectacles back on. “And there’s a mark cut in the lining where the courses begin, at the top, under the herders’ stone, where nobody’s seen it for two hundred years.”

“The comb,” said Riarhin.

“The comb and the dot.” Meret held out her drawing board. “And beside it, a number. I can read the numbers. They’re the old numbers, the same as the ones in the Ayna inscription. It says seven.”

Riarhin looked at the drawing.

“And there are seven courses,” said Meret. “Down to the water. I counted them. I counted them twice.”

Riarhin did not need to go and look anything up. She had it where she had put it.

The canal-mothers’ bench under the tamarind, in Mbaro, in the third week. The oldest of the northern water grants, so old that nobody drew on them any longer and the canal-mothers kept them only because nothing in Oshiira was ever thrown away. She had read them twice for the benefit of a man in a red coat, and she had attended, because she always attended; and one of them had been a grant to draw at the wells of a district called Imadhaq.

The grant had described the well it was drawing from, the way all the old grants did, so that nobody could claim to be drawing from the wrong one. The great well of Imadhaq, of seven courses, under the seal of the keepers, by the high road.

She had filed the words, at the time, as a formula. She had not known that a course was a thing you could count.

She said it aloud, sitting on the lip of the grandmother well at Beles with the wind blowing grit across Meret’s drawing board, and Meret looked at her and did not say anything, and Sada, who had come across from the station, stood still with her hand on the herders’ stones.

“It’s the same well,” said Sada.

“It’s a well of seven courses under the mark of the keepers,” said Meret. “On the high road. Two hundred years north of the last grant that anybody drew on.” She was very careful. “I can’t tell you it’s the one in the grant. There might have been other wells of seven courses. There probably were. It was a well-made road.” She looked at the drawing. “But the grant was for the great well of Imadhaq. And this is a great well. And it’s here.”

Riarhin sat on the lip of the well for a long time.

It was not proof. Meret was right; it was not proof. It was one measurement, cut in a stone lining, that agreed with a phrase in a grant that an old woman in Mbaro kept under a tamarind tree because nothing was ever thrown away. That was all it was.

It was the first thing the journey itself had given her. Everything before it she had brought with her: the leaves, the bill, the inscription, the seal, carried in her coat across a sea and four hundred miles of road. This she had come to, and found, at the bottom of a well, in the place the papers had said it would be.

It meant the next stage was not a guess. It did not mean anything more than that. She knew it did not, and she sat there with it anyway, for a while, because she had not had one before.

Kebede came back that evening from the cache, grey with dust, and heard about the well, and went and looked at Meret’s drawing by lamplight for a long time with his small pale eyes.

“Seven courses,” he said. “Under the herders’ ten feet.”

“Yes.”

He looked at it a while longer.

“Then the old road came this far with water,” he said. “And the wells were built to last, and they did.” He put the drawing down. “That’s good. It doesn’t tell us whether the next one did.”

“No.”

“Good,” said Kebede, and went to count the water.

On the last night at Beles, Riarhin sat outside the station on a rock at the edge of the flat country and looked north.

There was nothing to see. The stars came down to the horizon on every side, and under them the flat country ran away pale and grey and featureless into the dark, and the wind blew off it, dry and warm, carrying grit and the smell of nothing at all. Behind her the station was settling for the night: the camels groaning, a driver singing something low, Mulu laughing at something one of the Ridge-Watchers had said. The lamp in Sada’s tent. The smell of whatever Na’ar was cooking for the last supper he would cook with a full larder.

She put her tongue against her teeth, the way she had done nine times a day for months without deciding to, and found the tooth.

It was whole. It had been whole, she realised, for some while. She had not checked it since the Council hall. She could not remember the last day she had checked it, and she did not know when it had finished coming back; she had stopped attending, somewhere on the road, and it had grown back into her mouth without her. Nobody had noticed. Nobody ever had.

She sat on the rock and looked north into the dark, and ran her tongue along the whole row of her teeth, and did not know what to make of that, and let it go.

Behind her, Almaz came out of her house with a lamp and went across to the grandmother well and stood beside it for a while, with the lamp held up, looking down into it, as if she were seeing for the first time how deep it went.

Chapter Twenty-Two: The Road Under the Dune

They left Beles in the dark of the fifth morning, seven people and ten camels, and by noon of the first day the highlands behind them had become a blue line on the edge of the world, and then a grey one, and then nothing.

The country was gravel. It was flat grey gravel from one horizon to the other, as though somebody had spread a road across the whole of the world and never finished it, and the camels walked on it with a sound like a crowd of people eating, and the wind blew across it from the north without stopping, carrying a fine red dust that settled in the creases of everything. Riarhin’s cloak had been the colour of juniper in the highlands. By the afternoon of the first day it was the colour of the dust, a dull rust-red, and it had always been.

The two drivers were called Hailu and Zewdu. They were brothers, camel men from the salt road, in their thirties, and they had signed Kebede’s paper at the table in the yard behind his shed knowing that they would be going further north than the station and not as far as the city, if there was a city; that they would be the ones who stayed with the camels at whatever place Kebede chose when the five went on alone. They had asked a great many questions before they signed. They had asked what they would be paid if the five did not come back, and how long they were to wait, and whether they would be told the truth about the water, and Kebede had answered all of it. They talked to the camels the whole time, in a sing-song voice, and the camels talked back, and nobody else could understand either side.

At noon on the second day they came to Kebede’s rock and dug up the cache, and it was all there, and Kebede counted it and loaded it and put the three stones back on the rock, one on two, with the top stone on.

After that nobody had been anywhere.

The first ruin was a heap of stones on the afternoon of the second day, and Meret nearly walked past it.

It was not much. It was a low mound on the gravel, perhaps thirty paces across, with pale stone showing through the red dust at one end, and a line of stones sticking up at the other that might have been the top of a wall and might have been nothing. Meret stopped her camel and looked at it for a while, and then got down and walked over to it, and crouched, and began to brush the dust off the pale stone with her hand.

It was dressed. It had the same sharp arris and the same fine grain as the block in the wall at Ayna.

She drew it. It took an hour. Kebede sat on his camel and watched her with his small pale eyes and did not say anything about the time, because he had signed the same paper she had, and the paper said she could.

“A waystation,” she said, when she climbed back up. “Like the carters’ ruin. Walls, a court, a well in the middle that’s filled in.” She wiped her hands on her robe. “The carters said there were more of them further on. Every day or so.”

There were. There was another on the afternoon of the third day, and another on the morning of the fourth, each of them a mound of red dust and pale stone on the grey gravel, each of them a day’s walk from the last. Meret drew every one. None of them had water in it. All of them had a filled well in the middle of a court, and at the second of them Meret dug down into the fill with her hands for half an hour and came up with a fragment of a pot, glazed green, and held it up to the light and did not say anything and put it in her bag.

They were a line. They went north, one a day, across the gravel, in a straight line, like the stations on the salt road.

“Somebody kept these,” Riarhin said to Kebede on the fourth morning.

“Somebody built them,” said Kebede. “You don’t know yet that anybody kept them.”

The road came out of the sand on the fourth afternoon.

The gravel had been giving way to sand since the morning. It came in long low waves across the grey, red-gold and soft, lying in the lee of every rock and every rise, and the camels began to labour in it and the brothers began to talk to them more. And in the afternoon the waves became dunes, not tall, perhaps twice the height of a man, running across their line from west to east in long ridges with the wind blowing the crests off them in thin streamers.

At the foot of one of them, where the wind had scoured a hollow out of the lee side down to the bedrock, there was paving.

Na’ar saw it first, because he was walking. He had got down off his camel at noon, as he did every day for an hour, because he said a man who rides all day forgets that his legs are there; and he was walking at the head of the string in the lee of the dune when he stopped, and looked down, and said, “Ah.”

It was a stretch of stone perhaps ten paces long, laid in the hollow where the wind had cleared it. Flat stones, fitted edge to edge, pale under the red dust, worn smooth. A road. It came out of the sand on the south side of the hollow and went back into it on the north side, under the next dune, and there was nothing to show where it had come from or where it went.

Meret got down and stood on it.

She did not draw it, for once. She stood in the middle of the ten paces of stone in the hollow of the dune with the wind blowing red dust across her boots and looked at it, and then turned round and looked south, the way they had come, and then north, the way it went.

“It’s under all of it,” she said. “All the gravel. All the sand. The waystations were beside it. We’ve been walking beside a road for three days.”

They followed it north, and it came and went.

It came out of the sand in every hollow where the wind had scoured the lee of a dune, and went back under it at the other side. Sometimes it was ten paces of stone and sometimes fifty. Once, on the fifth morning, it came out of the sand for nearly half a mile, straight as a rule across a flat between two lines of dunes, and they walked along it in the early light, the camels’ feet ringing on the stone, and nobody said anything.

On that stretch they saw the drainage.

There was a channel on each side of the road. A shallow gutter, cut in stone, running along the edge of the paving on the left and on the right, with a lip on the outer side of it to keep the sand out. Every fifty paces or so the gutter on the right-hand side had a cut through its lip, and a short stone spout, pointing away from the road. There was no water anywhere. There had not been water here in anybody’s memory. Somebody had built a road across this country with gutters on both sides of it to carry the water off.

“Rain,” said Kebede. He had got down and was crouched at the side of the road with his hand in the gutter. “When they built this, it rained here.” He looked at the spout. “Enough that they had to take it off the road.”

“How long ago?”

Kebede looked at the gutter for a while.

“I don’t know,” he said. “I know about roads. I don’t know about that.”

The marker was at the north end of the half-mile.

It was a stone post, square, about the height of a man’s waist, standing at the edge of the road beside the right-hand gutter. The wind had scoured three faces of it to smoothness. The fourth face, the south face, the one that looked back down the road the way they had come, had been in the lee of the post for however long it had been standing there, and had writing on it.

Meret knelt in front of it for a long time.

“Numbers,” she said. “The old numbers. And a word. Two words.” She traced them with her finger without touching the stone. “The first one is to. The second one’s a name. I don’t know it.” She sat back on her heels. “I don’t know it and I’ve never seen it and I don’t think it’s in any archive in Taharka. And then a number. Twenty-six.”

“Twenty-six what?”

“I don’t know that either. Whatever they measured roads in.”

Kebede walked back down the road from the post, counting his paces aloud under his breath in the highland tongue. He walked until the road went back under the sand at the south end of the half-mile. Then he dug. It took him and Na’ar and the two brothers most of an hour, with the camel shovels, in the heat of the day, and the others sat in the lee of the dune and watched, and Riarhin did not say anything about the time, because she had signed the same paper too.

They found the next post down, under the sand, a thousand and some paces back. Its south face said to, and the name, and twenty-seven.

Kebede stood on the road between the two posts with sweat running down his face into his beard, and looked north.

“Twenty-six of those,” he said. “To wherever that is. Twenty-six of those is about four days’ walking on a road.” He wiped his face. “On a road. With water at the stations. Not on this.”

That night they camped in the lee of a dune beside the half-mile of road, and Meret sat up by the lamp with her drawing of the post, looking at the name.

“It’s not a waystation,” she said, to nobody. “You don’t put a distance-post on a road to a waystation. You don’t put gutters on both sides of a road to a shrine. You don’t build a waystation every day for a week in a straight line across a desert so that people can walk to one tomb.” She put the drawing down. “This was a road that went somewhere people needed to go. Lots of people. Often. With carts.”

“Carts?” said Sada.

“The ruts.” Meret pointed back at the road in the dark. “On the half-mile. Two lines worn in the stone. You saw them. Carts, going back and forth on it for long enough to wear the stone down a finger’s depth.”

Riarhin had seen them. She had attended to them the way she attended to everything, and had filed them as ruts, and had not asked herself how long it takes a cart to wear a rut a finger deep into stone.

Kebede was sitting across the fire with his back against a camel saddle and his eyes half closed.

“It’s not a processional way either,” he said.

They all looked at him.

“You’d think it might be,” said Kebede. “Straight road, posts, gutters. People build roads like that to temples. To palaces. To walk the king up to the gods on a feast day.” He did not open his eyes. “A road like that gets built once and swept and kept clean and nobody drives a cart on it. This one’s been mended.”

“Mended?”

“The whole way. You look at the edges.” He opened his eyes and looked at Riarhin. “Where the paving meets the gutter there’s a kerb. On the half-mile there’s three kinds of kerb-stone. The old kind’s the pale stone, like the waystations, cut square. Then there’s patches of a greyer stone, cut rougher, where the old kerb broke and somebody put a new piece in. And then there’s a third kind, just fieldstone, set in mud, where the grey stone broke and somebody put a rock in instead.” He looked at the fire. “Three lots of people, mending the same road. Each lot poorer than the one before. Each lot still mending it.” He closed his eyes again. “Nobody mends a road three times over unless they’ve got to use it.”

Nobody said anything for a while.

“It’s a working road,” said Riarhin.

“It’s a working road,” said Kebede. “Or it was.”

The grey camel began to founder on the sixth morning.

She was young, a female, the lightest of the ten, and she had been carrying water, and the sand north of the half-mile was the worst they had met: deep and soft and red, piled in long dunes that ran across their line, so that every mile was up one slope and down another with the sand sliding under the camels’ feet. The brothers had been talking to her all morning. By the fourth hour she was stopping every hundred paces with her legs spread and her head down and her sides heaving, and when Hailu pulled at her halter she groaned and sank to her knees in the sand and would not get up.

Hailu and Zewdu looked at each other. They had seen it before. Riarhin could see in their faces what they thought was coming.

Kebede got down off his camel and walked back to her and stood looking at her for a long moment with his hands at his sides. Riarhin watched him do the arithmetic. A camel carrying water that could not walk was a camel whose water had to be carried by the others, which were carrying as much as Kebede would let them carry, which was not quite enough.

Na’ar was already there.

He had been walking again, at the rear of the string, and he came up beside the grey camel in the sand and crouched down in front of her and looked at her face for a while. Then he put his hand on her neck and ran it down to her shoulder, and down her foreleg to the foot, and picked up the foot, which she let him do, and looked at it.

“She is not lame,” he said. “She is tired. It is the sand. Every step, she goes in to here.” He showed them, on his own shin. “She is lighter than the others and she is young and she is not so clever about where she puts her feet. The old ones walk on the tops of the ripples. She goes in the troughs.” He stood up. “She needs harder ground for an hour. Then she will go again.”

“There isn’t any harder ground,” said Kebede.

Na’ar did not answer. He was looking back along their line, south, the way they had come, to where the road had last come out of the sand at the foot of a dune a quarter of a mile behind them.

Then he walked back to it.

They watched him go. He went back down the line of their own tracks to the hollow where the road had last shown, and stood in it, and then walked along the edge of the paving to where it went under the sand on the north side. He went on walking. He walked along the line where the road would be, if it went straight, up the slope of the next dune, and the sand there was just as deep and soft as anywhere else; and then he moved a pace to the left, to where the kerb would be, and stamped.

He stamped again, and walked on along that line, stamping, all the way up the dune and over the crest and out of sight.

He came back a quarter of an hour later, over the crest, walking along the same line, and his boots were not sinking.

“The edge,” he said. “Where the kerb is. Under the sand. It is a hand down, maybe two. The sand is thin over it and the stone is under the sand. She will walk on that.” He took the grey camel’s halter out of Hailu’s hand. “Not on the road. The middle of the road is lower, the sand is deeper there. On the kerb.”

Hailu looked at him, and then at his brother, and then said something to the grey camel in the sing-song voice.

She got up.

They walked on the kerb all afternoon.

It was a line under the sand that nobody could see, a hand’s breadth wide, and Na’ar walked it at the head of the string with the grey camel’s halter in his fist, feeling for it with his feet. When he lost it he stopped and cast about, stamping, until he found it again. The others followed him in single file, every camel stepping where the one in front had stepped, and the brothers stopped talking to the camels and began talking to Na’ar instead, in the same sing-song voice, which he appeared to take as a compliment.

The grey camel walked on the kerb and did not go down again.

Riarhin walked beside Meret, behind the string, and watched Na’ar’s back going up the face of each dune along a line that nobody else could see.

“How did he know?” said Meret, very quietly.

“He didn’t.” Riarhin watched him stamp. “He knows tired animals. He knows footing. He knew there was a road under the sand because you told him, and he knew a road has an edge. And he went and looked.”

Meret was silent for a while.

“I’d been trying to work out whether it was a road or a processional way,” she said. “I’ve been trying to work it out for two days. I had it in three categories. And he just walked on it.”

“You’d have got there.”

“I’d have got there.” Meret pushed her spectacles up. “He got there first, with a camel.”

They followed the road for another day.

It went north. It went dead straight, under the dunes and out of them, with its posts every thousand and some paces: twenty-four, twenty-three, twenty-two, each one counting down toward a name that none of them could read. It went past another waystation, and another. It did not turn toward anything; there was nothing on either side of it but sand. It was going somewhere a long way off, somewhere that twenty-six posts away had been worth putting on a post, and it had been built to get there with carts, and mended three times by three sets of poorer people who had still needed to get there.

Too much, Riarhin thought, walking the kerb behind the grey camel on the seventh morning. It was too much road. Nobody built a road like this, with gutters and posts and waystations with wells in them every day, to carry one thing to one tomb. It was not the road to a hiding place. Somebody had used it for a hiding place, eighty years ago, the way you might use the high road out of Silkstrand to take a thing somewhere; but the road had not been built for that. It had been built for everybody.

She did not know yet what that meant. She knew that she had been thinking of the end of this journey as a place, the way the relic dealers thought of it: one place, at the end of nothing. And it was not at the end of nothing. It was at the end of a road that had been built for everybody.

On the seventh afternoon the road went into a cutting.

It was a place where the builders had cut down through a ridge of rock rather than go over it: two walls of red stone, perhaps twice the height of a man, with the road running between them. And the cutting was full. The wind had blown sand into it for however long there had been sand, and it had filled from wall to wall and from floor nearly to the top, a long smooth slope of red sand lying in the cut like water in a trough, soft and deep, with no kerb under it that anybody could reach.

Na’ar walked into the mouth of it and sank to his knees at the third step, and came back out.

Kebede looked at the cutting for a long time. Then he walked up onto the ridge beside it and looked at the country on the other side, and came back.

“We leave it,” he said.

Riarhin looked at the road going into the sand. She had been walking on it, or beside it, for three days. It was the one straight thing in the whole of the country, and it knew where it was going, which was more than she did.

“The ridge is gravel,” said Kebede. “It’s hard going for a mile and then the country on the other side is flat. We keep the road’s bearing. We don’t need to be on it to go where it goes.” He looked at her. “If we try to dig through that, we’ll be two days and all the water we’ve got for washing. If we try to take the camels over the top of it, they’ll go in to their bellies. It’s the worse choice.”

She looked at the cutting a while longer.

“We leave it,” she said.

They went up onto the ridge in the late afternoon and along it, over the hard gravel, with the road going on under the sand in the cutting below them on their right, and on the far side they came down onto flat ground and took the road’s bearing, by the sun, and went on north beside the place where it would be.

That night Kebede measured the water in the actual skins, and wrote the number down, and showed it to her without saying anything. It was above his line. It was nearer it than it had been at the cache.

Chapter Twenty-Three: Water That Has Already Been Spent

The cistern was the first thing they had found in eight days that looked as if it might still work, and Kebede would not let anybody near it.

It stood a mile north of the cutting, on the flat ground beside the line of the road, and at first Riarhin took it for another waystation. It was a mound, like the others, red dust and pale stone. But it was larger, and higher, and the stone was showing through the dust at the top in a long smooth curve, and when they came round the mound’s western side they found the curve was a roof. It was a dome, very low, built of courses of the pale dressed stone, and it had held. The wind had piled sand against its northern side almost to the crown and scoured its southern side nearly clean.

In the scoured side there was a door.

It was not really a door. It was a square opening, waist-high, with a lintel of a single long stone across it and steps going down inside, and the sand had been blown in over the lower steps and then blown out again, so that the top of the stair was clear. Kebede got down and went to it and put his head in, and came out, and stood looking at the dome with an expression that Riarhin had not seen on his face before.

“There’s water,” he said.

There was. Riarhin went down the steps behind him with a lamp and saw it.

The stair went down perhaps fifteen feet into a chamber under the dome, and the chamber was a cistern, round, lined with the same pale stone in courses, with a walkway round the wall at the foot of the stair. Below the walkway, filling the chamber from wall to wall, black and flat under the lamp, was water. It was still. There was no smell. The air was cool, much cooler than the air outside, and dry, and the lamplight lay on the black water in a long gold line and did not move.

Riarhin looked at it and thought of Kessary, and felt the back of her neck go cold, and then saw that it was not Kessary at all. The drops on the roof fell. She watched one fall, and heard it land, a small clear sound in the dark. The air moved, very slightly, drawn in at the door and out at some vent in the roof she could not see. It was only a cistern, full of water, in a desert where they were carrying their own.

Kebede stood on the walkway looking at it and did not move for a long time.

“Nobody touches it,” he said.

He said it again at the top of the stair, to everybody, when they had all come up, and he said it in the flat heavy voice he had used at the station to tell Almaz what to do when the survey did not come back.

Nobody was to draw from it. Nobody was to put a hand in it or a cup. Nobody was to put a drop of it into any skin, or water a camel from it, or wash in it. Not until he knew what it was.

“It’s water,” said Hailu, who had been down the stair behind Riarhin and had seen it, and whose face had changed in the lamplight in a way that she understood entirely.

“It’s water,” said Kebede. “I don’t know what’s in it. I don’t know how long it’s been there. I don’t know where it came from or whether anything’s been living in it, or dying in it.” He looked at Hailu, not unkindly. “If we put it in the skins and it’s bad, we’ve got forty skins of bad water and no way to clean them, eight days from the station. If we drink it and it’s bad, we’ve got seven people with the flux eight days from the station. We’d be better off without it.”

“And if it’s good?”

“If it’s good,” said Kebede, “it’s the best thing that’s happened to anybody on this road in two hundred years. And I’ll know it’s good before anybody drinks it.”

Meret went down with her drawing board and a second lamp, and was down for three hours.

When she came up it was mid-afternoon and the heat outside the dome was like standing in front of an oven door. She sat down in the thin shade on the dome’s southern side and put her drawing board on her knees and did not say anything for a while, and the others waited.

“It’s been altered,” she said at last.

She showed them. She had drawn the chamber from above and from the side, and she had drawn the walls course by course, and on the drawing it was very plain, once she pointed at it. The dome and the upper courses of the wall were the pale dressed stone, all of a piece, laid by the same people at the same time. The lower courses, the ones that went down into the water, were not. On the northern side of the chamber, below the waterline, there was a section of wall perhaps ten feet across where the pale stone stopped and a greyer stone began, rougher, set in a different mortar.

“That’s a repair,” said Meret. “Or a blocking. There was something there once, in the old wall, an opening, and somebody filled it in with grey stone. Later. The same grey stone as the second kerbs on the road.” She tapped the drawing. “And there’s a mark on the grey stone. Above the water. Cut in the middle of the patch.”

“The comb?”

“No.” Meret looked up. “A different one. I don’t know it. It’s not the keepers’ mark. It’s not the knot. It’s a circle with a line across it.”

She was quiet for a moment.

“And the stair’s been altered too. The top ten steps are the pale stone. The rest are grey. There was a different stair, once, and somebody took it out and put in the one that’s there now.”

There were two readings, and Meret and Kebede held one each, and both of them were right to hold them, and Riarhin sat in the shade of the dome for most of the afternoon listening to them argue and did not interrupt.

Meret’s reading was that the cistern was a reserve.

The original builders had made a great covered cistern here, beside the road, to feed the waystation or the road or the country round about, with an inflow somewhere and an outflow somewhere. And then, later, the grey-stone people had come, the same people who had mended the road the second time. And they had changed it: they had blocked the outflow and rebuilt the stair, and put their own mark on the blocking. They had turned the working cistern into a sealed one. A store. Water kept back against a bad year, behind a wall, with a mark on it that said this is not to be drawn.

“And if it’s a reserve,” said Meret, “then they sealed it with clean water in it. On purpose. To be drunk when it was needed.” She looked at the dome. “And it’s still sealed. Nobody drew it. Whatever they were keeping it back for, they didn’t come for it.”

Kebede’s reading was that it was a settling tank.

He had spent his life on the salt road and in the highland passes, and he had seen a great many cisterns, and a great many of them were not for drinking out of. They were the first chamber of a system: water came in off the land, or out of a channel, full of sand and silt and whatever the wind had put in it, and sat in the first chamber until the muck went to the bottom, and then went out through an outlet high up the wall into a second chamber, clean. You did not drink from the first chamber. The first chamber was where the dirt went.

“And if it’s a settling tank,” said Kebede, “the outlet’s the thing that’s missing. The opening in the old wall that the grey-stone people blocked. They blocked the outlet. Maybe the second chamber had fallen in. Maybe the channel had silted up. Maybe they didn’t need it any more.” He looked at the dome. “And when you block the outlet of a settling tank, the water in it doesn’t go anywhere. It stays. With everything that settled out of it. For as long as it takes.”

“It’s clear,” said Hailu. “I saw it. It’s clear and black.”

“The top of it’s clear,” said Kebede. “I’d like to know what’s at the bottom.”

Riarhin had to decide, and she did not want to.

She could see both of them. She could see Meret’s cistern: a store of clean water under a dome, sealed by people who had meant to come back for it, holding a great deal of water in a place where they were short of it, eight days out. And she could see Kebede’s: a pit full of two hundred years of the desert’s dirt, with a clear skin on top of it that would look exactly the same.

She could see what the water in the skins was. She had seen Kebede’s number last night. It was above the line. It was nearer the line than it had been.

There was a gate. Meret had found it. On the far side of the chamber, on the walkway, set into the grey stone of the rebuilt stair, there was a slab with an iron ring in it, very rusted, that closed off something below the walkway: a sluice, or a draw-off, where whoever had kept the cistern could let water out at the bottom. If they lifted it, they would know in a quarter of an hour what was at the bottom of the tank. They would also have let it out. And if it was Meret’s reserve, it would go down a drain somewhere into the sand and be gone; and if it was Kebede’s settling tank, two hundred years of silt would come up with it, and the whole chamber would be fouled, and the walkway, and the stair, and whoever was standing on them.

“Is there another way?” she said. “To see the bottom. Without the gate.”

Meret and Kebede looked at each other.

“There’s an inspection point,” said Meret slowly. “On the drawing. I didn’t know what it was.” She turned the board round. “In the wall, low down, on the walkway, opposite the stair. A small square stone, set in the wall with a different mortar. It’s a plug. Somebody would have taken it out to look at the level, or to take a sample from lower down.” She looked at Kebede. “It’s below the waterline. If we take it out, it’ll run.”

“How much?”

“Through a hole that size? Slowly.” Meret measured with her hands. “As long as it’s open. If we can put it back.”

Kebede looked at the drawing a long time.

“It’ll take the rest of today,” he said. “And tomorrow. To do it properly. To see what comes out and let it settle in a skin and look at it by daylight.”

That was a day. Riarhin could see it in his face; he knew what a day was worth out here as well as she did, better. A day was a day’s water for seven people and ten camels, spent sitting beside a dome.

“Do it,” she said.

They took the plug out in the evening, when the heat had gone, and it went wrong in the way Kebede had expected, which was the only thing that made it bearable.

Kebede did it himself, with Na’ar holding the lamp and Riarhin on the walkway beside him with an empty skin, one of the spares he had made them carry, folded, all the way from Adur-Gah. The plug was a square of stone a hand across, set in the wall at the height of a man’s knee above the walkway, with the waterline a foot above it. Its mortar was old and soft. Kebede worked round it with an awl from his repair bag, very slowly, and eased it, and eased it again, and it came out into his hand with a wet sucking sound.

Water came out after it.

It came in a thick dark rope, not clear at all, the colour of strong coffee, with a smell that filled the chamber in the time it took Riarhin to breathe in. It was the smell of a pond in summer that has been left too long, and under that something sharper, like rotten eggs, like the vents at Kessary. It hit the walkway and spread. It ran brown across the pale stone toward the stair.

Riarhin got the mouth of the spare skin under it and held it there and felt the skin go heavy in her hands.

“That’s the bottom,” said Kebede. He was looking at the brown water running across the walkway. “That’s what’s at the bottom.”

He tried to put the plug back, and the plug would not go.

The mortar that had held it had come away with it, and the seat in the wall where the plug had been was rough and crumbling, and the water was coming through it now faster than before, round the edges of the square stone even when Kebede pressed it home with both hands. It would not seat. Brown water was running out round it in four thin streams and spreading across the walkway, and there was a great deal more of it behind the plug, the whole of the lower part of the tank, pressing on it.

“If it keeps coming,” said Kebede, very calmly, “it’ll run down the stair and out the door. It’ll foul the whole walkway and the stair and the ground outside where the camels are. We’ll have to clean the ropes and the gear. And we’ll have to clean the camels’ feet before they walk on anything we want to use again.” He did not take his hands off the plug. “And the only water we’ve got to clean them with is ours.”

He fixed it with the repair bag.

Riarhin held the lamp for him. Na’ar held the plug. Na’ar knelt on the brown wet walkway with both of his hands flat on the square stone and his whole weight behind them, holding it into the crumbling seat against the water. He did not move for three quarters of an hour, while Kebede worked round him.

Kebede cut strips of the heavy leather he had made them carry for mending saddles, and soaked them in the tar he had made them carry for mending skins, and warmed the tar at the lamp until it ran. He worked the tarred leather into the gap round the plug with the awl, a strip at a time, packing it in, while the brown water ran out over his fingers. When the gap was packed he put more tar over it, and more leather over that, and bound the whole of it with thin cord through two iron nails from his bag, which he drove into the old mortar either side with the hammer from his bag, and pulled tight.

The water stopped.

It did not stop at once. It ran for a while round the edge of the patch, slower, and then slower, and then it was a seep, and then a drip, and then nothing. Na’ar took his hands off the plug, carefully, one at a time, and it held.

Nobody said anything. Kebede sat back on his heels on the fouled walkway with tar to his elbows and looked at the patch for a long time. Then he picked up the awl and wiped it on his robe and put it back in the repair bag.

“That’s the bag,” he said. “That’s what the bag’s for.”

They cleaned the ropes and the gear with sand. It took all the next morning.

Sand was the one thing in that country they had enough of. They scrubbed the ropes with it, and the lamp, and Riarhin’s boots, and Kebede’s arms, and let the sun do the rest, and none of it needed a drop of the water in the skins. The walkway and the stair they left as they were. The camels had never gone near the door; the brothers had kept them picketed on the far side of the mound all night, because Kebede had told them to before the plug came out.

The skin of brown water Riarhin had caught, Kebede hung on a pole in the sun on the dome’s southern side, and they watched it through the morning.

It settled. By noon there was a hand’s depth of black sludge at the bottom of the skin and a thin grey scum on the top and between them a yellowish water that nobody would have drunk if they were dying. Kebede cut the skin open along its seam and poured the water out on the sand and looked at the sludge. It was fine and black and smelled like the chamber. And in it, when he rubbed it between his fingers, there was grit: a pale hard crust, broken up, the kind of crust that grows on the inside of a kettle.

“That’s on the walls,” said Meret, who was looking at it with him. “Down below the waterline. I saw it. I thought it was the stone.” She rubbed the grit between her own fingers. “It’s a crust. It’s from water that sat. That sat for years, and dried down, and filled again, and sat.” She looked at Kebede. “And the line where the crust stops, on the wall. It’s high up. It’s at the height of the outlet. The one the grey-stone people blocked.”

“That’s where the water used to go out,” said Kebede. “Before they blocked it. That’s where it sat to. Clean water on top, going out over the lip into the next chamber. Muck on the bottom.” He wiped his fingers on the sand. “It’s a settling tank. With the outlet gone.”

Meret said nothing for a while.

Then she took out her drawing board, and turned to the page where she had drawn the chamber the day before, with the grey patch on the northern wall and the circle with the line across it, and under the drawing she had written, in her small neat hand: sealed reserve, later period.

She drew a line through sealed reserve. It was one clean line. The words were still perfectly legible under it. Beside it she wrote: settling chamber, outlet blocked in later period. See crust line and sample, second day. First reading was wrong.

And then she dated it.

Riarhin watched her do it.

She had watched a great many people be wrong. She had been wrong a great many times herself, and the thing she had done every time was to change her mind quietly and act on the new one and never mention the old one to anybody, so that the old one might as well never have been. She had done it on the floor of Serafine’s counting room, when eleven days east had become eleven days west. She had never once written the old one down, and drawn a line through it where it could still be read, and put the date beside it.

“You kept it,” she said.

Meret looked up at her over her spectacles.

“Of course I kept it,” she said. “It was a reasonable reading. It was what the evidence looked like on the first day. Somebody coming after me will look at that chamber and think the same thing I thought, because it looks like that. And they’ll need to know I thought it too, and why, and what showed it was wrong.” She closed the board. “Otherwise they’ll think it was obvious. It wasn’t.”

They lost the day. Kebede measured the water that evening in the actual skins, and it was a day’s water less than it would have been. He showed Riarhin the number, as he did every evening. She looked at it.

It was still above the line. It was nearer to the line than it had been at the cutting.

She did not say anything about the day. She had decided to lose it at the top of the stair, and it had been right to lose it; they had forty skins of clean water and not forty skins of bad, and nobody had the flux, and the camels’ feet were clean. She could see the day going out of the water in the skins, a hand’s breadth at a time. She could see that there were other days ahead that would go the same way, for other good reasons, and that one of them would be the day Kebede said stop.

She let it go.

She sat outside the dome in the last of the light and looked at the grey patch on Meret’s drawing, and the mark on it, the circle with the line across. Somebody had come to this cistern after the people who built it, and had blocked its outlet, and rebuilt its stair, and cut a mark of their own into the wall. They had been poorer people, with poorer stone, doing the same work. They had still been here, and still keeping it.

And then they had stopped. The water had sat in the tank with the outlet blocked for however long it had been since, and settled, and gone bad, and nobody had come.

Across the fire, Kebede was going round the water skins in the dark, one at a time, putting his hand flat on each one and pressing, when everybody else had finished.

Chapter Twenty-Four: A Country in the Ground

The first channel was a line in the gravel that Riarhin took for a crack.

It was the morning of the tenth day, and they had come a mile north of the dome onto a stretch of hard flat ground where the sand had thinned to a skin over grey stone, and the wind had scoured it in long streaks. The line ran across their path from west to east. It was perhaps a hand wide and a hand deep, straight, and the sand had drifted into it and been scoured out again so that its edges showed, sharp and square.

She stepped over it. Then she stopped and looked back at it.

It was too straight. It ran away from her in both directions, across the grey flat, as far as she could see, without a bend in it. Nothing the weather does runs that straight for that long.

Meret was already off her camel.

It was a field channel. There was another one fifty paces further on, parallel to the first, and then another, and then one that ran across all three at right angles, and at the place where they met there was a block of the grey stone set in the ground with a slot cut in it for a board: a sluice, to send the water down one channel or the other. The board was long gone. The slot was full of sand.

And on the block, cut in the top of it, there was a mark. Not the comb. A different one: a triangle with a line through its point.

Meret crouched over it and did not say anything for a long time.

“It’s a field,” said Kebede. He was standing on the second channel, looking along it. “They’re all fields. The whole of this.” He turned slowly round. “Channels, a sluice at the corners, the ground between them levelled. It’s irrigated land. Or it was.”

Riarhin stood on the hard grey ground and looked at it.

She had been looking at it for an hour, riding across it, and had seen nothing but a flat where the sand was thin. Now that Kebede had said it, she could see it everywhere: the lines of the channels running away across the flat, faint as the lines on a palm, crossing and recrossing, dividing the ground into long squares. She could see the difference in the ground between one square and the next, where one had been dug deeper and the next had not, and the wind had filled them differently. It went on to the horizon. It went on in every direction she could see.

“An outpost,” she said. “To feed the road.”

Nobody answered her.

By noon they had found the boundary.

It was a wall, very low, of fieldstone, no higher than a knee, running across the fields from east to west. It cut straight through the channels without regard for them; somebody had built it after the channels were dug, across land that had already been divided. Where it crossed a channel, there was a gap left in it for the water to pass, and at every gap there was a stone standing on its end, and on every stone there was a mark.

On the south side of the wall, the stones bore the triangle with a line through its point.

On the north side, the stones bore a different mark: two short parallel lines, like a door-frame.

Sada got down off her camel and walked along the wall from one gap to the next, looking at the stones, and Riarhin watched her walk and saw the change in how she held herself. It was the change she had seen at Gedo, on the fourth morning, when Sada had put her finger on three lines in three different histories and said there was an award.

“Two districts,” said Sada.

She said it quietly. She was standing at one of the gaps with a stone on either side of her, the triangle and the door-frame.

“Two districts, and a boundary between them, and the water going through it.” She put her hand on the triangle stone. “The water comes from the south. From the road, from the cistern, from wherever it came from. It comes through this district first. And then it goes through the gap, into that one.” She looked north across the wall. “And the gaps are all the same width. Every one. Somebody measured them.”

“For what?”

“So that the district upstream couldn’t take more than its share,” said Sada. “And the one downstream could see that it hadn’t.”

They camped that night in a house.

It stood on the north side of the boundary wall, in the door-frame district, among the fields: a building of the grey stone and fieldstone mixed, with the roof long gone and the walls standing to the height of a man’s shoulder. It had four rooms round a little court, and a stair at one corner going up to where a second floor had been. The wind had kept the court mostly clear. There were a great many of these buildings, scattered across the fields in every direction; they had passed a dozen that afternoon, and there were more on the horizon, low mounds of red dust with walls showing through, like the stumps of teeth.

Riarhin went round the house before dark, as she went round every building she slept in.

The stair was the first thing.

It went up from the court at the northeast corner, a flight of stone steps against the wall, perhaps a dozen of them. It had been wide once: wide enough for two people to pass, with the steps cut from single long slabs of the grey stone. And somebody, later, had built a wall up the middle of it.

It was a thin wall, of fieldstone and mud, running up the stair from the bottom step to the top, a little off centre, so that the stair on one side of it was the width of one person and the stair on the other side of it was not quite. The narrow side had been blocked at the bottom with more fieldstone. The wider side had been used. The treads were worn on that side, dished in the middle by feet going up and down; on the blocked side they were sharp.

She stood in the court and looked at it for a long time.

Somebody had needed a room. Somebody in this house had needed more room upstairs than the house had, and had taken the room out of the stair, and had built a wall up the middle of it to make a cupboard, or a store, or a narrow place to sleep. They had gone on living here, going up and down half a stair, for long enough to wear it.

She did not know why that should matter more than the fields. It did.

In the morning Meret found the schoolroom.

It was two houses further north, and it was not a schoolroom when it was built. It had been a store. It was a long narrow building of the pale stone, the old stone, the keepers’ stone, with thick walls and small high vents under the eaves and a floor raised on low stone piers to keep the damp out. It had held grain, Kebede said, when he saw the piers; he had seen stores like it on the salt road.

Somebody had built a room against the end of it, later. A small square room of fieldstone, leaning on the store’s gable wall, with a door and a window facing south and a stone bench running round three of its walls.

And on the floor of that room, under a drift of red dust that Meret brushed away with her hand, there were pieces of pot.

Not whole pots. Broken pieces, flat ones, the size of a palm, dozens of them, lying where they had fallen. Every one had writing on it.

Meret picked one up and turned it to the light and made a sound Riarhin had not heard her make before.

It was a row of letters. The old letters, the ones Meret could read slowly, the ones on the posts and the grant and the well. They had been written with a brush, in black, in a hand that was large and careful and not very steady, one after another along the flat of the sherd, the same letter over and over. Under the row, in a different hand, a smaller, quicker, surer hand in red, one of the letters had been written again, correctly. And under that, in the large careful black hand, the same letter again, trying to copy the red one, and not quite getting the tail right. And under that, in red again, the tail.

Meret held it for a long time. She did not say anything. After a while she held it out to Riarhin.

Riarhin took it.

It was a child’s. She knew it the way she knew anything she attended to: at once and completely. She had seen children write in eight countries and she had been one, once, in a courtyard in Silkstrand with the shutters open, learning the old court forms from a woman who corrected her in a quick sure hand. The tail of the letter went the wrong way the first time, and the second time. The red was patient. The red had written it twice.

She stood in the little room against the gable end of the old store with the sherd in her hand.

Somebody had turned a granary into a school. Or had built a school against a granary, because the granary’s wall was there and was thick and kept the heat off. And had sat on the stone bench with a pot of red and corrected a child’s letters twice, and then the child had got it, or had not, and the sherd had gone on the floor with the others at the end of the lesson. And nobody had swept the floor, and then nobody had come back.

There was a shrine that was an office.

They found it on the eleventh morning, in a cluster of buildings that might once have been a village, beside a crossroads where two of the secondary roads met the line of the high road. They had begun to find secondary roads the day before: narrower than the high road, unpaved, but embanked and ditched, running off it east and west across the fields toward other clusters of mounds on the horizon. This one had a shrine at its crossroads.

Meret knew it for a shrine at once: a small square building of the pale stone, with a niche in its back wall and a stone table in front of the niche, and the niche carved round its edge with a pattern of leaves. And somebody had put shelves in it. Stone shelves, set into the walls on either side of the niche, rough fieldstone slabs mortared in, one above the other, from floor to roof. And on the stone table in front of the niche, the old altar, there were pieces of clay, baked hard, lying in heaps: tablets, the size of a hand, covered in close small writing.

“They kept records in it,” said Meret. She was standing in the doorway, not going in. “The niche is empty. They took whatever was in it out, or it went. And they put shelves in, and used it as an office. A place to keep the accounts.” She looked at the heaps of tablets on the altar. “The water accounts, I’d guess. The shares. Who took how much through which gap.”

“They turned a shrine into an office,” said Sada.

“It was the best building at the crossroads,” said Meret. “It had a good roof and thick walls and nobody lived in it.” She pushed her spectacles up. “People do. Every archive in Adur-Gah is in a building that was something else.”

The inscription was on the shrine’s lintel, and Meret had read it wrong the day before, on a different lintel, and did not know it yet.

She had found the first one on the waystation north of the dome, two days ago: a line of the old letters, cut deep in the lintel over the waystation’s door, much weathered. She had read it by lamplight that night, slowly, and had told them what it said. To the city of — and then the name from the posts, the one nobody could read — that which is owed, from the wells of the high road. And she had written it on her drawing board, under the drawing of the lintel, as dedication: tribute to the city from the road stations.

It had made sense. A road, and a city at the end of it, and the road stations sending what they owed up the road to the city. Riarhin had filed it that way.

The shrine at the crossroads had the same inscription on its lintel. Almost the same. Meret read it aloud, standing in the doorway with her head back.

To the district of — the door-frame mark, cut into the stone among the letters — that which is owed, from the district of — the triangle — by the measure of the gaps.

She stopped.

She read it again, to herself, moving her lips. Then she turned round in the doorway and looked back south, across the fields, to the low boundary wall a mile behind them with its stones standing at every gap.

“It’s not tribute,” said Sada.

Meret looked at her.

“It’s not a gift going up the road to a capital,” said Sada. “It’s the same phrase you read on the waystation. That which is owed. And here it’s between two districts. The triangle owes the door-frame, by the measure of the gaps.” She was speaking slowly, the way she had spoken at the bridge at Gedo, reading the award aloud to both banks. “The upstream district owes the downstream one its share of the water. That’s what’s owed. It’s the water.” She looked at Meret. “And on the waystation it was the road stations and the city. The road owed the city its share. And the city, I would think, owed the road something back. It was all the same arrangement.”

Meret stood in the doorway with her drawing board against her chest.

“Obligations,” she said. “Between districts. Not tribute to one city.”

“I’ve been negotiating water shares between districts for nine years,” said Sada. “It reads exactly like one of mine.”

That night they camped in the shrine that was an office, because it had the best roof, and Meret sat up late with the lamp and her drawing board and the waystation page.
