She found the line she had written two days before, dedication: tribute to the city from the road stations, and she drew one clean line through it, so that it could still be read. Beside it she wrote: obligation of share, see shrine at crossroads, eleventh day. Same formula used between districts. First reading was wrong. And she dated it.

Riarhin watched her from the other side of the fire and did not say anything.

Nobody said anything for a long time. The wind had dropped. It was very quiet in the shrine, with the heaps of clay tablets on the old altar and the empty niche above them, and outside the stars down to the edge of the world on every side, over the fields.

It was Sada who spoke first.

“How many districts,” she said. It was not quite a question. “We’ve crossed two. There were others on the secondary roads, east and west. I counted six marks, on the stones at the crossroads today. Six districts, meeting here. Each one with a boundary and a share and a mark of its own. And a road through all of them, with a post every thousand paces, counting down to a city.”

“And the keepers of the wells,” said Meret. “Over all of it. The comb and the dot. On the posts and the waystations and the great well at Beles.”

“An office,” said Sada. “Over the districts. For the water. For the road.” She looked at the fire. “You don’t have an office over six districts for the water unless the six districts have agreed there’s to be one. Or have been made to. And you don’t have a road like that one, mended three times, unless there’s somebody at the end of it who can make people mend it.”

Kebede, who had been lying with his head on a saddle and his eyes closed, said without opening them: “And people living all along it. Using it. Carts. For long enough to wear the stone.”

Riarhin sat by the fire with her knees drawn up and her arms round them, and looked at the empty niche above the altar.

She had been calling them outposts. She had called them outposts on the morning of the tenth day, standing on the first channel, and nobody had answered her. She had been thinking of them as outposts since the road came out of the sand: little stations of somebody else’s, strung out along a road that went through nothing to somewhere. Waystations, a cistern, a village or two. Places that had been put down in an empty country by a power that lived elsewhere.

There was no elsewhere. The country had not been empty. A child had sat on a stone bench and got the tail of a letter wrong twice. Somebody had needed more room and built a wall up the middle of their own stair. Six districts had argued over the width of the gaps in a boundary wall until they had agreed on one, and had cut the agreement on a lintel. People had lived here, all of it, all the way from the great well of seven courses to wherever the posts were counting down to, for long enough to wear the stone.

They had been walking through a country. It had a name, and nobody alive knew what it was.

She did not call them outposts again.

In the morning, on the way out of the crossroads, she went round the last house on the northern edge of the cluster, because she went round every house.

It was a small house. It had two doors. One was on its south side, facing the crossroads and the shrine and the road, and it was the wider of the two, with a good lintel of pale stone. The other was on its east side, facing the fields, narrow and low, with a lintel of rough grey stone.

The threshold of the wide south door was sharp. The stone was cut square and its edges were clean. Nobody had worn it.

The threshold of the narrow east door was worn through to a hollow in the middle, a deep smooth dish where feet had gone in and out, in and out, for years and years.

Riarhin stood between the two doors and looked at them.

The south door was the easier way. It faced the road. It was wide and high and had the good lintel. And somebody had kept it shut, and had gone in and out, every day of their lives, by the narrow door on the side, facing the fields.

She did not know why. There was nothing to tell her why: no bar, no blocking, no mark on the lintel, nothing Meret could have read. It might have been the wind. It might have been that the fields were where the work was. It might have been a custom of this house, or of this district, or of the whole of the country, that nobody would have been able to explain to a stranger, the way nobody at the relay house at Harbu had been able to explain the room that must be left open and not entered.

She stood there a while longer. Then she put her foot in the hollow of the worn threshold, in the place where theirs had gone, and stood in it for a moment, and went on.

Chapter Twenty-Five: Too Many Successful Answers

The town had burned, and that was why they could read it.

It stood on a low rise two days north of the crossroads, where the high road met four secondary roads at once and the posts said fourteen: a great spread of mounds and walls on both sides of the road, far larger than anything they had passed, with a ring of something that might once have been a wall round the whole of it and a gate on the road where the ring crossed it. Meret said it had been the seat of a district. Sada said it had been the seat of several. Kebede said nothing, and went to see where they could camp out of the wind.

Riarhin went to the store.

She knew it for a store from the outside, by the piers under the floor and the high small vents under the eaves, the same as the granary with the schoolroom against its gable. But this one was larger, and it had burned. The roof had come down into it in a great fall of blackened beams and fused tile, and the walls had gone red and cracked in the heat, and the fire had got in among whatever had been kept on the shelves inside. The shelves had been tablets.

Clay. Thousands of them. They had been on wooden shelves from floor to roof, sun-dried, the way the tablets on the altar at the crossroads had been sun-dried; and when the roof came down the fire had baked them. The wooden shelves had burned away and the tablets had fallen into the ash in heaps and drifts, and they had come out of it as hard as tile, harder than they had ever been meant to be. The fire that had destroyed the store had kept everything in it.

Meret stood in the doorway and looked at the drifts of tablets in the ash for a long time.

“I can’t read all of this,” she said. “I can’t read a tenth of it. Not in a year.”

“How much can you read in a day?”

Meret looked at her.

“Not enough,” she said. “Give me the day.”

Kebede gave her the day. He did it by looking at the water and then at Riarhin and then at the water again, and not saying anything, which was how Kebede gave things.

They had been leaving water behind them. Riarhin had not seen it at first; Kebede had done it without saying anything to anybody except Hailu and Zewdu. At the dome, and at the crossroads, and at two of the waystations, he had buried a skin at the foot of the cairn they built, with the top stone on, so that on the way back they would find water every day and a half and would not have to carry it all from the start. It meant the camels were lighter going north than they would otherwise have been. It meant that every day they went further north was a day further from the last buried skin.

The line in his book was the water they would need to get from wherever they were back to the nearest buried skin, and from there to the next, and so on down to Beles, at the pace of a party that might be carrying somebody. And a margin.

They were above it. He showed her.

“One day,” he said. “Here. Then we go on.”

Riarhin could not read the tablets. She could look at them.

She sat in the ash beside Meret through the whole of the long hot day with the tablets in heaps round them, and Meret read, slowly, and wrote on her board, and Riarhin looked. She took each tablet as Meret finished with it and turned it over in her hands and looked at every line of it, all the shapes, the way she had looked at the courses in the grandmother well and the tail of a child’s letter; and she put each one down and took up the next.

She could not read any of them. She had never learned the old letters and she would not learn them in a day. But she kept them. Every shape she looked at, she kept, in the order she had looked at it; and after the first hour she began to be useful, because Meret would say there was one like this earlier, the same heading, the same mark at the corner, and begin to look for it in the heap, and Riarhin would say the eleventh one you gave me, it’s under the one with the chipped corner, and it was.

It did not help her understand anything. It helped Meret compare.

By the middle of the afternoon they had forty tablets laid out on a flat stone in the shade of the store’s one standing wall, in an order Meret had made, and Meret was sitting back on her heels looking at them with her spectacles pushed up and an expression that Riarhin did not like.

“They’re orders,” she said. “Most of these. From the town. To the districts. And reports back.”

“About what?”

“The water. The road. The stores.” Meret touched the first tablet in the row. “This one’s the earliest I can find. It’s an order about a shortage. One of the eastern districts, the one with a mark like a hook. Its wells are low. The order says the town will send water from its own reserve, by cart, along the secondary road.” She touched the next. “This is the report back. From the hook district. The water came. It came in six days. The district gives thanks.”

“Six days.”

“Six days is very fast,” said Meret. “For a cart from here to the hook district and back with a reply. I’d have said twelve.” She moved her finger along the row. “And then there’s another. And another.”

It went on like that. Meret read it to them that evening in the ruin of the store, with the lamp, and it took three hours, and nobody interrupted her.

A shortage, and an order, and relief. Always relief. Always quickly: quicker than it should have been, every time. A district’s wells went low, and the town sent water, and the water came in six days, and the district gave thanks. A road was washed out in a storm, and the town ordered it mended, and it was mended in a month, and traffic came back on it. A store ran short of grain, and the town ordered grain sent from a district that had a surplus, and the grain came, and the store was full.

It worked. Every order they could read had worked.

And then the next one was larger.

The hook district’s wells went low again, the next year, and this time the order sent water from the town’s reserve and also from a neighbouring district’s; and it came in five days, and the hook district gave thanks. The year after that the neighbouring district’s own wells were low, and the order sent water to it from a third district, and from the town, and it came. The washed-out road was mended and the traffic came back on it, and then more traffic came back on it than there had been before, because the other road, the one that went round by the west, had been washed out too and nobody had mended that one. They had put everything into the first. So the first road was the road, and everything went on it, and it had to be mended again, and it was mended, very fast.

“And people moved,” said Meret. She had a tablet in her hand. She held it very carefully. “This one’s a census. Or part of one. From the town. It’s a list of households that came into the town that year, from the districts. From the hook district, from the western districts where the road was out. Forty households.” She put it down and took up the next. “And this is two years later. A hundred and sixty.”

“Because the town had water,” said Kebede.

“Because the town was where the relief came from. Every order says so. The town will send. The reserve of the town.” Meret put the tablet down on the stone beside the others. “If you lived in a district where the wells kept going low, and every time they went low the water came from the town, in six days, very fast. After a while you’d go to the town.”

The last tablets were the ones Meret did not want to read, and she read them anyway.

They were not orders from the town. They were orders to it. They had come from somewhere else, from further north, up the road; and they had a heading on them that Meret had never seen before, and a seal-mark impressed in the clay at the corner that was not the comb or the triangle or the door-frame or any of the district marks. It was a circle with a line across it. The mark on the grey-stone blocking in the cistern.

They were very short. Meret read them in a voice that got quieter as she went.

The first said that the reserve of the town was to be sent north, up the road, to the city, where the need was greater. And the town sent it, and a report came back that it had been received, in four days, and that the city gave thanks, and that the need was met.

The second said that the districts were to send their share up the road to the city by the measure of the gaps, and that the gaps would be widened. And they were.

The third said that the secondary roads were to be closed, and their traffic turned onto the high road, so that it could be kept and watched and mended; and a report came back from the town that it had been done. The traffic all went up the high road now. The high road was mended every month.

The fourth said that the households of the town were to go north.

It did not say why. It said that there was water and grain and safety in the city, and that the town’s own reserve had gone north already, and that the households of the town were to follow it, by the high road, in good order, by district, with their animals.

There was a report back. It said that they had gone.

Meret put the tablet down on the stone at the end of the row and took her spectacles off and did not put them back on.

“That’s the last one I can find,” she said. “There might be more under the ash. That’s the last one on top.”

Nobody said anything for a long time. The lamp burned. The wind had come up outside and was moaning across the open roof of the store.

It was Sada who spoke first, because she was the one who had been reading orders like these for nine years.

“It’s a government,” she said, slowly. “In a bad time. Taking powers it didn’t have before, one at a time, because each one works.” She was looking at the row of tablets. “The town answers the first shortage and it works, so the town’s the place that answers shortages. The town takes the neighbours’ water to answer the next one and it works, so the town can take the neighbours’ water. The city takes the town’s reserve. The city closes the secondary roads.” She did not look up. “Every step’s an emergency measure. Every step’s reported as a success. And by the end there’s one road and one reserve and one city and everybody’s been told to go there.” She paused. “I’ve seen it. Not like this. Not so far. But I’ve seen a council in Mbaro take the Widow’s grants one bad year at a time, and every year it was the only thing to do.”

Kebede was sitting against the store’s standing wall with his arms on his knees.

“It’s a system with no spare,” he said.

They looked at him.

“Every time something broke, they mended the one thing that broke and put the load on it. The west road went and they didn’t mend it; they put everything on the high road. The hook district’s wells went low and they didn’t dig new ones; they sent water from the neighbours. The neighbours went low and they sent water from further off.” He rubbed his face. “You do that on the salt road, you get one well that all the caravans use, and one road that goes to it, and it works for a while very well. And then one year that well goes, and there isn’t another, because nobody’s used the others in ten years and they’ve silted.” He looked at the tablets. “They drove it till there was nothing left over. Nothing spare. And every year it worked. That’s the trouble. It worked every year until the year it didn’t.”

Meret had put her spectacles back on. She was looking at the circle with the line across it, impressed in the corner of the last tablet.

“The grey-stone people,” she said. “The ones who blocked the cistern. They had this mark. They were from the city, or they worked for it.” She touched the mark with one finger. “And they built with the old stones where they could. On the road, on the cistern. They used the keepers’ mark on the posts and they put their own on top.” She was quiet. “When you have something you can’t deal with, and you’ve inherited a set of forms from people who dealt with things before you, you use the forms. You put the old seals on the new doors. You try to shut it in the way the people before you would have shut it in.” She looked up. “There’s something in the later tablets that I think is a ward formula. The language of a closing. It’s on the orders from the city. As though they thought they were containing something.”

“Containing what?” said Riarhin.

“I don’t know,” said Meret. “I don’t know if they knew.”

Three readings. Riarhin sat in the ash and looked at the three of them, one after another, and each of them was right.

A government taking emergency powers, because every emergency power worked. A system driven past anything spare, because every time it was driven harder it held. A people using their fathers’ forms to shut something in that their fathers had never had to shut in. She could see all three. She could see Sada’s council and Kebede’s well and Meret’s door, and she could see that they were all looking at the same tablets and seeing the same thing from three sides.

And there was a thing none of them had said, because it was not a reading. It was not anything anybody could put on a drawing board. It was only the shape.

Every order had worked. That was what she kept coming back to, sitting in the ash with the shapes of forty tablets in her head in the order she had seen them. Not most of them. Every one they could read. Every shortage had been answered and every answer had come quickly, more quickly than a cart could go, and every answer had been larger than the last and had drawn from further off. And every one of them had left the next shortage a little bigger, and a little nearer to the town, and then to the city, until everything that could move had moved up the one road toward the one place that was answering.

She had heard of a thing once that gave you what you wanted and folded a clause into it.

She did not say it. She did not know it, and she was not going to say a thing she did not know in front of three people who were being careful. She did not even let herself finish thinking it. She put it with the shapes of the tablets, in the order she had seen them, and left it there.

She sat up late after the others had gone to sleep, in the doorway of the store, with the lamp out and the stars over the town.

The town went away from her on every side in the starlight, mound after mound, wall after wall, the whole of it. A hundred and sixty households in one year had come into it from the districts, because it had water. And then the town had been told to go north, in good order, by district, with their animals, and it had gone.

She thought of the schoolroom against the gable of the granary. The red hand, patient, correcting the tail of the letter twice. She thought of the house at the crossroads with its wide south door kept shut. She thought that the child who could not get the tail of the letter right had perhaps gone north up the high road too, with its family, in good order, by district, toward the place where there was water and grain and safety. Toward the answer.

She did not know. There was nothing to tell her.

She had carried the names of the dead for a very long time, in the order she had been given them, and had never once been able to put any of them down. She sat in the doorway of the store and looked out over a town that had been told to leave and had left, and found that she did not have a single name for any of them. Not the child. Not the red hand. Not whoever had built the wall up the middle of the stair. They had not left any, or she could not read them, and there was nobody to ask.

It was worse than having them. She had not known that it would be.

In the morning Meret packed the forty tablets in straw, in a spare pannier, all of them, in the order she had laid them out. Kebede looked at the weight of the pannier and then at the water and then at Meret, and then he took two skins’ worth of fodder off the grey camel and gave the pannier to her instead.

“They go back,” said Meret. “To the archive. They’re the only record anybody has of any of this.”

“They go back,” said Kebede. “The fodder doesn’t. We’ll be lighter coming home.”

They went out through the gate of the town onto the high road. It was clear of sand here, running straight and pale between the mounds, and they went up it north.

The posts said thirteen. Then twelve.

At the eleventh post, on the afternoon of the thirteenth day, Na’ar stopped his camel at the head of the string and did not go on. He sat there looking north, and after a while he pointed.

There was something on the horizon.

It was not a city. It was a line: low, dark, grey-brown, lying along the edge of the world from east to west as far as Riarhin could see. At first she took it for hills. It did not have the shape of hills. The top of it was not still. It moved, very slowly, the way smoke moves on a day with no wind, rising and folding over and rising again, and under the brown there was a darker colour, and in the darker colour, far off, there was a flicker, once, and then again, that was gone before she could look at it.

Nobody said anything.

The posts went on in front of them, north, along the high road, counting down toward the line.

Chapter Twenty-Six: The Storm Line

They watched it for two days before anybody went near it, and it was Kebede who made them.

The line did not come closer. That was the first thing, and it took Riarhin most of the first afternoon to be sure of it. They had gone on up the high road from the eleventh post toward it, and it had grown from a line on the horizon to a wall, and then, at the ninth post, to something that filled the whole of the northern sky from east to west; and there it had stopped growing. It was not coming to them. It was standing where it was. They were coming to it.

At the eighth post there was a waystation, larger than the others, with walls standing to the height of a man on three sides and a court in the middle. Kebede stopped the string there, in the lee of the south wall, and got down off his camel, and looked at the storm for a long time.

“Here,” he said.

It was a storm. There was no other word for it that Riarhin knew. It was a wall of moving air and dust perhaps a mile and a half north of the waystation, running across the country from horizon to horizon, and it went up into the sky higher than she could judge, a thousand feet, two thousand, brown at the bottom and grey above and then a dark bruised colour at the top where it met the ordinary blue. It did not move forward. It turned over on itself and went on turning. Dust rose up its face in great slow billows and fell back and rose again. Inside it, now and then, there was lightning. It did not fork. It lit the inside of the wall from within, a flat white flicker, and was gone, and there was never any thunder that reached them.

And around it the weather was ordinary.

That was the thing she kept coming back to. The sky over the waystation was blue, with a few high thin clouds going over from the west at the ordinary speed of high thin clouds. The wind at the waystation came from the north, steady and warm, the same wind that had blown on them every day since Beles. It did not come from the storm. It blew toward the storm, and into it, and did not come out the other side.

At the eastern edge of the world, where the wall of dust met the horizon, Riarhin could see a single ordinary cloud, white, going slowly past the end of the storm, and it did not bend toward it or away.

“It has an edge,” said Kebede. He was standing on the top of the waystation’s south wall with his hand over his eyes. “Look at the ground in front of it.”

She climbed up beside him and looked.

There was a line on the ground. It ran along the foot of the storm, perhaps a quarter of a mile in front of it, the whole length of it, as far as she could see in both directions. On the south side of the line the country was what they had been walking through for two weeks: gravel, sand, the pale mounds of ruins. On the north side of it the ground was scoured. It was scoured down to bedrock, grey and smooth and bare, as though somebody had taken a broom to it every day for a thousand years; and the scoured ground ran up to the foot of the storm and into it and was gone.

“That’s where it’s always been,” said Kebede. “That line. The ground on the other side’s been blown clean since before anybody put a post in this road.” He lowered his hand. “It doesn’t move. It’s been standing there, turning over, in the same place, for longer than the road’s been under the sand.”

They watched it for the rest of that day and all of the next.

Kebede made them. He made them sit in the lee of the waystation’s south wall and watch it, in turns, from dawn to dark, and write down what they saw, with the time. Riarhin did not argue. She sat her turns on the wall with Meret’s spare drawing board and a stick of charcoal and watched the storm turn over and wrote down what it did.

It did the same thing all day.

It turned. It flickered. The dust went up its face and came down. The lightning came and went inside it with no thunder. The line on the ground did not move and the ordinary clouds went past its eastern end. By the end of the first day she had forty lines on the board and they all said much the same thing, and she began to think Kebede had given them the job to keep them from going near it.

On the second morning, at the hour before dawn, it changed.

She was on the wall. It was the dark cold hour when the wind dropped, the only hour of the day when the wind on the high road ever dropped, and the stars were still out over the waystation and fading in the east. And the storm, in front of her, went quiet.

Not still. It did not stop turning. But the dust stopped going up its face. The billows lay down. The lower part of the wall, the brown part, thinned, the way a curtain thins when you hold it up to a lamp, and through it, for perhaps a quarter of an hour, she could see a darker shape standing up out of the scoured ground a mile beyond the line.

Walls. The shape of walls, very high, and towers on them, and behind the walls, higher up, something that caught the first grey light from the east and held it.

Then the wind came back, from the north, as it did every morning. The dust went up the face of the storm again. The wall thickened. The shape was gone.

She wrote it down, with the time, and her hand was not quite steady.

“The wind,” said Kebede, when she showed him.

He had got up in the dark to see it too, because he had been expecting something like it; he had been on the south wall beside her for the last part of it, and had not said anything until it was over.

“The wind drops before dawn,” he said. “Every day since Beles. For about a quarter of an hour. And the storm’s fed by it.” He looked north, at the wall, turning again in the grey light. “The wind blows into it. All day, from here, from the south. It doesn’t come out. It goes in and goes up and turns over and comes down again. And when the wind drops, there’s less going in.” He was quiet. “So for a quarter of an hour, it thins. At the bottom. Where the wind would be feeding it.”

“Every day?”

“I don’t know. I’ve seen it once.” He looked at her. “I’m not going to tell you it’ll do it again tomorrow. I’m not going to tell you a quarter of an hour tomorrow is a quarter of an hour. I’ve seen it once and I’ve got one line on your board.”

They went toward it at noon on the second day, to see what it would do, and it turned them back.

Kebede and Riarhin and Na’ar, on foot, with a rope between them. They walked north from the waystation across the gravel toward the line on the ground, and the wind blew at their backs, steady and warm, and the storm grew in front of them until it filled everything. At the line, where the gravel stopped and the scoured grey rock began, Kebede stopped and put his hand up.

The wind on the scoured side was different. Riarhin felt it as soon as she put a foot over the line. It was stronger, and it was not steady. It came in gusts that pushed at her back and then stopped and then pushed again, harder, and there was grit in it, fine grit off the scoured rock, that stung the backs of her legs through her trousers. And under the sound of it there was another sound, from in front of them: a low steady roar, like surf a long way off, that she felt in her chest rather than heard.

They went two hundred paces onto the scoured rock. The storm was a quarter of a mile away. The gusts were strong enough now that Na’ar, who was the heaviest of them, had to lean back into the rope to keep his feet.

Kebede put his hand up again.

He did not say anything. He looked at the storm for a long moment, and then back at the line on the ground behind them, and then he turned round and walked back. They went with him.

At the line, on the gravel, the wind was steady and warm again.

“What changed?” said Riarhin.

“The wind in front of it,” said Kebede. “It gets worse the nearer you go, all the way to the wall. At the wall, I don’t think you’d stand. Not in the middle of the day.” He wiped grit out of his eyes. “We marked it. Two hundred paces over the line, and I had to lean. That’s what changed.”

She wrote it on the board. It was a small thing, and she wrote it down, because Kebede had made it clear that the small things were the only things they had.

Kebede found the cutting in the afternoon.

He had been walking along the line on the ground, east, with Hailu, looking at it, for two hours. When he came back he had a different face on.

A mile east along the line, there was a channel. It came out of the south, across the fields, straight as the high road, and it was cut down into the ground perhaps ten feet deep and five wide, with walls of the pale stone on both sides, and it went across the line onto the scoured rock and on, north, toward the storm, still straight, still cut deep, until the storm took it.

“It’s a water-cut,” said Kebede. “A service channel. They brought water along it into the city, or out of it. It’s got steps down into it at the line, and a walkway along the bottom, for the men who kept it.” He sat down heavily in the lee of the wall. “And it’s cut down. Ten feet. Below the scoured rock. Below the wind.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“The wind goes over it,” said Kebede. “At the bottom of the cut, in the walkway, a man’s head is below the level of the ground. The gusts go across the top. I stood in it at the line and I could hear them going over, and down in the bottom there was hardly any wind at all.” He looked north. “It goes all the way to the storm. And into it. I don’t know how far in.”

“If we went along it at the hour before dawn,” said Riarhin, “when the storm thins.”

“If we went along it at the hour before dawn,” said Kebede, “we’d be in the bottom of a cut, below the worst of the wind, while the storm was thinnest. For about a quarter of an hour. If it does what it did this morning. Which I can’t tell you it will.” He looked at her. “And then the wind comes back, and the storm thickens, and wherever we are in the cut, we’re there.”

Meret found the crossing that night.

She had gone to the cutting with Kebede and a lamp after dark and walked along the walkway at the bottom of it as far as the line on the ground, and a little way beyond, onto the scoured side, below the wind. She came back two hours later and sat down by the fire in the court of the waystation and did not say anything for a while. Sill was on her shoulder. He did not usually ride on her shoulder. He had his claws in her robe and his round bronze eyes shut.

“There’s a gate,” she said at last. “In the cutting. Four hundred paces past the line. Where the walls of the cut go up to meet the old wall of the city, or the outer works of it, and the channel goes under.”

“A gate?”

“Not a door. There’s no door. There’s an arch where the cut goes under the wall, and the arch is open.” She held up her chain of iron rings in the firelight. “It’s a threshold. It’s been made one. By people who knew what they were doing. There’s a closing on it. I felt it as soon as I got near the arch, the way you feel a draught under a door. It’s old and it’s still working and it’s shut against wind.”

Nobody said anything for a moment.

“Against wind,” said Sada.

“Against weather. Against what’s outside coming in. The keepers put it there, or somebody before them.” Meret turned one of the rings in her fingers. “It isn’t shut against people. There’s nothing on it about people. I think it was there so that the men who kept the channel could get into the city along the cut when there was weather outside. It holds the weather back from the arch. A little way. Not far.”

“Can you use it?”

Meret was quiet for a long time.

“I can lean on it,” she said. “For a little while. I can find where it’s thin and put my weight behind it, the way you’d put your shoulder to a door that’s sticking. It’ll hold the wind back from the arch for as long as I can keep leaning.” She looked at the ring in her fingers. “Not the storm. I can’t do anything about the storm. Just the arch. Just that one crossing, for as long as it takes five people to go through it. And then I’ll have to let go.”

“And what does it cost?”

Meret looked up at her.

“It’ll cost me,” she said. “I don’t know how much. I’ve never leaned on anything that big.” She put the chain back inside her dress. “I’ll tell you when I know.”

Riarhin went first.

Nobody argued about it. It was what she was for. She went at the hour before dawn on the third day, alone, down the steps into the cutting at the line, with a rope round her waist and the other end in Na’ar’s fist at the top of the steps, and Kebede beside him with a lamp hooded down to a slit.

The walkway at the bottom of the cut was smooth pale stone, very old, worn in the middle by feet. The walls went up on both sides of her higher than her head. Above her, over the top of the cut, the wind was going across the scoured rock with a sound like a river, and down in the bottom it was almost still. The stars were over her in a strip between the walls. Ahead, at the end of the cut, the storm stood up into the sky, and it was thinning. She could see it thinning, the brown going grey, the grey going thin, and through it the dark shape of the walls.

She went along the walkway as fast as she could go and still see where she was putting her feet.

Four hundred paces past the line the walls of the cut rose up to meet a greater wall, and the channel went under it through an arch. She stopped.

Meret was right. She could feel it: a change in the air round the arch, a stillness, the way the air had been still round the great cistern at Kessary. It was not like Kessary. It did not press on her. It was the stillness of a room with the door shut against a wind.

She went under the arch.

On the other side there was a court. It was a square court of pale stone, open to the sky, perhaps forty paces across, with the channel running down the middle of it in a stone trough and walls on all four sides with doorways in them. The storm was over the court. She could see it, looking up, turning over in the dark, thinning; and in the court it was quiet. The dust came down into it very gently, like snow on a day with no wind, and lay on the stone.

She stood in the middle of the court with the rope trailing back behind her under the arch and looked at the doorways. One of them had a stair going down.

She did not go down it. She had told Kebede she would not. She stood in the court and counted the doorways and looked at each of them and kept them, and looked at the channel and where it went, and at the stair, and at the dust on the floor of the court, which was smooth and even and had no marks in it at all.

Then the wind came back.

She heard it before she felt it: the roar of the storm, which had been low and steady, rising. The dust coming down into the court began to swirl. She turned and went back under the arch at a run, and the moment she was through it the wind hit her in the face, down in the bottom of the cut, a hard gust full of grit coming along the walkway from the south where it should not have been; and she put her head down and went along the walkway into it with the rope in her hand, hauling on it, and felt Na’ar at the other end begin to haul too.

At the line, at the steps, he pulled her up out of the cut onto the gravel and sat her down, and held her there with a hand on her shoulder while she got her breath.

It had been, Kebede said, looking at the sky, just under a quarter of an hour.

She told them what she had seen, in the court of the waystation, while the storm turned over behind her in the morning light.

A court, open to the sky, still. A channel down the middle of it. Doorways on four sides. A stair going down. Dust on the floor, smooth, no marks.

“No marks,” said Sada.

“Nobody’s walked in that court in a long time.” Riarhin looked at her hands. “Or the dust comes down fast enough to cover it. I don’t know which.”

Kebede was writing it all down in his book.

“You were in there a quarter of an hour,” he said. “You didn’t go past the court. If we go in, we’ll need longer than that. We won’t come out again in the same quarter of an hour.”

“No.”

“So we’ll be in there till the next time it thins. A day. If it thins again. Which I don’t know.” He did not look up. “And if it doesn’t, or if it thins for less time, or if the wind comes back while we’re in the cut, we’ll be inside with what we’ve carried and nothing else.”

“Yes.”

He put the pen down and looked at her.

“Then here’s what I want,” he said.

He wanted the camels and the stores left at the waystation, with Hailu and Zewdu, behind its walls, where there was shelter from the north wind and a court they could hold. He wanted the brothers to have enough water there to go back down the high road to the nearest buried skin and on to Beles on their own, and more than enough, and he measured it out of the skins himself and put it in their charge in front of everybody.

He wanted them told what to do. He told them. They were to wait five days. Every morning at the hour before dawn they were to go to the head of the cutting and watch it, and if the five came back along it they were to be there with water. If the five did not come back by the fifth morning, they were to take the camels and go south, down the high road, to the station at Beles, and tell Almaz, and tell the warden. They were not to go into the cutting. Not for any reason. Not if they saw a light in the storm. Not if they heard somebody calling their names.

Hailu listened to all of it and asked a great many questions and then said that he understood. Zewdu said nothing at all and then went and stood by the camels with his hand on the grey one’s neck for a while.

He wanted the five of them to carry water for three days, each, on their backs. Not more. Three days would get them in and out of the city on two intervals, if there were intervals, and a day over. It would get them back out through the cut to the waystation, where there was more. It was a light reserve. It was all a person could carry and still go fast along a walkway in the dark.

And he wanted the condition said again, out loud, in front of everybody, at the head of the cutting, with their packs on. So on the fourth morning, in the dark, with the brothers standing at the top of the steps holding the lamp and the five of them loaded and roped, Riarhin said it.

“If the water on our backs falls to a day and a half,” she said, “we turn round. Wherever we are. Whatever we’ve found or haven’t. We go back to the court and wait for the storm to thin and come out.” She looked at Kebede. “If anybody’s hurt so that they can’t be carried along the cut at a run, we turn round. If Meret says there’s a door that’s shut for a reason, we don’t open it. And if Kebede says stop, we stop.”

“And if you can see it,” said Kebede. “If it’s there in front of you. And the water’s at a day and a half.”

“Then we turn round,” said Riarhin.

She said it looking north, at the storm.

The wind had dropped. It was the dark cold hour before dawn, and the storm had begun to thin, and through the brown of it she could see the walls again, the towers on them, and behind the walls, higher up, the thing that caught the first grey light from the east and held it. A dome, perhaps. Or a roof. Pale, and very high, standing up out of the dust in the middle of a city that nobody alive had a name for.

It was nearer than it had been. It was about a mile.

“Then we turn round,” she said again, so that Kebede could hear her say it twice.

Chapter Twenty-Seven: The City That Was Kept

The storm thinned late, and they were slow, and the wind came back while they were still in the cut.

Riarhin had known it would be different with five. She had gone along the walkway alone the morning before at the pace of a woman with nothing on her back and nobody to wait for, and it had taken her a third of the interval to reach the arch. Five people with three days’ water each and a rope between them do not go at that pace, however they try. Kebede was fifty-five and built for endurance and not for running. Meret had her drawing boards. Sada had never in her life gone anywhere at a run with a load on her back and did it now, grimly, with her white wrap tied round her waist and her face set, and did not complain once. Na’ar went last, carrying more than anybody, with the end of the rope in his fist and his eyes on the four in front of him.

They were two hundred paces from the arch when the roar of the storm began to rise.

Riarhin heard it and did not stop. She heard Kebede behind her say something short in the highland tongue and not stop either. The dust coming down into the bottom of the cut began to swirl, and then the first gust came along the walkway from behind them, from the south, the wrong way, full of grit, and hit them in the backs so that Sada stumbled and Na’ar caught her by the pack and set her on her feet without breaking his stride.

A hundred paces. The walls of the cut rose ahead toward the great wall and the arch in it. The gusts were coming harder, and the air in the bottom of the cut was full of grit now, and she could not see the arch clearly for it.

Fifty.

Then Meret went past her.

She did not run. She walked, fast, with her head down and one hand inside the neck of her dress, and she went past Riarhin on the walkway and on toward the arch, and at the arch she stopped under it and turned round to face them, and took her hand out of her dress with the chain of iron rings in it, and put her other hand flat on the stone of the arch beside her.

And leaned.

Riarhin did not see anything happen. There was nothing to see. There was a small woman in a dusty robe standing under an arch at the end of a stone channel with a chain in one hand and the other hand on the wall, leaning on the wall as if it were a door she was holding shut against somebody pushing from the other side.

And the wind stopped.

Not everywhere. Behind them, in the cut, it went on; she could hear it coming along the walkway from the south and feel it in her back. But for twenty paces in front of the arch, the air in the bottom of the cut went still. The grit fell out of it. The dust lay down on the walkway. It was as though a wall of glass had come down across the cut twenty paces from the arch, and the wind was going against the glass and not getting past it.

They went through it. Riarhin first, then Kebede, then Sada, then Na’ar with the rope. Past Meret, under the arch, into the still court beyond. And then Na’ar turned round in the court and took hold of the rope with both hands and said, “Now,” and Meret let go of the wall.

She came through the arch backwards, on the rope, because Na’ar pulled her, and the wind came through behind her like a door slamming.

And then it stopped again, at the arch, as it had stopped the morning before. The old closing held. Whatever Meret had leaned on was still there, as it had been since the keepers built it, holding the weather out of the court; she had only held it a little further out than it wanted to go, for a little while. In the court the dust came down gently out of the storm overhead, like snow on a day with no wind, and lay on the stone.

Meret sat down on the floor of the court.

It had cost her. Riarhin saw how much when she knelt down beside her.

Meret’s face had gone the colour of the dust on the walkway, grey and dry. There was blood coming out of her nose, not much, a thin line of it running down over her lip, and she did not seem to have noticed. Her hands were shaking. She was holding the chain of rings in her lap and looking at it.

One of the rings had cracked. It was an old ring, dark, with a mark cut in it that Riarhin could not read; and a line ran across it now from the outer edge to the inner, clean, as though somebody had struck it with a chisel.

“That one was my first,” said Meret. She said it quite calmly, in a voice that did not sound like hers. “My teacher’s teacher gave it to her. A cellar door at Thepyrgos. It was the first crossing I ever held.” She touched the crack with one finger. “It’ll hold together. It won’t hold anything else.”

Sill was on her shoulder with his claws in her robe. He had not let go since the waystation. He had his eyes open now, round and bronze, and he was looking at the arch.

“Can you walk?” said Kebede.

“In a while.” Meret wiped her nose with the back of her hand and looked at the blood on it with mild surprise. “Give me an hour. I’ll walk.” She looked up at Riarhin. “I won’t be able to do that again for a while. Not on the way out. You’ll need the interval.”

“I know.”

“I wanted you to know I know.” Meret closed her eyes. “An hour.”

They gave her the hour, and Riarhin spent it on the court, and on what she could see from the court, which was the city.

It was built for people.

She had not expected that, and she stood in the court with the dust coming down on her shoulders and looked at it for a long time before she understood why it was the thing she had not expected. She had seen the walls from outside, through the thinning storm: great walls, very high, towers on them. She had been thinking of it as a fortress, a vault, a place built to keep something in or keep something out. The walls were that. They were enormous, three rings of them, one inside the other, the outer ring the one the cut ran under and the inner ones rising behind it; and they were built of the pale keepers’ stone in blocks the size of a cart, fitted without mortar, so close that she could not have put Toothpick’s point between them. The work that had gone into them was a kind she did not have a measure for. She thought of Aske at Kessary burning one joint of lead in an afternoon, and of Kebede mending a single plug with an hour of tar and leather. Somebody had made these walls. It must have taken generations. It must have taken everything the six districts could send up the road, for longer than anybody had lived.

And inside the walls the city was the size of a person.

The court opened on its northern side into a street, and she went to the opening and looked along it. It was a street like a street in Mbaro or Adur-Gah, perhaps four paces wide, paved, with a gutter down one side, and houses on both sides of it. Houses. Doors at the height of a person, and windows at the height of a person looking out, and here and there a bench against a wall beside a door where somebody had sat in the evening. A stair going up the outside of a house to a flat roof. A niche beside a door with a lamp-ledge in it. The houses went up the street, two floors, three, and the street went up a slope between them, toward the inner walls and the pale roof beyond them that caught the light.

The dust lay on all of it. It lay on the benches and the steps and the lamp-ledges, smooth and even, a hand deep, and in the street it lay like snow, and there were no marks in it at all.

It was the stillest place she had ever stood in. The storm turned overhead with its long low roar, and the dust came down out of it, and in the street nothing moved.

She did not go into the street. She had said she would not, and it was not the way.

It was not the way because the court was not a court. It was the start of something, and Kebede and Meret, when Meret could walk, worked out what.

It was an intake. The channel that came along the cut and under the arch ran down the middle of the court in a stone trough, and at the north end of the court the trough went into a building: a long low block of pale stone against the inner side of the outer wall, with a row of arched openings along its front. The channel went in at one end. Inside, when they went in with the lamps, there were galleries. Long vaulted chambers one behind the other, each lower than the last, with the channel running through all of them in a series of basins and steps, and at the end of each basin a lip of stone where the water had spilled over into the next.

“Settling,” said Kebede. He stood in the first gallery with his lamp up, looking along the line of basins going away into the dark. “Like the dome. But done properly. The water comes in dirty from the channel and goes through basin after basin, and every basin it leaves something behind, and it comes out the other end clean.” He looked at the lips of the basins. “And the outlets are all open. Nobody blocked these.”

At the far end of the settling galleries there was a stair going up, and at the top of the stair a road.

It was a raised road. It ran along the top of a long wall inside the outer ring, above the level of the streets, paved with the pale stone and edged with a low parapet, and it went north from the top of the settling galleries in a long slow curve toward the inner walls, with the houses of the city below it on either side. It was wide. It was wider than the streets below it. It had been swept, once, and kept clean; there were no ruts in it, and no mending. It had been built once and never needed mending.

“That’s a processional way,” said Kebede. “That one.” He looked along it. “Nobody drove a cart on that.”

It went to the inner wall. At the inner wall there was a gate, and on the far side of the gate, Meret said, looking at her drawings of what could be seen from the court, there was a sanctuary. The great pale roof was over it. And beside the sanctuary, built against it, there was another long low block like the intake: more service buildings, with small high windows, and doors that Meret, looking at them through her glass from the top of the raised road, said were inspection rooms. Places where somebody had checked what came in.

And behind those, against the sanctuary’s own wall, with no windows at all, there were chambers that the raised road did not go to.

The inner gate was shut, and it had been shut more than once.

They reached it in the middle of the first day, along the raised road, walking slowly because Meret still could not walk fast and because the road was dust a hand deep and none of them wanted to go faster on it than they could see. It was a great arched gate in the inner wall, twice the height of a man, and it had been closed with a door of bronze that had gone green; and the door had been sealed.

The seals were of different dates. Meret stood in front of them for an hour.

The oldest was in the stone of the arch itself: the comb and the dot, the keepers’ mark, cut deep, the same as on the posts and the waystations and in the grandmother well at Beles. It had been cut when the arch was built.

Under it, on the bronze of the door, somebody had impressed a great seal of lead, and the lead had a circle on it with a line across it. The grey-stone people. The people from the city who had sent orders to the town. They had shut this door, and sealed it, and put their mark on it.

And on the lead seal, over the circle with the line across it, somebody had set a third seal, small, in brown wax, crushed and cracked with age. It was a square with a knot inside it, a knot that had no ends and went over and under itself four times.

Riarhin looked at it for a long time.

“Eighty years,” she said.

“About that.” Meret was looking at it through her glass. “It’s the same wax as your file. The same make.” She lowered the glass. “They came here. They came along the road and in along the cut and up the raised road to this gate. And they didn’t open it. They put their seal on the grey-stone people’s seal and left it shut.”

“Then how did they get in?”

Meret looked at the gate for a long moment.

“They didn’t come in this way,” she said. “They came to it. They sealed it. So that nobody else would come in this way either.” She turned round and looked back along the raised road. “They went in somewhere else.”

Riarhin found it by asking the only question that mattered, which was how you carry a case.

A light party. Six water-skins past the last water. Water enough to come back slowly. And a thing they were carrying, eighty years ago, that was small enough to be carried by a light party and important enough to have been carried at all. It would be in a case. Nobody carries a thing like that loose. The file had said the article twenty-two times and never once said how big the article was, but the bill from the dyers’ street had said what the saddler was paid to make: frames for six water-skins, harness for the animals, and one carrying-chest with poles.

She had it. She had not thought about it since Hawa’s front room, and she had it.

A chest with poles. Two people, one at each end. You cannot carry a chest with poles along a raised road and up to a gate and through it if the gate is shut, and you cannot carry it up a stair that turns tighter than the length of the poles, and you cannot carry it through a door that is lower than a man’s shoulder with the poles still in. A carried chest needs a straight way, and a wide one, and one that goes where you want it to go.

She went back along the raised road, looking at everything that went off it.

There were stairs down from the raised road into the streets on either side, every hundred paces, and she looked at every one. They were steep and they turned. She went down two of them to look at the bottom and they came out in the streets among the houses, in the smooth dust, and she came back up.

And near the top of the settling galleries, where the raised road began, there was a door in the inner face of the wall that carried it. It was a service door. It had no carving round it. It opened on a passage going north inside the wall, under the raised road, straight and level and lit by slits in the wall at intervals, and wide enough for two people side by side.

The door had been replaced. The frame was the old pale stone, but the hinges were iron, and the iron was newer than anything else she had seen in the city; it was rusted, but it was not rusted through. It had been put there by people who needed a door that would open and shut, long after the people who built the frame had gone.

And the floor of the passage was worn. Not everywhere. In two lines, a stride apart, running down the middle of the passage and away into the dark: the wear of feet walking in step, one behind the other, carrying something between them, a great many times. The dust had come in and lain on it, but the dust could not fill a hollow in stone.

She stood in the doorway with the lamp and looked at the two lines going away into the dark, and then she went back up and got the others.

The obvious way was quicker, and Kebede found out why they had not used it.

There was a stair. It was not a service stair; it was the great stair, at the end of the raised road, beside the sealed gate, going down off the processional way on the outside of the inner wall and round its foot, a broad flight of steps under an arcade, clearly built to be seen and walked on by a great many people on a great occasion. It went down to the level of the inspection rooms and the chambers behind the sanctuary. It would have taken them there in a quarter of an hour.

Kebede would not go down it. He went to the head of it and looked at it for a long time. Then he went down the service stair at the end of the settling galleries, to the level of the streets, and along the foot of the inner wall with his lamp until he was underneath the great stair; and he was gone for half an hour, and he came back grey with dust.

“It’s hollow,” he said. “Underneath. It’s built on vaults. Big ones, stone ones, holding it up.” He wiped his face. “And somebody’s been in the vaults and taken the stone out. Not all of it. The facing, the good dressed stone. From the piers that hold the vaults up.” He looked at the great stair. “They left the core. Rubble and mortar. It’s standing because nobody’s walked on it. It’ll stand for a while yet if nobody does.”

“Who took it?”

“The grey-stone people,” said Meret, before he could answer. “It’ll be the grey-stone people. They mended the road with it. And the cistern. And the kerbs. They didn’t have a quarry out here. They had the city.” She looked at the great stair. “They robbed the parts of the city they didn’t need to hold up the parts they did.”

Kebede nodded. He did not say anything else about it. He looked at the service door with its iron hinges, and the passage going away north inside the wall, straight and level and worn in two lines a stride apart.

“That one,” he said. “It’s ugly and it’s dark and somebody carried a chest down it on poles. That’s the one.”

They went into the passage in the afternoon of the first day, with the lamps, in single file: Riarhin, Kebede, Sada, Meret, Na’ar.

It went north inside the wall, under the raised road, for a long way. The slits in the wall let in a grey light from the storm and the dust came in through them in thin falling streams. The two worn lines went on in front of them in the floor, a stride apart. Now and then, where the passage met another passage going off to one side, there was a door, and the doors to the sides had been shut and barred and had the knotted seal in brown wax across the join, and the passage they were in went on straight past them.

Some routes intact because somebody had blocked the others. Riarhin walked past a dozen barred doors with the knot on them and understood that she was walking the one way through this part of the city that somebody, eighty years ago, had meant to be walked. Every other way had been shut, carefully, so that a person coming along this passage with a lamp would not have to decide which way to go. It had been decided for them.

She did not like it. She kept walking.

At the end of the passage, where it came up under the inner wall at the level of the inspection rooms, there was a gallery. And across the gallery, from wall to wall, there was a gate of bronze with a great iron wheel beside it, and behind the gate, in the dark, a sound that Riarhin had not heard since the rock at Kessary.

Water. Held back. A great deal of it, not moving, on the other side of a door.

Chapter Twenty-Eight: What the Building Still Does

Kebede stood in front of the gate with his lamp for a long time and did not touch anything, and Riarhin had learned by now that this was the most useful thing he ever did.

It filled the gallery. It was a single leaf of bronze, green and scaled, the full width of the passage and higher than a man, set in a slot in the stone floor and rising into a slot in the vault overhead. There was no handle on it. There was no lock. Beside it, on the right-hand wall, there was an iron wheel as big as a cartwheel, on an iron axle that went into the stone. And above the wheel, high up the wall, perhaps twenty feet up, there was a ledge with a low parapet, and a narrow stair cut in the wall going up to it, and on the ledge the dark shapes of more iron.

Behind the gate, water. They could all hear it now. It was not the roar of the storm, and it was not the running of a channel. It was the sound Riarhin had heard in the rock at Kessary, and at the dome: water in a very large space, held, not moving, pressing on whatever held it.

“It’s a lock,” said Kebede at last.

He worked it out with the lamp and with his hands and with Meret’s drawing board, and it took two hours, and he did not let anybody touch the wheel until he had finished.

The gate did not open by being pushed. It opened by rising. The bronze leaf was hung on chains that went up into the vault over a pair of great drums, and the drums were turned by the wheel; and turning the wheel by hand would not lift it, because it weighed as much as a house. What lifted it was water. Somewhere up in the wall above the vault there was a tank, and the tank was a counterweight, and when you let water into the tank it grew heavy and went down and pulled the gate up. The wheel only opened the way for the water.

And the water came from behind the gate. From the great held body of it that they could hear.

“You turn the wheel,” said Kebede, “and you let water out of there into the counterweight tank. The tank fills and drops and the gate rises. And then you’re through.” He moved the lamp. “And then the tank’s full, and the water in it has to go somewhere, or the gate stays up and never comes down, and that’s not how anybody builds a gate. So there’s a drain. At the bottom of the tank. When the tank’s done its work, you open the drain and the water goes out of it, and the gate comes down again.”

“Where does the drain go?”

Kebede looked at her. Then he looked down at the floor of the gallery, and at the passage behind them, the long straight passage under the raised road with its two worn lines a stride apart, going back to the settling galleries and the intake court and the cut and the arch and the way out.

“That’s the question,” he said.

It went down. Of course it went down. Water goes down, and the passage was the lowest thing near the gate.

Kebede found the outlet of the drain in the passage wall ten paces back from the gate, a round mouth of stone at the height of a man’s knee, with a grating over it. Behind the grating a pipe went into the wall and up. When the gate had last been used, the counterweight tank had emptied itself through that pipe, into the passage, and the passage had carried the water back the way they had come, down the slope to the settling galleries and the channel, where the water had gone on its way.

“And that’s fine,” said Kebede, “if you’re a keeper, and the settling galleries are working, and the channel’s running, and you’ve got another way out.” He crouched by the grating. “We haven’t. We’ve got this passage. If we let the counterweight tank empty into it, it’ll be under two feet of water from here to the galleries for as long as it takes to run away. Maybe a day. Maybe longer, if the galleries have silted.” He looked up. “And we’ll be on the other side of the gate, with the water between us and the cut.”

Nobody said anything for a while.

“The custodians came this way,” said Riarhin. “Eighty years ago. With a chest on poles. Through this gate.”

“Then they had a way to do it,” said Kebede. “And they didn’t flood their own road.”

The way was on the ledge.

Riarhin went up. The stair cut in the wall was too narrow for Kebede and too steep for Meret, and Na’ar was too heavy for the treads, which were worn and old and had never been meant for anybody who did not know them. She went up it with a lamp in her teeth and her hands on the wall, twenty feet, and came out on the ledge and stood there, and looked.

It was an inspection platform. It was where the keepers had stood to look down into the counterweight tank and see how full it was. The tank was below her, a great square pit in the stone, dry, the bottom of it lost in the dark, with the chains going down into it from the drums and the counterweight hanging at the end of them, a block of stone the size of a wagon. And in the wall of the tank, at its foot, there were two outlets.

One was the drain. She could see the round mouth of it, and the stone valve beside it, a great round stone set in a slot, and the lever above it that would lift the stone and let the water out, down the pipe, into the passage.

The other was a second valve. It was smaller, and newer. The lever for it was iron, and the iron was the same rusted but not rusted-through iron as the hinges on the service door; and the pipe it opened went not down into the wall toward the passage, but sideways, into the dark, the other way.

She called down what she saw. Kebede made her describe it twice.

“They put in a second drain,” he said. “The custodians. Or somebody. They didn’t want to flood the passage either.” She could hear him thinking. “Where does it go?”

She held the lamp out over the pit as far as she could reach and looked along the line of the sideways pipe, and could not see.

“I don’t know,” she said. “Away from the passage.”

“Is the lever free?”

She put her hand on it. It moved: a finger’s breadth, grinding, and then stopped. She looked at it closely with the lamp. There was a pawl on it, a toothed iron catch that held it open once it was lifted, and the pawl had rusted through at its pin and was lying loose in the slot, broken.

“It’ll lift,” she said. “It won’t stay lifted.”

It took all three of them, and it was the kind of work Riarhin would have liked to watch rather than do, and she did it.

Kebede came up the stair after all, slowly, one tread at a time, with Na’ar below him on the lower treads with a hand flat on his back, and the repair bag slung round his neck. On the ledge there was just room for two people. He knelt by the new lever and looked at the broken pawl for a long time in the lamplight. Then he took out the last of the iron nails and the hammer and a strip of leather, and began to make a new pin.

Na’ar could not come up. He was too heavy for the ledge. But the lever for the sideways drain had a second arm, Kebede found, that came down through a slot in the floor of the ledge and out through the wall of the gallery below; it had been built so that a keeper on the floor could hold the drain open while another up on the ledge saw to the tank. Na’ar took hold of it. When Kebede said now, Riarhin lifted the lever on the ledge with both hands, and it came up grinding, and Na’ar below took the weight on the lower arm and held it there.

He held it for as long as it took Kebede to drive a new pin through the pawl with the hammer, and bind it with the leather, and set it in the teeth, and drive a wedge of iron nail behind it to be sure. It was the better part of an hour. He did not move. He stood in the gallery below with both hands on the iron arm and his feet set on the old stone floor, and Riarhin, looking down at him over the parapet, could see the arm trying to come down against him every time Kebede’s hammer struck. It never came down a finger’s breadth.

“Let go,” said Kebede at last.

Na’ar let go, slowly. The lever stayed where it was. The new pawl held it.

Then Kebede went down the stair, slowly, and stood at the wheel, and looked at Riarhin on the ledge, and at Na’ar, and at the gate.

“If I’m wrong,” he said, “the passage floods and we’re on the far side of it. You’ll have a day to find another way to the cut, and so will I.” He put his hands on the wheel. “I don’t think I’m wrong. I think somebody else came here with the same problem and put in the second drain so they wouldn’t have to find another way.”

He turned the wheel.

Water came into the counterweight tank from somewhere in the wall with a sound like the sea coming into the cave at Kessary, all at once, a great hollow roar. Riarhin on the ledge above it felt the whole wall shake. The tank filled, black, rising up the walls of the pit toward her in the lamplight, and the great stone block at the end of the chains began to sink into it, and as it sank the drums in the vault turned with a long grinding shriek, and the bronze leaf of the gate began to rise out of its slot.

It rose to the height of a man and stopped. The tank was full.

Kebede took his hands off the wheel.

“Now the drain,” he said.

Riarhin had already put her hand on the lever of the old drain, the one that went down to the passage, and not lifted it. She did not lift it. She stood on the ledge with the lamp and waited, and below her the water in the counterweight tank lay black and full and did not go anywhere, because the only drain that was open was the new one, the sideways one, and it was already open, held by Kebede’s pawl.

And the water in the tank began, very slowly, to go down.

It went sideways. She could hear it: a new sound in the wall, running water going away into the dark somewhere to the east. It did not go into the passage. She leaned over the parapet and looked down at the round mouth of the old drain in the passage wall ten paces back from the gate, and it was dry.

“It’s going east,” she called down. “Not into the passage.”

Kebede did not answer. He was sitting on the floor of the gallery with his back against the wall, with his eyes shut.

The gate stayed up, because the counterweight was still heavy, and would be until the tank drained. They went under it into the dark on the other side, and the dark on the other side was the inspection rooms.

They were a row of chambers along the foot of the sanctuary wall, each opening off a corridor, each with a stone bench along one side and a stone table in the middle and a small high window; and on the tables, under the dust, there were tools. Brass scales. Measuring rods. Seals and seal-boxes. A pair of tongs. Whatever had come into the sanctuary had been brought here first, and put on the tables, and looked at, and weighed, and written down. Riarhin went along the corridor past the open doors and looked into every one.

At the end of the corridor there was a door that was not open.

It was a narrow door, in the sanctuary wall itself, with a lintel of a single block of the pale stone, and the door in it was stone too: a slab, smooth, with no handle and no hinges that she could see. The two worn lines on the floor went up to it, a stride apart, and stopped.

On the lintel there was an inscription. Meret was already reading it.

She read it for a long time. When she had finished she stood back from the door with her drawing board against her chest and her lamp held up, and looked at it, and did not say anything.

“It’s a ward,” she said at last. “It’s shut. It’s the same kind of closing as the arch, but it’s not against weather. It’s against people.” She held the chain up in the lamplight. “It admits one kind of person. It’s very old and it’s still working.”

“What kind?”

Meret looked at the inscription again.

“The heir of the house,” she said. “The heir of the house may enter, and none other.”

Nobody was the heir of the house. There was no house. It had been gone longer than anybody could count, and whoever had been its heir, and whoever had been their heir after them, and nobody alive knew so much as the name of the city.

Riarhin stood in the corridor and looked at the stone door with the worn lines going up to it and stopping.

“Can you lean on it?” she said. “Like the arch.”

“No,” said Meret.

She said it at once, flatly, and Riarhin turned and looked at her.

“The arch was holding weather out,” said Meret. “I leaned on it and pushed the weather back a little way and let it go again, and it went back to where it had been, and nothing changed. This isn’t that.” She was holding the cracked ring on her chain between her finger and thumb. “This is shut to keep people out of whatever’s behind it. I don’t know what’s behind it. I don’t know whether it’s shut to keep people out or to keep something in, and I don’t know if I lean on it whether it’ll go back afterwards. Or whether it’ll stay open. Or whether leaning on it will change what it’s holding.” She let the ring go. “I said I wouldn’t open anything I thought was shut for a reason. This is shut for a reason. I don’t know what the reason is. That’s the whole of why I won’t.”

“It’s the way they went,” said Riarhin. “The custodians. The lines go up to it.”

“Then they had a way to do it that wasn’t me leaning on it,” said Meret. “I’m not going to be the one who finds out whether there was another way by breaking this one.”

Riarhin stood there with the lamp.

She wanted to argue. She could feel it in her chest, the whole shape of the argument, ready: that the custodians had gone through, that they had come out again, that the ward had not hurt them, that there was a quarter of a day’s water less in every pack than there had been this morning. She could feel every word of it.

She had agreed to this. At a table in the royal archive, with a lizard on the threshold, before anybody had known there would be a door. If Meret says there’s a door that’s shut for a reason, we don’t open it.

She did not say any of it.

It was Sada who read it again.

She had been standing at the back of the corridor, out of the way, the whole time, with her white wrap tied round her waist and dust in her hair. She came forward now and stood beside Meret and looked up at the lintel, and read it herself, slowly, in the old letters, moving her lips.

“Heir,” she said. “Is that the only reading?”

Meret looked at her.

“It’s the usual one. It’s the word for somebody who has a house after somebody else.”

“It’s the word we use for that now,” said Sada. “In the chancery we’ve got four hundred years of copies of copies at Ayna, and half the words in the oldest ones don’t mean what they meant.” She pointed at the second word in the line. “What’s this?”

“House.”

“It’s not house. Or it’s house the way the chancery says the house of the Crown. It’s an office. The same word’s on the Ayna precedent. The carriers of the grain country, under the house of the king. It means the office. The people who hold a charge for the king.” She moved her finger along the line. “And this isn’t heir in that sense. It’s somebody who takes up a charge after somebody else has laid it down. A successor in office. A keeper after a keeper.” She was quiet for a moment. “And the rest of it. Read me the rest of it.”

Meret read it. It went on after and none other, in smaller letters, weathered, a line and a half that she had passed over because it was damaged and the first line had seemed to say everything.

“…who comes to remove what must not remain,” she read, slowly, “and has declared it, and will answer for it.”

Sada stood back from the door.

“It’s not a throne,” she said. “It doesn’t want a king. It wants a keeper. Somebody who holds the charge of removing what shouldn’t stay here, and has said so openly, and will be answerable for what they take.” She looked at Meret. “That’s an office. It’s an office with conditions. It’s somebody who comes under a commission.”

Nobody said anything for a long moment.

Riarhin looked at Sada in the lamplight. She thought of the paper in the warden’s office that Sada had signed with the lion seal, and the Council voting eight to three, and the terms: that anything recovered would be declared, truthfully and in full, to the Crown’s representative; that it would be answered for.

“You hold a commission,” said Meret slowly. “For the Crown. To declare what’s found and bring it back and answer for it.”

“I hold a commission from a Crown that didn’t exist when this door was made,” said Sada. “I’m not the keeper of anything here. I’m not the successor to anybody’s office. I’d be lying if I said I was.” She was very steady. “I can say what I am. That’s all.”

Meret looked at the door, and at the chain in her hand, and back at the door. Riarhin watched her think, and it was a kind of thinking she had not seen before: slow and exact and completely without hurry, the way she had drawn a line through sealed reserve on the second day at the dome.

“It doesn’t want a lie,” said Meret. “A ward like this. It isn’t asking who you are. It’s asking whether what you say you are is what it admits.” She lifted the chain. “I can put the question to it. Properly. In the form. Not here is the heir. That’d be false and it would know. But: here is one who comes under a commission to remove what must not remain, if it must not remain, and has declared it, and will answer for it. Which is true.” She looked at Sada. “If it’ll take that, it’ll take it. If it won’t, it won’t, and we go home.”

“And if you ask it,” said Riarhin, “does that change what it’s holding?”

“No,” said Meret. “Asking isn’t leaning. I’m not forcing anything. I’m putting a true application to a door that was built to hear one.” She paused. “It might say no.”

It took Meret a quarter of an hour to make the form, and Sada another quarter to say it, and Riarhin stood at the back of the corridor with Kebede and Na’ar and did nothing, and it was the longest half-hour of the whole journey.

Sada stood in front of the door with her hand flat on the stone. She did not raise her voice. She spoke in the old letters, slowly, reading from Meret’s board by the lamp that Meret held, and she said who she was and what she held and on whose authority, and what she had come to do if it needed doing, and that she had declared it, and would answer for it. Meret stood beside her with the chain of rings in her hand and her eyes shut.

When it was done nothing happened.

Then something did. It was not a sound, or a light. Meret opened her eyes and said, very quietly, “Oh,” and the stone slab in the doorway was not there any more. It had gone down, into the floor, into a slot, without a sound, so smoothly that Riarhin had not seen it go. Beyond it, a passage, dark, going on.

And behind them, at the far end of the corridor, back past the inspection rooms, there was a long low grinding, and a thud.

Na’ar went back to look. He came back.

“The gate,” he said. “The bronze one. It has come down.”

They had known it might. Kebede had said so, sitting against the wall with his eyes shut after the tank filled. A door that lets somebody in does not always let them back the way they came; and a keeper who comes to remove what must not remain would come in one way and go out another, with whatever they had removed, and would not want the way in to stand open behind them.

Kebede had found the other way while they were on the ledge. The sideways drain from the counterweight tank went east, and he had followed the sound of it along the inspection corridor with his ear to the wall, and at the east end of the inspection rooms, beyond the last of them, there was a culvert: a low stone channel going down through the foot of the inner wall, where the drained water went. It was wet now. It had not been wet for eighty years. It went east and down, toward the settling galleries, by a different road. It would be knee-deep in water from the tank for a while.

It would take them out.
