“It’s slower,” said Kebede. “It’s wet and low and we’ll be bent double for three hundred paces. It comes out at the east end of the settling galleries, near the court. It’ll do.” He looked at Riarhin. “It’s there. That’s what matters. We’ve got a way out that doesn’t need the gate and doesn’t need Meret.”

Riarhin looked at the dark passage beyond the ward, with the two worn lines on the floor of it going on.

“Go on, then,” said Kebede.

The failure came on the other side, in the first chamber, and it came the way Kebede had said on the salt road that failures always came: from something that had been sound when you looked at it once.

The passage beyond the ward went on for thirty paces and came out into a chamber with a floor of great slabs of the pale stone, laid over a void. Riarhin went across it first, with the lamp, looking at every slab, and it was sound. She went across it and back. Kebede went across it after her, slowly, and looked at the edges of the slabs where they met the walls, and said it would do. Sada crossed. Meret crossed.

Na’ar came last, and he was carrying Meret’s pannier of tablets from the burned town, and Kebede’s repair bag, and his own pack, and half of Meret’s, because Meret was still grey from the arch and could not carry her own.

The slab in the middle of the floor went under him.

It did not break. It tilted, all at once, the whole of it, like a trap, pivoting on some edge underneath that had been holding it level for as long as nobody heavy had stood on it; and Na’ar went down with it, sideways, into the dark underneath, with the pannier and the bag and the packs.

He caught the edge of the next slab with one hand.

Kebede was nearest. He got down on his belly on the far side and got hold of Na’ar’s wrist, and Riarhin was there a moment after him, and between them they got a grip on his pack straps. He was hanging over the void with his legs in the dark and the tilted slab against his side, and the slab was grinding back toward level with his weight still against it, closing on him like a door. He let go of the pannier. He did not let go of the bag. He got his other hand onto the edge and heaved, and they heaved, and he came up out of the gap onto the floor with the slab grinding shut behind him on the hem of his coat.

And his left leg did not come with the rest of him quite fast enough.

He did not shout. He lay on his back on the floor of the chamber breathing very hard through his teeth, and after a while he said something in Ykrul that Riarhin had heard him say perhaps twice in all the years she had known him.

The slab had closed on his shin as it came back to level. It had not broken the bone. Riarhin had her hands on it within a moment, and Kebede with her, and she could feel the bone under her fingers and it was whole. But it had opened the leg from the knee almost to the ankle in a long ragged tear, deep, and it was bleeding a great deal. And when he tried to sit up he put his hand to his side and stopped, and she saw his face go grey.

“Ribs,” he said. “On the edge. When I caught it.”

They bound the leg with everything in the repair bag and Sada’s white wrap, which she took off without a word and tore in strips. It stopped bleeding after a while. Kebede felt his ribs, carefully, one after another, and said that two of them were cracked and none of them were through, and that he would be able to breathe if he did not laugh.

“I have not been laughing,” said Na’ar.

He sat up. He did it slowly, with Riarhin’s arm behind his shoulders, and then he sat there on the floor of the chamber with his leg out in front of him wrapped in strips of a Taharkan envoy’s wrap, and looked at it.

“I can walk,” he said. “Not fast. Not carrying.” He looked at the gap in the floor where the slab had tilted, now closed and level again, sound as it had been. “The tablets are gone.”

Meret made a small sound and did not say anything.

Kebede sat back on his heels and did the arithmetic. Riarhin watched him do it. He did not hide it.

The water on their backs. The time it had taken to reach the gate, and to open it, and to put the question to the ward. The culvert out, which was slower than the passage, and wet, and three hundred paces bent double, with a man who could walk but not fast and could not bend double without his ribs. The court, and the interval, which was a quarter of an hour at dawn if it came at all, and the cut, and four hundred paces along it to the line.

A man with a torn leg and cracked ribs could not go four hundred paces along the cut in a quarter of an hour. Not with the storm thinning. He might, with a man at each side. He would need all of them.

“That was the margin,” said Kebede. “The day over. That’s what it was for. Somebody who couldn’t go fast.” He looked at Riarhin. “We’ve spent it.”

“How long have we got?”

“We go out through the culvert tonight. We’re in the court for the dawn interval. We take him along the cut with everybody on him.” Kebede looked at the passage going on beyond the chamber, into the dark, with the worn lines on its floor. “Whatever’s in there, you’ve got till then. The time to go and look and come back here. Once.” He was very steady. “If it’s not where the lines go, or it’s not something we can carry, or it’s not something we can find by then, we leave it.”

Riarhin looked at him. Then at Na’ar, sitting on the floor with his leg bound in a white wrap and his hand on his side. Then at the dark passage, and the lines.

“We leave it,” she said.

Na’ar looked up at her.

“Go and look,” he said. “I will sit here. I am not going anywhere without you, and you are not going anywhere without me, and it is a very small chamber and I have seen the floor.” He shifted his leg and made a face. “Go and look, and come back, and we will go home. I would like my supper.”

Chapter Twenty-Nine: Not All Prizes

The worn lines went on for forty paces and stopped at a room that somebody had built inside another room.

Riarhin went first with the lamp, and Meret behind her, and Sada, and Kebede. Na’ar came too. He had said he would sit on the floor of the tilting chamber and wait, and they had left him there; and a quarter of an hour later, while Riarhin was still standing in the doorway at the end of the passage looking at what was in front of her, she heard him coming along it behind them, slowly, one hand on the wall and his breath going in and out through his teeth. Nobody had asked him to. He came up beside her in the doorway and leaned on the frame and looked.

“I wanted to see,” he said. “After all that.”

The outer room was old. It was the pale stone, the keepers’ stone, a square chamber with a vaulted roof and niches round its walls, and it had been something once, a treasury or a strongroom or a chapel; there were marks on the walls where things had been fixed and taken down. The niches were empty. The floor was bare.

In the middle of it somebody had built a second room.

It was small, perhaps four paces on a side, and it had been built of the grey stone and of rubble and mortar, rough, without any care for how it looked. It did not reach the vault. It stood in the middle of the old chamber like a shed in a church. It had one doorway, low, with no door in it, facing the passage. On the lintel of the doorway, pressed into a daub of clay that had been smeared over the stone for the purpose, there was the knotted seal, the square with the closed knot in it, and the clay had dried and cracked and still held the shape.

The worn lines went up to the doorway and stopped.

Meret stood looking at the little rough room for a long time.

“They built it,” she said. “The custodians. Eighty years ago. They brought the stone for it from somewhere else in the city. It isn’t part of anything.” She looked at the rubble walls, the careless mortar. “They weren’t builders. They built it to be a place where one thing could be put and left alone.”

Sill climbed down off her shoulder, slowly, and went across the dust to the doorway of the little room and lay down on its threshold with his chin on the stone, and shut his eyes.

Meret watched him. So did Riarhin.

“He does that,” said Meret. “Any threshold. He did it at the Council hall.”

Inside, there was a box.

It was not a tomb. It was not anything that had been made for a tomb, or for a king, or for a god. It was a stone chest, long and low, about the length of a man’s arm and half as wide, cut from a single block of the grey stone, with a lid of the same stone lying on it. Riarhin had seen its like on the tables in the inspection rooms, smaller, holding seals: a keepers’ box, for something that had come in and been weighed and written down and needed to be kept. Somebody had brought it here from the inspection rooms, or from somewhere like them, and set it in the middle of the rough little room on the bare floor, and put the lid on it, and gone away.

It was the only thing in the room.

On the lid, cut into the grey stone in the old letters, there were two lines.

Meret knelt down in front of it with the lamp and her glass. She did not touch it. She looked at the letters for a long time, and moved the lamp, and looked again. She took out her drawing board and copied them, and looked at her copy, and then at the stone.

“They cut this,” she said. “The custodians. It’s new work. New for this place. They learned the letters from the stones, the posts and the lintels, and cut it in the old letters so that it would be read by whoever could read the rest of it.” She moved the lamp. “They weren’t very good at it. The second line’s damaged. Or they made a mistake and tried to mend it.”

“What does it say?”

Meret did not answer at once. She put her finger an inch above the second line and moved it along the letters without touching them, and stopped at one place, where the stone had flaked away in a small chip across the beginning of a word.

“If you read it the way it looks,” she said slowly, “it says all treasures are sought.” She kept her finger where it was. “But the verb’s the wrong shape for that. It’s the shape that takes the negative. And there’s the ghost of a stroke here, in the chip, that would be the negative, if it were whole.” She sat back on her heels. “It isn’t all treasures are sought. It can’t be. The grammar won’t have it. Somebody who knew the letters but not the tongue very well would read it wrong. The negative’s been knocked off.”

She read both lines aloud, then, in the old tongue, and then in Ecktorian, slowly.

Not all prizes are worthy.

Not all treasures are sought.

Nobody said anything.

It was very quiet in the little rough room. Sill was asleep on the threshold. The lamp burned. Outside, beyond the old chamber and the passage and the tilting floor and the corridor of inspection rooms and the walls, the storm was turning over with its long low roar, so far off that in here it was hardly a sound at all.

Riarhin looked at the two lines.

They did not say anything about a demon. They did not say anything about a wish. They did not say what was in the box, or how to open it, or what would happen if you did. They did not threaten. They were a warning, cut by people who had brought something here from very far away and built a room round it and left, in letters they had had to learn from the stones in order to cut it. They had thought whoever came next would read the rest of the city, so they had cut the warning in the city’s own letters.

Not all prizes are worthy. That she understood. She had heard it said, in other words, in eight countries, by people who had been disappointed.

The second line she did not understand at all, and she stood there with the lamp and looked at it until she knew she was not going to.

“It’s a warning,” said Sada quietly.

“It’s a warning,” said Meret. “It doesn’t say of what.”

The lid took them the better part of an hour, and they did it properly, because it was the only way they knew how.

It was heavy. It was a single slab of the grey stone, the whole length and breadth of the box, and it sat in a rebate cut round the box’s upper edge so that it could not be slid. It would have to be lifted, straight up, and then moved off. None of them could lift it. Na’ar could have, a day ago. He stood in the doorway with his hand on his side and looked at it and did not offer.

Kebede looked at it for a long time.

He had rope. He had the iron bar from the inspection rooms, a long one, used for something they had never found out. He had two of the tongs, and the last of the leather, and Meret’s drawing board, which he borrowed without asking and gave back without comment. He set the bar under the edge of the lid at one end, on a fulcrum of the tongs laid flat, and packed leather under the bar so that it would not chip the rebate; and he had Riarhin and Sada put their weight on the far end of the bar together while he watched the lid, and it came up a finger’s breadth at that end, and he pushed a wedge under it. Then the other end. Then the first end again, with a thicker wedge. It came up a finger at a time, level, with the wedges building under it, and nobody pulled on anything or pushed anything faster than Kebede said.

When it was up a hand’s breadth all round, he put the rope round it and over the old stone beam of the doorway lintel, which Meret said would take the weight, and Na’ar sat on the floor of the old chamber outside with the end of the rope round his back and his good leg braced against the wall, and took the weight on his body without using his arms, because his ribs would not have let him. And they walked the lid sideways off the box on the rope, a hand’s breadth at a time, and lowered it to the floor beside the box, and it was done.

Nobody had bled. Nobody had given anything up. Nothing had been asked of anybody except care and an hour.

Riarhin held the lamp over the open box.

It was very small.

That was the first thing and it stayed the first thing for a long time. She had been carrying it in her head for years as a word, and for a year as a road, and the word and the road had been so large that she had given it, without ever deciding to, a size to match. It lay in the bottom of the long stone box on a fold of cloth that had once been some colour and was now the colour of dust. It would have fitted in her closed hand.

A brass case. That was what it was, first. A small brass case, round, about the size of a large coin, hinged along one edge and closed, with a loop at the top and a short chain through the loop, dark with age. Round the case, tying it shut, there was a cord of old waxed thread; and on the cord, where its ends met, a seal of brown wax, crushed and cracked, with a square and a closed knot in it.

A brass reliquary. That was what the second description in Serafine’s file had said. A brass reliquary. She had it in the clerk’s hand, exact, on the floor of a counting room in Silkstrand with Lena holding the lamp.

Meret looked at it and did not move.

“Don’t,” she said. “Not yet. I said I’d look first. Let me look.”

She looked for an hour, and she did everything she had said she would do, and nothing she had said she would not.

She did not touch it with her hands. She took out the chain of iron rings and held it over the box, and went along it, one ring at a time, and held each ring over the brass case for as long as it took her to breathe in and out; and she did it with her eyes shut, and her face was quite still. When she came to the cracked ring she did not use it. She went on past it to the next. She did it twice, the whole chain, every ring but one.

Then she cut the cord.

She did it with Riarhin’s knife, which she asked for, and she cut it on the far side of the seal from the knot so that the seal stayed whole on its piece of cord, and she lifted the seal out and laid it on her drawing board. And then, with the tongs from the inspection rooms, not her fingers, she opened the brass case on its hinge.

Inside it, set in a frame of brass that fitted the case exactly, there was a disc of green glass.

It was dark green, the green of deep water, and about the size of the ball of Riarhin’s thumb, and slightly domed, and very clear; the lamplight went into it and came out the other side changed. The brass frame round it was fine work, finer than the case: a ring of thin brass leaves, pressed and chased, each leaf with a vein in it, closing round the glass like the petals of a flower that has not quite opened. And across the glass, from its upper edge toward the centre, there was a flaw. A thin pale line inside the glass, fine as a hair, running in a slight curve for perhaps a third of the disc’s width, and stopping.

Riarhin had it before she had finished seeing it.

The first description. The oldest of the three in Serafine’s file, in the first hand, eighty years ago and more. Green glass in a brass frame, the frame of leaves; a flaw in the glass like a hair, from the upper edge toward the centre, curved. She had read it on the floor with the lamp. She had not thought about it since, because she had been told for years, by everybody, that the description was somebody else’s stone.

Saikou’s stone, from the repository by the mine, had been a green stone in a setting of brass, not to be worn. There had been nothing in that about leaves, or a flaw like a hair.

This had leaves. This had the hair.

“I can tell you what I haven’t found,” said Meret.

She had put the tongs down. She was sitting on her heels beside the open box with the chain of rings in her lap, and her face was as grey as it had been under the arch, and there was a line between her eyebrows that Riarhin had not seen there before.

“There’s no answer in it,” she said. “To any of the rings. I held every one I’ve got over it that I could use, and I’ve got closings in this chain from eleven houses of the Gate and from the Mistlands and from two shrines at Thepyrgos and from a well in the Himadri that my teacher’s teacher said held something back for three hundred years. If there were a patron in it, one of those would have known. None of them did. There’s no spirit in it that I can find. There’s no invocation on it, or in it, or cut round it. There’s no ward on it: nothing’s holding it, nothing’s keeping it in. The ward on the door out there was holding people out of this room, and the room was built round the box, and the box is only a box.” She looked at the green glass in its frame of leaves. “And there’s no curse on it of any kind that I know how to look for. I know how to look for a great many.”

She was quiet.

“I haven’t found any of those things,” she said. “That’s what I can tell you. I’ve looked for them with everything I have and I haven’t found them.” She looked up at Riarhin, and her eyes behind her spectacles were very steady. “That’s not the same as saying they’re not there. It’s not the same as saying it’s safe. It’s what I found. I won’t say more than that, and I won’t say less, and I’ll write it down exactly like that.”

“That’s all I asked,” said Riarhin.

“I know.” Meret looked back at the glass. “I’d have liked to find something. It would be easier.”

Then there was the decision, and it was Riarhin’s, and she made them all say their piece first.

She did not have to. She had the commission; she was its leader; the warden had written her name on it. She could have reached into the box. She stood with the lamp and looked at the four of them in the rough little room, and at Na’ar in the doorway, and asked them.

Sada said it first, because it was hers to say. “If we take it, it’s under the commission. Declared. Here, now, to me, in front of all of you. I’ll write it down. It goes back to Adur-Gah under the Crown’s custody and it’s examined there before anybody decides anything else about it, before it’s sold or kept or given or thrown in a well.” She was very steady. “That’s what the door out there let us in on. I said we’d answer for it. If we take it, we answer for it.”

“And if we don’t?”

“Then I write down that we found it and left it, and why.” Sada looked at the box. “Either way it’s written down.”

Kebede said his next. “Whatever we’re doing, we stop doing it now. The examination’s done. Meret’s done it. If we look any longer we’re eating into the time to get Na’ar out of here.” He did not look at the box at all. He looked at Riarhin. “Take it or leave it. Then we go.”

Na’ar, from the doorway, said: “I would like to go home.” He shifted his leg. “I can walk if somebody holds me up. I would like to start soon.”

Meret said nothing. She had said what she had found.

Riarhin stood over the open box with the lamp.

She wanted it. She could feel that she wanted it the way she had felt, at Ayna, that she wanted to leave the delegation at the next fork and go up the goat track alone. She had wanted it since a room above a tavern in Kolmar, when she had put her hand flat on four sheets of paper and said a word. She had come across a sea for it, and through a country, and up a road nobody had used in two hundred years. It was in front of her. It was the size of the ball of her thumb.

She could also give reasons. She made herself give them, out loud, because the others had given theirs.

“It’s the thing,” she said. “Not something like it. The description with the leaves and the hair is the oldest one there is, and it’s not the Thepyrgos stone, and this matches it. The seal on the cord is the same seal that’s on the last entry in my file and on the gate outside. The custodians brought it here; the lines on the floor go up to this door. It’s what they carried.” She looked at the glass. “It’s small. It’s on its own. It isn’t holding anything up; Meret says nothing’s holding it in. We’ve got a way to carry it out that doesn’t touch it, and a Crown that’s said it’ll take custody of it and look at it before anybody does anything else. That’s what we agreed. At the warden’s table, before anybody knew what it would be.”

She stopped.

“And there’s a warning,” she said. “On the lid. From the people who brought it here. They didn’t say why.” She looked at the two lines cut in the grey stone. “It’s a reason not to. It’s not a reason that tells us what the danger is, or how to keep clear of it. It’s a reason to be careful. It’s the only reason on the other side, and it’s a real one.”

Nobody argued with her. She had not expected them to. She had said everything that was true, and it was not enough to settle anything, and it was all there was.

She reached into the box.

She picked it up.

The brass was cool. It was no heavier than a brass case the size of a coin with a glass in it. The chain was dark and a little stiff and moved between her fingers the way old chain moves.

Nothing happened.

She stood there with it in her hand and waited, because she could not help waiting; she had carried the paragraph for too long. She waited for warmth, or for light in the glass, or for a voice. She waited for the storm to change its note. She waited for the floor of the room to move, or for something to stand up in the old chamber outside, or for Meret to make a sound.

Nothing happened.

The lamp burned. Sill slept on the threshold. The storm went on turning over far away outside, at exactly the pitch it had been turning at since they came into the cut.

She closed the brass case over the glass, carefully, with her thumb, and it closed on its hinge with a small click. She took a square of clean linen out of the pack at her waist, one of the squares Tigist had put in the physician’s bag at Adur-Gah for dressing wounds, and folded the case into it, and folded it again. She tied it with a length of thread. She did not put the chain over her head. She did not put it round her wrist, or on a finger, or anywhere against her skin. She put the wrapped thing into the inside pocket of her coat, on the right, where there was nothing else, away from the nine leaves and the Crown’s paper and the three grey pieces of a letter that said nothing on the left.

She did not say anything to it. She did not ask it anything. She thought, for one moment, standing there, of Na’ar’s leg bound in strips of white wrap, and of the old paragraph and what it had promised; and she put the thought down where she put things she had decided not to look at, and closed the pocket.

Nothing happened.

“Declared,” said Sada. She had her journal open on her knee and she was writing. “In the presence of the survey. One brass reliquary, round, hinged, with chain, containing a disc of green glass in a brass frame of leaves, with a flaw. Taken from a stone chest in a built room in the old chamber behind the sanctuary. Under the seal of the knot, which is retained.” She wrote. “Examined in place by Meret, Runekeeper of the Sealed Gate, who found no answer to any closing, no patron, no spirit, no invocation, no ward, no curse.” She looked up. “That’s what you found?”

“That’s what I found,” said Meret. “Write found none, not there is none.”

Sada wrote it.

“Held by Riarhin,” she said, “under the Crown’s custody, until the return to Adur-Gah.”

She closed the journal.

Kebede was already standing. He had the lamp. He did not look at the box, or at the lid on the floor, or at the inscription. He looked at the doorway, and at Na’ar in it.

“That’s the day,” he said.

They left the lid on the floor beside the open box. Meret wanted to put it back and Kebede said there was no time and he was right. They left the rough little room with its knotted seal cracked on the lintel, and the old chamber round it with its empty niches, and went back along the passage with the worn lines on its floor, Na’ar between Kebede and Sada with an arm over each of their shoulders. Meret went ahead with the lamp, and Sill rode on her shoulder with his claws in her robe, and Riarhin came last.

At the doorway she stopped for a moment and looked back.

The stone box lay open in the middle of the rough little room in the lamplight from the passage behind her, empty, with the fold of dust-coloured cloth in the bottom of it and the lid lying on the floor beside it with its two lines. Not all prizes are worthy. Not all treasures are sought. She had it now, the room and the box and the lines, in order, exactly, as she had everything she had looked at; and she did not know what it meant, and she was not going to, and she had it anyway.

She put her hand over the pocket on the right of her coat, once, from the outside. It was a small hard shape under the cloth, the size of a coin.

Then she turned round and went after the others, into the dark, toward the culvert and the court and the interval, which would come at dawn if it came at all.

Chapter Thirty: The Return Weight

They crossed the tilting floor along the wall.

It was the only way, and it was slow. Riarhin went first with the lamp, with her back flat to the old stone and her heels on the outermost slabs, the ones that met the wall and rested on it; and she stamped each one before she put her weight on it, the way Na’ar had stamped along the kerb under the sand. Kebede came behind her with Na’ar’s right arm over his shoulders, and Na’ar came crabwise along the wall on his good leg with his hand flat on the stone and his teeth shut, a step at a time. Sada was on his other side where there was room for her, which was not everywhere. Meret came last with the second lamp. The slab in the middle of the floor lay level and sound in the lamplight, exactly as it had lain when Riarhin first walked across it, and nobody looked at it.

It took them a long time. Nothing tilted.

The ward door was open. The slab had gone down into its slot in the floor when Sada put her application to it, and it was still down, and the doorway stood empty with the old letters on its lintel. Meret stopped under it for a moment with her eyes shut and her hand on the frame, and then opened her eyes and nodded.

“It’s the same,” she said. “It let a keeper in. It’s letting a keeper out. That’s what it’s for.”

They went through it. Nothing closed behind them.

The culvert was the worst of it.

It went down out of the east end of the inspection corridor through the foot of the inner wall, a low stone channel, and it was knee-deep in water from the counterweight tank. The water was cold and black and moving, slowly, going the same way they were going, east and down; and the roof of the culvert was so low that Kebede, who was the shortest of them, could not stand up in it. Three hundred paces, he had said. It was nearer four.

Na’ar could not bend double. His ribs would not let him. He went through it on his knees in the water, with his torn leg out behind him, dragging, and his hands on the floor of the channel under the water, and Kebede in front of him bent double with a hand back to take his wrist, and Riarhin behind him with her hands on his belt. He did not make any sound. Now and then he stopped, and they stopped, and waited in the black cold water in the dark, and then he said, “Yes,” and went on.

Riarhin, with her hands on his belt, felt him shaking. She did not say anything about it. There was nothing to say that would have been any use to him, and she knew he would rather she did not.

At the far end the culvert came up into the easternmost of the settling galleries, where the water poured out of it into the last basin and went away down the channel toward the court. They came up out of it one after another and lay on the stone at the side of the basin and did not move for a while.

Nothing had fallen. Nothing had shifted. The water in the culvert had been the water they had let into the tank themselves, going where they had sent it. The galleries were what they had been in the morning. The only thing in the whole of the building that had changed was the dust on the floors, where they had walked, and a lid lying on a floor forty paces behind a ward.

They waited in the intake court for the dawn.

It was a long night. The storm turned over above the court and the dust came down into it out of the dark, gently, and lay on them, and nobody slept. Na’ar lay on his back on a folded coat with his leg up on a pack and his hand on his side, and Riarhin and Kebede changed the binding on his leg by lamplight with the last clean strips of Sada’s wrap and water out of the packs, and the leg was angry and hot round the edges of the tear but not worse than it had been. Kebede measured the water in the packs, every one, with his hands, and told Riarhin the number.

It was above the line. It was the return allowance: the water they had carried in for coming out slowly, with somebody who could not go fast. It was enough.

Meret sat against the wall of the court with her drawing board on her knees and did not draw. She had the knotted seal on its piece of cut cord in her lap, and she turned it over now and then in her fingers and looked at it.

Sada sat beside Riarhin. She did not say anything for most of the night. Toward the end of it, when the stars were beginning to fade in the strip of sky over the court, she said, very low, “Is it doing anything?”

Riarhin put her hand on the outside of her coat, over the pocket on the right.

“No,” she said.

Sada nodded, and did not ask again.

The interval came late, and it came short, and they went along the cut with everybody on Na’ar.

Kebede and Riarhin had him between them, one on each side, with his arms over their shoulders. Sada went in front with a lamp and Meret behind with the other. Na’ar went on his good leg and on theirs, a hop and a step, a hop and a step, with his torn leg held off the ground and his breath coming in hisses through his teeth. Riarhin felt his weight come onto her shoulder at every hop, and it was a great deal of weight, and she took it.

They went under the arch into the cut. The storm was thin overhead, grey going to brown. The wind was down. Four hundred paces.

At two hundred the roar began to rise.

Nobody said anything. Riarhin felt Kebede on the other side of Na’ar change his grip, lower, taking more of the weight, and she did the same, and Na’ar said something short in Ykrul and began to hop faster, and it hurt him; she could feel how much in the way his arm went rigid across her shoulders. The first gust came along the walkway from the north, behind them, full of grit, and pushed them forward, and Sada in front stumbled and caught herself and did not look back.

A hundred.

The wind was hard now, behind them, coming out of the storm down the cut in gusts, and the grit was so thick in the air that Riarhin could not see Sada’s lamp. She could see the walls of the cut on either side of her, pale and close, and the walkway under her feet, and Na’ar’s bound leg swinging between her and Kebede, and nothing else. She counted steps. She did not know what she was counting toward.

And then there were steps going up, and hands reaching down out of the dust, and a voice she knew, calling in the sing-song voice that the camels liked.

Hailu and Zewdu were at the head of the cutting with the lamp and the water, where Kebede had told them to be.

They had been there every morning, at the hour before dawn, for three mornings. They had watched the storm thin and the cut stay empty and the storm thicken again, three times, and gone back to the waystation. They had been there this morning. And when five people came up out of the dust at the head of the steps, three of them carrying a fourth, the brothers did not wait for anybody to tell them anything.

Zewdu had Na’ar off Riarhin’s shoulder before she had got her own breath. He and Hailu took him between them, one under each arm, the way they would have taken a camel that had gone down in the sand, and walked him three paces back from the steps onto the gravel, out of the gusts, and laid him down; and Hailu had the binding off the leg and was looking at it in the lamplight while Riarhin was still standing at the top of the steps with her hands on her knees.

He said something short to his brother. Zewdu ran back toward the waystation.

“He’s gone for the salt and the clean cloth,” said Kebede. He was sitting on the gravel where he had put himself down, breathing hard, with the dust thick on his beard. “And the camel’s milk. They’ll clean it with salt water. It’ll hurt.” He wiped his face. “They know what they’re doing. They’ve done it on the salt road more than I have.”

Na’ar, lying on his back on the gravel with Hailu bent over his leg, turned his head and looked at Riarhin.

“They are going to put salt in it,” he said.

“I heard.”

“I would like it noted,” said Na’ar, “that I walked out.”

Riarhin sat down on the gravel beside him, all at once, because her legs had decided it for her. She put her hand on his shoulder. He did not say anything else, and neither did she, and after a while Zewdu came back at a run with the salt, and it hurt, and Na’ar said so, at length, in Ykrul, and Hailu laughed at him and went on.

Hailu and Zewdu had counted. Riarhin saw them do it, afterwards, when it was light, and the storm had thickened again behind them to a wall of turning brown, and the five of them were sitting in the court of the waystation drinking water out of the brothers’ skins. Zewdu went round the court and looked at each of them, one after another, with his lips moving. Then he went and stood by the grey camel with his hand on her neck for a while, and did not say anything to anybody.

They went south in the morning, and it was the same road going the other way, and it was not the same at all.

Na’ar rode. There was no question of him walking; Kebede put him on the grey camel, who had been carrying water and now carried him, and who had got her legs back on the kerb under the sand and did not seem to object to anything any more. The leg was bound in clean cloth soaked in salt water, and Hailu looked at it every evening and made Na’ar swear at him, and it did not get worse. The ribs he could do nothing about.

They were lighter. Kebede had seen to that. The fodder had gone out of the packs on the way north. Meret’s pannier of tablets had gone into the dark under a tilting floor. They carried nothing south that they had not carried north, except a small hard shape the size of a coin in an inside pocket.

And there was water every day and a half. Kebede had buried it on the way up, a skin at the foot of a cairn, with the top stone on; and every day and a half they came to a cairn, and dug, and there it was.

At every cairn, Riarhin knocked the top stone off.

She did it herself. Nobody asked her to and nobody offered. At every cairn they passed, after the skin had been dug up and loaded, she went to the flat rock where the three stones stood, one on two, and put her hand on the top stone and pushed it off, so that it lay on the rock beside the other two. It was the sign she had given Almaz in the yard at Beles, in front of twenty people and a clerk. If Getu finds a cairn with the top stone off, it means we’ve passed it going home.

She did it at the waystation north of the town that had burned. She did it at the crossroads, by the shrine that was an office. She did it at the dome. She did it at the cutting full of sand, and at the waystations beyond it, and every time she did it she stood for a moment afterwards with her hand on the rock and did not quite know why.

They saw the smoke on the evening of the ninth day south.

It was a thin grey column, going straight up into the still air of dusk, far off to the south, from the direction of the flat country beyond the first cache. Green wood. Riarhin knew it from the way it rose: thick and slow and pale, the way green wood burns, the way nobody would burn wood in a desert unless they wanted it to be seen.

Kebede stopped his camel and looked at it.

“Twenty-fifth day,” he said. “Or twenty-sixth. Getu. At the third cairn.” He did not smile, but something in his face let go. “That’s where they said they’d be.”

They came to the third cairn the next morning, the rock a day and a half north of Beles where Kebede had buried the first cache, and Getu was there. He was sitting on the rock with the younger Ridge-Watcher and two drivers from the station and four camels loaded with water, and a fire of green wood smouldering beside him in a ring of stones, and when he saw them coming across the gravel he stood up on the rock.

He did not wave. He stood on the rock with his hand over his eyes and watched them come. Riarhin could see him counting.

Five.

Two camels at the front, one with a litter pole lashed across its saddle sticking out on both sides, because Kebede had lashed it there on the first morning south without saying anything to anybody. A very big man on the grey camel with his leg bound out in front of him. A woman in front of the string, walking, in a plain dark dress with no wrap, because the wrap had gone in strips round the very big man’s leg; but Getu would know her. And at the back, a woman on foot with a short pale bow slung on her shoulder, curved back on itself at the ends, like nobody else’s.

Getu got down off the rock and came across the gravel at a run.

He went straight past Riarhin and Sada to the grey camel and looked up at Na’ar’s leg, and said something to the Ridge-Watcher, who was already coming with a skin; and then he turned round and looked at the rest of them, all five, one after another, the way Zewdu had looked at them in the court of the waystation.

And then he laughed. It burst out of him, the laugh of a boy of eighteen who has sat on a rock at the third cairn for two days lighting green wood at dusk and trying not to think about what he would say to his mother if he had to go home alone.

“He says,” said the Ridge-Watcher, who spoke the herders’ tongue, “that he knew it was you by the bow.”

They came down into Beles on the evening of the twenty-seventh day, in the last of the light, and Almaz was standing at the head of the track with her staff, where she had stood when they came down out of the highlands the first time.

She watched them come. She did not say anything. When they reached her she looked at each of them in turn, and at Na’ar’s leg, and at the litter pole across the camel’s saddle, and last at Riarhin, and at her cloak.

She said something to Berihun.

“She says,” said Berihun, “that she’s glad the woman in the red came back.”

Riarhin looked down at her cloak. It was red. It was the red of the dust of the flat country, the colour it had been since the first day north of Beles.

It had been the colour of juniper when she came down out of the highlands to Almaz’s wells. She had stood in this yard in it and told Almaz what she would look like coming back, and had not said the cloak, because she had known. And Almaz remembered it red now, and had always remembered it red, and was not wrong.

“Tell her thank you,” said Riarhin.

Mulu had Na’ar in the physician’s tent inside a quarter of an hour. She did not ask anybody’s leave. She looked at the leg and at the salt-water binding and said something sharp to Hailu about camel men, and then something grudging, and then she sent everybody out and got to work, and they heard Na’ar through the tent wall explaining to her in bad Ecktorian that he had walked out.

Kebede went round the water.

Meret sat down on the lip of the grandmother well in the last of the light with her drawing board on her knees and a fresh page, and began to write. Riarhin went and stood beside her for a moment and looked over her shoulder. At the top of the page, in her small neat hand, Meret had written the date, and under it: What could be established. And under that, a list, and the first thing on the list was the seven courses of dressed stone in the lining of the well she was sitting on.

She did not look up. Riarhin went away and left her to it.

She gave Sada the custody account that night.

She had written it on the road south, in the evenings, by the lamp, on the last of Tesfaye’s paper: everything. The box and the lid and the room built round it. The seal on the gate and the seal on the cord, and the seal kept, on its piece of cut cord, in Meret’s charge. The two lines cut in the grey stone, with Meret’s reading, and Meret’s note about the chipped negative and why the grammar required it. The brass case and the frame of leaves and the flaw like a hair, and the first description in the file that matched it, which she wrote out word for word in the clerk’s own spelling, from memory, because she had it. Meret’s examination, and what Meret had found, which was nothing, and exactly how Meret had said it: found none, not there is none. The ward and what Sada had said to it. The tilting floor and the tablets under it. The culvert. The cut.

She signed it. With her own name.

Sada read it by the lamp in her tent, all of it, slowly, and did not say anything until she had finished. Then she looked up.

“It’s all here.”

“It’s all there.”

“The thing itself?”

Riarhin put her hand into the inside pocket on the right of her coat and took out the small wrapped shape in its square of Tigist’s linen, tied with thread, and held it out on her palm in the lamplight, so that Sada could see it was there and was what it had been. She did not unwrap it. Sada looked at it for a long moment. Then she nodded, and Riarhin put it back.

“You carry it to Adur-Gah,” said Sada. “That’s what we agreed.”

“That’s what we agreed.”

“And then the Council.”

“And then the Council.” Riarhin sat down on the chest by the tent-flap. “I’m coming back with you. To Adur-Gah. With the others. Not ahead, not round by some other road.” She looked at her hands. “I’m not saying anything past that. I don’t know anything past that. I’m saying I’ll be on the road down from here with the rest of you, all the way, and I won’t leave at the first fork.”

Sada looked at her across the lamp for a long time.

“I know you won’t,” she said. “You’ve said it now.”

She looked at the pocket on the right of Riarhin’s coat for a moment.

“They’ll examine it,” she said. “Properly. The chancery, and Meret again, with the archive’s books open, and whoever else the Council trusts. If none of them finds anything either, I don’t think they’ll want to keep it. Dejen won’t have it in the north. Nobody on that bench is going to vote to be the fifth lot of people who tried to put it somewhere safe and failed.” She paused. “They’ll want it declared and carried out of Taharka by somebody who’ll sign for where it goes. That’s how I’d write it, if they asked me.”

Riarhin did not answer that. She found that her hand had gone, not to the right-hand pocket, but to the left, where three grey pieces of a letter lay folded together with nothing written on them any more; and she took it away again, and did not look at why.

There was an argument, and it had somewhere to happen, and it did not happen tonight. They both knew where it was: in Adur-Gah, in front of a Council, and after that on a set of steps, and after that somewhere neither of them could see yet. It would be about the thing in the pocket, and about the report, and about what came next, and whose next it was. It would not be settled quickly. Neither of them pretended otherwise.

Sada moved over on the blanket where she was sitting and made room.

Riarhin looked at it. She looked at it the way she looked at every room she came into: the two ways out, the tent-flap and the loose place at the back where the pegs had not been driven home; the lamp; the space on the blanket. She had been looking at rooms that way since an alley behind a cooper’s off Bell Street when she was twelve, and she had never once improved the criteria.

She got up off the chest and sat down in the space, and did not find a reason to stand again.

Na’ar came out of the physician’s tent at supper on a crutch Mulu had made him out of a tent pole, and stood at the edge of the fire and looked at the pot.

The station’s cook had made it. It was lentils, with onions, and it had been made well enough for twenty-three people who had been eating station food for four weeks. Na’ar looked at it for a long time with the expression of a man who has been badly let down by somebody he had hoped better of.

“You have put the onions in at the start,” he said.

The cook looked at him.

“They should go in at the end. Half of them. The other half at the start, for the sweetness, and half at the end, so that they are still onions.” Na’ar lowered himself onto a saddle beside the fire with enormous care and a great deal of breath through his teeth. “Give me the other onions.”

The cook gave him the other onions, and a knife, and after a moment, and some visible thought, a board. Na’ar began to cut. He did it sitting down with his leg out in front of him and one hand held against his side, and he did it slowly, and he talked the whole time about what was wrong with the lentils. The cook, who had been cooking for the station for four weeks and did not have to listen to any of it, sat down on the other side of the fire and listened to all of it.

Kebede, across the fire with his book open on his knee and the water written in it, looked at Na’ar’s leg for a while. Then he looked at his book. He crossed something out.

“Half a stage tomorrow,” he said, to nobody. “Not a whole one. As far as the first water on the goat track and no further.”

Nobody argued.

She lay down that night in Sada’s tent, on the blanket, in the dark, with Sada asleep beside her and the wind off the flat country moving the canvas and the camels groaning outside.

There was something under her hip. She shifted, and it was still there, and she put her hand down and found it through the cloth of her coat: a small hard shape, round, the size of a coin, in the inside pocket on the right, where she had rolled onto it.

She took it out, still wrapped in its square of linen, still tied with thread. She did not unwrap it. She held it for a moment in the dark. It was no warmer than her hand had made it. It was a brass case the size of a coin with a glass in it.

She put it down on the blanket by her head, on the other side from Sada, where she would not roll on it again, and turned over, and went to sleep.

THE END
